# **Commentary on Lacan, *The Four Fundamental Concepts of Psychoanalysis* (Seminar XI, 1964\)**

*Text established by Jacques-Alain Miller, Éditions du Seuil, 1973\. Cited by the Seuil printed pagination.*

The seminar comprises twenty lessons in five parts. Each lesson divides into Lacan's own numbered sections; the commentary treats those sections paragraph by paragraph, at equal depth. The keyword blocks under each lesson title are Miller's editorial *sommaires*, not Lacan's divisions — they are noted where they orient the reading but carry no headings. The "Réponses" closing several lessons are treated as terminal units of their lesson.

**The traps this text sets, guarded against throughout.** (1) The unconscious "structured like a language" is neither the Romantic depth of instinct nor Jung's imaginal reservoir nor the merely not-conscious; it is a pulsating gap that opens and closes, with no substantial persistence behind it. (2) The *objet petit a* is not the object desire aims at but the object that *causes* desire — the remainder the signifier's cut leaves behind; it must not be collapsed into the Kleinian part-object or the good object. (3) The *gaze* is not the act of looking; it is an object fallen on the side of the visible, looking back. (4) *Automaton* and *tuché* are not synonyms for chance: *automaton* is the insistence of the signifying network, *tuché* the encounter with the real that interrupts it. (5) Transference is not rapport or "the relationship"; the *sujet supposé savoir* is a structural function, not a report on what the analyst knows. (6) Alienation and separation are logical operations constituting the subject, not developmental stages. (7) Above all, "the subject" throughout is the subject of the unconscious — the barred subject of the signifier — never the ego, the person, or the self; hear "subject" psychologically and nearly every thesis in the book inverts.

Philosophical interlocutors are marked as they arise — Aristotle on cause, Descartes on certainty, Kant on negative magnitudes, Plato's *Symposium*, Hegel, Heidegger on aletheia, Merleau-Ponty on the visible, Spinoza — as are the psychoanalytic targets: Freud first, then the ego-psychology of Hartmann, Kris and Loewenstein, Kleinian object-relations, and the affective doctrine of transference Lacan takes his contemporaries to have put in Freud's place.

## **I. Excommunication**

*15 January 1964\.* The seminar does not open on a concept. It opens on the speaker's right to speak. Expelled from the International Psychoanalytical Association the previous autumn — his removal from the training roster was the negotiated price of his society's readmission — Lacan stands in a hall of the École normale, delegated by Fernand Braudel's section of the Hautes Études, brought there through Lévi-Strauss's vigilance, and describes himself as a *refugee*. The title says what the institution did without saying it: excommunication. And the apparently autobiographical preliminary — *by what am I authorized?* — is the seminar's first conceptual instrument, because a field in which authorization is granted and revoked by bodies that behave like churches is a field whose scientific status is exactly what is in question.

His ten-year seminar, he notes, was not a commentary on psychoanalysis but an element of it: directed at the formation of analysts, it belonged to the praxis itself. Foundations and transmission are therefore one question. Whoever asks what psychoanalysis *is* is already inside the machinery by which analysts are made — the machinery that just cast him out. He suspends, provisionally, whether the casting-out touches his qualification, and thanks Braudel and Flacelière with a pointed word: *nobility* is what it takes to receive a man in the position of a refugee.

He recalls his encyclopedia article — the one a directing committee of analysts fought to have withdrawn from Henri Ey's encyclopedia — where he defined psychoanalysis, ironically and for want of better, as *the treatment dispensed by a psychoanalyst*. The definition is circular on purpose: practice defined by practitioner, practitioner by practice. Either that circle confesses the field has no external criterion, or it accurately describes a field whose object is not the kind of object external criteria fit. The lesson works toward the second reading. The question it circles he calls, from that article, a *bat-question* (*question chauve-souris*) — neither bird nor mouse, to be examined in daylight: what is psychoanalysis?

**What specifies a science.** Is psychoanalysis a science? Lacan dismantles the ready criteria one by one. A science is said to have a defined object at a reproducible level of operation called experience — but the object of a science mutates across its history; the object of physics is not what it was at its seventeenth-century birth, nor chemistry's what it was at Lavoisier. So "an object" decides nothing. He refuses Duhem's requirement that a science refer to a unitary System of the World — a covertly idealist demand, resting on the need for identification — and dispenses with the positivist's implicit transcendent complement, the final unity of all fields. The tree of science need not have one trunk; perhaps, as in the first chapter of Genesis, there are two. The light touch on the myth conceals a serious opening: room is being cleared for a science discontinuous with physics — a second trunk — and psychoanalysis might even shed light on why there are two.

Experience as the field of a praxis fails as a criterion too, since it would admit mystical experience — and submitting an experience to scientific examination always insinuates that the experience has scientific standing of its own, which mysticism does not. Then the decisive counterexample: alchemy. Rereading a little opuscule he takes for Diderot's, Lacan asks what makes us say at once that alchemy, whatever the sparkle of its histories, is not a science. One thing is decisive: *the purity of the operator's soul* was, expressly and by name, an essential element of the operation. There is the criterion the whole lesson has been hunting: a science is a field from which the subjectivity of the operator has been subtracted. Where the state of the operator's soul conditions the result, there is no science yet.

The remark is not accessory, and Lacan says so: someone will raise the analogous point about the analyst's presence in the analytic Great Work — that the purity of the operator is exactly what training analysis pursues, and that Lacan himself, sailing openly toward the question he has made central, *what is the desire of the analyst?*, is saying the same thing. He does not defuse the parallel. On the classical criterion, the analyst's desire — internal to the operation — is precisely what would disqualify analysis from science. The seminar's wager is to refuse the disqualification without expelling the desire: to think a field in which the operator's desire is not an impurity but a structural function.

**The desire of the analyst.** What must the analyst's desire be for the operation to work correctly? In the securest modern sciences no one asks the analogous question; it takes crises for an Oppenheimer to make everyone interrogate the desire at the bottom of modern physics, and even then it is filed as a political incident. But the analyst's desire cannot be exiled from the field, because the problem of *training* poses it: the training analysis can serve nothing else than to lead the candidate to the point Lacan's algebra writes as the desire of the analyst. He leaves the question open — it will be the seminar's hidden pole through to the final lesson — but its place is now fixed: internal to the field's operation, which marks the field's peculiar scientific status rather than its simple failure.

He offers a calibrating analogy. Is agriculture a science? Yes and no — and the hesitation exposes a distinction: agriculture defined by an object versus agronomy defined by a field. This yields a further dimension, formalization, the *mise en formules*. But formalization does not settle it either: a false science formalizes as readily as a true one. Every quick criterion — object, experience, formulas — has now been tried and found insufficient, which forces the question onto the only terrain Lacan thinks decisive: the status of the field's concepts.

**The status of Freud's concepts.** What do psychoanalysis's formulas bear on? What drives the sliding of its object? Is it not astonishing, in the history of the sciences, that Freud should be the first *and have remained the only one* to introduce fundamental concepts into this supposed science? Without that trunk, that mast, that piling, where would practice moor? And are they even concepts — or concepts in formation, in movement, awaiting revision? The nautical figure is exact: a practice without conceptual moorage drifts, and the drift has a name — the psychoanalytic literature, that wearying, repellent chain no one but analysts reads, where Freud's terms are kept with near-religious fidelity while the concepts are falsified, adulterated, broken, and the difficult ones quietly pocketed. Fidelity of vocabulary, betrayal of concept: everything elaborated around *frustration*, for instance, is flatly retrograde and pre-conceptual next to the Freudian concepts it derives from. No one, outside rare exceptions in Lacan's circle, still troubles over the *ternary* structure of the Oedipus complex or over castration.

Nor does Fenichel's grand-collector enumeration secure a theoretical status — flattening the accumulated material of experience into chapters where everything seems explained in advance. Analysis is not the retrieval, in a case, of the theory's differential trait, followed by the belief that one has thereby explained *why your daughter is mute* — Lacan borrows Molière's sham doctor deliberately. Subsuming the case under a diagnostic tag explains nothing, because what is at stake is *making her speak*, and that effect proceeds from a type of intervention that owes nothing to the differential trait.

**Making the mute speak.** Analysis makes the subject speak; it could be summed up, at the limit, as the lifting of mutism — what was once called the analysis of resistances. The symptom is first of all mutism in the subject supposed to speak. If he speaks, he is cured of his mutism — but that tells us nothing about *why he began to speak*. The diagnostic trait (here, as expected, hysteria) and the dynamic event (the onset of speech) are two different things, and the naive reading fuses them. Cure is not correct classification; it is the passage into speech, whose cause the classification does not touch.

The hysteric's differential trait, though, is itself instructive: *it is in the very movement of speaking that the hysteric constitutes her desire*. No wonder Freud entered through that door into what was really at issue — the relations of desire to language — and there discovered the mechanisms of the unconscious. That this relation did not stay veiled from him is a mark of his genius; it does not mean he elucidated it fully — least of all through the *massive* notion of transference. The double judgment sets the seminar's program: credit Freud with the founding insight, locate where his own concepts fall short of it, and rebuild transference so that it stops obscuring the desire–language relation it was meant to handle.

The shortfall is precise. To cure the hysteric, the best way is to satisfy her hysteric's desire — which is to pose her desire *before our gaze* as unsatisfied desire. But this leaves wholly outside the field the specific question of *why she can sustain her desire only as unsatisfied*. Hysteria thus puts us on the trace of an *original sin* of analysis. There must be one, and it is perhaps a single thing: *Freud's own desire — the fact that something in Freud was never analyzed*. The gap in the theory points back to a gap in the founder. Lacan converts the doctrinal question (what is psychoanalysis?) into a genealogical one (what was the desire that opened the field?), and makes the second the condition of the first.

**The Names-of-the-Father.** This is exactly where he stood when, "by a singular coincidence," he had to resign his seminar. What he had to say on the Names-of-the-Father aimed at nothing else than to interrogate the origin: by what privilege Freud's desire found, in the field he named the unconscious, the door of entry. Going back to that origin is essential to setting analysis on its feet. The interrupted seminar of November 1963 — one session given, then silence — is not simply lost; its question is carried into this one, and the coincidence is itself a datum: the institution struck at the moment the founder's desire was being put in question.

The path forward is then announced: the mode of interrogation will be guided by asking what conceptual status to give four terms Freud introduced as fundamental — *the unconscious, repetition, transference, the drive* — and by considering how Lacan's past teaching situated all four in relation to one more general function that encompasses them and gives them their operative value: *the function of the signifier as such*, underlying, implicit. That single sentence is the architecture of the whole seminar. The four concepts are not four pillars; they are four faces of one operation.

He closes with a housekeeping rule that is not housekeeping: he will stop at twenty-to-two to leave room for questions. The Réponses are thereby instituted as part of the teaching — the seminar as a scene of address in which the hearers' desire is engaged, consistent with what Lesson II will say about public discourse touching subjects at the navel.

**Réponses.** Michel Tort asks whether referring psychoanalysis to Freud's desire and the hysteric's desire does not invite the charge of psychologism. Neither reference is psychological, Lacan answers. He restates the question underneath: Lévi-Strauss's *pensée sauvage*, set at the base of society's statutes, is an unconscious — but does it suffice to lodge the unconscious as such, and if so, does it lodge the *Freudian* unconscious? The path to the properly Freudian unconscious is the one the hysterics taught Freud; that is where the hysteric's desire was made to play, with the indication that Freud did not stop there. Two unconsciouses are thereby distinguished — the structural combinatory and the Freudian — and the next lesson makes their difference its explicit business.

Freud's desire he sets higher still: the Freudian field remains dependent on a certain original desire, ambiguous but prevalent in the transmission of psychoanalysis, and its problem is no more psychological than the unresolved problem of *Socrates' desire*. A whole thematics of the subject's status opens when Socrates says he knows nothing except what concerns desire — and Socrates puts desire not in the position of an original subjectivity but in the position of an *object*. It is likewise desire *as object* that is at stake in Freud. The parting formula seeds everything to come: desire approached as object points to the *objet a*, and Freud, like Socrates, founds a transmission on a desire that remains, at the origin, unanalyzed — the transference-to-the-founder that Part Four will formalize as the *sujet supposé savoir*.

## **The Unconscious and Repetition**

### **II. The Freudian Unconscious and Ours**

*22 January 1964\.* The title states the lesson's operation: to separate the Freudian unconscious from "ours" — the structural, combinatory unconscious that Lacan's own teaching, via Lévi-Strauss and linguistics, has made current. The first belongs to the second as an event belongs to an order: the combinatory establishes that there is something objectifiable under the word "unconscious"; the Freudian unconscious is what stumbles, trips, and flashes up inside it. Miller's keywords — *there is cause only in what limps; gap, stumbling, find, loss; discontinuity; Signorelli* — all name that event-character.

Lacan begins by reading Aragon's "Contre-chant" from *Le Fou d'Elsa*: the lover's image cannot enter the mirror-eye that only shows it; "my eye, like them, is empty and, like them, inhabited by the absence of you, which makes its blindness." He dedicates the poem to those nostalgic for the interrupted seminar on anxiety and the *objet a*. The choice is doctrinal, not ornamental: an eye that is empty, that reflects without seeing, inhabited by an absence — this is the scopic object, the gaze as object, which Part Two will develop; and Aragon's enigmatic coda ("Thus said An-Nadji, as he had been invited to a circumcision") lets Lacan point to the correspondence between the forms of the *objet a* and the central symbolic function of the *moins-phi* (−φ), castration. The lesson thus opens with a cipher of everything the excommunication interrupted.

**Against the refusal of the concept.** Some are entering his teaching now through writings already dated. They should know one coordinate: the degree of contempt, or simple misrecognition, that practitioners can reach toward their own instrument — speech. For years his effort went to revalorizing that instrument, restoring its dignity against its treatment as devalued currency. Hence the misunderstanding that he was haunted by some philosophy of language, "even Heideggerian": that reference was *propaedeutic* — scaffolding, not doctrine — and speaking now in a philosophers' hall will not make him speak more as a philosopher. What he attacks under its proper name is *the refusal of the concept*: the analytic community's resistance to thinking its field conceptually at all. Against that refusal stand the four major Freudian concepts. Two are on the blackboard — the unconscious and repetition; transference will follow, leading directly to the algorithms of technique; the drive is so difficult, so unbroached, that it can come only after transference. The ordering is an argument: from the concept most amenable to formalization to the one that most resists it.

The blackboard also carries two little arrows pointing from *the unconscious* and *repetition* to a question mark, and Lacan glosses them with a model: the concept, as he understands it, is formed the way the infinitesimal calculus forms its objects. A concept shapes itself in an approach to the reality it must grasp, but it achieves itself — realizes itself — only by a leap, a *passage to the limit*. The task is therefore to say in what the conceptual elaboration called "the unconscious" can be completed *as a finite quantity*; likewise repetition. This is the precise sense of "reading Freud's concepts at the limit" promised in Lesson I: not exegesis but the leap that converts an approximation into a concept. The other two terms on the board — *the subject* and *the real* — are the coordinates within which the science question will finally be posed.

**The unconscious before the subject.** The thesis most of the room knows — *the unconscious is structured like a language* — refers to a field today far more accessible than in Freud's time: the one Lévi-Strauss explores and has pinned with the title *pensée sauvage*. Before any experience, any individual deduction, even before the collective experiences referable to social needs, something organizes the field and inscribes its initial lines of force. That is the truth of the totemic function, reduced to its real operation: the primary classificatory function. Before properly human relations establish themselves, certain relations are already determined, taken up in whatever nature offers as supports arrayed in themes of opposition. Nature furnishes *signifiers*, and these signifiers inaugurally organize human relations, give them their structures, model them.

What matters is the level this reveals: before any subject who thinks and situates himself, *it counts, it is counted, and in the counted the counter is already there*. Only afterward must the subject recognize himself in it — recognize himself *as* the one counting. Hence the tester's stumble over the child who says: *I have three brothers, Paul, Ernest, and me*. The child is right twice. First the three are counted — Paul, Ernest, and me — and then, at another level, there is the *me* who counts and who is asked to reflect on the first. The subject of the signifier is split from the start between the counted "me" and the counting "me," and that split precedes every psychology of self-recognition. The counter is an effect of the count.

Linguistics — the human science of our historical moment, to be distinguished from all psycho-sociology — has for its model the combinatory play operating spontaneously, all alone, *pre-subjectively*. That structure is what gives the unconscious its status: it guarantees that under the term "unconscious" there is something qualifiable, accessible, objectifiable. But — and here the lesson pivots — when Lacan urges analysts onto this terrain, does he claim thereby to hold the concept Freud introduced under the same word? No. *The Freudian unconscious is something else.* The combinatory establishes the object's scientific legitimacy; it does not deliver the Freudian thing. What follows is the attempt to make the difference graspable.

**Cause against law.** Calling the unconscious a "dynamic" concept explains nothing: force is the commonest name for a place of opacity, mystery in general substituted for this mystery in particular. Lacan reaches instead for the function of *cause* — knowingly stepping onto mined philosophical ground. In Kant's *Essay on Negative Magnitudes* one can watch how closely the function of cause hugs a gap that has always resisted conceptual seizure: cause is there, in the end, unanalyzable — ungraspable by reason, since reason's rule (*Vernunftsregel*) is always some *Vergleichung*, some equivalence — while in the function of cause there remains an essential *béance* (the *Prolegomena*'s own term). Lacan is not doing philosophy, he insists, and will not adjudicate Aristotle's four causes; he needs only what Kant isolated — a residue in "cause" that no logic of equivalence absorbs. In that residue he will lodge the unconscious.

The distinction that carries everything: *cause is not law*. What is determining within a chain — action and reaction, say — holds in one piece; the falling body's mass is not the cause of what it receives back from its living force, it is integrated into the force that returns to dissolve its coherence. One term, no gap, except at the end. But wherever we genuinely say *cause*, something anti-conceptual, undefined remains. The phases of the moon are the cause of the tides — the word lives there. Miasmas are the cause of fever — meaning nothing, and precisely there: a hole, with something oscillating in the interval. Hence the formula: *there is cause only in what limps* (*il n'y a de cause que de ce qui cloche*). Where law closes the chain, no cause is needed; cause names the point where the chain fails to close.

The Freudian unconscious is situated at exactly that point: between the cause and what it affects there is always the limp. Whether the unconscious "determines" the neurosis is not the important thing — Freud washes his hands of it with a Pilate's gesture; let humoral determinants turn up some day, it is all the same to him. What the unconscious shows is *the gap through which the neurosis reconnects to a real* — a real that may itself be undetermined. In that gap something happens; plug the gap and the neurosis is perhaps not cured but becomes other — a simple infirmity, a *scar*, as Freud says: not the scar of the neurosis but *of the unconscious*. Etiology is thereby subordinated: the analytic stake is not the causal chain behind the symptom but the opening itself, which no eventual physiology would touch.

What does Freud find in the hole, the cleft, the gap characteristic of cause, starting from the aetiology of the neuroses? *Something of the order of the unrealized* (*non-réalisé*). Not refusal — that goes too fast, and no one now knows what he means by it. The unconscious first manifests as something waiting in the region of the *unborn*; that repression pours into it is no surprise — it is the abortionist's relation to limbo. The images are chosen for their danger: this dimension is neither unreal nor de-real but *unrealized*, and one never stirs the zone of larvae without risk. Perhaps it belongs to the analyst's position to be *besieged* — really — by those in whom he has evoked that world without always bringing them to the day. No discourse is inoffensive here, including the one Lacan has held for ten years: even in public one aims at subjects and touches them at what Freud calls the *navel* of the dream, the center of unknown — which is nothing else, like the anatomical navel that figures it, than the gap itself. Nietzsche knew that a certain discourse can address only the most distant. The theory folds back on the theorist: stirring the unconscious in public has effects, and Lesson I's siege — the excommunication — was one of them.

This whole dimension, Lacan says, was forgotten exactly as Freud predicted. The unconscious closed back over its message through the care of those active *orthopedists* that second- and third-generation analysts became, who by psychologizing the theory set about *suturing* the gap. The verb is the polemic: ego-psychology is not a rival doctrine but a surgical closure of the very opening Freud discovered. Lacan reopens it, he adds, only with precaution.

**The One of the rupture.** He is now positioned to introduce, into the domain of cause, *the law of the signifier* at the place where the gap is produced. But to complete the concept — to carry it to its limit — one must return to the times of its forging. The Freudian unconscious has nothing to do with the forms of unconscious that preceded, accompanied, or still surround it: open Lalande, read Dwelshauvers's enumeration of eight or ten unconsciouses that teach nobody anything, designating the not-conscious, the more-or-less conscious, with a thousand psychological varieties besides. It is not the Romantic unconscious of imagining creation, not the dwelling of the divinities of night — though not without relation to where Freud's gaze turns: Jung, relay of the Romantic terms, was repudiated by Freud, which says enough. And with Eduard von Hartmann's catch-all unconscious one must go slower, since Freud himself cites him in a note to Chapter VII of the *Traumdeutung* — the distinction must be made in the texts, not by fiat.

What Freud opposes to every unconscious affiliated with an obscure primordial will, with a before of consciousness, is the revelation that at the level of the unconscious there is something *in every point homologous with what happens at the level of the subject*: it speaks, and it functions as elaborately as at the level of the conscious — which thereby loses what seemed its privilege. Read "The Forgetting of Dreams" in Chapter VII: Freud refers to nothing there but plays of the signifier. This is the language-thesis at its Freudian source, and its edge is the dethronement of consciousness: not that the unconscious is a dimmer consciousness, but that articulation was never consciousness's property.

But Lacan will not rest on the massive reference; he has spelled the functioning out point by point. In the dream, the parapraxis, the joke, what strikes first is the mode of *stumbling* in which they appear: *achoppement, défaillance, fêlure* — impediment, failure, crack. In a spoken or written sentence something trips, and Freud is magnetized to these phenomena; there he seeks the unconscious. There something *else* demands to be realized — apparently intentional, but of a strange temporality: intentional, yet belonging to no present intention. What is produced in this gap — in the full sense of *production* — presents itself as *the find* (*la trouvaille*): at once a solution, however incomplete, carrying the accent Theodor Reik detached (only detached — Freud noted it first): *surprise*, by which the subject feels overtaken, finding at once more and less than he expected, and in any case something of unique price relative to expectation.

And the find, as soon as it presents itself, is a *re-find* (*retrouvaille*); moreover it is always ready to steal away again, instituting the dimension of *loss*. Lacan's figure: *Eurydice twice lost* — the most sensible image myth offers of the relation of Orpheus-the-analyst to the unconscious. What surfaces in the gap is not new but the return of what was always already lost, and turning toward it loses it again. Whence the ironic contrast: the unconscious stands at the strictly opposite edge from love, which is always unique, and to which "one lost, ten found" applies best — love disavows loss by substitution; the unconscious *is* loss, refinding only the irreplaceable and re-losing it.

*Discontinuity*, then, is the essential form in which the unconscious first appears as phenomenon — discontinuity in which something manifests as vacillation. And the decisive question: must this discontinuity, absolute and inaugural in Freud's path, be set — as the analysts afterward tended to set it — against the ground of a *totality*? Is the One prior to discontinuity? No: everything Lacan has taught turns that demand for a closed One aside — the mirage of the "envelope-psyche," a double of the organism where a false unity would reside. The One introduced by the experience of the unconscious is *the One of the cleft, of the trait, of rupture*. Here a misrecognized form of the One springs up: the One of the *Unbewusste*. The limit of the *Unbewusste* is the *Unbegriff* — not the non-concept, but *the concept of lack*. The German prefix does the work: as *un-* negates *bewusst*, so *Unbegriff* would read "non-concept" — and Lacan converts the negation into content. The unconscious's unity is not a whole with a hole in it; it is the unity *of* the hole.

Where then is the ground — absence? Not that either. The rupture, the cleft, the trait of the opening *makes absence surge up* — as the cry does not stand out against a ground of silence but, on the contrary, makes the silence surge up *as* silence. The inversion completes the logic of cause: no plenitude precedes the cut; the cut produces the very absence it seems to presuppose, as the cry produces the silence it breaks.

Hold that structure and you are protected from partializing the unconscious — even into so plausible a formula as "the subject alienated in his history, where the syncope of discourse conjoins with his desire." More radically the unconscious is to be situated in a *synchrony*: at the level of a being that can bear on anything — the level of the *subject of enunciation*, who, phrase by phrase, mode by mode, loses himself as much as he refinds himself; who in an interjection, an imperative, an invocation, even a lapse, is always the one posing you his enigma and speaking; the level at which everything that flowers in the unconscious spreads, like the *mycelium* Freud names apropos the dream, around a central point. *It is always a matter of the subject as indeterminate.* The diachronic subject-of-a-history is a partial aspect; the deeper register is synchronic, and its subject is the evanescent one who flickers in the very acts of speech.

**Effacement, censorship, Signorelli.** *Oblivium* — from *lēvis* with the long e: polished, smooth. Oblivion is what effaces — effaces what? *The signifier as such.* Here is the basal structure that makes it operatively possible for one thing to take the function of barring, striking out, another — a level structurally more primordial than repression, of which more later. This operative element of effacement is what Freud designated from the origin as *censorship*: the censor's scissors, the Russian blacking-out, or the German — see Heine at the opening of the book on Germany: "Herr and Frau So-and-so have the pleasure of announcing the birth of a child beautiful as *liberty*" — and Doctor Hoffmann, censor, strikes the word "liberty." What becomes of the word's effect through this properly material censorship is another problem — but *that* is what the dynamism of the unconscious bears on, most efficiently. Repression is thereby grounded in something simpler and more material than any guardian-of-morals: the signifier's capacity to bar a signifier, whose effect persists in the very strike-out.

Then the inexhaustible first example: Freud's forgetting of *Signorelli* after Orvieto. Is it possible not to see rising from the text itself, not the metaphor but the *reality* of disappearance, of *Unterdrückung*, suppression — passage into the depths? The term *Signor*, *Herr*, passes below: the absolute master — death, to say it — is what has disappeared there. Behind it, everything that necessitates Freud to find in the myths of the death of the father the regulation of his desire. Here he meets Nietzsche's "God is dead" — perhaps on the same grounds, for that myth (of which Lacan is far less sure than most contemporary intellectuals, *as myth* — neither theism nor faith in resurrection) is perhaps only *the shelter found against the threat of castration*. Read the Orvieto apocalypse frescoes if you can; if not, read the conversation in the train — it turns entirely on the end of sexual potency, and the interlocutor before whom the name will not return is precisely the doctor describing how dramatic that end is for his patients. The forgotten name, the Last Judgment on the walls, the talk of impotence: one field, organized by one suppressed signifier — the master, death, the father — with castration underneath.

So the unconscious always manifests as what vacillates in a *cut of the subject* — from which resurges a find that Freud assimilates to *desire*: desire to be situated, provisionally, in the bared *metonymy* of the discourse in question, where the subject seizes himself at some unexpected point. Desire runs along the chain; the subject catches himself in it where he did not expect to be.

The close returns to Freud's person, binding the lesson to Lesson I's "original sin." On the father, Freud's whole effort led him only to confess the question remained entire — *What does a woman want?* — never resolved, as his uxorious character (Jones's delicate word) attests. Freud would have made an admirable passionate idealist, had he not devoted himself to the other in the form of the hysteric. The founding scene repeats in summary: rescued from idealism by the hysteric, arrested before the question of feminine desire and the dead father — the unanalyzed remainder on which the field still turns. Stopping at his appointed time, Lacan notes he has not closed the function of the unconscious; the session's questions and answers are missing from the record.

### **III. On the Subject of Certainty**

*29 January 1964\.* The lesson makes the seminar's boldest pair of claims: the unconscious is neither being nor non-being — its status is finally *ethical*, not ontic — and Freud's procedure is *Cartesian*, founding a certainty on doubt itself. Lacan opens from Jacques-Alain Miller's intervention of the previous week: Miller had traced the structuring function of a lack through Lacan's earlier writings, joined it "by an audacious arc" to the *manque-à-être* of desire, and asked Lacan about his *ontology*. The question was apt, Lacan grants — because the gap by which he introduced the unconscious *is* an ontological function, though of a kind ontology cannot keep.

**Neither being nor non-being.** The gap of the unconscious may be called *pre-ontological*. Its first emergence — too forgotten, and not insignificantly — was of what does not lend itself to ontology: what showed itself to Freud, to the discoverers, and still shows itself to anyone in analysis who accommodates his gaze to it, is *neither being nor non-being but the unrealized*. The modal category of Lesson II is thereby raised to a principle: the unconscious cannot be filed under being (a hidden substance) or nothing (a mere absence); it occupies the register of the not-yet-realized — limbo again, or the intermediate beings of the Gnostics, sylphs and gnomes, ambiguous mediators.

Freud knew what he was opening. He set the *Traumdeutung* under the line *Flectere si nequeo superos, Acheronta movebo* — if I cannot bend the powers above, I will move Acheron — and it is remarkable that after sixty years the menace is completely forgotten: what announced itself as an infernal opening has been so thoroughly *aseptized*. Equally telling is what did *not* happen: the deliberate opening onto a lower world made almost no serious alliance with metapsychic research, spiritism, evocation, necromancy — Myers's "gothic psychology" trailing telepathy. Freud touched such facts only in the direction of an elegant, rationalist *reduction*; what today attaches, in the analytic circle, to phenomena sterilized under the label *psi* (Servadio's researches) is exceptional, even aberrant. Two errors are barred at once: re-mystifying the unconscious into the occult, and mistaking its later aseptization — the ego-psychological drying-out into a *herbier*, a specimen-book, a would-be natural classification — for its truth.

**Finitude of desire.** Against the traditional psychology that celebrates human desire as unmasterable and infinite — the print of some divine hoof — analytic experience states the *limited* function of desire. Desire, more than any other point of the human span, meets its limit somewhere. The distinction that carries this: pleasure is what limits the human span — the pleasure principle is a principle of *homeostasis* — whereas desire finds its ring, its fixed relation, its limit, and *sustains itself as such* in relation to that limit, crossing the threshold the pleasure principle imposes. Desire is not boundless yearning but a function constituted against a limit it transgresses; its place is structurally "beyond the pleasure principle." Nor is it Freud's personal quirk to repudiate the *oceanic aspiration* in religious sentiment: experience reduces that aspiration to a fantasy and files it where Freud filed religion — under *illusion*.

**The evasive.** What is *ontic* in the function of the unconscious is only this: the cleft through which something, whose adventure in our field seems so short, is for an instant brought to the day — an instant, because the second phase, closure, gives the seizure a vanishing aspect. Our technical habits have grown so touchy about the functions of time (for reasons yet to be analyzed) that distinctions essential everywhere else feel, in this discipline, like special pleading. Yet at the level of Freud's own working definition — built by touches, apropos the primary process — what happens in the unconscious is inaccessible to contradiction, to spatio-temporal localization, and to the function of time. The classical ontology of enduring substances therefore has no grip on it.

Whence a genuine antinomy. Desire only ferries toward an always short, always limited future what it sustains from an image of the past — yet Freud calls desire *indestructible*. Indestructibility affirmed of the most inconsistent of realities: if indestructible desire escapes time, in what register of the order of things does it stand, since a thing is precisely what endures, identical, for a time? The answer requires distinguishing, beside duration — the substance of things — another mode of time: *logical time*, which Lacan had articulated in his early paper on the sophism of the three prisoners. The indestructible-yet-inconsistent finds its register there: the vanishing apparition occurs *between* the two terms of logical time — between the *instant of seeing*, where something of intuition itself is always elided, even lost, and the elusive *moment of concluding*, where the seizure of the unconscious does not conclude, where recovery is always a deluded recovery. Ontically, then, the unconscious is *the evasive* — but it can be circumscribed in a temporal structure never before articulated as such. What being refuses, structure captures.

**Gewissheit.** \[The lesson's second movement turns to Chapter VII of the *Traumdeutung*, "The Forgetting of Dreams."\] Freud, at the most fascinating point of his material, deliberately turns us away from it — he does not exploit; he weighs, appraises, tastes — to enter a discussion of the forgetting of the dream and the value of its transmission by the subject. And the major term of that debate is not *truth*. It is *Gewissheit*, certainty. *Freud's procedure is Cartesian*: it sets out from the foundation of the subject of certainty — from what one can be certain of. To that end the first thing to overcome is what connotes the whole content of the unconscious wherever it must emerge from the dream — what floats everywhere, punctuates, spots, stains every dream-report: *I am not sure, I doubt*. And who would not doubt, given the manifest abyss between the dream lived and the dream told?

Then the stroke on which Freud puts all his force: *doubt is the support of his certainty*. He motivates it: doubt is the sign that there is *something to preserve* — doubt is the sign of resistance. The function is admittedly ambiguous, since what is to be preserved may equally be what has to show itself — whatever shows, shows only under a *Verkleidung*, a disguise, a false front that may sit badly. But the convergence Lacan is after stands: Descartes says, *I am assured, from the fact that I doubt, that I think* — or, in the formula Lacan prefers, no more prudent but sparing us the debate over the *I think*: *from thinking, I am* (*de penser, je suis*). In eluding the "I think," he notes in passing, he eludes the discussion that follows from the fact that the "I think" cannot be detached from its being formulated *to us*, implicitly — which Descartes forgets. The reservation is a loaded gun: the cogito is an enunciation addressed to an Other, and that forgotten address is exactly where Lacan will later split the Cartesian subject.

Analogically, exactly: Freud, where *he* doubts — for these are his dreams, and he is at first the doubter — is assured that *a thought is there*, an unconscious thought, meaning one that reveals itself *as absent*. To that place, as soon as he deals with others, he summons the "I think" through which the subject will be revealed. In sum, he is sure this thought is there, all alone with all its "I am," so to speak — provided, and this is the leap, *someone thinks in his place*. Then the dissymmetry with Descartes: not in the initial procedure of founded certainty, but in this — that in the field of the unconscious, *the subject is at home* (*chez lui*). The Cartesian form is kept and its content inverted: what is certain is a thought present as absent, owned by no one, thought in the subject's place. The certainty founds not the transparent ego but the split subject. Miller's *sommaire* says it in four words — *everything is to be redone in theory* — beginning with what "subject" means.

**Freud's Cartesianism and the God who guarantees.** In the inaugural cogito — Lacan advances this for discussion, expecting the Cartesians to concede it — what the *I think* aims at, as it tips over into the *I am*, is a *real*. But the true remains so far outside that Descartes must then assure himself of an Other who does not deceive — an Other whose mere existence guarantees the bases of truth: guarantees that his own objective reason contains what is needed for the real just secured to attain the dimension of truth. The consequence of this handing-over of truth to the Other — here the perfect God, whose affair truth is, since whatever he had willed to say would have been the truth; had he said two and two make five, it would have been true — was prodigious: *we* can now begin to play with the little letters of algebra that turn geometry into analysis; the door opens to set theory; anything may be permitted as a hypothesis of truth. Lacan's reading of the Cartesian God is functional, not pious: by absorbing truth entirely, the guarantor releases the field of the letter from every prior necessity — the condition of modern formal science. The subject of Descartes is the subject of that science; and what begins at the level of the subject is never without consequence, *provided we know what the term means*. Descartes did not, except that it was the subject of a certainty and the rejection of all prior knowledge. We know, thanks to Freud, that the subject of the unconscious manifests itself — that *it thinks* before entering certainty. We have that on our hands; it is our embarrassment; and it is henceforth a field that cannot be refused.

**The deceived Other.** From this point the correlate of the subject is no longer the *deceiving* Other of the Second Meditation but the *deceived* Other. Analysis touches this concretely at once: what the subject fears most is deceiving us, setting us on a false track — or simply that we, whose faces show it plainly enough, err like anyone. Descartes needed a God who could not deceive; the analyst is an Other who can be deceived, and the patient's anxiety orbits exactly that. It does not trouble Freud. As the first paragraph on the forgetting of dreams must be understood: the signs cross-check; everything must be counted; one must free oneself — *sich frei machen* — from the whole scale of appraisal, *Preisschätzung*, of the sure and the unsure. The frailest indication that something enters the field must be held of *equal value as a trace* concerning the subject. There is the analytic rule in its purity: the reliability of the report is irrelevant; certain or uncertain, every element counts equally as signifier. The Other's fallibility does not undermine the operation because the operation never rested on sincerity.

The limit case proves it. In the observation of the young homosexual woman, Freud mocks those who say: where now is your famous unconscious, road to a *divine* truth, when the patient laughs at you — making dreams in analysis expressly to persuade you she is coming round to men? Freud sees no objection: *the unconscious is not the dream.* In his mouth that means the unconscious can operate in the direction of deception, and this has no value as objection — for how would there not be a *truth of the lie*? The truth that makes it perfectly possible, against the alleged paradox, to assert *I am lying*. The lying dream does not refute the unconscious; deception is one of the unconscious's own vectors, and its truth persists through the lie — the register in which "I am lying" is sayable and true.

**Dora and the young homosexual woman.** Where Freud did fail, on that occasion, was in formulating correctly the *object* of the hysteric's desire and of the homosexual's desire — and by that failure, with Dora as with the homosexual, he let himself be overtaken and the treatment broke off, his own interpretation hesitant, a little too early, a little too late. Lacking the structural markers Lacan hopes to supply, Freud could not see what blazes from the case history: *the hysteric's desire is to sustain the desire of the father* — in Dora's case, to sustain it by proxy. Dora's flagrant complaisance toward her father's affair with Frau K., her letting Herr K. court her, is exactly the game by which it is *the man's desire* she must keep in play. And the passage to the act — the slap — comes at the precise signifier: not when Herr K. says "I am not interested in you," but when he says *"my wife is nothing to me."* The third term must be preserved, because only through it does desire subsist — desire in every case *unsatisfied*: the father's, which she fosters precisely as impotent; and her own, unable to be realized *as desire of the Other*. The case verifies the formula born from hysteria and here set at its proper level: *man's desire is the desire of the Other* — the hysteric maintains desire as unsatisfied by triangulating it.

The young homosexual finds the other solution to the same term, the father's desire: *defy it*. Reread the case: the whole conduct is showy provocation — attaching herself to a demimondaine of established reputation, parading her chivalrous devotions — until the day she crosses her father and reads in his gaze evasion, contempt, the annulment of what is played out before him; whereupon she throws herself over the parapet of the railway bridge. Literally, she can no longer conceive otherwise than by self-abolition the function she had: showing the father *how one is, oneself, an abstract, heroic, unique phallus consecrated to the service of a lady*. Her position was constituted entirely in the father's gaze; annulled there, it can complete itself only as fall — Freud's own text puns her *niederkommen*, to fall and to be delivered of a child. And the lying dreams are one more move in the same game: a defiance concerning the father's desire — *you want me to like men? You shall have all the dreams of loving men you please* — challenge in the form of derision. Even the deception of the analyst is structured by the desire of the Other.

Lacan has pushed the digression this far only to fix the position of Freud's procedure with respect to *the subject* — since it is the subject that is at stake in the field of the unconscious — and thereby to distinguish the function of the subject of *certainty* from the search for *truth*. Next time, repetition: how Freud coordinates experience, as *disappointing*, with a real henceforth situated — in the field of science — as *what the subject is condemned to miss, but whose very missing reveals it*. The formula pre-loads the *tuché*: the real circumscribed not by what the subject reaches but by the structure of his failures to reach it.

**Réponses.** Asked whether logical time and the time-substance of things are not after all identical, Lacan recalls the three moments — the *instant of seeing* (not without mystery, though roughly captured in the psychology of *insight*), the *time for understanding*, the *moment of concluding* — and adds the operative premise: at the outset *the signifying battery is given*. On that basis two terms must be introduced, which the function of repetition will require — chance and the arbitrary, which he tags with Freud's German, *Willkür* and *Zufall*: the hazard of the dream's transcription and the arbitrariness of its connections (why relate this to that rather than to anything else?). \[Lacan's assignment inverts the ordinary values of the two German words — *Willkür* is standardly "arbitrariness," *Zufall* "chance" — without damage to the point.\] Freud thereby carries us to the heart of what modern science poses: what can be founded on chance. And the answer: nothing is founded on chance — no calculus of probabilities, no strategy — that does not presuppose a *limited structuration of the situation in terms of signifiers*. Game theory works because the map is already drawn: the signifying reference-points are inscribed, and no solution exceeds them. So with the unconscious: Freud reduces everything that comes within earshot to the function of *pure signifiers*, and from this reduction a *moment of concluding* can appear — the moment he finds the courage to judge. This belongs to what Lacan has called Freud's *ethical testimony*: the "ethical status of the unconscious" is cashed in the act of concluding, the risk of judgment taken on the signifying reduction alone.

Experience then teaches Freud limits on the subject's side — non-conviction, resistance, non-cure. Recollection always has a limit; other routes (hypnosis, say) can push it further, but they are inoperative as to cure. Hence the true relation of *recollection* and *repetition*: between them, no temporal orientation, no reversibility — simply, *they do not commute*. Beginning with recollection and running into repetition's resistances is not the same as beginning with repetition to prime recollection. And non-commutativity is a category that belongs only to the register of the signifier — proof that the time-function here is logical, tied to a signifying formatting of the real. The clinical primacy of repetition is thereby grounded in a formal property, and the road is open to formulating repetition through Aristotle's *Physics*.

To Pierre Kaufmann — relating last year's thesis, *anxiety is what does not deceive*, to ontology and certainty — Lacan answers that anxiety is the crucial reference precisely because it does not deceive; but anxiety can be *lacking*, and in practice it must be channeled, dosed, lest it submerge — a difficulty correlative to that of conjoining the subject with the *real*, the term he will try to circumscribe next time against the ambiguity persisting among his students. What can ratify, for the analyst, what happens in the unconscious? Freud localizes truth by a certain signifying scansion, and what justifies that confidence is a reference to the real — but the real does not yield easily. Witness the Wolf Man, whose exceptional importance in Freud's œuvre is to show that the plane of fantasy functions *in relation to* the real: *the real supports the fantasy, the fantasy protects the real.* To elucidate that relation he will next take up the Spinozan cogitation — substituting another term for the *attribute*. The closing promise marks the distance from Spinoza already opened by the *cogitatio adaequata* to come: where Spinoza's attribute subsumes thought under the one substance, the term to be substituted is the one nothing subsumes — the real, and the object that shields it.

### **IV. On the Network of Signifiers**

*5 February 1964\.* The lesson runs in two movements: first, the demonstration that Freud's own texts — Chapter VII of the *Traumdeutung*, Letter 52 to Fliess — already articulate the unconscious as a network of signifiers, so that Lacan's theory is a reading and not an import; second, laid on the table "with no cards up the sleeve," the first formulation of *repetition* as the relation of thought to the real. Between them stands the corrected translation of *Wo es war, soll Ich werden*, the seminar's charter-text.

Lacan opens with an announcement in place of the ski holiday the snowless winter has cancelled: the travel agency thanked him warmly, having just booked a journey for *eight members of the Société française de psychanalyse* — eight of its most eminent teachers, off to London to discuss how to parry the effects of his teaching. He reports the good deed in the Gospel's terms — the left hand must not know what the right is doing — and wonders aloud whether the British society covered the costs, as his own once covered those of visitors who came to take a close interest in its functioning. The sarcasm keeps the frame of Lesson I in view: the teaching proceeds under active institutional counter-measure, and he says he makes the announcement so that songs of gratitude may cover certain small signs of nervousness in the room.

**The subject at the place of the cut.** Résumé, with a raise: last time the concept of the unconscious was put in inaugural relation with the *Unbegriff* — the *Begriff* of the originary *Un*, namely the *cut* — and the cut was bound to the function of the subject in its constituting relation to the signifier. That it seems new to invoke *the subject* apropos the unconscious is granted; but everything happens at one place, the place of the subject — the point to which the Cartesian experience reduced the foundation of inaugural certainty, and which thereby took on an *Archimedean* value: the fulcrum on which science pivoted into its wholly new direction, namely from Newton on. The identification is now explicit and load-bearing: the subject of the unconscious is the Cartesian point-subject of modern science, and the unconscious sits at the cut where that subject meets the signifier. There is no psychological remainder in the word.

He has insisted throughout on the *pulsative* function of the unconscious — the vanishing that seems inherent to it, whatever appears in its cleft seeming destined by a kind of pre-emption to close again, to slip away (Freud's own metaphor). And he states the hope this pulsation carries: that from it may be renewed the trenchant, decisive crystallization already produced once in physics — this time in another direction, *the conjectural science of the subject*. Less paradox than it seems: the object's mode of being (flash and closure) is not an obstacle to science but the very form a science of the subject must take.

**Gedanken.** When Freud understood that the dream-field would confirm what the hysterics had taught him, and advanced with unprecedented boldness, what did he say the unconscious was? Constituted essentially not by what consciousness can evoke, extend, repair, draw up from the subliminal, but by what is *by essence refused* to consciousness. And his name for the refused is Descartes' own word for his fulcrum: *Gedanken* — thoughts. There are thoughts in the field beyond consciousness, and they can be represented only in the same homology of determination in which the subject of the *I think* stands to the articulation of the *I doubt*. The convergence is literal, not decorative: the unconscious is a field of thoughts without an owner — thinking that no cogito appropriates — which is exactly the split the cogito's own structure harbors.

Descartes seizes his *I think* in the *enunciation* of the *I doubt*, not in its statement, which still hauls all the knowledge to be doubted. Freud goes a step further — and the step legitimates the association of the two names: he invites us to integrate into the text of the dream what Lacan christens *the colophon of doubt* — the little printed hand that old typography set in the margin to point. *The colophon of doubt is part of the text.* Wherever the dreamer says "I'm not sure," the hesitation is not noise around the message; it is another sign within it, and often the pointing one. Which shows where Freud lodges his certainty: in the *constellation of signifiers* as they result from narrative, commentary, association — retraction included, since retraction too furnishes signifier. Everything supplies material for the *Gewissheit* that is *his*; the experience begins only with his procedure — this is why it deserves the name Cartesian.

Not that Freud introduces the subject into the world: that was Descartes — the subject as distinct from the psychic function, which is a myth, a confused nebula. Freud's novelty is what he *addresses to* the subject: *here, in the field of the dream, you are at home* — *Wo es war, soll Ich werden.* Not, as "some filth of a translation" renders it, *the ego must dislodge the id*: a formula whose resonance equals the pre-Socratics, flattened into ego-hygiene. The *Ich* under Freud's pen, start to finish — when one knows how to recognize its place — is the complete, total *place of the network of signifiers*: the subject. Where *it* was — where the dream was, from all time — there the subject is to come to be. In that place the ancients recognized, on occasion, messages of the gods — and why should they have been wrong? Perhaps the gods are even still there; it is all the same to us. What interests us is the *tissue* that envelops the messages, the network in which, on occasion, something is caught. The gods' voice may sound on; our ears have long been returned, in their regard, to their original state — made not to hear. But the subject is there to find his way back to where *it* was — Lacan anticipates — *the real*: as those who have followed him know, *the gods belong to the field of the real*. The anticipation is the bridge to everything that follows: "where it was" will be re-read, from Lesson V on, as the place of the missed encounter.

Where it was, the *Ich* — the subject, not psychology — *must come to be*. And there is exactly one method for knowing one is there: *spot the network*. How is a network spotted? By the fact that one returns, comes back, crosses one's own path — that *it recuts, always, in the same way*. In all of Chapter VII there is no other confirmation of Freud's *Gewissheit* than this: speak of chance, gentlemen, if it pleases you; in my experience I find nothing arbitrary here, for it cross-checks in a way that *escapes* chance. Certainty rests on recurrence, not resemblance — on the signifying network's insistence on recrossing itself, which is what defeats the appeal to hazard. This is the operational content of "structured like a language."

**Letter 52: the signifiers before the letter.** Lacan re-evokes the letter to Fliess of 6 December 1896, commenting the scheme that the *Traumdeutung* will present as "optical": layered strata traversed by something like light, refracted differently layer to layer — the locus where the affair of the subject of the unconscious is played. Not a spatial, anatomical locus, Freud says — how else conceive it as presented: an immense spread, a special spectrum situated *between perception and consciousness*, as one says between skin and flesh (*entre cuir et chair*). Those two terms will later be soldered into the perception-consciousness system of the second topography — but do not then forget the *interval* that separates them, for in that interval is *the place of the Other, where the subject is constituted*. The reading converts neurology into topology: the essential datum of Freud's scheme is the *between*, and the between is where the Other and the subject will be lodged — the gap that Part Four formalizes as alienation.

How do the *Wahrnehmungszeichen*, the perception-signs, function there? Freud deduces from experience the necessity of *absolutely* separating perception and consciousness: for something to pass into memory, it must first be *effaced* in perception, and reciprocally. And he designates a stage where these perception-signs are constituted *in simultaneity*. What is that, if not *signifying synchrony*? Freud says it all the better for not knowing he is saying it fifty years before the linguists — so we may give the *Wahrnehmungszeichen* straight off their true name: *signifiers*. The reading is doubly secured: returning to the locus in the *Traumdeutung*, Freud designates further strata where traces are constituted by *analogy* — the functions of contrast and similitude essential to the constitution of *metaphor*, which for its part is introduced from a *diachrony*. The whole Saussurean-Jakobsonian apparatus — synchrony, diachrony, the metaphoric axis — is found already drawn in Freud's 1896 stratigraphy; Lacan's linguistics is exhibited as exegesis.

One precision before moving on: this synchrony is not a closed network of associations by chance and contiguity. The signifiers could constitute themselves in simultaneity only owing to a very definite structure of *constituting diachrony* — *diachrony is oriented by structure*. And Freud himself bars the miracle: at the last stratum of the unconscious, where the diaphragm operates and the pre-relations of primary process to preconscious are set, *it must, he says, have to do with causality*. So the loop closes on Lesson II: the network is under the regime of cause — the limping cause — and the cross-checks that ground certainty are its marks, not chance's. Whence our own Ariadne's threads, Lacan adds: we read these texts before giving our theory of the signifier, without always understanding them on the spot; that we then found the enigmatic, unexplained indication of the *causal gap* at the heart of Freud's text is the mark that we progress along the path of *his* certainty. And he ends the movement on the formula that seals it: *the subject of certainty is here divided — the certainty is Freud's.* The one who is certain and the one about whom there is certainty are not the same subject; certainty belongs to the founder's act, division to the subject it discovers.

**Is psychoanalysis, already, a science?** What distinguishes modern science from science at its dawn — the science discussed in the *Theaetetus* — is that at the dawn *a master is always present*. Freud, no doubt, is a master. But if the analytic literature is not sheer buffoonery, it still functions entirely under him as master — which poses whether that pedicle can ever be lightened. Psychoanalysis's diagnosis by its own criterion: a field still at the dawn-stage, organized by the presence of its master — continuous with Lesson I's unanalyzed desire of the founder, and awaiting Part Four's account of the *sujet supposé savoir* to say what "master" structurally means.

Facing Freud's certainty stands the subject — who, Lacan dares to state as a truth, *has been waiting there since Descartes*: the Freudian field became possible only a certain time after the emergence of the Cartesian subject, because modern science begins only with Descartes' inaugural step. On that step depends the very possibility of calling the subject home into the unconscious — *for it matters to know whom one calls*. Not the soul of the ages, mortal or immortal; not shade, double, phantom; not some psychosphere-carapace, seat of defenses and other schematisms. *It is the subject who is called* — and therefore only he can be chosen: many called, few chosen, as in the parable, but none chosen who were not called. To understand the Freudian concepts one must start from this foundation: the one called is the subject — of Cartesian origin.

That foundation gives *recollection* its true function. Analytic *remémoration* is not Platonic reminiscence — not the return of a form, an imprint, an *eidos* of the beautiful and the good descending from a supreme true beyond. It is something *humble*: born at the level of the lowest encounters, of the whole speaking throng that precedes us, of the structure of the signifier, of languages spoken stumblingly — and whose constraints find their echoes, their model, their style, curiously, in today's mathematics. Anamnesis is thus de-Platonized in one stroke: what returns in analysis descends not from the intelligible but from the inherited babble of the signifying chain, whose laws are those of a combinatory.

**Wiederkehr, and where certainty really comes from.** The function of *return*, *Wiederkehr*, is essential — and not only as return of the repressed: *the very constitution of the field of the unconscious is assured by the Wiederkehr*; there Freud anchors his certainty. But it is evident the certainty does not *come* to him from there. It comes from his recognizing *the law of his own desire* — he could not have advanced on his wager of certainty had he not been guided, as the texts attest, by his self-analysis. And what is Freud's self-analysis, if not the inspired mapping of the law of desire as suspended from *the Name-of-the-Father*? Freud advances sustained by a certain relation to his desire, and by what is his *act*: the constitution of psychoanalysis. Lesson I's genealogy here receives its exact statement — the epistemic ground of the field (recurrence of the network) and its historical ground (the founder's desire, mapped onto the dead father) do not coincide, and the second is what made the first available.

Reluctant to leave the terrain, Lacan adds one indication of how far Freud identifies subject and signifier: the Freudian notion of *hallucination* as regressive investment of perception necessarily implies a subject *completely subverted* — which in fact occurs only in extremely fleeting moments. The account leaves untouched hallucination proper, in which the subject does not believe and does not recognize himself implicated; and it may be only a mythical pinning — Freud speaks too quickly of confusional hallucinatory psychosis as perceptual regression of arrested desire. But that Freud can so much as *conceive* the subject's total subversion shows how far he identifies the subject with what is *originally subverted by the system of the signifier*. Even where Freud's particular theory fails, its very possibility testifies to the deeper thesis.

**Repetition, laid on the table.** Leaving the time of the unconscious: what follows is new enough — though secured by everything articulated of the signifier — that Lacan states it at once, no cards held back. The function of repetition has *nothing to do* with the open or closed circuits of the *Wiederkehr* just discussed. Freud — does not introduce, but for the first time *articulates* — repetition in the 1914 paper *Erinnern, Wiederholen und Durcharbeiten*: the text on which analysis founded its greatest stupidity, culminating in Chapter V of *Jenseits des Lustprinzips*. Read that chapter line by line — in any language but French; the English, for those without German, will amuse: the uniform rendering of *Trieb* by *instinct* installs the whole Standard Edition "on the plane of absolute contresens," since *Trieb* and instinct have nothing in common — and in this text the discord becomes so impossible that the translator cannot finish a sentence with *instinctual* and must add a note conceding that *Trieb* conveys the urgency better. *Le Trieb vous pousse plus au cul, mes petits amis* — the drive shoves you from behind; that is its whole difference from so-called instinct. "That is how psychoanalytic teaching is transmitted." The philological joke banks a thesis for Part Three: the drive is a montage foreign to any biological finality, and the translation that erases the difference is one more suture.

How does *Wiederholen* enter? In relation to *Erinnerung*, recollection. The subject at home in the signifying network, the re-memorialization of the biography — all of it works *up to a limit, and the limit is called the real*. Lacan forges a Spinozan formula for it: *cogitatio adaequata semper vitat eamdem rem* — adequate thought, as thought, at the level where we are, always avoids the same thing (be it to refind it, afterward, in everything). *The real is what always returns to the same place* — to the place where the subject, insofar as he cogitates, the *res cogitans*, does not meet it. The definition inverts Spinoza's adequation from within: where the adequate idea was to coincide with its object, adequate thought here structurally *avoids* its *res* — and the real is defined by that avoidance, as the fixed place thought circles without occupying. Lesson III's promise ("what the subject is condemned to miss") is now a formula.

The whole history of Freud's discovery of repetition as a function is defined by this pointing of the relation between thought and the real. It was beautiful at the start, with the hysterics — how convincing recollection was in the first hysterics\! But what was at work in that recollection could not be known at the outset: not yet knowing that the hysteric's desire is the father's desire to be sustained, one could hardly wonder that, for the benefit of him who occupies the father's place, things got remembered *to the dregs*. The cathartic golden age is thus explained away structurally: recollection flowed as a gift to the analyst-in-the-father's-place — transference avant la lettre, not a royal road.

Fix this in Freud's texts, where there is never a wobble: *repetition is not reproduction* — *Wiederholen* is not *Reproduzieren*. Reproduction is what the age of catharsis believed possible: the primal scene *in reproduction*, the way one has the old masters in reproduction at nine francs fifty. Freud's very next steps establish that nothing is seized, destroyed, or burned except symbolically — *in effigie, in absentia*. And yet repetition first appears in a form that is anything but clear: as reproduction, or presentification, *in act*. This is why *The act*, with a large question mark, stands at the bottom of the blackboard: it will remain on the horizon as long as repetition's relations with the real are in question.

Curiously, neither Freud nor any epigone ever tried to recollect what everyone has within reach concerning the act — the human act, since to our knowledge only man acts. *Why is an act not a behavior?* Take the unambiguous case: opening one's belly under certain conditions — not "hara-kiri"; the name is *seppuku*. Why do they do it? Because they believe it vexes the others; because, in the structure, it is an act done *in honor of something*. Do not rush; retain only this: a true act always has a part of structure, in that it concerns *a real that is not self-evidently caught up in it*. The act is severed from behavior by its reference to a real it addresses without exhibiting — which is why the act and the real keep to the same horizon.

**Traumatic repetition and the divided kingdom.** Nothing has posed more of an enigma — above all against the pleasure/reality bipartition that structures all Freudian psychology — than this *Wiederholen*: near kin, the soberest etymologists say, to *haler*, hauling, as on a towpath — the subject's hauling, dragging his thing down a path he cannot leave. Why did repetition first appear in *traumatic neurosis*? Freud, against every neurophysiologist, marked the scandal: the subject reproduces in dream the memory of the bombardment from which his neurosis dates — while awake, it leaves him neither hot nor cold. What is this function of traumatic repetition, when nothing, far from it, can justify it from the pleasure principle? *Mastering* the painful event, one will say — but *who* masters? Where is the master here, that there should be mastering? Why speak so fast, when we cannot even situate the agency that would perform the operation? The ego-psychological answer is dismissed by its own presupposition: it borrows the very unified agency whose existence is in question.

Freud's own indication, at the end of the series whose two essential texts have been named: what happens in the dreams of traumatic neurosis is conceivable only at the level of the *most primitive* functioning — that at which the *binding of energy* is to be obtained. Do not presume some mere deviation, some distribution of function of the kind found at an infinitely more elaborate level of the real. On the contrary: here is a point the subject can approach only by *dividing himself* into a certain number of agencies. Say of it what is said of the divided kingdom: there perishes every conception of the unity of the psyche — the alleged totalizing, synthesizing psychism ascending toward consciousness. Traumatic repetition, seated below the pleasure principle at the level of binding, cannot be housed in a unitary psyche at all; the subject's division is the condition of approaching it.

And a final clinical hinge: in the first times of the experience — recollection substituting for itself, closing in on a kind of focus, a center where every event seems about to be surrendered — *precisely at that moment* there appears what Lacan will call, in quotation marks and with all three words to be re-understood, *the resistance of the subject*: which at that moment becomes *repetition in act*. The paradox is the doctrine: resistance is not the ego's shield against memory; it is the point where what cannot be remembered *presents itself* — enacted instead of recalled. Part Three will read the transference from here.

What comes next, he announces, will appropriate the admirable fourth and fifth chapters of Aristotle's *Physics*, where Aristotle turns and manipulates two terms absolutely resistant to his theory of cause — the most elaborate ever constructed — two terms improperly translated *chance* and *fortune*. The task will be to revise the relation Aristotle establishes between the *automaton* — which, at our point in modern mathematics, we know to be *the network of signifiers* — and what he designates as *tuché*: for us, *the encounter with the real*. The pair that titles Lesson V is set: insistence of the chain on one side, interruption by the real on the other — and neither is "chance." The session's questions and answers are missing from the record.

### **V. Tuché and Automaton**

*12 February 1964\.* The lesson delivers what Lesson IV promised: repetition formulated through the pair Aristotle could not domesticate in *Physics* II, 4–5 — *tuché* and *automaton* — and demonstrated on the most famous dream in the *Traumdeutung*, the burning child. Miller's keywords chart the arc: *psychoanalysis is not an idealism; the real as trauma; theory of the dream and of waking; consciousness and representation; God is unconscious; the objet petit a in the fort-da.*

Lacan opens by clearing a reproach. Psychoanalysis has been accused of idealism — of reducing the experience that solicits us to find in conflict, struggle, even the exploitation of man by man, the reasons for our deficiencies; of leading to an ontology of tendencies held to be primitive, internal, given in advance with the subject's condition. Retrace the experience from its first steps and the contrary appears: no praxis is more oriented than analysis toward what, at the heart of experience, is *the kernel of the real*. The claim is the lesson's thesis in advance: far from dissolving the world into dream — *life is a dream* is precisely the aphorism analysis forbids — psychoanalysis is the praxis that runs up against the real, and repetition is the name of that running-up.

**The encounter that is missed.** Where do we meet this real? What psychoanalysis discovered is of the order of the *encounter* — an essential encounter, an appointment to which we are always called *with a real that steals away*. First term on the board: *tuché*, borrowed from Aristotle's vocabulary in his search for cause, and translated: *the encounter with the real*. The real lies *beyond the automaton* — beyond the return, the coming-back, the *insistence of the signs* to which we find ourselves commanded by the pleasure principle. The real is what always lies behind the *automaton*; and that this is Freud's true concern is evident through all his research. The translation-decisions do the conceptual work: Aristotle's *automaton* ("spontaneity," the chance of things) becomes the signifying network's insistence — the lawful, pleasure-principle-governed return worked out in Lessons II–IV — while *tuché* (Aristotle's "luck," chance befalling beings capable of action) becomes the encounter that interrupts the network from outside it. Neither is chance; together they split what "chance" confusedly names.

Recall the Wolf Man — central for us — to grasp Freud's true preoccupation as the function of fantasy revealed itself to him: he presses, almost in anguish, the question of the *first encounter*, the real that can be affirmed *behind* the fantasy. One feels that real dragging the subject along through the whole analysis, so directing the research that one may ask today whether this fever, this presence, this desire of Freud is not what conditioned, in his patient, the late accident of his psychosis. The remark cuts two ways: it verifies that the real-behind-the-fantasy is the analytic stake par excellence, and it re-poses Lesson I's question — Freud's own desire operating, unanalyzed, inside the treatment, with effects on the patient.

Hence a triple demarcation: repetition is to be confused neither with the *return of signs*, nor with *reproduction*, nor with a modulation of conduct into acted recollection.
