Repetition's true nature is *always veiled in analysis* because analysts conceptualize repetition and transference as identical — and there precisely the distinction must be driven. Freud expressed the relation to the real at stake in transference by saying nothing can be apprehended *in effigie, in absentia* — and yet is transference not given to us as effigy, as relation to absence? The ambiguity will be untangled only from the function of the real *in repetition*. The tangle is set out for Part Three to undo: if transference is effigy and absence, and yet the real refuses effigies, then transference cannot simply *be* repetition — the identification of the two is the screen that keeps repetition veiled.

What repeats always occurs *as if by chance* — the expression itself betrays its relation to the *tuché*. Analysts are never duped by this, on principle: when the subject reports that something happened that day which prevented his coming to the session, we do not take things at the letter of his declaration — for what we are dealing with is exactly this stumbling, this snag, refound at every instant. The trivial clinical example carries the Aristotelian analysis: "as if by chance" (*hōsper apo tuchēs*) is how the *tuché* presents itself inside experience — as accident, excuse, contingency — and the analytic ear, by principle, hears the appointment in the accident.

**The real as trauma.** The function of the *tuché* — the real as encounter, the encounter insofar as it can be missed, insofar as it is *essentially the missed encounter* — first presented itself in the history of psychoanalysis in a form that alone should arrest us: *the trauma*. Is it not remarkable that at the origin of the analytic experience the real presented itself in the form of *what is unassimilable in it* — the trauma, determining all that followed and imposing on it an apparently accidental origin? Here is the core of what makes the pleasure/reality-principle opposition radical, and why the reality principle can never be conceived as having, by ascendancy, the last word. The definition compresses the seminar's ontology of the real: unassimilable, accidental in appearance, structuring in effect — the encounter that founds precisely by having been missed.

For the trauma is conceived as something to be buffered by the *subjectivating homeostasis* that orients all functioning under the pleasure principle. But experience then poses the problem: *within the primary processes themselves* we see the trauma's insistence on recalling itself to us preserved — the trauma reappears there, often with face unveiled. How can the dream, bearer of the subject's desire, produce what makes the trauma resurge repeatedly — if not its very face, at least the screen that still points to it behind? Conclude: however far the reality system develops, it leaves *captive in the nets of the pleasure principle an essential part of what is nonetheless well and truly real*. The paradox dismantles the developmental optimism of ego-psychology: "reality" as constituted by the reality principle does not progressively absorb the real; a piece of the real remains inside the pleasure-system, insisting — and that insistence is repetition.

This is what must be sounded — the reality whose presence is required if the motor of development, as a Melanie Klein for instance represents it, is not to collapse into "life is a dream." To this requirement answer those radical points in the real Lacan calls *encounters*, which make us conceive reality as *unterlegt, untertragen* — underlaid, borne-beneath — which French renders, in the superb ambiguity of the word, as *en souffrance*: reality is there *in suffering / in abeyance*, waiting, like an undelivered letter. And the *Zwang*, the compulsion Freud defines by the *Wiederholung*, commands the very detours of the primary process. The bilingual play is doctrinal: the real underlies (*unterlegt*) representation as what representation holds in abeyance (*en souffrance*) — pending, painful, undelivered — and the compulsion to repeat is the pressure of that pending real on the circuits of the pleasure principle. The primary process — nothing other than what the preceding lessons defined as the unconscious — must once more be grasped in its experience of rupture between perception and consciousness, in that *intemporal* place which compels Freud to posit, with homage to Fechner, *die Idee einer anderer Lokalität* — an other locality, another space, another scene, *the between* of perception and consciousness. Fechner's "other scene" is re-read through Lesson IV's Letter 52: the other locality is the interval — the place of the Other.

**The knock at the door.** The primary process can be caught at any instant. Lacan offers himself: woken the other day from a short sleep by knocking at his door — but before waking, the hurried knocks had already formed a dream, a dream manifesting *something other* than the knocks. Waking, he becomes conscious of the knocks — the perception — only insofar as he reconstitutes his whole representation around them: he knows where he is, at what hour he fell asleep, what he sought in that sleep. When the noise reaches not perception but *consciousness*, it is because consciousness reconstitutes itself around the representation — he knows he is under the blow of waking, *knocked*.

But then he must ask what he *is* at that moment — at the instant, so immediately before and so separate, when he began to dream *under* the very knock that apparently wakes him. He is, so far as he knows, *before he wakes up* (*avant que je ne me réveille*) — and the expletive *ne* of the French, already flagged in one of his écrits, is the very mode of presence of that *I am* prior to waking: not expletive at all, but the expression of his *impléance*, his non-fullness, wherever it has to show itself. French defines it in use: *Aurez-vous fini avant qu'il ne vienne?* — what matters to me is that you finish; God forbid he come first. The grammatical excursus is a micro-demonstration: the subject "before waking" exists in the mode of that untranslatable *ne* — a subject registered in the signifier as a shadow of negation, present as not-yet, neither asserted nor denied. Language itself carries the pre-ontological status Lesson III assigned the unconscious.

Note the symmetry this points to: after the knock of waking, the subject can apparently sustain himself only in a relation to his *representation* — which apparently makes of him nothing but consciousness: an involutive reflection — in my consciousness it is only my representation that I recover. Is that all? Freud said often enough that he would have to return — he never did — to the function of consciousness. Perhaps we shall see better by grasping what motivates the surging-up of represented reality: *the phenomenon, the distance, the gap itself that constitutes waking*. Waking is thereby made a problem instead of a criterion: consciousness does not receive reality; it reconstitutes representation across a gap — and the question of what wakes us, and from what into what, is now open for the great example.

**Father, can't you see I'm burning?** Return to the dream — itself built on a noise — that everyone has had time to refind in the *Traumdeutung*: the father who, after his child's death, takes some rest in the next room, leaving the body watched over by an old man, and is reached, awoken — by what? Not only the reality, the shock, the *knocking* of a noise made to recall him to the real: the dream itself translates the near-identity of what is happening — the overturned candle setting fire to the bed where his child lies. On its face, little suited to confirm the *Traumdeutung*'s thesis that the dream is the realization of a wish; here surfaces, almost for the first time in the book, an apparently secondary function — the dream satisfying only the need to prolong sleep. Why does Freud set *this* dream *here*, insisting that it is in itself the full confirmation of his thesis?

If the dream's function is to prolong sleep, and the dream can approach so closely the reality that provokes it, could that reality not be answered *without leaving sleep* — there are somnambulisms, after all? So the question all Freud's prior indications entitle us to produce: *What wakes?* Is it not, *in the dream*, an *other reality*? — the reality Freud gives in the child's words: *Vater, siehst du denn nicht, dass ich verbrenne?* — Father, can't you see that I'm burning? There is more reality in this message than in the noise by which the father also identifies the strange reality of what is happening next door. Does there not pass, in these words, *the missed reality that caused the child's death*? Freud himself says these words perpetuate for the father what is forever separated from the dead child — words said to him, Freud supposes, perhaps because of the fever — but who knows, perhaps they perpetuate the father's *remorse*: that the man set to watch — *der greise Wächter* — might not be equal to his task. And indeed: he has fallen asleep. The reversal is complete: what wakes the father is not the external noise (which the dream could absorb) but the dream's own kernel — the child's sentence, in which the missed encounter itself speaks. One wakes *from* the dream not into reality but *away from* a real that the dream has brought too close.

The fever-sentence — does it not evoke "the cause of the fever" from Lesson II, the miasmas, the hole in causality? However pressing the action of parrying what is happening in the next room, is it not also felt as in any case *too late* now — with respect to what is at stake, the psychic reality manifest in the sentence? Is the continued dream not essentially *homage to the missed reality* — the reality that can no longer be made except by repeating itself indefinitely, in an indefinitely never-reached waking? What encounter can there still be with that being forever inert — even being devoured by the flames — if not the one that happens exactly when, by accident, as if by chance, the flame comes to touch him? Where is reality in this accident, if not that *something more fatal repeats itself, by means of reality* — a reality in which the man charged to watch beside the body is still asleep, even when the father arrives after waking. Between the dream and the waking, between the one who still sleeps (whose dream we shall never know) and the one who dreamed only so as not to wake, *the encounter, forever missed, has passed*. The formula gathers the whole lesson: the *tuché* is lodged neither in the dream nor in waking life but in the passage between them — the appointment with the dead child kept only in the instant of its missing.

If Freud, marveling, sees his theory of desire confirmed here, that is the sign that the dream is *not merely* a wish-fulfilling fantasy. For the dream does not sustain the thesis that the son still lives: the dead child taking his father by the arm — atrocious vision — designates a *beyond* that makes itself heard in the dream. Desire presentifies itself there in the *loss imaged at the object's cruelest point*. Only in the dream can this truly unique encounter occur; only a rite, an act always repeated, can commemorate this immemorable encounter — since no one can say what the death of a child *is*, except the father as father — *that is to say, no conscious being*. Whence the sentence the whole lesson has been building: *the true formula of atheism is not "God is dead"* — even in founding the father-function on the father's murder, Freud protects the father — *the true formula of atheism is "God is unconscious."* The Nietzschean formula, Lesson II already suspected, shelters against castration by keeping God in the register of being-then-nonbeing; Lacan's formula relocates the divine — the father as father, the one for whom the immemorable exists — in the register that is neither: the unconscious. The father's knowledge of his child's death exists nowhere but there.

Waking shows us the subject's consciousness waking *in the representation* of what happened — the unfortunate accident of reality, now merely to be dealt with\! But what was the accident, when everyone sleeps: the one who wanted rest, the one who could not sustain the vigil, and the one at whose bed some well-meaning person must have said *he looks as if he's sleeping* — when we know only one thing: that in this wholly slumbering world, *only the voice was heard* — Father, can't you see I'm burning. The sentence is itself a *firebrand* — alone it carries fire wherever it falls — and one does not see what burns, for the flame blinds us to the fact that the fire bears on the *Unterlegt*, the *Untertragen* — *on the real*. The image executes the doctrine: the visible blaze (representation, the manifest accident) blinds precisely to what it consumes — the underlaid real held *en souffrance* — and the burning voice is the real's one apparition.

Recognize then, in this dream-sentence detached from the father in his suffering, *the inverse* of what will be, once awake, his consciousness — and ask what, in the dream, is the correlate of representation. Here the dream shows as the very *lining* of representation: the dream's imagery — occasion to underline what Freud, speaking of the unconscious, designates as what essentially determines it: the *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz*. Which means not, as it has been drably translated, the "representative representative," but *the stand-in for the representation* (*le tenant-lieu de la représentation*). Its function will appear later. The translation-correction banks the concept Part Three requires: what is repressed and what determines the unconscious is not a representation but the signifier that *takes the place of* representation — the delegate holding a vacant seat.

This maps the place of the real: *from the trauma to the fantasy* — the fantasy being never anything but the screen dissimulating something absolutely first, determining, in the function of repetition. Whence the ambiguity of waking and of the real's function in it: the real may be represented by the accident, the little noise, the *peu-de-réalité* testifying that we are not dreaming; but on the other side, this reality is not little, for what wakes us is the *other* reality hidden behind the lack of what stands in for representation — *the Trieb*, Freud says. Careful: we have not yet said what this *Trieb* is — and if, for lack of representation, it is not there, what *Trieb* is at issue? We may have to consider it only as *Trieb to come* (*Trieb à venir*). Waking is thus double: waking into constituted, represented reality is a doubling of function; *the real is to be sought beyond the dream* — in what the dream has wrapped, enveloped, hidden from us, behind the lack of representation of which there is only a place-holder there. That real commands our activities more than any other, and psychoanalysis designates it for us. The forward-reference is exact: the drive, deferred since Lesson II as the hardest concept, is here located in advance as what stirs behind the *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz* — not yet present, "to come," both in the analysis and in the seminar's own order.

**Kierkegaard: repetition against reminiscence.** So Freud brings the solution to the problem on which the most acute of the soul's questioners before him — Kierkegaard — had already centered: *repetition*. Reread the text of that title, dazzling in lightness and ironic play, truly Mozartian in its Don-Juanesque way of abolishing the mirages of love. With acuity, beyond reply, Kierkegaard's young man — portrait at once moved and derisive — addresses, in his love, only himself *through the intermediary of memory*. Is there not something here deeper than La Rochefoucauld's remark that few would experience love had its ways not been explained to them? Yes — but *who began?* Does everything not begin essentially with deception of the first one to whom love's enchantment was addressed — passing the enchantment off as exaltation of the other, making oneself prisoner of that exaltation, creating with the other the falsest demand, that of narcissistic satisfaction, whether of the ego-ideal or of the ego that takes itself for the ideal? Kierkegaard's discovery is aligned with Freud's: the young man's "recollected love" is love in the regime of reminiscence — narcissism addressed to itself through memory — and Kierkegaard's category of repetition, opposed to Platonic recollection as the forward to the backward, is claimed as the pre-Freudian articulation of what Freud will ground.

In Freud, as in Kierkegaard, repetition never sits in the natural; it is no return of need. The return of need aims at consumption in the service of appetite; *repetition demands the new*. It turns toward the ludic, which makes this newness its dimension — Freud says so too in the chapter cited last time. But everything that *varies* in repetition, everything modulated, is only *alienation of its meaning*. The adult, even the older child, demands novelty in his games — and that sliding veils the true secret of play: *the more radical diversity that repetition constitutes in itself*. See it in the child at his first formation as a human being: the demand that the tale be always *the same*, its recounted realization ritualized, textually identical. This demand for a consistency distinct from the tale's details signifies that the realization of the signifier can never be careful enough in its memorization to attain *the primacy of significance as such* — so that developing the tale by varying its significations is, in appearance, an evasion of it: variation makes one forget the aim of significance by turning its act into play with happy discharges under the pleasure principle. The paradox is rigorous: repetition demands the new *because* what it aims at — significance as such, the pure mark — is never delivered by any performance; each variation is a flight from the identical that repetition wants and cannot have. The child's tyrannical "again, the same\!" is closer to repetition's truth than the adult's appetite for novelty.

**The fort-da and the little a.** When Freud grasps repetition in his grandson's game, the reiterated *fort-da*, he may well stress that the child buffers the effect of his mother's disappearance by making himself its agent — but this phenomenon is secondary. Wallon notes: it is not at once that the child watches the door where his mother went out, marking that he expects to see her again there; before that, his vigilance bears *on the very point where she left him, the point she abandoned beside him*. The gap introduced by the traced absence, always open, remains cause of a centrifugal tracing in which what falls is not the other as a figure onto which the subject projects, but *that reel, tied to himself by a thread he holds* — in which is expressed what detaches itself from him in this trial, *the self-mutilation from which the order of significance will take perspective*. For the reel-game is the subject's answer to what the mother's absence has created on the frontier of his domain, at the edge of his cradle: *a ditch*, around which he has only to play at jumping.

The reel is not the mother reduced to a little ball by some game worthy of the Jivaro — *it is a small something of the subject that detaches itself while still being his, still held*. This is the place to say, in imitation of Aristotle, that *man thinks with his object*. With his object the child leaps the frontiers of his domain transformed into a well, and begins the incantation. If the signifier is the first mark of the subject, how not recognize — from the sole fact that the game is accompanied by one of the first oppositions to appear — that the object to which this opposition applies *in act*, the reel, is where we must designate *the subject*? To this object we will later give its name in Lacanian algebra: *the petit a*. The re-reading of Freud's most cited page is the lesson's second summit. Against the standard account (symbolization of the mother's absence; mastery of loss), Lacan makes three corrections that define the *objet a*: the game answers the *Spaltung* — the split the mother's departure causes *in the subject* — not the need for her return; the reel is a detached piece *of the subject*, not a stand-in for the mother; and the phonemic opposition *fort/da* aims at what is essentially *not there* as represented — the game itself being the *Repräsentanz* of the *Vorstellung*. The whole activity symbolizes repetition — repetition of the mother's departure *as cause of a division in the subject*, overcome by the alternating game whose alternation aims only at being *fort* of a *da* and *da* of a *fort*. And the question that will not close: what becomes of the *Vorstellung* when, once again, this *Repräsentanz* of the mother — in its drawing marked with the touches and gouaches of desire — comes to lack?

Lacan adds his own witnessed case, eyes unsealed by maternal divination: a child traumatized by his leaving despite the precociously sketched appeal of the voice, an appeal not renewed for whole months — and long after, taken in Lacan's arms, letting his head fall on his shoulder to drop into sleep, *sleep alone capable of restoring him access to the living signifier that Lacan had been since the date of the trauma*. The counter-case shows what happens when the game does not form: where the *fort-da* masters the gap by an object and an opposition, this child can rejoin the traumatic signifier only by unconsciousness — the difference between symbolized and unsymbolized loss.

The sketch of the *tuché* given today will be essential, Lacan notes, for rectifying the analyst's duty in the interpretation of the transference. And one accent to close the movement: not in vain does analysis claim to modulate more radically that relation of man to the world which has so long been taken for *knowledge*. When theoretical writings relate knowledge to something like ontogenesis-recapitulating-phylogenesis, it is by confusion: the whole originality of analysis is *not* to center psychological ontogenesis on the alleged stages — which have literally no locatable foundation in biologically observable development. If development is animated entirely by the accident, the stumbling of the *tuché*, it is insofar as the *tuché* brings us back to the same point where pre-Socratic philosophy sought to motivate the world itself.

**Democritus' den.** It needed a *clinamen* somewhere. Democritus, attempting to designate it — already opposing a pure function of negativity in order to introduce thought into it — says: it is not the *mēden* that is essential; and, showing that from philosophy's "archaic stage" the manipulation of words was practiced just as in Heidegger's time, he adds: *not a mēden but a den* — in Greek, a coined word. He did not say *hen*, so as not to speak of the *on*: he said — answering the question that was ours today, the question of idealism — *Nothing, perhaps? Not nothing — but not-nothing* (*pas rien*). The fragment (Democritus B156) closes the ring opened by the lesson's first sentence. Against the charge of idealism, the status of the real is given its minimal formula: the *den*, carved out of *mēden* by dropping the negation's first syllable — less than being (*hen*, *on*), more than nothing — the "not-nothing" that is Lacan's pre-Socratic warrant for the *objet a* and the real: what falls outside the alternative of being and non-being, exactly the pre-ontological register of Lesson III.

**Réponses.** Françoise Dolto objects that describing the formation of intelligence before three or four cannot dispense with the *stages*, needed for the fantasies of defense and the veil of castration alongside threats of mutilation. Lacan's answer subordinates rather than abolishes: the description of the libido-forming stages must not be referred to a pseudo-natural maturation, which stays opaque. *The stages organize themselves around castration anxiety.* The copulative fact of sexuality's introduction is traumatizing — a snag of size — with an organizing function for development. Castration anxiety is like a thread perforating all the stages, orienting even the relations prior to its proper appearance — weaning, anal discipline — crystallizing each moment in a dialectic centered on a *bad encounter*. If the stages have consistency, it is by their possible registration in terms of the *mauvaise rencontre*. The central bad encounter is at the level of the sexual — which does not mean the stages take a sexual tint diffusing from castration anxiety; on the contrary, *it is because this empathy does not occur that one speaks of trauma and of the primal scene*. The answer converts the lesson's whole apparatus into developmental theory: the stages are consistent not as biological maturation but as a series ordered by the missed encounter — the *tuché* at the level of the sexual — so that "trauma" and "primal scene" name precisely the non-assimilation, the failure of any pre-established harmony between the subject and the sexual real. Part Three's thesis that the sexual reaches the subject only through the defiles of the signifier is here in germ.

## **The Gaze as Objet Petit a**

### **VI. The Split between the Eye and the Gaze**

*19 February 1964\.* Part Two opens, and with it the seminar's most celebrated invention: the gaze (*le regard*) as *objet a* in the scopic field. Miller's keywords give the itinerary — *split of the subject; facticity of the trauma; Maurice Merleau-Ponty; the philosophical tradition; mimicry; the omnivoyeur; in the dream, it shows.* The occasion is precise: Merleau-Ponty's posthumous *Le Visible et l'invisible* has appeared that very week, and Lacan reads it as both homage and demarcation — the philosophical tradition arriving at its terminus in the eye, and psychoanalysis stepping past it toward the gaze.

"Je continue." *Wiederholung*, recalled through the etymology of *haler*, hauling, with its weary connotation. Hauling, drawing — drawing what? Playing on the French *tirer*: drawing lots — *tirer au sort*. The *Zwang* then directs us to the *forced card*: if there is only one card in the deck, I can draw no other. The set-character (in the mathematical sense) of the batch of signifiers — opposed, say, to the indefiniteness of the whole numbers — lets us conceive a schema to which the forced card immediately applies: if the subject is the subject of the signifier, determined by it, one can imagine the synchronic network as yielding *preferential effects* in diachrony. Not unpredictable statistical effects: *the structure of the network itself implies the returns*. This is the figure that Aristotle's *automaton* takes for us, through the elucidation of what we call strategies — and it is indeed by "automatism" that we translate the *Zwang* of the *Wiederholungszwang*, the compulsion to repeat. The opening paragraph completes Lesson V's translation-work with a formal model: because the signifying battery is a finite set, its combinatory deals forced cards — lawful returns mistaken for chance — and the repetition-compulsion is that forcing, not a mysterious demonic drive.

**The kernel, the real, and destiny.** He promises later the facts suggesting that in the child's monologue, imprudently called egocentric, properly *syntactic* games are observable — games belonging to the preconscious but making, so to speak, the bed of the *unconscious reserve*, to be heard as an Indian reservation inside the social network. Syntax is preconscious; what escapes the subject is that his syntax *is in relation with the unconscious reserve*. When the subject tells his story, something latent acts, commanding this syntax and making it tighter and tighter. Tighter with respect to what? To what Freud, from the start of his description of psychic resistance, calls a *kernel* (*noyau*). The image is topological: the unconscious is not below the preconscious but *reserved within it*, an enclave around which the syntax of the subject's narration contracts as the analysis nears it.

To say the kernel refers to something traumatic is only an approximation. One must distinguish, from the resistance of the subject, this first *resistance of discourse* as it tightens around the kernel — for "resistance of the subject" implies only too readily a supposed ego, and near this kernel it is not sure that "ego" is still a founded qualification. The kernel must be designated as *real* — real insofar as *identity of perception* is its rule; at the limit it rests on what Freud points to as a kind of sampling assuring us that we are in perception, by the feeling of reality that authenticates it. And on the side of the subject this is called *waking*. The chain of Lesson V is re-forged link by link: the kernel that discourse cannot absorb is the real; its regime is the identity of perception (the primary process's rule); and its subjective signature is the wake — which the burning-child dream had already made a problem.

Hence the choice of that dream — closed, doubly and triply closed, since unanalyzed — precisely where the dream-process in its ultimate spring is in question. Is the reality that determines waking really the slight noise against which the empire of dream and desire maintains itself? Is it not rather what is expressed in the depth of the dream's anxiety — the most intimate of the relation of father to son, surging up not so much in the death as in what it is *beyond*, in its sense of *destiny*? Between what happens *as if by chance* when everyone sleeps — the tipped candle, the fire in the sheets, the senseless event, the accident, ill fortune — and the poignancy, however veiled, of the *Father, can't you see I'm burning*, there is the same relation we deal with in repetition: what is figured for us in the names *destiny neurosis* and *failure neurosis*. *What is missed is not adaptation, but tuché — the encounter.* The clinical categories are re-derived in one stroke: the neuroses of destiny do not fail to adapt; they keep missing an appointment — the same structure as the father's dream, where accident (candle) and address (the child's sentence) stand in the relation of *automaton* to *tuché*.

Here Aristotle is directly overturned. His thesis — that *tuché* can come to us only from a being capable of choice, *proairesis*; that fortune, good or bad, cannot come from an inanimate object, a child, an animal — is disproved (*controuvé*) by the very accident of this exemplary dream. And Aristotle marks at this point the same limit that stops him at the edge of the extravagant forms of sexual conduct, which he can only call *teratōdes*, monstrosities. The philosophical connection is exact: in *Physics* II Aristotle restricts luck to the sphere of deliberate agents, and in the *Ethics* he expels monstrous sexuality beyond the thinkable; Lacan makes the two limits one — the encounter with the real comes precisely from where Aristotle bars it (the candle, the child, the dead), and the sexual real is precisely what his teleology cannot house.

The closed relation between the *accident that repeats* and the *veiled meaning that is the true reality* leads toward the drive — and gives certainty that demystifying the treatment-artifact called transference does not consist in reducing it to the "actuality of the situation." The here-and-now reduction of the session has not even propaedeutic value. The just concept of repetition must be sought in another direction than the ensemble of transference-effects; how transference can lead to the heart of repetition will be the problem when transference is broached. The polemical target is the then-fashionable "here and now" technique: if repetition is relation to the real, no analysis of the present relationship touches it — the tangle of Lesson V (transference as effigy vs. the real that admits none) is reserved intact for Part Three.

That is why repetition must first be founded *in the very split (schize) produced in the subject at the place of the encounter*. This split is the characteristic dimension of analytic discovery and experience — it makes us apprehend the real, in its dialectical incidence, as *originally unwelcome* (*malvenu*). And precisely there the real is, in the subject, most complicit with the drive — which we shall reach only last, since only this traversed path can show what it is about. Then the battery of questions the primal scene poses: why is it so traumatic? why always too early or too late? why does the subject take too much pleasure in it — as we first conceived the traumatizing causality of the obsessional — or too little, as in the hysteric? why does it not wake the subject at once, if he is so profoundly libidinal? why is the fact here *dustuchia* — ill-encounter? why is the alleged maturation of the pseudo-instincts shot through, transfixed, with the *tychic*? The Greek does the arguing: *dustuchia*, misfortune, is *tuché* gone wrong — the sexual arrives for the subject always as mistimed encounter, never as ripening instinct; the "stages" of Dolto's question are strung on this thread.

For now the horizon is what appears *factitious* in the fundamental relation to sexuality. Analytic experience starts from this: if the primal scene is traumatic, it is not sexual empathy that sustains the modulations of the analyzable, but a factitious fact — like the one so fiercely tracked in the Wolf Man: *the strangeness of the disappearance and reappearance of the penis*. "Facticity of the trauma" (Miller's phrase) means: the sexual enters the subject's history not as nature unfolding but as an anomalous, artifact-like detail — the organ that vanishes and returns — around which the real of the scene crystallizes.

Where, then, is the split of the subject? After waking it persists — between the *return to the real*, the representation of the world fallen back on its feet, arms raised, *what a misfortune, what happened, what a horror, what a fool, the one who fell asleep* — and the consciousness re-weaving itself, which knows it lives all this as a nightmare but catches hold of itself: *it is I who live all this, I have no need to pinch myself to know I am not dreaming*. Yet this split is only the representative of the deeper split: between what refers the subject *within the machinery of the dream* — the image of the approaching child, the gaze full of reproach — and, on the other side, *what causes it and into which he falls*: invocation, the child's voice, solicitation of the gaze — *Father, can't you see...* The lesson's title-concept arrives by this route: the deep split is not dream/waking but, inside the dream itself, image versus gaze-and-voice — the represented scene versus the objects (voice, gaze) that cause the subject and into which he drops. The scopic object is introduced from the burning-child dream, not from optics.

**Merleau-Ponty and the terminus of the philosophical tradition.** Free to choose his path, Lacan passes his curved needle through the tapestry and jumps to the crossroads question shared with all who try to think the subject's path: is that path, as search for truth, to be broken in our style of adventure — with its trauma reflecting facticity — or is it to be located where the tradition has always put it: at the level of the dialectic of the true and apparent, taken from perception in what it has of the fundamentally ideic, aesthetic in kind, accented by a *visual centering*? It is no mere chance — no pure *tychic* — that this very week brings within reach the posthumous book of "our friend" Maurice Merleau-Ponty, *Le Visible et l'invisible*. Lacan recalls their dialogue, Bonneval, and pays homage to Claude Lefort's long and difficult transcription.

The book marks for us *the moment of arrival of the philosophical tradition* — the tradition beginning with Plato's promotion of the *idea*: setting out from an aesthetic world, determining itself by an end given to being as sovereign good, attaining a beauty that is also its limit. And not for nothing does Merleau-Ponty recognize its regent in *the eye*. The compressed history is a thesis: from *eidos* (a seeing-word) to the sovereign good, philosophy has been governed by the visual — by form offered to an eye — and its idealism is the vertigo of that regency: how ever rejoin the doubling that representation became with what it is supposed to cover? The *Phenomenology of Perception* answered by the regulative function of *form*, presided over not by the subject's eye alone but by his whole expectation, movement, grip, muscular and visceral emotion — his constitutive presence as total intentionality.

Now Merleau-Ponty takes the next step, forcing the limits of that very phenomenology. The paths he opens are not merely a phenomenology of the visual, for they go to refind — the essential point — *the dependence of the visible on what places us under the eye of the seer*. Even that says too much: this eye is only the metaphor of something Lacan would rather call *the shoot* (*la pousse*) of the seer — something *before* his eye. What must be circumscribed, along the path Merleau-Ponty indicates, is *the pre-existence of a gaze* — *I see only from one point, but in my existence I am looked at from everywhere.* The formula is the seminar's hinge. The tradition put the subject at the punctual origin of vision (the geometral point); the analytic step, prepared by Merleau-Ponty's "flesh" and his chiasm of seeing/seen, is the inversion: before I see, I am seen — a seeing to which I am originally submitted, whose ontological turning Merleau-Ponty sought in a more primitive institution of form.

Occasion, Lacan says, to answer someone (Miller, from Lesson III): of course he has his ontology — everyone has one, naive or elaborated. But what he tries to trace in his discourse, centered on the particularity of the experience it maps, makes no claim to cover the whole field of experience; even the between-two that the apprehension of the unconscious opens interests us only as what the Freudian order designates for the subject to take possession of. And one addition: maintaining the aspect of Freudianism customarily called *naturalism* seems indispensable, for it is one of the rare attempts — perhaps the only one — *to give body to psychic reality without substantifying it*. The methodological aside answers the ontology-question definitively: psychoanalysis is not a regional ontology and not a worldview; its "naturalism" (energetics, the drive-body) is retained precisely as the device that keeps psychic reality embodied yet unsubstantialized — the pre-ontological status again.

In the field Merleau-Ponty gives us — polarized, moreover, by the threads of our experience, the *scopic field* — the ontological status presents itself through its most factitious, most caducous incidences. But it is *not* between the invisible and the visible that we shall have to pass. The split that interests us is not the distance owing to forms imposed by the world, toward which phenomenological intentionality directs us. *The gaze presents itself to us only in the form of a strange contingency, symbolic of what we find on the horizon and as the stop of our experience — the constitutive lack of castration anxiety.* *The eye and the gaze — that, for us, is the split in which the drive manifests itself at the level of the scopic field.* Here the demarcation from Merleau-Ponty is made exact: his split (visible/invisible, within the flesh) is replaced by eye/gaze; and the gaze is tied at once to the *objet a* series and to the *moins-phi* — it appears as a contingent fragment symbolizing the central lack. Phenomenology's chiasm becomes psychoanalysis's drive.

**Mimicry, the stain, and the eye's imposture.** In our relation to things as constituted by the way of vision and ordered in the figures of representation, *something slips, passes, is transmitted, from level to level, always to be to some degree eluded there — that is what is called the gaze.* To make it felt, an extreme: the enigma of *mimicry*. Much absurdity has been said of it — for instance that mimicry is explained by an adaptive end. Not Lacan's view; he sends us to Caillois's *Méduse et compagnie*, where the adaptive reference is criticized with particular perspicacity: on one side, the determining mutation of mimicry in the insect must be effective at once and from the start; on the other, its alleged selective effects are annihilated by the finding that predators' stomachs contain as many "protected" insects as unprotected ones. The recourse to Caillois is strategic: if mimicry is not explained by survival, the visible display is a *function in its own right*, not an instrument — nature exhibits a showing that serves nothing, a pure given-to-be-seen.

But the most radical problem is whether mimicry must be attributed to some formative power of the organism itself. For that to be legitimate we would have to conceive the circuits by which such a force could master not only the form of the mimicked body but its relation to the milieu — distinguishing itself from it or blending into it. And, as Caillois pertinently recalls, apropos the mimetic manifestation that most evokes the function of eyes — the *ocelli*, the eye-spots — the question is whether they impress (as in fact they do, on predator or presumed victim) *by their resemblance to eyes, or whether, on the contrary, eyes are fascinating only by their relation to the form of the ocelli*. In other words: must we not here distinguish the function of *the eye* from that of *the gaze*? The inversion is the lesson's discovery restated from nature's side: perhaps eyes do not explain eye-spots; eye-spots explain the fascination of eyes — the gaze-function (the stain that fixes) is prior to the organ, and the organ borrows its power from it.

This distinct example — chosen for its locality, facticity, exceptional character — is only a small manifestation of a function to isolate: say the word, *the stain* (*la tache*). It is precious for marking *the pre-existence, to the seen, of a given-to-be-seen* (*un donné-à-voir*). No need to invoke some universal seer: if the stain-function is recognized in its autonomy and identified with the gaze, we can seek its thread at every level of the constitution of the world in the scopic field — and we shall find that the function of stain and gaze is at once *what most secretly commands* that field and *what always escapes* the grasp of that form of vision satisfied with itself in imagining itself as consciousness.

For consciousness's power to turn back on itself — to seize itself, like Valéry's Jeune Parque, as *seeing itself see itself* (*se voyant se voir*) — represents a sleight of hand (*escamotage*): an avoidance of the function of the gaze. The critique lands on the whole reflexive tradition at once — the cogito's self-transparency, idealism's *percipere/percipi* couple, Valéry's emblem: "seeing oneself see oneself" is not the ground of the subject but the trick by which the subject *eludes* being seen, converting the gaze it is under into a reflection it performs. This can be located in the topology built last time from the subject's position in the dream's imaginary forms as against waking. Likewise in narcissism — whose essential structure Lacan restored by reference to the specular image — in the satisfaction, the complaisance diffused from it, where the subject finds support for so fundamental a misrecognition; and does its empire not extend to that reference of the philosophical tradition, the *plenitude* the subject meets in the mode of *contemplation*? What is eluded in all of it is the function of the gaze.

We are beings looked at, in the spectacle of the world — Merleau-Ponty points it. What makes us consciousness institutes us by the same stroke as *speculum mundi*, mirror of the world. And is there not satisfaction in being under that gaze — the gaze that circles us and makes us first of all looked-at beings, *but without showing us that it does*? The spectacle of the world appears, in this sense, *omnivoyeur* — all-seeing. There is the fantasy found in the Platonic perspective of an absolute being to whom the quality of the all-seeing is transferred; and at the level of phenomenal contemplation itself, the omnivoyeur side shows in the satisfaction of a woman in knowing herself looked at — *on condition that one does not show her that one knows*. The world is all-seeing, but it is *not exhibitionist* — it does not provoke our gaze. When it begins to provoke it, the feeling of *strangeness* begins too. The phenomenology is exact and two-sided: the comfort of contemplative being-in-the-world rests on a gaze that stays discreet; the uncanny is the moment the world's gaze declares itself — the moment the stain looks back.

**In the dream, it shows.** What does this mean, if not that in the waking state there is *elision of the gaze* — elision of the fact that not only does it look, *it shows* (*ça montre*). In the field of the dream, by contrast, what characterizes the images is that *it shows*. Take any dream-text, replace it in its coordinates, and this *it-shows* comes to the fore — with its characteristics: absence of horizon, closure (against the contemplated openness of waking), the emergent, contrasting, *stain*\-like character of its images, the intensification of their colors — such that our position in the dream is finally that of *the one who does not see*. The subject does not see where it leads; he follows; he may even detach himself, tell himself it is a dream — but in no case can he seize himself in the dream the way the Cartesian cogito seizes itself as thought. He can say *it's only a dream*; he cannot grasp himself as the one who says *all the same, I am consciousness of this dream*. The waking/dreaming asymmetry receives its final form: waking is the regime where the gaze is elided (the world shows without showing that it shows); dreaming is the regime where the showing is naked — and correlatively the subject cannot occupy the reflexive position. The cogito is a creature of the wake.

*In a dream, he is a butterfly.* What does that mean? *It means he sees the butterfly in its reality of gaze.* All those figures, drawings, colors — what are they but the gratuitous *given-to-see* in which the primitiveness of the essence of the gaze is marked for us? A butterfly, my God, not so different from the one that terrorizes the Wolf Man — Merleau-Ponty knew its importance, referring to it in an unintegrated note. When Zhuangzi wakes, he can ask whether it is not the butterfly dreaming it is Zhuangzi. He is right, and doubly: first, it proves he is not mad — he does not take himself as absolutely identical with Zhuangzi; second, he does not know how well he speaks. It is in fact *when he was the butterfly* that he grasped himself at some root of his identity — that he was, and is in his essence, *that butterfly painting itself with its own colors* — and by that, at the last root, he is Zhuangzi. The proof: *when he is the butterfly, it does not occur to him to ask whether, as waking Zhuangzi, he is not the butterfly he is dreaming of being*. Dreaming himself butterfly, he will later testify that he *represented himself* as butterfly — but this does not mean he is captivated by the butterfly: *he is a captured butterfly, but captured by nothing, for in the dream he is a butterfly for no one*. It is when awake that he is Zhuangzi *for others*, caught in their butterfly-net. The famous parable is made to carry the whole doctrine: in the dream, the subject coincides with the showing itself — a self-painting patch of color, gaze without spectator, identity at its root because *for no one*; awake, he is delivered over to the Other's net, an identity held from outside. Whence the phobia: the butterfly can inspire in the Wolf Man the terror of recognizing that the beating of the little wings is not far from the beating of *causation* — the primitive striping (*rayure*) marking his being touched for the first time by the grid of desire. The wing-beat, the pulsation, the stripe: the gaze-object flickers at the rhythm of the causal gap itself, and the phobic sees in it his own marking by desire.

Next time, Lacan announces, the essential of *scopic satisfaction*: the gaze can contain in itself the *objet a* of the Lacanian algebra *where the subject comes to fall* — and what specifies the scopic field, engendering its proper satisfaction, is that there, for structural reasons, *the fall of the subject always remains unperceived, since it reduces to zero*. Insofar as the gaze, as *objet a*, can come to symbolize the central lack expressed in castration, and is an *objet a* reduced by nature to a punctiform, evanescent function, it leaves the subject in ignorance of what is beyond appearance — *the ignorance so characteristic of all the progress of thought in the way constituted by philosophical research*. The closing indictment is enormous and precise: philosophy's serenity before appearance — its ability to move from perception to essence without vertigo — rests on the scopic drive's structural anesthesia: in vision the subject's fall into the object shrinks to a zero-point and is never felt. Philosophy is the beneficiary of the eye's elision of the gaze.

**Réponses.** Xavier Audouard asks to what degree, in analysis, one should let the subject know that he is being looked at — that the analyst is placed as the one watching, in the subject, the process of watching himself. Lacan takes it from higher up: his discourse has two aims, one for analysts, one for those here to know whether psychoanalysis is a science. Psychoanalysis is neither a *Weltanschauung* nor a philosophy claiming the key to the universe; it is commanded by a particular aim, historically defined by the elaboration of the notion of the *subject* — posing it anew by leading the subject back to his *signifying dependence*. To go from perception to science seems self-evident, since the subject had no better test-bench for grasping being — the very path Aristotle follows, taking up the pre-Socratics. But analytic experience obliges rectifying that path, *because it avoids the abyss of castration*. One sees it, for example, in that the stain enters theogony and genesis only under a punctiform aspect. The answer generalizes the lesson's indictment: the perception-to-science road (Aristotle's, the tradition's) is a detour *around* castration, and the near-absence of the stain from cosmogony is its symptom.

He is trying to grasp how the stain is represented in visual apprehension; he will show that *the tychic point in the scopic function is at the level of the stain*. Which means: the plane of reciprocity between looking and looked-at is, more than any other, propitious for the subject's *alibi*. Hence the technical consequence: our interventions in the session should not establish the subject on that plane; on the contrary, we should *truncate him from that last point of gaze, which is illusory*. Whence our great prudence — we do not say to the patient at every turn *my, what a face you're making\!* or *your top waistcoat button is undone*. It is not for nothing that analysis is not conducted face to face. The couch receives, at last, a structural justification: face-to-face installs the reciprocal look — the imaginary plane where the subject shelters from the gaze in the exchange of looks — and analysis works by subtracting that alibi. The split between gaze and vision will allow adding the *scopic drive* to the list — and Freud, read rightly, already puts it in the first rank in *Instincts and their Vicissitudes*, showing it is not homologous with the others: *it is the drive that most completely eludes the term of castration*. The last sentence stores the charge for Lessons VII–IX: the scopic drive's privilege — its apparent completeness, its zero-cost satisfaction — is exactly its power of eluding castration, which is why the field of vision is at once the most philosophical and the most defended of the drive-fields.

### **VII. Anamorphosis**

*26 February 1964\.* Miller's keywords — *on the foundation of consciousness; privilege of the gaze as objet a; optics of the blind; the phallus in the picture* — chart a demolition and a construction: consciousness's "I see myself seeing myself" is dismantled as the elusion of the gaze; then geometral perspective is exhibited as a science of space that misses vision entirely; and Holbein's *Ambassadors* is produced as the picture that paints the missing term — the annihilated subject, the phallic ghost, the death's head.

Lacan reopens with Aragon's "Contre-chant" entire — the mirror-eye that reflects but cannot see, inhabited by the absence that makes its blindness — confessing he did not know, when he first read it, how much development he would give the gaze; he was inflected toward it by the way he presented Freud's concept of repetition. The digression on the scopic function sits *inside* the explication of repetition — induced, no doubt, by the appearance of *Le Visible et l'invisible*; a happy encounter (*tuché* again, and *eutuchia* this time), destined to punctuate how, *in the perspective of the unconscious, we can situate consciousness*. For some shadow — some *reserve*, in the sense of the un-dyed reserve of a cloth exposed to tinting — marks the fact of consciousness in Freud's own discourse. The framing matters: the gaze-theory is not an excursus into aesthetics but the answer to the question Freud left unarticulated (he said he must return to the function of consciousness and never did); and the textile figure — consciousness as the reserved, untinted patch — inverts the usual picture in advance: consciousness is a spared blank in the field, not the field's source.

A terminological grumble first: *tychique* was heard last time as a sneeze. It is the adjective of *tuché* as *psychique* is of *psuché* — and the analogy was intentional, for in any conception of psychic development as analysis has lit it, *the fact of the tychic is central*. Today's discourse too will be ordered with respect to the eye — with respect to *eutuchia* and *dustuchia*, the lucky and the unlucky encounter. The pun is a program: where the tradition built the psyche on the *psuché*, analysis builds development on the *tuché* — not soul but encounter — and the scopic field is where the encounter is luckiest and unluckiest at once.

**"I saw myself seeing myself."** The Jeune Parque's line — *je me voyais me voir* — has its full, complex sense in Valéry's theme, femininity; we are not there yet. Here it serves as the philosopher's formula: one of the essential correlates of consciousness in its relation to representation. What evidence attaches to it? How does it remain correlative to the fundamental mode of the Cartesian cogito, in which the subject grasps itself as thought? What isolates that self-seizure of thought is methodic doubt, bearing on everything that could support thought in representation. How then does the *I see myself seeing myself* remain its envelope and ground — perhaps, more than one thinks, *found* its certainty? For compare: *I warm myself warming myself* is a reference to the body as body — the sensation of warmth diffuses from some point in me and localizes me as body. Whereas in the *I see myself seeing myself* there is no sense in which I am comparably *invaded* by vision. The asymmetry is the wedge: reflexive touch is anchored in the body it reports; reflexive vision floats free — and that unanchored reflexivity, the phenomenologists confirm, is even self-contradictory: they have articulated with confounding precision that I see *outside*, that perception is not in me but on the objects it apprehends. And yet I grasp the world in a perception that seems to arise from the immanence of the *I see myself seeing myself*. The subject's privilege seems established in this bipolar reflexive relation: as soon as I perceive, *my representations belong to me*.

By this the world is struck with a presumption of idealization — the suspicion of delivering me only my representations. Practical seriousness shrugs; but the philosopher, the idealist, is put in embarrassment before himself and his hearers. How deny that nothing of the world appears to me except in my representations? — Bishop Berkeley's irreducible move; and note in passing, concerning his subjective position, that *belong to me* of representations, evoking *property*. At the limit, the process of this reflecting reflection reduces the subject grasped by the Cartesian meditation to *a power of annihilation* (*néantisation*). The mode of my presence to the world is the subject insofar as, reduced to the sole certainty of being subject, it becomes *active annihilation*. The sequel of philosophical meditation tips the subject toward transformative historical action, ordering around that point the configured modes of active self-consciousness through its metamorphoses in history; and the meditation on being that culminates in Heidegger restores to being itself that annihilating power — or at least poses how being can relate to it. In four sentences Lacan runs the whole post-Cartesian line — Berkeley's proprietary idealism, Hegel-Marx's negating self-consciousness making history, Sartre's *néantisation*, Heidegger's nihilating *Nichts* — as so many avatars of one structure: the subject of reflexive vision, thinned to a point whose only power is the negative.

Merleau-Ponty leads there too — and at just that point chooses to *step back*, proposing a return to the sources of intuition about the visible and invisible, to what is before all reflection, thetic or non-thetic, to locate the *surging-up of vision itself*. For him it can only be a reconstruction, a restoration — not a path run in reverse — of the way in which, not from the body but from something he calls *the flesh of the world*, the original point of vision could arise. In the unfinished work one seems to see sketched the search for an unnamed substance from which I, the seer, extract myself: from the nets — or rays, if you like — of a shimmer of which I am first a part, I surge up as eye, taking emergence from what could be called *the function of seeingness* (*la voyure*). A wild odor emanates from it, letting one glimpse on the horizon the hunt of Artemis — whose touch seems to associate itself with the moment of tragic failing in which we lost the one who speaks. The paragraph is at once exegesis and elegy: Lacan renders Merleau-Ponty's flesh-ontology exactly — the seer as a fold extracted from the tissue of visibility — and lets the myth (Actaeon torn by his own hounds at the sight of the goddess) name both the danger of that vision and Merleau-Ponty's own death in mid-work.

But was that really the path he wanted? The traces left of the meditation to come permit doubt: the reference-points given there — especially to the properly psychoanalytic unconscious — let us glimpse that he might have directed himself toward research original with respect to the philosophical tradition, toward the new dimension of meditation on the subject that *analysis* permits us to trace. Lacan, for his part, is struck by certain notes — less enigmatic to him than to other readers — that overlap exactly with schemas he will promote here: read the note on the *finger-of-a-glove reversal* (*retournement en doigt de gant*) — the way the skin envelops the fur in a winter glove — where it seems to appear that consciousness, in its illusion of *seeing itself see itself*, finds its foundation in *the turned-inside-out structure of the gaze*. The working notes of *Le Visible et l'invisible* are thus claimed as convergent testimony: the reflexive illusion is a glove-reversal of a prior structure — the gaze — of which consciousness is the inverted lining.

**What is the gaze?** Lacan restarts from the point of first *néantisation*, where a break is marked in the field of the subject's reduction — warning of the need for another reference: the one analysis takes in *reducing the privileges of consciousness*. Analysis considers consciousness irremediably bounded and institutes it as a principle not only of idealization but of *misrecognition* — as, in a term that takes new value from the visual domain, *scotoma* (introduced into analytic vocabulary by the French school). Simple metaphor? We refind the ambiguity striking everything inscribed in the register of the scopic drive. Consciousness matters for us only in its relation to what Lacan, for propaedeutic ends, tried to show in the fiction of the *incomplete text* — from which the subject is to be recentered as speaking *in the very lacunae* of what he first presents himself as speaking. But that states only the relation of preconscious to unconscious; the dynamics attached to consciousness as such — the attention the subject brings to his own text — remains, as Freud underlined, outside the theory, not yet articulated.

Here Lacan advances his thesis: *the interest the subject takes in his own split is bound to what determines it — namely, a privileged object, arisen from some primitive separation, from some self-mutilation induced by the very approach of the real, whose name in our algebra is objet a.* The sentence welds Part One to Part Two: the split (Lesson VI's *schize*) is not merely suffered; the subject is *interested* in it, and the interest is anchored in the object that the fort-da already exhibited — the detached piece of the subject (the reel, the "self-mutilation") produced where the real approaches. In the scopic relation, the object on which the fantasy hangs — the fantasy from which the subject is suspended in an essential vacillation — is *the gaze*. Its privilege — and equally the reason the subject could so long misrecognize his dependence on it — lies in its very structure.

Schematically: as soon as the subject tries to accommodate himself to this gaze, *it becomes that punctiform object, that point of vanishing being, with which the subject confounds his own failing*. Of all the objects in which the subject can recognize his dependence in the register of desire, the gaze is specified as *ungraspable*. That is why it is, more than any other object, misrecognized — and perhaps also why the subject so happily symbolizes his own vanishing, punctiform trait in the illusion of consciousness *seeing itself see itself*, in which the gaze is elided (*où s'élide le regard*). The mechanism of the whole lesson is here: the reflexive formula is not false consciousness in general but a precise substitution — the subject's real punctiform vanishing (his fall into the gaze-object) is converted into the punctiform *mastery* of the self-seeing point. The scotoma is self-flattering.

If the gaze is this *underside* of consciousness, how imagine it? The expression is not undue, for the gaze can be given body. Sartre, in one of the most brilliant passages of *L'Être et le Néant*, brings it into function in the dimension of the existence of others: the other would remain suspended in the partially irrealizing conditions of objectivity were there not the gaze. The Sartrean gaze is the gaze *by which I am surprised* — surprised insofar as it changes all the perspectives and lines of force of my world, ordering it, from the point of nothing where I am, in a sort of radiated reticulation of organisms. And the gaze would have such privilege as the locus of my relation, annihilating subject, to what surrounds me, that it makes me *scotomize* the eye of whoever looks at me as object: *as long as I am under the gaze*, Sartre writes, *I no longer see the eye that looks at me — and if I see the eye, then it is the gaze that disappears.*

Is this phenomenologically just? *No.* It is not true that when I am under the gaze — when I ask for a gaze, when I obtain it — I do not see it *as* gaze. Painters have grasped this gaze as such *in the mask* — evoke Goya and you feel it. The gaze *is seen* — precisely the gaze Sartre speaks of, the gaze that surprises me and reduces me to shame (the feeling he draws as most accented). The gaze I encounter — locatable in Sartre's own text — is *not a seen gaze but a gaze imagined by me in the field of the Other*. The correction is surgical. Sartre's disjunction (either the eye or the gaze) is refuted empirically (Goya's painted gazes are seen *as* gaze) and then his own text is turned as evidence: far from staging the gaze's entrance through the organ of sight, Sartre invokes a sudden rustle of leaves while hunting, a footstep in the corridor — and at what moment? *the moment he has presented himself in the action of looking through a keyhole*. A gaze surprises him in the function of voyeur, routs him, capsizes him, reduces him to shame. The gaze in question is indeed the presence of the other as such. But does this mean the gaze is originally grasped in the subject-to-subject relation, in the function of the other's existence as looking at me? Is it not clear that the gaze intervenes here only insofar as what feels surprised is not the annihilating subject, correlative of the world of objectivity, but *the subject sustaining himself in a function of desire*? The keyhole scene decides it: what the gaze catches is not consciousness but the desiring subject in flagrante — the voyeur at his slot. Sartre's shame is right; his derivation (intersubjectivity) is wrong. The gaze belongs to the register of desire, which is why — Lacan closes the section on the word — it can be *conjured away* (*escamoter*): desire's objects are exactly what can be elided.

**Geometral optics, or vision without sight.** The privilege of the gaze in the function of desire can be grasped by sliding down the veins through which the domain of vision was integrated into the field of desire. Not for nothing did the *geometral* dimension of optics develop in the very epoch when the Cartesian meditation inaugurated the function of the subject in its purity. The reference for those who want more: Baltrušaitis, *Anamorphoses*. What is a simple, non-cylindrical anamorphosis? Suppose a portrait on this flat sheet, and the blackboard oblique to it: transferring, by ideal threads, each point of the image to the oblique surface yields a figure enlarged and deformed according to the lines of what one may call a perspective — such that, from the right point, the impression is sensibly the same. And he sends around the room a reproduction of a picture dated 1533 — Holbein's *The Ambassadors* — to be considered attentively; he will come to it.

Vision is ordered, in general, by *the function of images*, defined by point-by-point correspondence of two units in space: whatever the optical intermediaries, virtual or real image, the point-to-point mapping is essential. Whatever is of the image-mode in the field of vision is thus reducible to the simple schema that permits anamorphosis: *the relation of an image, as bound to a surface, with a certain point that we shall call the geometral point* — the straight line playing its role as the light-ray's path. Here art mingles with science — Leonardo the dioptrician and artist, Vitruvius nearby, Vignola and Alberti progressively interrogating the geometral laws of perspective — and around perspective-research centers a privileged interest in vision whose relation to *the institution of the Cartesian subject* cannot be missed: the Cartesian subject too is a sort of geometral point, a point of perspective. Around geometral perspective, the *tableau* — a function we shall return to — organizes itself in a way wholly new in the history of painting. The historical thesis is a structural one: perspective construction and the cogito are the same invention twice — a punctual subject facing a mapped field — so the critique of one is the critique of the other.

Now Diderot: the *Letter on the Blind for the Use of Those Who See* makes palpable that this whole construction *lets vision entirely escape*. For the geometral space of vision — even including the imaginary parts in the virtual space of the mirror, of which Lacan has made much — is *perfectly reconstructible, imaginable, by a blind man*. What geometral perspective handles is the *mapping of space, not sight*: the blind man can conceive that the field of space he knows as real might be perceived at a distance and as if simultaneously — he has only to grasp a temporal function, instantaneity. See Descartes' *Dioptrics*, where the action of the eyes is represented as the conjugated action of *two sticks*. The geometral dimension of vision therefore does not exhaust — far from it — what the field of vision as such proposes as the original subjectivating relation. The argument is elegant and devastating: Descartes himself models the eye on the blind man's staff; therefore the science of perspective is a haptics of space in which light figures only as straight line — and everything proper to *seeing* (light, color, the lure, the shimmer) is missing. That missing everything is where the gaze lives; hence the importance of accounting for perspective's *inverted* use in anamorphosis.

**The phallus in the picture.** Dürer himself invented the perspective apparatus — the *portillon*, the little gate: a lattice traversed by straight lines (rays or threads) linking each point of the world to the point where the canvas is crossed. Instituted to establish the correct perspective image — but *reverse its use*, and one obtains, with delight, not the restitution of the world but the deformation, on another surface, of the first image: the delicious game of making anything appear in a particular stretching. Such enchantment had its epoch — Baltrušaitis recounts the furious polemics and considerable treatises; the convent of the Minims near the rue des Tournelles bore, on the very long wall of a gallery, a fresco of Saint John at Patmos ("as if by chance," the seer of the Apocalypse) that had to be viewed through a hole for its deforming value to reach its height. Deformation can lend itself to every paranoiac ambiguity, and every use has been made of it from Arcimboldo to Dalí; this fascination *complements* what geometral perspective-research lets escape of vision.

Then the stroke: how is it that no one has ever thought to evoke here... *the effect of an erection*? Imagine a tattoo traced on the organ *ad hoc* in the resting state, taking in another state its developed form. How not see here, immanent to the geometral dimension — a *partial* dimension in the field of the gaze, a dimension that has nothing to do with vision as such — something symbolic of the function of *lack*, of the apparition of the *phallic ghost*? The obscene joke is a theorem: anamorphic stretching *is* the tumescence-detumescence of the phallus — the geometral field, precisely because it is vision-less mapping, carries inscribed in it the symbol of lack, the phallus as what appears only in deformation, presence indexing absence. Perspective's proud science is host to the *moins-phi*.

Now *The Ambassadors*. What do you see? What is that strange, suspended, oblique object in the foreground before the two figures? The two personages are frozen, stiffened in their *monstrative* ornaments; between them, the whole series of objects that in the painting of the epoch figure the symbols of *vanitas* — at the very time Cornelius Agrippa writes *De vanitate scientiarum*, aiming at sciences and arts alike — objects symbolic of the trivium and quadrivium. Before this monstration of the domain of appearance in its most fascinating forms, what is that object — here flying, here inclined? *You cannot know — for you turn away, escaping the fascination of the picture.* Begin to leave the room where it has long captivated you; turning around as you go — as the author of *Anamorphoses* describes — you grasp under this form — what? *A death's head.* The staging is essential to the doctrine: the skull is legible only in the act of exiting — only from the oblique, lost point where the spectator no longer occupies the geometral throne. The picture's truth is dealt to the viewer on the way out, at the moment of his own fall from the point of mastery.

Not thus does the figure first present itself — the author compares it to a cuttlebone; to Lacan it rather evokes Dalí's two-pound loaf perched on the head of an old woman chosen expressly miserable, filthy, and unaware, or the same painter's soft watches — whose signification is evidently no less *phallic* than that of what is drawn in flying position in the foreground. All of this manifests that *at the very heart of the epoch in which the subject is being drawn and geometral optics sought, Holbein makes visible something that is nothing other than the subject as annihilated* — annihilated in a form that is, properly speaking, the imaged incarnation of the *moins-phi* \[(−φ)\] of castration, which for us centers the whole organization of the desires through the frame of the fundamental drives. The reading gathers every thread of the lesson: the age of the cogito and of perspective produces, in its most official double portrait — power, learning, the quadrivium — the anamorphic stain that annihilates the subject of that very perspective; and the skull, cuttlebone, loaf, soft watch are one series: the phallic object as index of castration, *vanitas* rewritten as *moins-phi*.

But the function of vision must be sought further still. Then will be drawn from it not the phallic symbol, the anamorphic ghost, but *the gaze as such, in its pulsatile, dazzling and spread-out function*, as it is in this picture. For this picture is nothing but what every picture is: *a trap for the gaze* (*un piège à regard*). In any picture whatever, it is precisely in seeking the gaze in each of its points that you will see it disappear. The next lesson is thereby set: beyond the emblematic reading (the skull as memento mori, even as −φ) lies the structural one — painting as an apparatus that captures and tames the spectator's gaze, which vanishes wherever it is sought point by point.

**Réponses.** François Wahl asks for precision on the grasp of the gaze in the direction of desire, given the critique of Sartre. Without valorizing the dialectic of desire, Lacan answers, one cannot understand why the other's gaze should *disorganize the field of perception*: the subject in question is not that of reflexive consciousness but that of desire. One believes it is a matter of the geometral point-eye, whereas it is a matter of a quite different eye — *the one flying in the foreground of The Ambassadors*. Pressed — "one does not see how the other will reappear in your discourse" — Lacan deflects with a joke ("listen, the important thing is that I don't fall flat on my face"), an honest IOU: the Other's reappearance is deferred to the transference-lessons. Wahl then offers a reading Lacan endorses: subject and real are to be taken not in themselves but in their relation, with a *topological* definition — situated on either side of the split, in the resistance of the fantasy; the real is, in a way, *an experience of resistance*. Lacan: that is how my discourse is spun — *each term is sustained only by its topological relation with the others*, and the subject of the cogito falls under the same law. Asked whether topology is a method of discovery or of exposition: it is the *registration of the topology proper to our experience as analysts*, which can afterward be taken up in the metaphysical perspective — Merleau-Ponty was going that way; see the second part of the book, the reference to the Wolf Man and the finger-of-a-glove. Pierre Kaufmann notes that Lacan has given a typical structure of the gaze but said nothing of *the dilation of light*. Answer: the gaze is not the eye — except in that flying form in which Holbein has the nerve to show me *my own soft watch*... Next time, *light incarnate*. The two answers fix the agenda and the method at once: the seminar's concepts hold only relationally-topologically (no substances anywhere, subject and real included), and Lesson VIII owes an account of light — the element geometral optics reduced to the straight line.

### **VIII. The Line and Light**

*4 March 1964\.* The lesson pays Kaufmann's debt from the last Réponses: an account of *light* as against the line — of what geometral optics, a science fit for the blind, leaves out. Miller's keywords: *desire and the picture; story of a sardine can; the screen; mimicry; the organ; you never look at me from the place where I see you.* Its yield is the second triangle — the one in which I am not the seeing point but the seen picture — and, superposed on the first, the schema of the subject as *screen* and *stain*.

The opening widens the frame to biology: the function of the eye can lead the seeker far afield — since when have the organ, and first its mere presence, appeared in the living line? The subject's relation *to the organ* is at the heart of our experience: among all the organs we deal with — the breast, the feces, others — there is the eye, and it is striking how far back it goes: you innocently consume oysters without knowing that at that level of the animal kingdom the eye has already appeared. The zoological aside is a placement: the eye enters the seminar not as the noblest sense but as one organ in the psychoanalytic series (breast, feces...), which is exactly the register — organ, separation, *objet a* — in which the lesson will end.

**The triangle and its failure.** On the board, the simple triangular schema: *geometral point — object — image/tableau*, recalling the optics of the operative montage that testifies to perspective's inverted use, dominant in painting between the fifteenth and seventeenth centuries. Anamorphosis showed that painting is not a realistic reproduction of the things of space (an expression itself needing many reservations); and the schema also shows that *this* optics lets vision escape — it is *within reach of the blind*. Diderot's Letter demonstrated how much of what vision delivers of space the blind man can render, reconstruct, imagine, speak — Diderot building on this a permanent equivocation with metaphysical undertones that animates his text and gives it its bite. For us the geometral dimension lets us glimpse *how the subject who interests us is caught, maneuvered, captured in the field of vision*. Holbein showed it at once — Lacan, showing his hand more than usual: the singular floating object is there *to look at*, to catch in its trap, the looker — that is, us. The subject is *literally called into the picture, and represented there as caught*: the secret is delivered as we withdraw slightly leftward and turn back, and the magical floating object reflects to us *our own nothingness, in the figure of the death's head* — a use of the geometral dimension of vision *to captivate the subject*, an evident relation to desire that remains enigmatic. What desire is caught, fixed in the picture — and what desire motivates the artist to put something, and what, into the work? Such is today's path.

**Light against the thread.** In this matter of the visible, everything is a trap — and singularly, as Merleau-Ponty's chapter-title says so well, *entrelacs*, interlacing: not one of the divisions, not one of the double slopes of the function of vision but presents itself as a maze; the more fields we distinguish, the more they cross.
