In the geometral domain, it first seems that *light* gives us the thread — the thread linking us to each point of the object, functioning as thread indeed where it crosses the screen-network on which the image is registered; and light propagates in straight lines, that is assured. Yet reflect: *this thread has no need of light — it needs only to be a stretched thread*. That is why the blind man can follow our whole demonstration — feel an object of a certain height, follow the taut thread, learn to distinguish by fingertip on a surface a configuration reproducing the mapping of images — exactly as, in pure optics, the diversely proportioned, fundamentally homological correspondences of point to point always come down to situating two points on the same thread. Such a construction gives no grasp of *what light delivers*. The reduction is completed and reversed in one gesture: geometral optics is literally a string-theory of sight — its "light" is only the straightness of a cord — so everything proper to light remains to be thought.

Whence the classical philosophy of perception is dispatched. The philosophers — Alain latest and most brilliant, back through Kant, up to Plato — all exercise themselves on the alleged deception of perception, and all emerge masters of the exercise by pointing out that perception finds the object *where it is*, that the appearance of the cube as parallelogram is precisely, by reason of the rupture of space subtending perception, what makes us perceive it *as* cube. The whole conjuring-trick (*passez-muscade*) of classical dialectic around perception holds because it treats of *geometral* vision — vision situated in a space that is not in its essence visual. Alain's chestnut (we perceive the cube *through* its distorted appearance) is granted and dismissed: it proves the intellect masters perspectival *space*; it says nothing of seeing. The essential of the relation of appearance to being, of which the philosopher so easily makes himself master by conquering the field of vision, is *elsewhere*: not in the straight line but in *the point of light* — point of irradiation, streaming, fire, gushing source of reflections. Light propagates in straight lines, no doubt, but it *refracts, diffuses, floods, fills* — let us not forget that cup which is our eye — it *overflows* it too; it necessitates, around the ocular cup, a whole series of organs, apparatuses, defenses. The iris reacts not only to distance but to light, protecting what happens at the bottom of the cup; the eyelid blinks, contracts into its well-known grimace. Nor is the eye alone photosensitive: the whole surface of the integument — under diverse titles, not all visual — can be; and pigment-spots are a first sketch of photosensitive organs. In the eye, pigment functions fully and with infinite complexity — rhodopsin in the cones, the layered retina — coming and going in functions not all immediately clear, suggesting the depth, complexity and unity of the mechanisms of the relation to light. The physiology is doing philosophical work: against the eye-as-geometral-point, the eye is a *cup* that light fills and exceeds, ringed with defenses — an organ in commerce with an element, not a vertex in a diagram. The subject's relation to light is thereby announced as ambiguous — as the two triangles on the board show, inverting as they superpose: first example of the interlacing, the *chiasm*, that structures the whole domain.

**The sardine can.** To show that the subject's place in relation to light is other than the geometral point, the true story from around his twentieth year. The young intellectual, wanting immersion in some direct practice — rural, hunting, even maritime — is in a small boat with a family of fishermen in a little port, in the Brittany before trawler-industry, when fishing was done in a nutshell at one's own risk. Petit-Jean — who, like all his family, would die early of the tuberculosis that was then the ambient element of that whole social layer — points to something floating on the waves: a little can, a *sardine can*, glittering in the sun, witness to the canning industry they were charged to feed. And Petit-Jean says: *You see that can? Do you see it? Well, it doesn't see you\!* He found the episode very funny; Lacan, less so. Why less? Instructive. First: if it means anything that the can does not *see* him, it is because in a certain sense, all the same, *it looks at him* (*elle me regarde*). It looks at him at the level of the point of light — *the point at which everything that looks at me is situated* — and that is no metaphor.

And the story's bite: if someone tells him a story like that, it is because he, at that moment — as he has painted himself, among men wrenching their existence from what was for them rough nature — *made a picture* in a fairly unspeakable way. To say it all: *he made something of a stain in the picture* (*je faisais tache dans le tableau*). Feeling it is what made the joke, at his address, not so funny. The apologue is the lesson's hinge and one of the seminar's most quoted pages, and its logic should be taken exactly. Petit-Jean's joke runs on the first triangle (the can, eyeless, cannot occupy the geometral point). Its failure to amuse runs on the second: the glittering can *looks at* Lacan from the light-point because he, the intellectual afloat among the tubercular poor, is not the spectator of this scene but an incongruous *patch in it* — seen, placed, exposed. The class-discomfort is not decoration; it is the phenomenological proof that being-looked-at is a position in the picture, not an act of any eye.

The structure taken at the level of the subject reflects something already inscribed in the eye's natural relation to light. *I am not simply that punctiform being locatable at the geometral point from which the perspective is grasped. No doubt, at the bottom of my eye, the picture is painted. The picture, certainly, is in my eye. But I — I am in the picture.* What is light *looks at me* (*me regarde* — the French carrying both "looks at me" and "concerns me"), and by that light something is painted at the bottom of my eye — not the constructed relation, the object the philosopher dwells on, but *impression*, the streaming of a surface not situated for me in advance at its distance — something bringing in what the geometral relation elides: *depth of field*, with all its ambiguity, its variability, its being in no way mastered by me. It rather seizes me, solicits me at every instant, and makes of the landscape something other than a perspective, other than what I have called the picture.

The correlate of the picture — to be situated at the same place as it, *outside* — is *the point of gaze*. And what mediates between the two, what is between them, is of another nature than geometral optical space: something that operates not by being traversable but, on the contrary, *by being opaque* — *the screen* (*l'écran*). In what presents itself to me as the space of light, *what is gaze is always some play of light and opacity*: always that glittering (*miroitement*) at the heart of the little story, always what retains me, at each point, from being screen, what makes the light appear as shimmer overflowing it. To say it all: *the point of gaze always participates in the ambiguity of the jewel.* And I, if I am something in the picture, it is likewise in the form of the screen, which I earlier named the *stain*. The second triangle is thus complete — point of light → screen → picture, with the subject on the *picture* side — and its middle term is the great novelty: between gaze and picture stands not a transparent window but an opaque screen, and the subject's being-in-the-picture is his being-screen, being-stain. The jewel-formula compresses it: the gaze glitters — it is never pure source nor pure surface but the play of the two.

**Neither synthesis nor survol: mimicry.** "Subject" here is not the subjective of idealism; the *survol*, the hovering-over Lacan invokes to give the picture consistency, is not merely representative. Several ways to go wrong. There is the central-synthesis fact — Merleau-Ponty's examples in the *Phenomenology*: masking with a screen part of a field composing colors (two rotating wheels) changes utterly how the composition is seen; there we grasp the purely subjective note of central mechanism, the arranged light-play differing from what the subject perceives. There are reflection-effects — a yellow field beside a blue modifies it; and color as such is only subjective, no objective correlate in the spectrum attaching quality to wavelength. Something subjective in each case — but differently situated, and *not* what Lacan means by the subject's relation to the picture. Then Ruyer's *Néo-finalisme*, which to situate perception teleologically must posit the subject in *absolute survol* — needlessly, when his example, the perception of a checkerboard, belongs by essence to geometral optics, to the space *partes extra partes* that always objects to the grasp of the object. The survey of rivals fixes the claim by elimination: neither retinal synthesis, nor the subjectivity of qualia, nor a finalist hovering subject touches the phenomenon; what does is a domain "infinitely more extended than the privileged points where it appears" — *the facts of mimicry*, the only facts that can only be articulated in the phenomenal dimension of the survol *by which I situate myself in the picture as stain*.

Adaptation first, to clear it: at a stretch one can speak of adaptive coloration — Cuénot's probable cases where color adapting to ground is a defense *against light*: in a green-radiant milieu the animalcule makes itself green to send back the light as green and shelter from its effects. *But mimicry is something else entirely.* An example almost at random — no privileged case: the little crustacean *Caprella acanthifera*, nesting among those limit-animals the bryozoans, imitates — what? *What in that quasi-plant is a stain*: at one phase an intestinal loop makes a stain, at another something like a colored center functions. To that stained form the crustacean accommodates itself. *It makes itself stain, it makes itself picture, it inscribes itself in the picture.* There, properly speaking, is the original spring of mimicry — and from it, the fundamental dimensions of the subject's inscription in the picture appear infinitely better justified than groping divination could give. The zoology delivers the ontology: the organism does not copy another organism or match a background; it enters the *picture-function* — becomes a patch among patches — which is exactly the structure claimed for the human subject in the scopic field.

Caillois again, with the incontestable penetration sometimes granted the non-specialist: the facts demonstrate that *almost nothing of adaptation* — as ordinarily conceived, tied to survival-needs — is implicated in mimicry, which mostly shows itself inoperative or operating strictly *against* the presumed adaptive result. Instead, Caillois's three rubrics, the major dimensions of mimetic activity: *travesty, camouflage, intimidation* (*le travesti, le camouflage, l'intimidation*). In this domain the dimension presents itself by which the subject has to insert himself in the picture. Mimicry *gives something to be seen insofar as it is distinct from what could be called an itself that is behind*: its effect is camouflage in the properly technical sense — not harmonizing with the ground but, on a mottled ground, *making oneself mottle* (*se faire bigarrure*), exactly as human war-camouflage operates. In travesty a certain sexual finality is aimed at — and nature shows that aim proceeding by effects essentially of disguise, of *masquerade*: here a plane constitutes itself distinct from the sexual aim itself, playing an essential role in it, not to be too quickly labeled deception; the function of the *lure* is something else, before which judgment should be suspended until its incidence is measured. And intimidation involves that *over-value* (*survalue*) the subject always tries to attain in his appearance. In each case, beware of hastily invoking intersubjectivity — of thinking too quickly of the *other* who would supposedly be imitated: to imitate is doubtless to reproduce an image, but fundamentally it is, for the subject, *to be inserted in a function whose exercise seizes him*. The triad will map, in Lessons IX and XI, onto the human scopic field (masquerade, the tamed gaze, the evil eye); the present point is structural: mimicry is not a relation to another (no intersubjectivity) but capture by a function — the given-to-be-seen — that precedes any seer.

**Painting, the *dompte-regard*, Zeuxis and Parrhasios.** Caillois guides one step further: mimetic facts are analogous, at the animal level, to what in the human being manifests as *art, or painting* — the only objection being that for Caillois painting is apparently clear enough to explain something else by. What *is* painting? Not for nothing was the function where the subject must locate himself named the *tableau*. When a human subject undertakes to make of it a picture — to put into work that something whose center is the gaze — what is at stake? Some say the artist in the picture wants to *be subject*, painting distinguishing itself from all arts in that the artist means to impose himself on us *as gaze*; others answer by valorizing the object-side of the art-product. Something more or less appropriate in each — neither exhausting the matter. Lacan's thesis: certainly something of the gaze always manifests in the picture — the painter knows it, whose morality, research, quest, exercise is truly the *selection of a certain mode of gaze*; look at pictures most deprived of what is commonly called the gaze (a pair of eyes), Dutch or Flemish landscapes empty of the human figure, and you will end by seeing, as in watermark, something so specified for each painter that you feel the presence of the gaze — though that is research-object and perhaps illusion.

But the *function* of the picture — with respect to him to whom the painter, literally, *gives his picture to see* — has a relation to the gaze that is not, as first apprehension suggests, that of a gaze-trap. One might think the painter, like the actor, aims at the *m'as-tu-vu*, the look-at-me, desiring to be looked at. No: there is a relation to the gaze of the beholder, but more complex. The painter gives to the one who stands before his picture something that, in a whole part of painting at least, could be summed up: *You want to look? Well then, see this\!* He gives something as *feed for the eye* (*en pâture à l'œil*), but he invites the one to whom the picture is presented *to lay down his gaze there, as one lays down one's arms*. There is the *pacifying, Apollonian effect of painting*: something given not so much to the gaze as to the eye, something involving abandonment, *deposit*, of the gaze. This is the doctrine Lesson IX will name the *dompte-regard*, the gaze-taming: classical painting is not exhibitionism but a disarmament ritual — the wild gaze surrendered at the door in exchange for the eye's feed. What makes a problem is that a whole face of painting separates from this field — *expressionism*, which gives something going in the direction of a certain *satisfaction* — in Freud's sense, satisfaction of the *drive* — to what is demanded *by the gaze*. The exception proves the schema: expressionism refuses the pacification and feeds the gaze itself.

**The organ.** Now the question of the eye *as organ*. "Function creates the organ" — pure absurdity; it does not even explain it. Whatever is in the organism as organ always presents a great multiplicity of functions: in the eye the discriminatory function isolates maximally at the *fovea*, elected point of distinct vision; something else happens on the whole rest of the retina, unjustly labeled by specialists the scotopic field — and there the chiasm returns, since this field, supposedly made for lesser illumination, gives the *maximum* possibility of perceiving effects of light: to see a star of fifth or sixth magnitude (the Arago phenomenon), do not fix it straight on — it appears precisely by looking a little to the side. The astronomical fact is an emblem: the light-function culminates off-center, beside the point of fixation — as the gaze is grasped only askance, never at the focus.

These functions do not exhaust the character of the organ *as it surges up on the couch* — where it determines what every organ determines: *duties* (*des devoirs*). The fault of the confused reference to instinct is not seeing that instinct is *the way an organism has to extricate itself, for the best ends, with an organ* — and the animal scale abounds in cases where the organism succumbs before the surplus, the hyper-development of an organ. The alleged pre-adaptations of instinct amaze us; *the marvel is that of its organ the organism can make something at all.* The dictum reverses vitalist piety into a comic ethics of the organ — the organism saddled with its organ as with an obligation — and clears the ground for Part Three's thesis that the drive is a *montage*, not a natural aim. For us, in our reference to the unconscious, *it is the relation to the organ that is at stake*: not the relation to sexuality, nor even to sex (if that term can be given specific reference), but *to the phallus — insofar as it is lacking to what could be reached of the real in the aim of sex*. It is insofar as, at the heart of the experience of the unconscious, we deal with that organ — determined in the subject by the insufficiency organized in the castration complex — that we can grasp to what degree *the eye is caught in a similar dialectic*.

From the first approach, the dialectic of eye and gaze shows *no coincidence, but fundamentally lure* (*leurre*). *When, in love, I ask for a look, what is fundamentally unsatisfying and always missed is that — You never look at me from the place where I see you.* Conversely, *what I look at is never what I want to see.* The two chiasmatic sentences are the lesson's résumé and among the seminar's most cited: eye and gaze never meet; love's demand for the look founders on the non-reciprocity of the two triangles; and the scopic field is through and through the field of the lure. Whence the painter-beholder relation is a game — a *trompe-l'œil* game, whatever one says, with no reference to the improperly-called figurative if that smuggles in some underlying reality. The antique apologue proves it: Zeuxis's merit is grapes that drew the birds — the accent not on the grapes' perfection but on *even the birds' eye being deceived*; and Parrhasios triumphs over him by painting on the wall *a veil*, so lifelike that Zeuxis, turning to him, said: *Well, now show us what you've painted behind it.* By which it is shown that what is at stake is indeed *deceiving the eye* — *the triumph, over the eye, of the gaze*. The two painters divide the field exactly: Zeuxis's grapes lure the animal eye (the natural lure of mimicry); Parrhasios's veil lures the *human* gaze — for what Zeuxis asks is to see *what is behind*, and the veil is the painting of that very demand. The gaze desires what appearance conceals; paint the concealment and you have caught the gaze itself. Desire, not resemblance, is the principle of *trompe-l'œil*.

**Réponses.** Safouan asks: in contemplating the picture, does the eye *rest* from the gaze? Lacan takes up the dialectic of appearance and its beyond: *if, beyond appearance, there is no thing-in-itself, there is the gaze* — in that relation the eye is situated as organ. Pressed — beyond appearance, the lack or the gaze? — he answers with the general theory: at the scopic level, insofar as the drive plays there, one refinds *the same function of the objet a locatable in all the other dimensions*. *The objet a is something from which the subject, to constitute itself, has separated itself as organ. It is valid as symbol of the lack — that is, of the phallus, not as such but insofar as it makes lack.* Hence it must be an object that is, first, *separable*; second, *having some relation to the lack*. Incarnations: at the *oral* level, it is the *nothing* — what the subject was weaned from is no longer anything for him; in anorexia nervosa the child *eats the nothing* — by which one grasps how the weaning-object can come to function at the level of castration, as privation. The *anal* level is *the place of metaphor* — one object for another, giving the feces in place of the phallus; whence the anal drive is the domain of oblativity, of the gift — where one is caught short, where for lack one cannot give what there is to give, one always has the resource of giving something else; that is why, in his morality, man inscribes himself at the anal level — *especially the materialist*. At the *scopic* level, we are no longer at the level of demand but *of desire, of the desire of/to the Other*; likewise at the *invocatory* level, the drive closest to the experience of the unconscious. In general: the relation of the gaze to what one wants to see is a relation of lure — the subject presents himself as other than he is, and what he is given to see is not what he wants to see. *It is by this that the eye can function as objet a — that is, at the level of the lack (−φ).* The answer is a doctrinal condensation Part Three will unfold: the table of the four objects (nothing, feces, gaze, voice) ordered by demand and desire, each a separable organ-piece standing for the phallus-as-lacking — the Kantian echo deliberate and inverted: where the thing-in-itself stood, the gaze; behind appearance not a noumenon but an object fallen from the subject.

### **IX. What Is a Picture?**

*11 March 1964\.* The closing lesson of Part Two takes up the wager announced in its first sentence: to hold the ground where the *objet a* is *most evanescent* in its function of symbolizing the central lack of desire — the lack always pointed univocally by the algorithm (−φ). Miller's keywords: *being and its semblance; the lure of the screen; gaze-taming and trompe-l'œil; the gaze from behind; the gesture and the touch; the given-to-be-seen and invidia.* On the board: *the objet a in the field of the visible is the gaze*; beneath it, bracketed, "in nature — as (−φ)"; and below, the two triangular systems now *superposed* — the geometral triangle that puts at our place the subject of representation, and the second that makes me myself a picture. On the right-hand line sit both the apex of the first triangle (the geometral subject-point) and the place where I become picture *under the gaze*, inscribed at the apex of the second. The superposition is the diagram of the whole of Part Two: in the scopic register I occupy simultaneously the seeing point and the seen surface, and the two never fuse.

**I am looked at, that is, I am a picture.** The section opens on the thesis in its strongest form: *in the scopic field, the gaze is outside — I am looked at, that is to say, I am a picture.* This is the function found at the most intimate level of the institution of the subject in the visible: what fundamentally determines me in the visible is the gaze that is outside. *It is through the gaze that I enter the light, and it is from the gaze that I receive its effect* — whence the gaze is the instrument through which light is incarnated and through which — decomposing the word as usual — *I am photo-graphed*. The hyphenated pun is exact: light (*photo-*) writes (*\-graphed*) me; the subject of the visible is not the photographer but the plate.

Not the philosophical problem of representation, then. In that perspective, faced with representation, I assure myself as the knowing consciousness that knows it is *only* representation and that beyond is the thing, the thing-in-itself — behind the phenomenon, the noumenon; I can do nothing about it, since my transcendental categories, as Kant says, do as they please and force me to take the thing their way — and at bottom that is fine, everything happily arranges itself. The mock-Kantian shrug measures the distance: critical philosophy converts the beyond of appearance into a reassurance (the noumenon safely out of reach); psychoanalysis starts instead from *a fracture, a bipartition, a split of being to which being accommodates itself, from nature on* — observable across the modulated scale of what is inscribable, at the last term, under the general heading of mimicry.

That split enters play manifestly in sexual union and in the fight to the death alike. Being decomposes there, sensationally, *between its being and its semblance*, between itself and that *paper tiger* it offers to be seen. In the parade (most often the male animal's) or the grimacing inflation of combat-intimidation, the being gives of itself — or receives from the other — something that is *mask, double, envelope, detached skin* — detached to cover the frame of a shield. *It is through this form separated from itself that the being enters play in its effects of life and death*, and one can say that it is by means of this doubling of the other, or of itself, that the conjunction is realized from which proceeds the renewal of beings in reproduction. The zoology of Lesson VIII is here raised to an ontological thesis with a Maoist wink (the paper tiger): nature itself splits the living being from its display, and both Eros (parade, reproduction) and Thanatos (combat) transact *in the semblance*, not in the being.

The lure thus plays an essential function — and nothing else seizes us at the level of clinical experience when, with respect to what one might imagine of attraction between the poles, masculine consigned to feminine, we apprehend *the prevalence of the travesty, the masquerade*: without any doubt, *it is through the intermediary of masks that the masculine and the feminine meet in the most acute, the most burning way*. The sentence quietly detonates under the whole edifice of "genital harmony": there is no immediate sexual rapport of being to being; the sexes meet via masks — a thesis Lacan will radicalize years later, but whose ground is laid here in the scopic doctrine.

Only — and here the human difference — the subject, the human subject, *the subject of desire that is the essence of man*, is not, unlike the animal, entirely caught by this imaginary capture. *He finds his bearings in it.* How? Insofar as *he isolates the function of the screen and plays with it*. Man knows how to play with the mask *as that beyond which there is the gaze*. *The screen is here the locus of mediation.* The animal is captured by the lure; the human subject, split by the signifier, can take the screen *as* screen — whence masquerade, painting, and the whole civilization of the semblance. Merleau-Ponty's Gelb-and-Goldstein examples give the perceptual base: when an isolated beam of light dominates us, appearing as a milky cone that prevents seeing what it lights, the mere introduction of a small screen — cutting into what is lit without being seen — makes the milky light retreat into shadow and *the object it hid surge up*. At the perceptual level, the phenomenon of a relation to be taken in a more essential function: *in its relation to desire, reality appears only as marginal*. The formula, dropped almost in passing, is among the seminar's deepest: reality is not the field's center but its *margin* — what appears around the screen once the captivating light is cut; the center is occupied by the lure, the stain, the gaze.

Whence a remark on pictorial composition: it is a captivating game to refind in the picture its dividing-lines of surfaces, lines of flight, lines of force, the frames where the image finds its status — but Lacan is astonished that a remarkable book calls these *charpentes*, scaffolding, thereby eluding their principal effect; with irony, the book's back cover reproduces a Rouault whose circular tracing shows the essential. For there is something whose *absence* one can always note in a picture — unlike perception: *the central field, where the eye's separative power is exercised at its maximum in vision. In every picture it can only be absent, replaced by a hole* — a reflection, in sum, of the pupil behind which is the gaze. Consequently, insofar as the picture enters a relation to desire, *the place of a central screen is always marked* — which is precisely that by which, before the picture, *I am elided as subject of the geometral plane*. Whence the section's last word: the picture does not play in the field of representation; *its end and its effect are elsewhere*. The structural discovery is elegant: painting has no fovea. The picture reproduces everything of vision except the point of maximal discrimination, and that structural blank — pupil-hole, central screen — is where the beholding subject is dropped from his geometral throne.

**They have eyes that they might not see.** In the scopic field everything is articulated between two antinomic terms: on the side of things there is the gaze — *the things look at me* — and yet *I see them*. In this sense must be heard the word hammered in the Gospel: *They have eyes that they might not see.* Not to see *what*? *Precisely, that the things look at them.* The scriptural citation is re-pointed with perfect economy: the eyes are not failing at sight; they are *organs of not-seeing* — apparatuses for eliding the gaze of things, exactly the elision Lessons VI–VIII established as the waking state's regime.

This is why painting entered our field by Caillois's little door (whom he had called René in a slip — "God knows why"; the slip-confession is left standing, a small enactment of Lesson II inside Lesson IX). Not an occasion for psychoanalyzing the painter — always slippery, scabrous, provoking the hearer's modesty; nor art-criticism (someone close to him was troubled that he seemed to approach it — the danger is real, confusion to be avoided). No formula gathers the infinitely diverse aims, ruses, tricks that the subjectivating structure's variations across time have imposed on painting. And the *dompte-regard* formula announced last time — the beholder always led by painting to *lay down his gaze* — was at once corrected: *expressionism* situates itself in a completely direct appeal to the gaze — Munch, James Ensor, Kubin; a painting one could locate geographically as *besieging* what today concentrates itself of painting in Paris (when will the limits of that siege be forced? — for André Masson, with whom he spoke recently, the most present question). To give such references is not to enter the moving historical game of criticism; Lacan places himself at *the radical principle of the function of this fine art*.

Underline first: it was from painting that Merleau-Ponty was most specially led to overturn the relation always made by thought between the eye and the mind (*L'Œil et l'esprit*). That the painter's function is something quite other than organizing the field of representation in which the philosopher held us in our status as subject — this he admirably located, starting from what he calls, with Cézanne himself, those *little blues, little browns, little whites*, those touches that *rain from the painter's brush*. What is that? What does it determine, and how? It already gives form and incarnation to the field into which the psychoanalyst advanced after Freud — with what in Freud is *mad audacity* and in his followers quickly becomes imprudence.

Freud always marked, with infinite respect, that he did not intend to pronounce on what makes the true value of artistic creation: concerning painters as poets, there is a line at which his appreciation stops — he cannot say, he does not know, what makes the value of art for all who look or hear. Nevertheless, studying Leonardo, he seeks the function played in his creation by his original fantasy — his relation to the two mothers he sees figured in the Louvre painting and the London cartoon by that double body, branching at the waist, seeming to blossom from a mingling of legs at the base. Is that the way to seek? Or should the principle of artistic creation be seen in its *extracting* — recall the translation of *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz* — that something which *takes the place of* representation? Certainly not — save in very rare works, an oneiric painting, rare and barely situable in painting's function; there, perhaps, the limit where one would designate so-called psychopathological art. The double refusal is methodologically sharp: neither the pathography (Freud's Leonardo, the fantasy behind the work) nor a direct exhibition of the unconscious's stand-in structure (dream-painting, art brut) touches the principle of painting; the painter's creation is structured otherwise — and the new algorithms permit asking how, at the level of *sublimation* and its value in a social field.

Freud's formula, vague and precise at once, concerning only the work's success: if a creation of desire, pure at the painter's level, takes commercial value — a gratification one may call secondary — it is because its effect has something *profitable for society*. Stay vague: the work *appeases* people, comforts them, by showing them that some can live from the *exploitation of their desire*. But for it to satisfy them so much, there must also be the other incidence: that *their* desire to contemplate finds some appeasement there. It *elevates the soul*, as one says — that is, *it incites them to renunciation*. Does not something here indicate the function of the *dompte-regard*? The sociology of art is given its analytic cash-value with beautiful irony: the "elevation" praised by aesthetics is the gaze laid down — renunciation induced; the market pays the painter for a public disarmament ritual.

**Trompe-l'œil against the Idea.** The *dompte-regard* also presents under the face of *trompe-l'œil* — apparently against the tradition, which holds the two functions quite distinct. Return to Zeuxis and Parrhasios and the ambiguity of two levels: the natural function of the lure, and *trompe-l'œil*. If birds rushed at Zeuxis's surface, taking the picture for peckable grapes, note that this success in no way implies the grapes were admirably reproduced — like those in the basket of Caravaggio's Bacchus at the Uffizi. Had they been such, the birds would hardly have been deceived — why should birds see grapes in that style of tour de force? *There must be something more reduced, closer to the sign, in what can constitute for birds the grape-prey.* But Parrhasios's opposite example makes clear that to deceive *a man*, what one presents him is *the painting of a veil — that is, of something beyond which he demands to see.* The bird is caught by the sign-stimulus (the ethologists' releaser, "more reduced" than any likeness); the man is caught by his desire — the demand to see behind. Two lures, two subjects.

Here the apologue shows why *Plato protests against the illusion of painting*. The point is not that painting gives an illusory equivalent of the object, even if Plato appears to say so. It is that *the trompe-l'œil of painting presents itself as something other than what it is*. What seduces and satisfies us in trompe-l'œil? What captivates and delights us? *The moment when, by a simple displacement of our gaze, we can perceive that the representation does not move with it, and that there is only trompe-l'œil* — for at that moment it appears as something other than it gave itself as, or rather *it now gives itself as being that other thing*. *The picture does not rival appearance; it rivals what Plato designates beyond appearance as the Idea. It is because the picture is that appearance which says it is what gives appearance, that Plato rises against painting as an activity rival to his own.* And *this other thing is the petit a*, around which turns a combat whose soul is the trompe-l'œil. The re-reading of *Republic* X is the lesson's philosophical summit and deserves its full weight: Plato's fury at the painter (third from the truth, imitator of imitations) is re-diagnosed — painting is dangerous to philosophy not because it copies appearances badly but because it *occupies the very place of the beyond-of-appearance*, delivering there not the Idea but the *objet a*: an appearance that avows itself appearance and thereby captures the desire the Idea was supposed to command. Painter and philosopher are rivals for the same beyond — and the painter holds it with a veil.

Figure the painter's position concretely in history and he appears as the source of something that can pass into the real and is at all times *taken on lease* (*pris à ferme*): the painter no longer depends on noble patrons, but nothing fundamental changes with the picture-dealer — another patron of the same stamp; before the noble patron, the religious institution gave him his living with the holy image. *There is always a Société fermière of the painter* — a leaseholding company — and always it is a matter of the *objet a*: or rather of reducing it — mythical as this may sound at a certain level — to an *a* with which, at the last term, it is the painter as creator who dialogues. But far more instructive is how the *a* functions in its *social* repercussion.

**The gaze from behind.** The icons — the triumphant Christ of the vault at Daphni, the admirable Byzantine mosaics — manifestly have the effect of holding us under their gaze. One could stop there; but it would miss the spring of what engages the painter to make the icon and what it serves in being presented to us. There is gaze in it, of course — *but it comes from further away. What makes the value of the icon is that the god it represents also looks at it. It is meant to please God.* The artist operates here on the *sacrificial* plane: playing on the fact that there are things — here, images — *that can awaken the desire of God*. God is a creator, indeed, by creating certain images — Genesis indicates it with the *tselem Elohim*, the image of God; and iconoclastic thought itself still preserves this: that there is a god *who does not like that* — he is indeed the only one. Lacan declines to go further into a register that would carry to the heart of one of the essential elements of the mainspring of the Names-of-the-Father: *that a certain pact can be established beyond every image*. Where we are, the image remains the go-between with the divinity: if Yahweh forbids the Jews to make idols, *it is because they please the other gods*. In a certain register, it is not God who is not anthropomorphic — *it is man who is asked not to be*. The suspended seminar surfaces for a moment, and what it deposits is precise: the icon is a lure *for God's eye* — art's first patron is the divine gaze — while monotheism's iconoclasm marks the possibility of a pact beyond the image, a symbolic beyond the scopic; and the inversion (man asked not to be anthropomorphic) is left glinting.

Next stage, the *communal*: the great hall of the Doge's Palace, painted with all the battles, Lepanto and the rest. The social function, already sketched at the religious level, shows itself plainly. Who comes to such places? Those who form what Retz calls *les peuples*, the peoples. And what do the peoples see in these vast compositions? *The gaze of those persons who — when the peoples are not there — deliberate in this hall. Behind the picture, it is their gaze that is there.* You see: *there are always lots of gazes behind* (*tout plein de regards là-derrière*). Nothing new is introduced in this regard by the epoch Malraux distinguishes as modern, dominated by what he calls *the incomparable monster* — the gaze of the painter claiming to impose itself, all alone, as the gaze. There has always been gaze back there. But — the subtlest point — *that gaze: where does it come from?* The state-room example is worth pausing on: the picture is the *screen of power* — the peoples contemplate battle-scenes and are held, through them, under the absent councillors' gaze; painting installs the Other's gaze in the room. Malraux's romantic monster (the artist-gaze) merely privatizes a structure as old as Daphni.

**The rain of the brush.** Back to Cézanne's little blues, little whites, little browns — and to Merleau-Ponty's example in *Signes*: the slow-motion film of Matisse painting, by which *Matisse himself was overwhelmed*. Merleau-Ponty underlines the paradox: the gesture, enlarged by time's distension, lets us imagine the most perfect deliberation in each touch. *A mirage*, he says. At the rhythm at which these little touches *rain from the painter's brush* toward the miracle of the picture, it is not a matter of choice but of something else. Can we formulate that something else? Take the question at its closest: *if a bird painted, would it not be by letting its feathers fall — a snake its scales, a tree by un-caterpillaring itself and raining down its leaves?* What accumulates here is *the first act in the deposition of the gaze*. A sovereign act, since it passes into something that materializes and that will, from this sovereignty, render caducous, excluded, inoperative whatever, come from elsewhere, presents itself before this product. The theory of painting is completed from the painter's side: as the beholder deposits his gaze before the canvas, the painter has *already* deposited his — molted it, feather by feather, touch by touch — and the two depositions meet in the object. Painting is a double laying-down of arms around the *a*.

Do not forget: *the painter's touch is something in which a movement terminates*. We face something giving a new and different sense to the term *regression* — the motor element in the sense of a *response that engenders, behind itself, its own stimulus*. The original temporality by which the relation to the other is situated as distinct is here, in the scopic dimension, *that of the terminal instant*. What, in the identificatory dialectic of the signifier and the spoken, projects itself forward as *haste* is here, on the contrary, *the end* — what, at the departure of every new intelligence, will be called *the instant of seeing*. This terminal moment lets us distinguish *a gesture from an act*. It is by the gesture that the touch is applied to the canvas — and so truly is the gesture always present there that the picture is first felt by us (as the terms *impression*, *impressionism* say) as more affined to the gesture than to any other type of movement; every action represented in a picture appears as a battle-scene — theatrical, made for the gesture. And this insertion in the gesture is why a picture — figurative or not — *cannot be hung in reverse*: flip a slide and you perceive at once if left is shown in place of right; *the sense of the hand's gesture designates sufficiently this lateral symmetry*. The logical-time apparatus of Lesson III is here run backward with complete control: in the dialectic of the signifier, the instant of seeing is *initial* and haste drives toward the conclusion; in the scopic register the temporal order inverts — the instant of seeing is *terminal*, the freeze in which the gesture completes and deposits itself. The proof from the flipped slide is a small marvel: paintings are chiral because they are frozen gestures, not mapped spaces.

So the gaze operates in a certain *descent* — a descent of desire, no doubt, but how say it? *The subject is not completely in it; he is remote-controlled* (*téléguidé*). Modifying the formula of desire as unconscious — man's desire is the desire of the Other — Lacan says it is a matter of a sort of *desire to the Other* (*désir à l'Autre*), at the end of which is *the given-to-be-seen* (*le donner-à-voir*). And how does this given-to-be-seen appease anything, if not in that *there is an appetite of the eye in the one who looks*? That eye-appetite to be fed makes the charm-value of painting — to be sought on a much less elevated plane than one supposes: in the true function of the organ of the eye, *the eye full of voracity, which is the evil eye*.

**Invidia.** Striking, given the universality of the evil-eye function, that there is *nowhere any trace of a good eye, of an eye that blesses*. What can this mean but that the eye carries with it the fatal function of being *in itself* endowed — playing on several registers — with a *separative power*? And this separative goes far beyond distinct vision: the powers attributed to it — drying up the milk of the animal it falls on (a belief as widespread in our time as in any, and in the most civilized countries), carrying illness and misfortune — where better to image them than in *invidia*, envy? *Invidia comes from videre*, to see. The most exemplary *invidia*, for us analysts, is the one Lacan long ago lifted from Augustine, who gives it its whole fate: the little child looking at his brother hanging at his mother's breast, looking at him *amare conspectu* — with a bitter look — that decomposes him and has on himself the effect of a poison. The Augustinian scene (*Confessions* I) has been Lacan's touchstone since the mirror-stage essay; here it receives its final placement — not in the imaginary of aggressive rivalry but in the scopic drive.

To understand *invidia* in its gaze-function, do not confuse it with jealousy. What the small child — or whoever — envies is not at all necessarily what he *might want* (as one improperly says: what he could have *envie* of). The child looking at his little brother — who says he still needs to be at the breast? Everyone knows envy is commonly provoked by the possession of goods that would be, to the envier, *of no use whatever*, and whose true nature he does not even suspect. *Such is true envy. It makes the subject pale before what? — before the image of a completeness that closes back on itself* — before the fact that *the petit a, the separated a from which he is suspended, can be for another the possession with which that other satisfies himself, the Befriedigung.* This is the seminar's definitive analysis of envy, and it is structural, not psychological: envy is not wanting the other's good (useless to the envier) but being *decomposed* by the spectacle of a closed circuit — another subject enjoying, as possession, the very object whose separation constitutes me. The nursing infant at the breast is the image of the un-separated; the onlooking child pales before his own constitutive loss exhibited as another's satisfaction. It is to this register — *the eye desperate-ized by the gaze* — that one must go to grasp the appeasing, civilizing, charming spring of the picture-function. And the fundamental relation of the *a* to desire, Lacan closes, will serve as exemplar in what he now introduces concerning *the transference*. The bridge to Part Three is laid exactly here: from the object that causes desire in the field of the visible to the object at stake in the analytic relation.

**Réponses.** Tort asks for the relation between the *gesture* and the *instant of seeing*. What is a gesture — a gesture of threat, say? *Not a blow that is interrupted*: something *made to stop and be suspended*, inscribing itself *backward* (the blow may be carried through afterward, but *as threat-gesture* it is registered behind). This very particular temporality — defined by the term *arrest*, creating its signification behind itself — distinguishes gesture from act. Witness the Peking Opera's combats: fighting as it has always been done, far more with gestures than with blows; in those ballets no one ever strikes, gliding in different spaces where sequences of gestures unfold that yet, in traditional combat, have their value *as weapons* — at the limit sufficing as instruments of intimidation. Primitives go to war with grimacing, horrible masks and terrifying gestures; don't think it is over — American marines were taught, to answer the Japanese, to grimace as much as they. *Our recent weapons too can be considered gestures. Heaven grant they hold to that status\!* The nuclear aside lands the theory of the gesture in 1964: deterrence is intimidation-display — the whole arsenal a suspended blow, inscribed backward — and civilization's survival hangs on weapons remaining gestures.

Then, unprompted, a coda on the painter's material: the authenticity of what comes to light in painting is diminished, in us humans, by the fact that *our colors we must go seek where they are — in shit* (*dans la merde*). The allusion to birds that could pluck their own feathers was made because *we have no feathers*: the creator will only ever participate in the creation of *a small dirty deposit, a succession of small dirty deposits juxtaposed*. By this dimension we are in scopic creation: *the gesture as movement given to be seen*. The scatological turn is doctrinally precise, joining the anal object (Lesson VIII's Réponses: feces, the gift) to the scopic: human painting sublimates deposit — pigment as excrement given form — where the bird would simply molt its own splendor.

Pressed by Tort on the temporality: Lacan marks the *suture, the pseudo-identification* between the terminal arrest-time of the gesture and what, in the other dialectic (identificatory haste), he posits as the *first* time, the instant of seeing. *They overlap but are certainly not identical, since one is initial and the other terminal.* And the supplement he lacked time for: this terminal gaze-time that completes a gesture stands in the closest relation to the evil eye. *The gaze in itself not only terminates the movement, it freezes it.* The dances just mentioned are punctuated by arrest-times in which the actors halt in a blocked attitude — that halt, that freeze, is *the fascinatory effect*: it is a matter of *dispossessing the evil eye of the gaze, to conjure it. The evil eye is the fascinum*, that whose effect is to arrest movement and literally *to kill life*: at the moment the subject halts, suspending his gesture, *he is mortified*. The anti-life, anti-movement function of this terminal point is the *fascinum*, and it is precisely one of the dimensions in which the power of the gaze is exercised directly. The instant of seeing can intervene here only as *suture*, junction of the imaginary and the symbolic — taken up again in the dialectic of haste, the forward élan *that concludes on the fascinum*. Then the demarcation: unlike the invocatory field — vocatory, vocational — where the subject is essentially *indeterminate*, in the scopic field *the subject is properly determined* — determined by the very separation that the cut of the *a* determines, that is, by what the gaze introduces of the fascinatory. ("Are you a bit more satisfied? Completely?" — "Almost.") The exchange delivers a theoretical asymmetry of the first importance, easy to miss in the banter: of the two "higher" drives, the invocatory leaves the subject indeterminate (closest to the unconscious, Lesson VIII said), while the scopic *determines* him — freezes him — via the fascinum. Sight fixes; the voice suspends.

Wahl objects that Mediterranean civilization also knows the *prophylactic eye*, protective across a journey, tied to movement rather than arrest. Lacan sorts the amulets: what is prophylactic is *allopathic* — the horn, coral or not, and clearer cases like the *turpicula res* described by Varro: *quite simply a phallus*. For *it is insofar as all human desire is based on castration that the eye takes on its virulent, aggressive function — and not simply its luring one, as in nature.* Among the amulets one can also gather counter-eye forms — that is *homeopathic*; by that route the prophylactic function is introduced. And a confession of philological defeat: he looked in the Bible for passages where the eye confers *baraka*, blessing — hesitated at a few small places — *decidedly no. The eye can be prophylactic, but in any case it is not beneficent — it is maleficent. In the Bible, and even in the New Testament, there are no good eyes; evil ones are in every corner.* The exchange completes the doctrine of the eye: its apotropaia are either the phallus (like against like: castration's symbol against castration's agent) or a counter-eye — never a blessing eye, because the organ's separative power is original, its benevolence nonexistent.

Miller closes the session with the philosophical question: since Merleau-Ponty subverts Cartesian space not to open the transcendental space of the relation to the Other but to reach intersubjectivity or the pre-objective, savage, primordial world — does *Le Visible et l'invisible* incite Lacan to change anything in his *Temps modernes* homage? — *"Absolutely nothing."* (With the prior correction, wryly delivered: he had not said Merleau-Ponty posited the landmarks of the unconscious, only supposed that "the few traces there are of the unconscious mustard in his notes" might have brought him to pass into Lacan's field — "but I am not sure.") The two-word answer is the demarcation made final: homage entire, convergence real, identity nil — the flesh is not the signifier, the chiasm is not the split, and Part Two closes with the gaze secured as an analytic, not a phenomenological, object.

## **Transference and the Drive**

### **X. Presence of the Analyst**

*15 April 1964\.* After the Easter break, Part Three opens on the third fundamental concept. Miller's keywords: *problems of transference; obscurantism in analysis; ablata causa; the Other, already there; the unconscious is outside; an article in the International Journal.* The lesson's work is to wrest the concept of transference from the two receptions that neutralize it — transference as affect, and transference as mere repetition — and to establish its paradox: transference is the *closure* of the unconscious, and yet the point where interpretation takes effect. Lacan opens on a gift-matchbox inscribed *the art of listening is almost the equal of that of speaking well* — "that distributes our tasks; let us hope we are up to them."

**The received semantics.** In common opinion transference is represented as an *affect*, vaguely qualified positive or negative. It is generally received, not without foundation, that positive transference is love — though the term as used here is thoroughly approximate. Freud very early posed the question of the *authenticity* of the love produced in transference; the general tendency is to hold it a sort of false love, a shadow of love — but Freud was far from tipping the balance that way, and not the least interest of the experience of transference is to pose, further perhaps than it has ever been carried, the question of authentic love, *eine echte Liebe*. As for negative transference, one is more prudent — never identifying it with hate, preferring "ambivalence," a term that masks even more confused things. More justly: positive transference is when you have a soft spot for the fellow in question — the analyst — and negative, when you keep an eye on him (*on l'a à l'œil*). The joke is doctrinal in miniature: even colloquially, the negative transference is scopic — the analyst held *in the eye* — a wink back at Part Two. There is also the usage in which transference *structures* all the particular relations to that other who is the analyst, discounting every thought that gravitates around it — whence the parenthetical, suspicious note: *he is in full transference*. Double semantic bearings, sufficient for now — but not to be rested in, since the aim is the *concept*.

The concept is determined by the function it has in a praxis: it directs the treatment of patients, and inversely the treatment commands the concept. Does this settle whether transference is tied to analytic practice as its product, even artifact? Ida Macalpine pushed that attempt furthest — whatever her merit ("a very stubborn person"), the extreme position cannot be received. Yet even were transference a product of the analytic situation, the situation cannot create the phenomenon *from whole cloth*: to produce it there must be, outside it, possibilities already present to which it will give their — perhaps unique — composition. Nothing excludes there being, where no analyst is on the horizon, transference-effects exactly structurable like the play of transference in analysis; analysis, discovering them, simply provides their experimental model, not necessarily different from the natural one. So that making transference emerge in analysis, where it finds its structural foundations, may well be the only way to introduce the concept's universal application — it will suffice to cut the cord mooring it to the analytic sphere, and more, to the *doxa* adjoining. All truism, he concedes — but worth staking the boundary at the entry: transference is neither an artifact of the couch nor a mere instance of a general suggestibility; it is a structure that analysis isolates and that exceeds analysis.

**The presence of the analyst and the loss.** The introduction recalls a coherence-requirement among the founding concepts, already marked in the way the unconscious was approached: it could not be separated from *the presence of the analyst*. A very fine term — wrongly reduced to the tearful preachifying, the serous bloat, the slightly sticky caress that incarnates it in a book published under that title. *The presence of the analyst is itself a manifestation of the unconscious* — so that when it manifests nowadays, in certain encounters, as *refusal of the unconscious* (an avowed tendency in some quarters), that very refusal must be integrated into the concept of the unconscious. Here is quick access to the formulation put in the forefront: a movement of the subject *that opens only to close*, in a certain temporal pulsation — a pulsation more radical than the insertion in the signifier that no doubt motivates it but is not primary to it at the level of essence, "since of essence I have been provoked to speak." The thesis is stated with maximum compression and should be unpacked: the analyst's presence is not a benevolent accompaniment to the unconscious but one of its formations — including when that presence functions as the very refusal of the unconscious (the ego-psychological analyst *is* a closure-phenomenon); and the pulsation of opening/closing is declared more radical even than the signifier-structure.

The Rome Report's "new alliance" with Freud's discovery is recalled in its exact formula: *the unconscious is the sum of the effects of speech on a subject, at the level at which the subject constitutes himself from the effects of the signifier*. "Subject" here designates neither the living substrate needed for the subjective phenomenon, nor any substance, nor any being of knowledge in its pathos, nor an incarnated logos — but *the Cartesian subject, appearing at the moment doubt recognizes itself as certainty* — except that by our approach the foundations of this subject reveal themselves much wider, and by the same stroke much more *servile* with respect to the certainty it misses. *That is what the unconscious is.* The definition is the seminar's tightest: same subject as Descartes', wider base, deeper servitude — certain in form, missing in fact.

There is a link between this field and the moment, Freud's moment, at which it reveals itself — expressed by aligning Freud's step with Newton's, Einstein's, Planck's: *a-cosmological*, in that all these fields trace in the real a furrow new with respect to any knowledge attributable from all eternity to God. And then the paradox that secures the Freudian field's surest subsistence: *it is a field that, by its nature, is lost. It is here that the presence of the psychoanalyst is irreducible — as witness of this loss.* At this level nothing more can be drawn from it: a dead loss (*perte sèche*), settled by no gain, save its resumption in the function of pulsation. The claim inverts every scientistic hope for analysis: the field does not accumulate; it loses itself by nature (the unconscious closes; the discovery is forgotten "exactly as Freud foresaw" — Lesson II), and the analyst is not the field's engineer but the *witness of the loss*. His presence is irreducible for that reason and no other.

The loss occurs necessarily in a zone of shadow — designated by the oblique stroke dividing the blackboard formulas facing each term: unconscious, repetition, transference. And this zone comports, as to the facts of analytic practice, a certain reinforcement of *obscurantism*, very characteristic of the condition of man in our time of so-called information — an obscurantism Lacan credits the future with finding unheard-of. The function psychoanalysis has taken in propagating the style that names itself the *American way of life* is exactly what he designates by that term, marked by the revalorization of notions long refuted in the analytic field — such as *the predominance of the ego-functions*. On this ground the analyst's presence — by the very slope on which the vanity of his discourse appears — *must be included in the concept of the unconscious*. Today's analysts must reckon with this dross (*scorie*) in their operations, as the *caput mortuum* of the discovery of the unconscious. It justifies maintaining, inside analysis, a conflictual position necessary to analysis's very existence. The alchemical figure closes Lesson I's circle with a vengeance: *caput mortuum* is the alchemist's dead residue after distillation — the ego-psychological establishment is the inert precipitate of Freud's discovery — and Lacan's own war-footing (the "conflictual position," his excommunication) is thereby made internal to the concept.

If analysis rests on a fundamental conflict, on an initial and radical drama with respect to everything that can be put under the rubric of the psychic, then the innovation called *recalling the field and function of speech and language in psychoanalytic experience* claims no position of exhaustion with respect to the unconscious — it is itself *intervention in the conflict*, with an immediate *transferential incidence*. Indeed his seminar has been reproached with playing, toward its audience, a function the orthodoxy deems perilous: intervening in the transference. Far from disowning it, this incidence seems to him radical — constitutive of the renewed alliance with Freud's discovery. Whence: *the cause of the unconscious — and the word cause is to be taken here in its ambiguity, a cause to be defended, but also the function of cause at the level of the unconscious — must fundamentally be conceived as a lost cause. And that is the only chance one has of winning it.* The pun (cause as *causa* and as partisan cause) is exact on both faces: Lesson II's limping cause is structurally "lost" (the gap never closes), and the institutional cause of the unconscious is lost in the worldly sense — defensible only as lost, winnable only so.

Hence the accent placed, in the misrecognized concept of repetition, on the mainspring of *the always-avoided encounter, the missed chance*. *The function of missing (ratage) is at the center of analytic repetition. The appointment is always missed — this is what constitutes, with respect to the tuché, the vanity of repetition, its constitutive occultation.* The concept of repetition drives us onto a dilemma: either assume purely and simply our implication as analysts in the eristic character of the discord of every exposition of our experience — or polish the concept at the level of something impossible to objectify except by a *transcendental analytic of the cause*. The latter would be formulated from the classical *ablata causa tollitur effectus* — remove the cause, the effect is removed — with one emendation: underline the singular of the protasis (*ablata causa*) and put the apodosis in the plural (*tolluntur effectus*): *the effects only get along well in the absence of the cause*. All the effects are subject to the pressure of a transfactual, causal order demanding to enter their dance; but if they held hands nicely, as in the song, they would obstruct the cause's insinuating itself into their round. The scholastic maxim is turned inside out with a nursery-rhyme: instead of "no cause, no effects," Lacan reads "the effects flourish *because* the cause is absent" — the signifying chain (the ring-dance of effects) closes ranks precisely to keep the cause out, and the unconscious cause presses on the chain from its exclusion. This is Lesson II's gap-cause given its final, almost juridical form.

At this point the unconscious cause must be defined *neither as a being, nor as a mē on* — a non-being of possibility, as (Lacan believes) Henri Ey makes it. *It is the mē on of the interdiction that carries to being an existent in spite of its non-advent; it is a function of the impossible on which a certainty is founded.* The Greek is chosen with care: not *ouk on* (flat non-being) but *mē on* — the negation of prohibition and of the subjunctive, the "not" of *what is barred yet presses*: Lesson III's unrealized, now written as modality — the impossible as ground of certainty.

**The Other, already there; the unconscious is outside.** This leads to the function of transference: that indeterminate of pure being with no access to determination, that primary position of the unconscious articulated as constituted by the subject's indetermination — *to this, transference gives us access, in an enigmatic way*. A Gordian knot, leading to this: *the subject seeks his certainty. And the certainty of the analyst himself concerning the unconscious cannot be extracted from the concept of transference.* Whence the striking multiplicity, plurality, plurivalence of the conceptions of transference formulated in analysis — no exhaustive review, but a chosen exploration.

At its emergence in Freud, a slippage lies in wait (not imputable to him): seeing in the concept of transference *nothing but the concept of repetition itself*. Do not forget how Freud presents it: *what cannot be remembered is repeated in conduct* — conduct delivered, to reveal what it repeats, to the analyst's *reconstruction*. One may come to believe that the opacity of the trauma — as then maintained in its inaugural function by Freud's thought; for us, *the resistance of signification* — is held nominally responsible for the limit of remembering; and one might settle comfortably, in our own theorization, for recognizing there a significant moment of *transfer of powers* from the subject to the Other — the great Other, the locus of speech, virtually the locus of truth. Is that the point of the concept's appearance? So it appears, and there one often stops. But look closer: this moment in Freud is not simply the limit-moment corresponding to the closure of the unconscious in its temporal pulsation. When Freud brings in the function of transference, he takes care to mark that moment as *the cause* of what we call transference. *The Other, latent or not, is present from before, in the subjective revelation. It is already there when something of the unconscious has begun to deliver itself.* The analyst's interpretation only covers over the fact that the unconscious — if it is what Lacan says, play of the signifier — *has already, in its formations (dream, slip, joke, symptom), proceeded by interpretation. The Other, the great Other, is already there in every opening of the unconscious, however fugitive.* The etymological trap in "transference" (a handing-over of powers to the Other) is thus sprung: nothing is transferred *to* the Other, because the Other precedes; the unconscious does not await the analyst to interpret — it is interpretation, addressed, from the first.

What Freud indicates from the first is that *transference is essentially resistant — Übertragungswiderstand. Transference is the means by which the communication of the unconscious is interrupted, by which the unconscious closes back up. Far from being the handing-over of powers to the unconscious, transference is on the contrary its closure.* Essential for marking the paradox expressed commonly enough — found even in Freud — that the analyst must *wait for the transference* before beginning to interpret. Lacan accents this question because *it is the dividing line between the good and the bad way of conceiving transference.* The paradox is now fully armed: the phenomenon that closes the unconscious is also the condition of interpretation — a knot, to be treated as such (Gordian or not, to be seen), which incited his years of topological considerations.

Among the divergent practices, some differences are partial (readings of the relation to ego-ideal or superego merely lateralize what is essentially the relation to the great Other). But one conception is irreducible, and where formulated can only *contaminate practice*: that the analysis of transference proceeds on the basis of *an alliance with the healthy part of the subject's ego*, appealing to his good sense to point out the illusory character of his conduct within the relation to the analyst. This thesis *subverts what is at stake — the presentification of the split of the subject, realized here, effectively, in the presence.* To appeal to a healthy part of the subject, there in the real, apt to judge with the analyst what happens in the transference, is to misrecognize that *precisely that part is the one interested in the transference — that it is the one that shuts the door, or the window, or the shutters, as you like — and that the beauty with whom one wants to speak is there behind, asking only to reopen the shutters.* That is why interpretation becomes decisive at that moment: *it is to the beauty that one must address oneself.* The polemic against the "therapeutic alliance" is here at its sharpest, and the fable does the arguing: the "healthy ego" recruited as co-analyst is the very agent of closure — the servant who bars the shutters — while the subject of the unconscious waits behind them; an interpretation contracted with the ego is a conversation held with the door.

One indication of the reversal this schema comports with respect to the model in people's heads: Lacan says somewhere that *the unconscious is the discourse of the Other*. Now the discourse of the Other that is to be realized, that of the unconscious, *is not beyond the closure — it is outside* (*au-dehors*). *It is that discourse which, through the mouth of the analyst, calls for the reopening of the shutter.* The topological correction is decisive and easy to miss: the unconscious is not an interior behind a door (depth psychology's picture) but an *exteriority* — the discourse of the Other circulating outside — and the analyst's speech is not a key inserted inward but that outside discourse itself, knocking. The lesson's title-thesis follows: the analyst's presence belongs to the concept of the unconscious because he is, literally, standing where it is.

Nonetheless a paradox remains in designating, in this movement of closure, the initial moment where interpretation can take effect — and here the permanent conceptual crisis in analysis concerning transference reveals itself: the contradiction of its function, grasped as the impact-point of interpretation *in the very respect in which it is, toward the unconscious, a moment of closure* — which necessitates treating it as what it is: a knot.

**Szasz, or the limit-case.** There is a crisis in analysis, and Lacan is entitled — since nothing partial is involved, the author not being of mediocre mind — to choose the latest text manifesting it most strikingly: a tight, gripping article by Thomas Szasz — "who writes to us from Syracuse, which does not, alas, make him kin to Archimedes, for this Syracuse is in New York State" — in the latest *International Journal of Psychoanalysis*, inspired by a genuinely moving search for the authenticity of the analytic path. Striking that an author most esteemed in his circle — that of exactly American psychoanalysis — considers transference *nothing other than a defense of the psychoanalyst*, and concludes: transference is the pivot on which the entire structure of psychoanalytic treatment rests; it is a concept he calls *inspired* — Lacan, wary of false friends, weighs the translation: not *inspiré* but something like *officieux* — an officious concept, as indispensable as it is unofficial — yet harboring the germs not only of its own destruction but of the destruction of psychoanalysis itself. Why? *Because it tends to place the person of the analyst beyond reality-testing as his patients, colleagues and he himself can hold it.* This hazard must be frankly recognized; neither professionalization, nor raised standards, nor coerced training analyses can protect against the danger; only *the integrity of the analyst and of the analytic situation* can save us from the extinction of *the unique dialogue*.

This entirely forged impasse is, for its author, necessitated by the very fact that he cannot conceive the analysis of transference except in terms of *an assent obtained from the healthy part of the ego*, the part apt to judge reality and decide of illusion. Logically his article begins: *transference is similar to concepts like error, illusion, fantasy*. Once the presence of transference is obtained, it is a matter of agreement between analysand and analyst — except that the analyst being judge without appeal or recourse, every analysis of transference is denominated a field of pure risk, without control. Lacan takes the article only as a limit-case, but demonstrative: it incites us to restore a determination bringing another order into play. *That order is the order of truth. Truth is founded only on the fact that speech, even lying speech, appeals to it and gives rise to it.* This dimension is always absent from the logical positivism dominating Szasz's analysis of the concept. The diagnosis is philosophically precise: grade transference on the scale reality/illusion and the analyst becomes an unappealable judge of fact — whence Szasz's honest despair; introduce the register of *truth* (the truth of the lie, Lesson III), where even deception attests the dimension it betrays, and the impasse dissolves — the analyst is not the judge of the patient's illusions but the addressee of a speech whose truth-dimension operates through its very lures.

His own alleged "intellectualization" — for putting the function of the signifier in the first rank — is returned to sender: the real intellectualization lies in that operative mode where everything plays on confronting a *reality* with a connotation of *illusion* borne on the phenomenon of transference. Far from considering two subjects in a dual position discussing an objectivity deposited there like the fallout of a compression in behavior, *we must make the domain of possible deception arise*. Recall the Cartesian subject of certainty as necessary point of departure: in Descartes the essential counterweight is the Other who must in no case deceive. *In analysis, the danger is that this Other be deceived.* Not the only dimension of transference to apprehend — but admit that if there is a domain where, in discourse, deception has some chance of succeeding, love assuredly gives its model. *What better way of assuring oneself, on the point where one is deceived, than to persuade the other of the truth of what one advances\! ... In persuading the other that he has what can complete us, we assure ourselves of being able to continue to misrecognize precisely what we lack.* The circle of deception, in that at the appointed moment it makes the dimension of love arise — this will serve as exemplary door for demonstrating the trick next time. Lesson III's inversion (deceiving → deceived Other) is here given its erotic mechanism and its formula, one of the seminar's finest: love as the mutual insurance of misrecognition — I persuade you that you complete me, precisely so as not to know what I lack. Transference-love will be read through this door.

But that is not all, "for it is not what radically causes the closure that transference comports. What causes it — and it will be the other face of our examination of the concepts of transference — is, referring to the question mark inscribed in the left-hand, shadow-side, reserved part \[of the blackboard schema\]: *what I have designated by the objet a*." The last sentence sets Part Three's full program: the deception-circuit of love is transference's imaginary face; its cause — like desire's — is the object, and the analyst's place will have to be defined by it.

**Réponses.** Wahl asks to what existing theory of knowledge the first half of the lecture could be attached. Since the novelty of the Freudian field is precisely to give in experience something fundamentally grasped *thus*, it is not so surprising that no model of it is found in Plotinus. That said — despite his refusal of Miller's opening question on an ontology of the unconscious — he has let out a bit of rope with very precise references: the *on*, the *ouk on* (alluding exactly to Henri Ey's formulation — whose competence concerning the unconscious is not the greatest — situating the unconscious somewhere in his theory of consciousness), and the *mē on* of the interdict, of the *said-no* (*dit-que-non*). Not far-reaching as properly metaphysical indication, and no transgression of his self-fixed bounds; but it structures transmissibly the points of the question. *In the unconscious there is a knowledge (savoir), which is not at all to be conceived as a knowledge destined to complete itself, to close.* And then the retraction that guards the guard: *on, ouk on, mē on — it is still to substantify the unconscious too much to give formulas of the kind. That is why I avoid them very carefully.* What is beyond — "the beauty behind the shutters" — is what is at stake and was not broached today: locating how something of the subject is, from behind, *magnetized* — magnetized at a profound degree of dissociation, of split. There is the key point of the Gordian knot. The exchange performs the pre-ontological discipline of Lesson III in real time: the Greek modal apparatus is offered, used, and immediately withdrawn as too substantifying — with one durable deposit left standing: the unconscious contains a *savoir* that does not totalize, the germ of the *sujet supposé savoir* to come.

Kaufmann asks the relation between the *dross* (*scorie*) and what Lacan earlier called the *remainder* (*reste*). *The remainder is always, in human destiny, fecund. The dross is the extinguished remainder.* Here the term is completely negative: it aims at the veritable regression that can occur on the plane of the theory of psychological knowledge insofar as the analyst, placed in a field he can only flee, seeks assurances in theories that operate toward an orthopedic, conformizing therapeutics, managing the subject's access to the most mythical conceptions of *happiness* — which, with the uncritical handling of evolutionism, makes the ambiance of our epoch. *The dross here is the analysts themselves, nothing else — while the discovery of the unconscious is still young, and it is an unprecedented occasion of subversion.* The distinction is worth its weight: *reste* is the seminar's honorific term (the *objet a* is a remainder, fecund by essence); *scorie* is its dead double — and the closing sentence, aimed at the profession that expelled him, gives Part Three its fighting registration: the discovery is young; its guardians are its slag.
