### **XI. Analysis and Truth, or the Closure of the Unconscious**

*22 April 1964\.* Miller's keywords: *telling the truth, lying, deceiving oneself; the "I am lying" and the "I think"; homunculus and $; the validity of psychology; illusion and its rectification; transference is the enactment of the reality of the unconscious.* The lesson finishes the reading of Szasz, resolves the liar paradox on the graph of enunciation and statement, deflates the cogito into an "abortion," and closes on the aphorism that governs the rest of Part Three.

**Szasz continued; the sujet supposé savoir in embryo.** According to Szasz, the analyst is supposed to point out to the patient the discordance-effects arising with respect to the *reality* of the analytic situation — the two real subjects present in it. Sometimes the discordance is blatant: Spitz — "an old hand who knows a thing or two about amusing his public" — recounts a patient whose transference-dream (amorous realizations with her analyst, Spitz himself) equips him with hair as blond as it is abundant; anyone who has glimpsed the egg-skull of the personage will grant the analyst an easy demonstration of the distortions to which the unconscious has pushed the subject. But when it is a matter of qualifying a patient's conduct as disobliging toward the analyst — one of two things, says Szasz: either the patient agrees, or, if not, who will decide, save the principled position that *the analyst is always right*? Which throws us back on that at once mythical and idealizing pole Szasz calls the analyst's *integrity*. What can that mean, Lacan asks, *if not the recall to the dimension of truth*? The article can thus only be situated where its author places it — operating eristically, not heuristically, manifesting in its impasse a veritable crisis of conscience in the analyst's function — a crisis of only lateral interest here, since it was shown to follow necessarily from a certain unilateral way of theorizing the analysis of transference, a slope long since denounced.

Back to the near-phenomenological data. The relation instituted in analysis is *not reciprocal, not symmetrical* — which Szasz notes only to deplore, quite wrongly: *in this relation of the one to the other, a search for truth is instituted in which the one is supposed to know* (*est supposé savoir*) — *at least, to know more than the other*. From the latter, the thought at once arises not only that he must not be mistaken (*se tromper*), but that *one can deceive him* (*on peut le tromper*). And *the being-mistaken is, by the same stroke, thrown back onto the subject* — not statically, as lack or error, but movingly: in his discourse the subject is essentially situated in the dimension of *self-deception* (*le se tromper*). Here, in a subordinate clause, the seminar's most famous coinage makes its first appearance: *supposé savoir* — the one supposed to know. Its context should be memorized against later misuse: the supposition is not the analyst's pretension but a structural effect of the asymmetry itself, and its immediate correlates are the three verbs of the keyword-block — the Other can be deceived, must not err, and the subject is delivered to self-deception.

Nunberg's 1926 paper "The Will to Recovery" gives the clinical proof. *Recovery* — well-chosen word — is not exactly cure but restoration, return; and what can push the patient to ask the analyst for something he calls health, when his symptom, theory tells us, is made to bring him satisfactions? Nunberg shows with humor that a few steps into analysis often suffice for it to burst out that what motivated the search for health, for balance, was precisely the patient's *unconscious aim in its most immediate scope*: what a shelter analysis offers, for instance, for restoring the peace of one's household — when from the first it appears the patient desires, in the form of a provisional suspension of his presence at home, *the contrary* of what he proposed as the analysis's first aim: not the restitution of his marriage but its rupture. We are thus placed, at the maximum — in the very act of entering analysis — in contact with *the profound ambiguity of every assertion of the patient*, its intrinsic double face. *It is first as instituting itself in, and even by, a certain lie that we see the dimension of truth install itself — in which the lie is not, properly speaking, shaken, since the lie as such posits itself in that dimension of truth.* Lesson III's "truth of the lie" is here made the very portal of analysis: the founding demand is a lie that tells the truth, and the treatment begins inside that structure, not before it.

**"I am lying" and the graph.** This is why the relation of subject to signifier is the bearing placed in the forefront of a general rectification of analytic theory — as primary and constituent in the institution of the analytic experience as in the radical function of the unconscious. Didactically, this limits the unconscious to its narrowest platform; but at this point of division no error of *substantification* can be made. Center things on the four-cornered graph, knowingly distinguishing *the plane of enunciation from the plane of the statement*. Its use is illustrated by the absurdities an overly formal logicism introduces in seeing an antinomy of reason in the statement *I am lying* — when everyone knows there is none. It is quite false to answer that if you say *I am lying* you are telling the truth and therefore not lying, and so on. *The "I am lying," despite its paradox, is perfectly valid*: the I of the enunciation is not the same as the I of the statement — the shifter that designates it in the statement. From the point where I enounce, it is perfectly possible to formulate validly that the I — the I then formulating the statement — is lying, lied shortly before, lies after, or even, in saying *I am lying*, affirms an intention to deceive. Witness the Jewish story of the train: *"I am going to Lemberg," says the one. "Why do you tell me you are going to Lemberg, since you really are going there — and if you tell me, it is so that I should believe you are going to Cracow?"* The resolution is not a logical trick but the seminar's linguistics in action: Russellian type-theory dissolves the paradox by stratifying languages; Lacan dissolves it by splitting the *subject* — enunciation against statement — the same split that constitutes the unconscious. The Lemberg joke (Freud's own, from the *Witz* book) shows the split operating in ordinary bad faith: truth used to deceive, at the level of enunciation.

On the graph: from the *I am lying* at the level of the chain of the statement — *lying* being a signifier, part in the Other of the treasury of vocabulary, where the retroactively determined *I* becomes a signification engendered at the level of the statement — what results at the level of enunciation is an *I am deceiving you* (*je te trompe*). And this *I am deceiving you* arrives from the point where the analyst awaits the subject and — according to the formula — *sends back to him his own message in its true signification, that is, in inverted form*: *in the "I am deceiving you" that you send as message, it is I who express what you are doing; and in doing so, you are telling the truth.* On the path of deception where the subject ventures, the analyst is positioned to formulate this *you are telling the truth* — *and our interpretation never has meaning except in that dimension.* Lacan's oldest formula (the sender receives his own message back inverted) is here given its analytic cash-value: interpretation does not unmask lies against reality (Szasz's court of illusion); it certifies the truth spoken *in* the deception, from the place of the Other.

Now transfer the Cartesian *I think* onto the same schema. The enunciation/statement distinction makes its ever-possible slippage and eventual stumbling-point. What the cogito institutes is the register of thought as extracted from an opposition to extension — a fragile status, but sufficient in the order of signifying constitution; its certainty comes from *taking its place at the level of the enunciation*. But the status of the *I think* is as reduced, as minimal, as punctual as that of the *I am lying* — it could as well be tagged *that means nothing*; perhaps, reduced to the punctuality of assuring itself only of absolute doubt concerning every signification including its own, its status is even *more* fragile. Whence the audacity: *the Cartesian "I think" participates, in its effort of certainty, in a kind of abortion* (*avortement*). The difference in the status the Freudian unconscious gives the subject *is desire* — desire to be situated at the level of the cogito: *whatever animates, that of which every enunciation speaks, is desire*. (And in passing: desire as Lacan formulates it says more than what Freud brings.) The demotion is exact, not rhetorical: the cogito is validated as enunciation and convicted of delivering almost nothing — a point without content; what fills the enunciative level, what every act of saying is animated by, is desire — which is why the Freudian subject succeeds where the Cartesian miscarries.

The cogito's function is pinned with the term *avorton*, runt — *homunculus*: illustrated by the relapse, never failing in the history of so-called thought, of taking the I of the cogito for the homunculus long represented whenever psychology is done — whenever psychological inanity or discordance is explained by the presence, inside man, of the famous little man who governs him, the driver of the chariot, nowadays the point of *synthesis*. Already denounced in its function by pre-Socratic thought. Against it, our symbol: *$, the barred S — the subject as constituted second with respect to the signifier.* To illustrate: the *unary trait* (*trait unaire*). The first signifier is the notch by which it is marked, for example, that the subject has killed one beast — whereby he will not entangle himself in his memory when he has killed ten others: he need not remember which is which; from the unary trait he will count them. The subject locates *himself* by the unary trait — first marking himself with a tattoo, first of signifiers. When this signifier, this One, is instituted — *the count is one one* (*un un*). *It is at the level not of the one but of the "one one," at the level of the count, that the subject has to situate himself as such.* Whereby the two ones already distinguish themselves — *and thus is marked the first split that makes the subject as such distinguish himself from the sign with respect to which, at first, he could constitute himself as subject.* The three-brothers arithmetic of Lesson II here receives its formal engine: the tally-notch counts the beasts but cannot count itself among them; the subject arises in the gap between the mark and the counting of marks — split from his own first sign. And the guard: do not confound the function of $ with *the image of the objet a* — the way the subject sees himself *redoubled*, constituted by the reflected, momentary, precarious image of mastery, imagining himself a man only because he imagines himself. To locate the subject with respect to *reality*, as supposed to constitute us, and not with respect to *the signifier*, is to fall already into the degradation of the psychological constitution of the subject.

**The validity of psychology; the internal object.** Any departure taken from the subject's relation to a real context can have its reason in some psychologist's experiment — producing results, effects, tables. But always in contexts where *we make the reality* — tests organized by us. That is the domain of validity of what is called psychology, which has nothing to do with the level at which analytic experience is sustained, and which — the barb lands cleanly — *incredibly reinforces the destitution* (*dénuement*) *of the subject*. What Lacan has called the *psychological isolate* is not the old, or ever-young, monad traditionally instituted as center of knowledge — the Leibnizian monad is not isolated: center of knowledge, inseparable from a cosmology, the point in the cosmos from which contemplation or harmony is exercised. The psychological isolate is refound in the concept of the *ego* — which, by a deviation that is only a detour, finds itself confounded in psychoanalytic thought with *the subject in distress in the relation to reality*. Leibniz is used with precision as a foil: even rationalism's most windowless self was cosmologically anchored; the modern psychological ego is an isolate without cosmos, and ego-psychology has enthroned exactly that impoverishment.

But this way of theorizing is in full discord with something experience obliges us to promote and that cannot be eliminated from the analytic text: *the function of the internal object*. Introjection and projection are used haphazardly; still, even in this limping theorization, something comes to the fore from all sides — the internal object, polarized at the extreme in the *good or bad object* around which, for some, turns everything in a subject's conduct representing distortion, inflection, paradoxical fear, foreign body. It is also the operative point on which, under conditions of urgency — selection of subjects for various directive, cybernetic, responsible employments, forming aviation pilots or locomotive drivers — some have proposed concentrating a rapid analysis, a flash-analysis, or personality tests. We cannot not pose the question of this internal object's status: is it an object of perception? By what do we approach it? Where does it come from? — and, in the sequel of this rectification, in what would the analysis of transference consist? The Kleinian object is thus neither adopted nor dismissed: it is registered as an irreducible datum of experience whose theorization limps, and whose true status the seminar's own apparatus (the *objet a*, the *nasse*) is about to supply.

**The lobster-pot.** A model — to be much perfected, take it as problematic; the illusion-rectification schemas have such adhesive power that nothing can too prematurely be launched against them. If the unconscious is what closes as soon as it has opened, in a temporal pulsation; if repetition is not stereotypy of conduct but repetition with respect to something always missed — then transference, represented as a mode of access to what hides in the unconscious, can only be by itself a *precarious* way. If transference is only repetition, it will be repetition, always, *of the same missing* (*ratage*); if it claims through that repetition to restore the continuity of a history, it will do so only by making resurge a relation that is, by nature, *syncopated*. Transference as operative mode cannot be content to be confounded with the efficacy of repetition, with the restoration of what is occulted in the unconscious, or with the catharsis of unconscious elements.

When the unconscious is spoken of as appearing in temporal pulsation, the image may come of the *nasse* — the lobster-pot, the fish-trap — that opens slightly, at the bottom of which the catch will be realized. Whereas in the figure of the *besace*, the double sack, the unconscious is something reserved, closed inside, into which *we* must penetrate from outside. *I therefore invert the topology of the traditional imagery.* The schema of the nasse is drawn; and it is to be made to overlap with the optical model from the "Remarks on Daniel Lagache's report," concerning ideal ego and ego-ideal: there one sees that *it is in the Other that the subject constitutes himself as ideal*, that he has to regulate the focus of what comes as ego — or ideal ego, *not* ego-ideal — that is, to constitute himself in his imaginary reality. And the schema makes clear — underlined via the recent elements on the scopic drive — that *the place where the subject sees himself* (where the real, inverted image of his own body is forged, given in the schema of the ego) *is not the place from which he looks at himself*. It is in the space of the Other that he sees himself; and the point from which he looks at himself is also in that space. *Now, this is also the point from which he speaks* — since insofar as he speaks, it is in the locus of the Other that he begins to constitute that *truthful lie* by which what participates in desire at the level of the unconscious gets under way. The optical model and the gaze-doctrine are spliced: seeing-oneself, looking-at-oneself, and speaking are three functions all located in the Other's space and never coinciding — the triple non-coincidence in which the "truthful lie" of the founding demand is uttered.

The subject, then, is to be considered with respect to the nasse — particularly its *orifice*, its essential structure — *as being inside*. What matters is not what enters there, conformably to the word of the Gospel, *but what comes out*. The closure of the unconscious can be conceived by the incidence of something playing the role of a *shutter* (*obturateur*) — *the objet a, sucked, aspirated, at the orifice of the nasse*. Picture those great spheres in which lottery numbers are churned: what is concocted in the great roulette of the first statements of free association *comes out in the interval in which the object does not stop up the orifice*. A brutal, elementary image — but it restores the constituting function of the symbolic in its reciprocal contraposition: the subject's game, at the *even-and-odd* of his re-encounter with what comes to presentify itself in the effective action of the analytic maneuver. The Matthew allusion ("not what goes into the mouth defiles...") is turned to strict theory: the unconscious is defined by its emissions, not its contents; and the *objet a* receives its Part Three function — the valve of the unconscious, whose intermittent plugging of the orifice *is* the closure that transference enacts. A bulldozer-schema, Lacan concedes — but it accords the notion that *transference is at once obstacle to remembering and presentification of the closure of the unconscious — which is the missing, always right on time, of the good encounter.*

All this could be illustrated by the multiplicity and discordance of analysts' formulas on transference. Transference is one thing, the therapeutic end another; nor is it a simple means. The extreme poles of the literature are situated here: how often one reads formulas associating transference with *identification* — when identification is only an arrest-time, a *false termination of analysis very frequently confounded with its normal termination*; its relation to transference is close, but precisely by way of *the transference that has not been analyzed*. Inversely, transference is formulated as the means of realizing rectification — against which today's whole discourse goes. It is impossible to situate transference correctly in any of these references. And since *reality* is the issue, on that plane the critique bears; whence the closing aphorism, introducing next time: *the transference is not the enactment* (*mise en acte*) *of the illusion that would push us to that alienating identification which every conformization constitutes, even to an ideal model, of which the analyst in no case can be the support — the transference is the enactment of the reality of the unconscious.* The aphorism does triple duty: against Szasz (transference graded as illusion), against Balint (termination as identification with the analyst), and for the deferred consubstantiality — for what has been left in suspense in the concept of the unconscious, "singular thing, the very thing more and more forgotten," is what Freud underlined as strictly *consubstantial* with its dimension: *sexuality*. Having ever more forgotten what the unconscious–sexuality relation means, analysis has inherited a conception of reality that no longer has anything to do with reality as Freud situated it at the level of the secondary process. The departure for next time is thus set: transference as enactment of the reality of the unconscious — *and that reality is sexual*.

**Réponses.** Rosolato offers three reflections: the schema singularly resembles *an eye* — might the petit a play the role of crystalline lens, even of cataract?; what of ideal ego and ego-ideal precisely on this schema?; and what is meant by *mise en acte*? On the last: *enactment is a promissory word* — defining transference by it is necessary so that transference not be the place of alibis, of insufficient operative modes, taken by biases and detours not necessarily inoperative, which account for the limits of analytic intervention; the false definitions of termination (Balint's identification with the analyst) were pointed today; taken at the wrong level, transference yields only partial incidences — the correct level is next seminar's subject. On the eye-analogy: amusing — but in everything topological one must guard severely against giving it a *Gestalt* function. Which does not mean certain living forms do not sometimes give the sensation of being a kind of effort of the biological to forge something resembling the torsions of the fundamental topological objects developed in the *Identification* seminar — the *mitre*, that surface which, projected in three-dimensional space, recuts itself; one could designate this or that point of anatomical configuration figuring the touching effort of life to rejoin the topological configurations. And only such considerations give the image of what is at stake *when what is inside is also outside* — which is why they are particularly necessary for the unconscious, represented *at once as what is of the interior of the subject, but which is realized only outside, in that locus of the Other where alone it can take its status.* Lesson X's "the unconscious is outside" is here given its topological warrant: not paradox but the ordinary behavior of self-intersecting surfaces — inside and outside continuous.

The object is a shutter — *it remains to know how*: not the passive plug first imaged to launch thought on a track; a more complete representation will come, where kinships with the structure of the eye may indeed reappear. It is singular that the eye's structure presents a general form so easily evoked whenever we try to figure the subject's relations to the world — doubtless not by chance, though not to be adhered to too narrowly. And, profiting from the remark, the difference from Freud's own schema — the ego as the *lens* through which perception-consciousness operates on the amorphous mass of the *Unbewusst*: Freud's schema is worth what it is worth, as limited in scope as Lacan's own in a certain way — *but note the difference: had I wanted to put the ego somewhere, it is i(a) that I would have written. Here, it is the a that is in question.* The last line is the sharpest possible summary of the rectification: where Freud's optical apparatus (and all ego-psychology after it) installs the ego-lens, Lacan's nasse installs the object — not the image of the other, i(a), but the *a* itself, cause and shutter; the whole difference between an analysis addressed to the ego and one addressed to its cause.

### **XII. Sexuality in the Defiles of the Signifier**

*29 April 1964\.* Miller's keywords: *on Chinese astronomy; against Jung and against hermeneutics; desexualization of reality; the entry-door of the unconscious; Anna O. and Freud's desire.* The lesson develops the aphorism left at the last close: transference is the enactment of the reality of the unconscious — *and that reality is sexual*. Its path runs from the archaic marriage of combinatory thought with sexual rite, through the double refusal of Jung and Ricoeur, to the interior eight and the thesis that will scandalize: the desire at stake in transference is *the desire of the analyst*, demonstrated on the primal scene of psychoanalysis itself — Breuer and Anna O.

The formula pleased, Lacan notes, which he can attribute only to its promises, aphorisms being undeveloped; and it announces exactly what one most tends to avoid in the analysis of transference. His own teaching puts him in a problematic position: the unconscious as effects of speech on the subject, structured like a language — a direction well made to wrest the grasp of the unconscious from any aim other than the constitution of the subject. Yet that teaching had an avowedly *transferential* aim — to recenter the hearers he cared most about, the psychoanalysts, in a sight-line conformed to analytic experience; and the very handling of the concept must reckon with the effects of formulation on the hearer. *All of us, whoever we are, including the one who teaches, are in a relation to the reality of the unconscious that our intervention not only brings to light but, up to a point, engenders.* The reflexive turn is not modesty but method: a teaching about transference is itself transference at work, and the teacher's desire — like Freud's — is inside the field taught.

**The unsustainable truth.** To the point: *the reality of the unconscious is — unsustainable truth — sexual reality.* On every occasion Freud articulated it *mordicus*, tooth and nail. Why unsustainable? Since the 1900s some scientific progress has been made on sex. We know that sexual division, reigning over most living beings, is what ensures the maintenance of the being of a species — whether with Plato one ranks the species-being among the Ideas, or with Aristotle says it is nowhere but in the individuals that support it, matters little: the species subsists in the form of its individuals; the survival of the horse as species has a meaning — *each horse is transitory, and dies*. Whence one grasps that *the link of sex to death — to the death of the individual — is fundamental*. Existence, thanks to sexual division, rests on copulation, accented in two poles that secular tradition characterizes as male and female — there lies the mainspring of reproduction. And around this fundamental reality have always grouped and harmonized other characteristics more or less tied to reproduction's finality; modern structuralism has specified it best, showing that it is at the level of *alliance*, as opposed to natural generation, to the biological line, that the fundamental exchanges are exercised — *at the level, therefore, of the signifier* — and there the most elementary structures of social functioning are refound, to be inscribed in the terms of a combinatory. The Lévi-Straussian point is set exactly where Lacan needs it: kinship is a signifying combinatory *erected on* sexual reality — alliance against generation — so the question becomes unavoidable: *is it not by this route that the signifier came into the world — which is to say, that man learned to think?*

What would legitimate maintaining that the signifier entered the world *through sexual reality* is the recent field opened by a more correct study of *meiosis*: the maturation of the sexual cells operates by a double process of *reduction* — the loss of a certain number of visible elements, the chromosomes; whence genetics, and from genetics the dominant function, in determining certain elements of the living organism, of *a combinatory that operates at certain of its stages by the expulsion of remainders*. Referring here to the function of the *petit a*, Lacan does not rush into analogical speculation — he indicates only *an affinity of the enigmas of sexuality with the play of the signifier*. The prudence is worth remarking: meiosis — a combinatory with expelled remainders — is offered not as biological foundation but as a structural *affinity* between life's own arithmetic and the signifier-plus-objet-a apparatus; nature rhymes with the algebra without grounding it.

History supplies the positive form of the thesis: primitive science rooted itself in a mode of thought that, playing on a combinatory — Yin and Yang, water and fire, hot and cold — *made them lead the dance*: the word chosen for its more-than-metaphorical bearing, since their dance is founded on dance-rites fundamentally motivated by the sexual repartitions of society. No abridged course in Chinese astronomy — but open Léopold de Saussure ("there are, from time to time, men of genius in that family") and see: Chinese astronomy is founded on the play of signifiers reverberating from top to bottom of politics, social structure, ethics, the regulation of the least acts — *and it is nonetheless a very good astronomical science*. Up to a certain moment, the whole reality of the sky can be inscribed in nothing other than a vast constellation of signifiers. At the limit — go to the extreme — primitive science would be *a sort of sexual technique*; but the limit cannot be traced, *for it is indeed a science*: perfectly valid observations, an efficacious system for predicting diurnal and nocturnal variations at a very early epoch — datable, thanks to their signifying registration, by the precession of the equinoxes marked in the figure of their sky, the pole star not where it stands today. No dividing line between the experimental collation valid for all and the principles that guided it — any more than, as Lévi-Strauss underlines, one can call primitive magic all fantasy and smoke, since an enormous collation of perfectly usable experience is stored in it. The historical epistemology is doing precise work: the signifier's marriage to sexual reality is not pre-scientific error — it *produced* science; the combinatory worked as knowledge while remaining a sexual liturgy.

Yet a moment comes when the mooring to the sexual initiation of the mechanism is broken — and, paradoxically, *the rupture comes the later, the more implicit and unregistered the function of the signifier*. Illustration: well after the Cartesian and Newtonian revolutions, at the heart of positivist doctrine, a religious theory of the earth as *great fetish*, perfectly coherent with Comte's pronouncement that we shall never know anything of the chemical composition of the stars — the stars remaining nailed to their place, that is (in another perspective) *in pure function of signifiers*. "No luck" (*manque de pot*): almost at that moment, spectral analysis let us see in the stars a thousand things at once, including their chemical composition. *Then the rupture of astronomy from astrology is consummated* — which does not mean astrology does not still live for very many people. The Comte anecdote is the perfect emblem: positivism itself, unaware of the signifier, kept the heavens as a signifying constellation (fetish, fixed names) — and was refuted within years by the spectroscope; science's cut from the sexual-signifying cosmos happens late, blindly, and never completely.

**Against Jung, against hermeneutics.** Where does this tend? To the question whether the unconscious should be considered *a remanence of that archaic junction of thought with sexual reality*. That solution took historical form in Jung: restoring the level at which man's thought follows the slopes of sexual experience, reduced by the invasion of science — incarnating the relation of the subject's psyche to reality under the name *archetype*. Now Jungism — making of these primitive modes of the world's articulation something subsistent, the *kernel*, he says, of the psyche itself — is necessarily accompanied by *the repudiation of the term libido*: the neutralization of that function by recourse to a notion of psychic energy, a far more generalized notion of *interest*. Not a mere school-variant: for what Freud means to presentify in the function of libido is *no archaic relation, no primitive mode of access of thoughts, no world subsisting as the shadow of an ancient world across ours. The libido is the effective presence, as such, of desire.* What now remains to be pointed of desire — which is not substance, which is there at the level of the primary process, and which commands the very mode of our approach. The demarcation is the decisive one for the whole lesson: Lacan's own thesis (signifier wedded to sexual reality at thought's origin) sounds dangerously Jungian, and he cuts the resemblance at the exact joint — for Jung the archaic junction *persists* as archetypal kernel (hence libido dissolves into neutral energy); for Freud-Lacan what persists is not a world but *desire* — actual, present, operative — the live wire of the libido, not the shadow of a cosmology.

The other refusal: Ricoeur. Rereading what "someone from outside" said of the unconscious apropos the 1960 Bonneval congress — trying to advance as far as he can, from where he stands, to conceptualize our domain — Lacan grants that Ricoeur went far enough to accede to what is hardest of access for a philosopher: *the realism of the unconscious* — that the unconscious is not ambiguity of conducts, not a future knowledge that already knows itself in not knowing itself, but *lacuna, cut, rupture inscribed in a certain lack*. Ricoeur concedes something of this dimension is to be reserved — *only, philosopher that he is, he appropriates it for himself. He calls it hermeneutics.* Much is made these days of hermeneutics, which objects not only to our analytic adventure but to structuralism as enounced in Lévi-Strauss. And what is hermeneutics, if not to read, in the sequence of man's mutations, the progress of the signs by which he constitutes his history — a history that may also, at the edges, prolong itself into more indefinite times? Whereupon Ricoeur consigns to *pure contingency* what analysts deal with at every step. Granted, from outside, the corporation of analysts does not give an impression of fundamental accord fit to impress him — no reason, though, to leave him conquered terrain. The engagement with Ricoeur (whose *De l'interprétation* would appear the next year) is conducted with respect and a single incision: hermeneutics converts the cut into meaning-history, re-absorbing the unconscious's lacuna into the progress of sense — appropriation, not reception; and the "pure contingency" to which it relegates the analyst's daily material is precisely the *tuché*\-level where the seminar has placed the real.

**Desidero: the Freudian cogito.** The counter-claim: it is at the level of analysis — if a further step can be made — that the nodal point must reveal itself by which *the pulsation of the unconscious is bound to sexual reality. That nodal point is called desire* — and the theoretical elaboration of recent years shows, step by step with the clinic, how desire is situated in *dependence on demand* — which, articulating itself in signifiers, *leaves a metonymic remainder running beneath it*: an element not indeterminate, a condition at once absolute and ungraspable, an element necessarily in impasse, *unsatisfied, impossible, misrecognized*, called desire. This is what makes the junction with the field Freud defines as the sexual instance at the level of the primary process. And the formula that seals the seminar's Cartesian thread: *the function of desire is the last residue of the effect of the signifier in the subject. Desidero — that is the Freudian cogito.* Where Descartes' *cogito* miscarried into a contentless point (Lesson XI), Freud's founding certainty has a content, and it is a verb of desire: the enunciative level is not empty thinking but wanting — the exact filling-in promised by "everything enunciation speaks of is desire."

From there the essential of the primary process institutes itself. Observe what Freud says of the field where the impulse satisfies itself essentially by *hallucination*: no mechanism-schema will ever account for what is given as a regression on the reflex arc. What comes in by the sensorium must leave by the motorium; if the motorium fails, it flows back. *But, the devil — if it flows back, how conceive that this makes a perception?* — unless it is the image of a stopped current making energy reflow in the form of a lamp lighting up — *but for whom?* *The dimension of the third is essential in this alleged regression* — conceivable only in a form strictly analogous to the duplicity, drawn on the board the other day, of the subject of the statement and the subject of the enunciation. *Only the presence of the subject who desires, and desires sexually, brings us that dimension of natural metaphor from which the alleged identity of perception is decided.* The critique of the reflex-arc model is philosophically pointed: hallucination cannot be energy in a tube, because a perception is *for someone*; the "for whom" smuggled into Freud's schema is the split subject — and its desiring, not its plumbing, is what confers the mark of perception.

Freud maintains libido as the essential element of the primary process — meaning (contrary to the look of texts where he tries to illustrate the theory) that in the simplest hallucination of the simplest need — little Anna Freud's alimentary dream: *tart, strawberries, eggs and other small delicacies* — there is no pure presentification of the objects of a need. *The hallucination of the dream is possible only by reason of the sexualization of these objects — for, note, little Anna hallucinates only forbidden objects.* To be discussed case by case, but the dimension of *signification* is absolutely essential in every hallucination for grasping the pleasure principle: *it is from the point where the subject desires that the connotation of reality is given in hallucination. And if Freud opposes the reality principle to the pleasure principle, it is precisely insofar as reality there is defined as desexualized.* Recent theories speak of desexualized functions — the ego-ideal resting on desexualized libido: "it seems to me very difficult to speak of a desexualized libido." But that *the approach to reality comports a desexualization* — that is indeed the principle of Freud's *Two Principles of Psychic Functioning*. The reversal is complete and quietly enormous: "reality" in the analytic sense is not the neutral ground against which sexual fantasy is measured (Szasz's court); it is itself a *product of desexualization* — reality is what remains when desire has been subtracted — so any analysis of transference by "reality-testing" tests the patient against the very operation that represses what transference enacts.

Which is to say: *it is in the transference that we must see inscribed the weight of sexual reality*. For the greatest part unknown and up to a certain point veiled, it *runs beneath* what happens at the level of analytic discourse — which, as it takes form, is indeed the discourse of *demand* (not for nothing has all experience tipped it toward frustration and gratification). Then the board: the topology of the subject according to the sign named in its time the *interior eight* (*huit intérieur*) — recalling Euler's circles, except that this is a *surface* one can fabricate, its edge continuous save that at one point it is occulted by the previously unrolled surface; seen in a certain perspective it can appear to represent two intersecting fields. *The libido is inscribed at the point where the lobe defined as the field of the development of the unconscious covers and occults the other lobe, that of sexual reality.* The libido would thus be what belongs to both — the intersection, as one says in logic. *But that is precisely what it does not mean: for the sector where the fields appear to overlap is, if you see the true profile of the surface, a void.* The topology corrects the Venn-diagram it mimics: unconscious and sexual reality do not share a common part; their apparent intersection is an *emptiness* — the libido is located at a void of overlap, not a region — which is why desire (the "line of desire" bound to demand, by which the sexual incidence presentifies itself in experience) is a *junction at a gap*, not a common content. Completed (edges joined two by two by a complementary surface, as a sphere completes a circle), the figure yields the *cross-cap*, the mitre — a Moebius-related surface *whose right side continues its wrong side*, necessarily traversing itself. The unconscious/sexual-reality relation has the structure of a self-intersecting one-sided surface: no inside/outside, no shared region — junction by void and traversal.

This image figures desire as the place of junction of the field of demand — where the syncopes of the unconscious presentify themselves — with sexual reality. All depends on the line of desire. *And this desire — what is it? Do you think it is here that I designate the agency of transference? Yes and no.* The thing will not go all by itself, "if I tell you that the desire in question is *the desire of the analyst*."

**Anna O., or Breuer's desire.** So as not to leave the room in sideration before an assertion that may seem reckless, only a reminder of *the entry-door of the unconscious* on Freud's horizon: Anna O. — "let us drop this business of the O.," call her by her name, *Bertha Pappenheim*, one of the great names of German social work (a student recently brought Lacan, for his amusement, a stamp struck in Germany with her image — she left some traces in history). It is apropos of her that transference was *discovered*. Breuer was enchanted with the operation — it went like clockwork (*comme sur des roulettes*). At that time no one would have contested the signifier, had the Stoic word been revived: the more signifiers Anna gave, the more *jaspinage* (patter), the better it went — the *chimney-cure*, the sweeping. *Not a trace, in all that, of the least troublesome thing — take up the case again: no sexuality, neither under the microscope nor through the telescope.*

Sexuality's entry is nonetheless made — *by Breuer*: something starts coming back to him, and it comes back to him at home — *you're rather much occupied with her*. Whereupon the dear man, alarmed, a good husband besides, finds that enough is enough — upon which, as everyone knows, the O. displays the magnificent and dramatic manifestations of what scientific language calls *pseudocyesis* — quite simply *the little balloon* — the pregnancy called nervous. What does she show there? One can speculate — but let us not rush upon "the language of the body." Say simply: *the domain of sexuality shows a natural functioning of signs.* At this level they are not signifiers — for the false balloon is a *symptom*, and, per the definition of the sign, *something for someone*; *the signifier, being something else entirely, represents a subject for another signifier.* The sign/signifier distinction is here given its canonical formulation on the most famous body in psychoanalysis: the hysterical pregnancy is a sign (addressed, readable, for someone) but not yet a signifier — a difference to be articulated "for good cause," since the whole tradition rushes to say *it was Bertha's fault*.

Then the reversal: suspend thought on this thesis — why not consider Bertha's pregnancy according to the formula *man's desire is the desire of the Other* — that is, as *the manifestation of Breuer's desire*? *Why not go so far as to think it was Breuer who had a desire for a child?* A beginning of proof: Breuer, departing for Italy with his wife, hastens to make her a child — as Jones recounts, imperturbable Welshman, adding that the child, born in those conditions, had, at the moment Jones was speaking, just committed suicide. Leave aside what one may think of a desire to which even that issue is not indifferent. But observe what Freud says to Breuer: *What\! What a business\! The transference is the spontaneity of the said Bertha's unconscious. It's not yours, not your desire* — "I don't know if they used tu, but it's probable" — *it's the desire of the Other.* In which, Lacan considers, *Freud treats Breuer as a hysteric*, since he tells him: *your desire is the desire of the Other*. Curiously, he does not de-culpabilize him — but he assuredly *de-anguishes* him (those who know the difference Lacan makes between guilt and anxiety can take the indication). The primal scene of psychoanalysis is thus re-cut: the standard account (poor Breuer fleeing his patient's erotic transference) is inverted — the pseudocyesis reads as the *answer of the Other's desire*, Breuer's own child-wish signified on Bertha's body; and Freud's founding intervention, which saved the discovery by assigning the desire wholly to the patient's unconscious, was itself a *hystericizing* of Breuer — a reassurance ("it isn't your desire") that de-anguished the witness at the price of deflecting the whole theory of transference away from the analyst's desire.

This introduces the question of *what Freud's desire decided* — deviating the entire grasp of transference in the direction now arrived at its last term of absurdity, where an analyst can say the whole theory of transference is only a defense of the analyst. Lacan tips the extreme term over, showing exactly its other face: *it is the desire of the analyst.* Follow him — this is not done merely to turn things upside down. With this key, read any general review of transference and the contribution each author brings is one *in which his desire is perfectly legible* — apart from Freud. Abraham could be analyzed simply from his theory of part-objects: there is not only what the analyst means to make of his patient, there is what the analyst means his patient to make of *him* — *Abraham wanted to be a complete mother*. Ferenczi's margins could be punctuated with Georgius's song *Je suis fils-père* — I am son-father. Nunberg too has his intentions: in his truly remarkable "Love and Transference" he stands as arbiter of the powers of life and death — one cannot fail to see the aspiration to a divine position. Amusement, perhaps — but in the course of such a history one can isolate the functions reproduced on the board. The pages perform what they assert: each classic of transference-theory read as a symptom of its author's desire — mother, son-father, god — with Freud alone excepted, because his desire is not legible *in* the theory but *decided* it, the deeper exception Lessons I and IV prepared.

To conjoin the nasse-schema with the earlier optical models: make the shutter a *camera shutter* — except that it would be *a mirror*. In this little mirror, obturating what is on the other side, the subject sees profiled the play by which — per the inverted-bouquet illusion, a real image — he can *accommodate his own image around what appears, the petit a*. In the sum of these accommodations of images the subject must find the occasion of an essential integration. And what do we know of all that, if not that at the whim of the driftings in the history of analysis — of the engagement of each analyst's desire — this or that small detail, complementary observation, refinement has been added, permitting us to qualify *the presence, at the level of desire, of each of the analysts*? The apparatus now has all its parts: the orifice of the unconscious, plugged not by a passive cork but by a *mirror-shutter* — the analyst's person offered as reflecting surface on which the subject dresses his own image i(a) around the *a*; transference in one figure: closure of the unconscious *by* the imaginary capture staged on the analyst.

Whence the closing meditation on Freud's *band* — "that troop, as he says, that follows him." *After all, the people who followed Christ were not so shiny. Freud was not Christ, but he was perhaps something like Viridiana* — those photographed so ironically in Buñuel's film with a little camera invincibly evoke, sometimes, the much-photographed group of Freud's apostles and epigones. Does that diminish them? No more than the apostles: precisely at that level they could bear the best witness — *it is by a certain naivety, a certain poverty, a certain innocence that they have most instructed us*. True, around Socrates the company was far more brilliant — and teaches no less on transference, as those who remember the *Transference* seminar can attest. Next time: articulating *the pregnancy of the function of the desire of the analyst*. The double comparison (Freud's shabby apostles / Socrates' brilliant ones — Viridiana's beggars posing as the Last Supper) is exact on both edges: the disciples' very mediocrity makes their transference legible, as Alcibiades' brilliance made his; in both schools the founder's desire is the unanalyzed relic around which the band forms.

**Réponses.** Miller poses the relation of the two discourses — the scientific discourse and the discourse of the Other, the unconscious: unlike the discourses preceding its emergence, science is not founded on the unconscious combinatory; it institutes itself by establishing with the unconscious *a relation of non-relation* — disconnected — though the unconscious does not thereby disappear and its incidences continue to be felt; would reflecting the scientificity of analysis lead to writing a new history of scientific thought? Lacan sees a double putting-in-question take shape: *if we can hitch psychoanalysis to the train of modern science — despite the essential and developing incidence of the desire of the analyst — we are entitled to pose the question of the desire behind modern science.* There is certainly disconnection of scientific discourse from the conditions of the discourse of the unconscious — *one sees it in set theory: in a time when the combinatory is hitched to the capture of sexuality, set theory cannot come to light. How is this disconnection possible? It is at the level of a desire that we shall be able to give the answer.* The exchange compresses the lesson's historical thesis into a research program: mathematics' purest combinatory (set theory) required the *unhitching* of the signifier from the sexual — Cantor after the Cartesian God of Lesson III — and the price and agent of that unhitching is a desire, as yet unanalyzed, "behind modern science," twin of the desire behind psychoanalysis.

### **XIII. The Deconstruction of the Drive**

*6 May 1964\.* The fourth concept, announced from Lesson II as the hardest and reserved for last, is finally broached — *démontage*, dismantling, taking-apart: the drive stripped down to Freud's four terms and shown to be not an organic datum but a *montage*. The lesson works line by line through *Triebe und Triebschicksale* (1915), whose title Lacan re-translates on the way: not "avatars" (that would be *Triebwandlungen*) but *Schicksal* — adventure, *vicissitude*.

The recap fixes the stakes: the topology of the last session — a partition, a perimeter involuting on itself, which is what one improperly calls the analytic situation — aims at conceiving *the point of disjunction and conjunction, of union and frontier, that can be occupied only by the desire of the analyst*. To go further, the fourth concept must be put forward — an *Einführung* possible only in Freud's wake, *since in Freud this notion is absolutely new*. The term *Trieb* has a long history — in psychology, physiology, physics itself, and not by chance did Freud choose it — but he gave it so specified an employment, and it is so integrated into analytic practice, that its past is occulted: where the past of "unconscious" weighs on every use of that term, *Trieb* is used by everyone as the designation of a sort of radical given of experience. It is even invoked *against* Lacan's doctrine of the unconscious — as neglecting what every analyst knows by experience, *the drive-level* (*le pulsionnel*): something irrepressible through the very repressions ("if there must be repression, it is because beyond there is something that pushes"), the element that makes the clinical weight of every case, needing no adult analysis — child practice suffices. Reference, it seems, to a last datum, to the archaic, the primordial — the very recourse Lacan's teaching invites one to renounce for the unconscious seems here inevitable. The objection is stated at full strength before being dismantled: if the drive is bedrock-biology, the signifier-doctrine caps out — which is exactly why the drive was saved for last.

**Grundbegriff, convention, fiction.** Is the drive of the organic register? Is that how to read the *Jenseits* passage where the *Trieb* represents *die Äusserung der Trägheit* — some manifestation of inertia in organic life — completed by the mooring of that inertia in fixation, *Fixierung*? Not only does Lacan not think so; a serious examination of Freud's elaboration goes against it. *The drive is not the pressure* (*la pulsion n'est pas la poussée*): *Trieb* is not *Drang* — if only because in the 1915 article Freud says four terms must be distinguished in the drive: *Drang* (pressure), *Quelle* (source), *Objekt* (object), *Ziel* (aim). Natural-seeming enumeration — and *the whole text is made to show that it is not so natural*.

Recall first that Freud calls the drive a *Grundbegriff*, a fundamental concept, adding — good epistemologist — that from the moment he introduces it into science, one of two things: the concept will be kept or rejected; kept *if it functions* — Lacan glosses: *if it traces its way into the real to be penetrated* — as with all the *Grundbegriffe* of the scientific domain. What Freud has in mind are the fundamental concepts of physics: his physiological masters were those who promoted integrating physiology into the concepts of modern physics, especially energetics — and how many times in history have energy and force known re-takings of their thematic over a reality more and more englobed\! Progress of knowledge, Freud says, tolerates no *Starrheit*, no fascination with definitions. Elsewhere he says the drive belongs to *our myths* — Lacan sets "myth" aside: in this very text, first paragraph, Freud uses *Konvention*, much closer to the matter, which Lacan renames with a Benthamite term he has flagged before: *a fiction*. Preferable, in passing, to the overused *model* — for a model is never a *Grundbegriff*: in a given field several models can function correlatively; not so a fundamental concept, nor a fundamental fiction. The epistemological placement is deliberate and load-bearing: the drive has the status of Bentham's fictions — entities with no substance that nonetheless structure a practice and trace a way in the real — neither observed thing nor arbitrary model; exactly the status the seminar has given the subject and the *objet a*.

**Drang: constant force, not kinetic.** Looked at closely, the four terms *can only appear disjoint*. The pressure first: identified with a pure and simple tendency to discharge, produced by a stimulus — the transmission of the surplus energy admitted at the stimulus-level, the famous Qη of the *Entwurf*. But Freud at once makes a far-reaching remark: the *Reiz*, the excitation at stake in the drive, differs from any stimulation coming from the outer world — it is an *internal Reiz*. Meaning need, as manifested at the level of hunger or thirst? *From the first lines Freud posits, in the most formal way, that in the Trieb it is absolutely not a matter of the pressure of a need like Hunger or Durst.* And in examining the *Trieb*, does Freud refer to an agency exercised at the level of the organism in its totality — does the real, the living being as a whole, make its irruption here? No: *it is always, specifically, the Freudian field itself*, in the most undifferentiated form Freud gave it at the outset — the *Real-Ich*, conceived as supported not by the whole organism but by the *nervous system*: a planified, objectified subject-character. Lacan underlines the *surface*\-characters of this field by treating it topologically — taking it as a surface answers all the needs of its handling. This point is essential: the *Triebreiz* is that by which certain elements of this field are, Freud says, *triebbesetzt*, drive-invested — placing us on the terrain of an energy, and not any energy: a *potential* energy, for — Freud articulates it most pressingly — *the characteristic of the drive is to be a konstante Kraft, a constant force*; he cannot conceive it as a *momentane Stosskraft*.

On *momentane*: the Parisians during the 1870 siege mocked Bismarck's *psychologische Moment* — the French, ticklish till recently about exact word-use, found this "psychological moment" hilarious; it simply meant the psychological *factor*. The *momentane Stosskraft* is perhaps to be taken not as factor but as *moment* in the kinematic sense: the *Stosskraft*, shock-force, is nothing but a reference to live force, *kinetic energy*. In the drive it is not a matter of kinetic energy, of something regulated with movement; *the discharge in question is of a wholly other nature and lies on a wholly other plane*. And the decisive clinical mark: *the constancy of the pressure forbids any assimilation of the drive to a biological function, which always has a rhythm*. The first thing Freud says of the drive is — so to speak — *that it has no day or night, no spring or autumn, no rise and fall. It is a constant force.* One should, after all, take account of the texts — and of experience. The dismantling of *Drang* thus yields the drive's first anti-biological signature: everything vital pulses and cycles; the drive alone burns flat and constant — potential, not kinetic; pressure without weather.

**Satisfaction and the impossible.** At the other end of the chain, Freud writes — in full letters, but with a pair of quotation marks — *die "Befriedigung"*, satisfaction. What does satisfaction of the drive mean? You will say: reaching its *Ziel* — the wild beast leaves its lair *quaerens quem devoret*, and having found what to sink its teeth into, is satisfied, digests. That such an image can be evoked shows it resonates with the *mythology* of the drive, properly speaking. One thing objects at once — remarkably unremarked, an enigma sustained like a wager to the end of Freud's life without his deigning further explanation: the third of the four fundamental vicissitudes (curious that there are four vicissitudes as there are four elements) is *sublimation* — and in this article Freud says a thousand times over that *sublimation is also satisfaction of the drive, although it is zielgehemmt, inhibited as to its aim — although it does not attain it. Sublimation is nonetheless satisfaction of the drive, and that without repression.* In other terms: *at this moment, I am not making love, I am speaking to you — well, I can have exactly the same satisfaction as if I were.* Which poses, moreover, the question of whether in fact I am. Between the two terms an extreme antinomy is established, reminding us that *the use of the function of the drive has no other bearing for us than to put in question what satisfaction is.* The crude example is load-bearing: if aim-inhibited activity satisfies fully and without repression, then satisfaction cannot be defined by the aim's attainment — the *Ziel* is dismantled next to the *Drang* — and the drive's whole function in theory is to problematize satisfaction itself.

Every analyst here should feel the essential accommodation-level this brings. Our patients do not, as one says, satisfy themselves with what they are — *and yet we know that everything they are, everything they live, their very symptoms, is a matter of satisfaction*. They satisfy something that no doubt runs counter to what they might satisfy themselves with — or better, *they satisfy to something* (*ils satisfont à quelque chose*: they meet its requirement). They are not content with their state, but in that so-uncontenting state, *they content themselves*. The whole question is *what is the "self" that is contented there*. In first approximation: what they satisfy by the paths of displeasure is nonetheless — as is commonly received — the law of pleasure; say that for this sort of satisfaction *they give themselves too much trouble* — and up to a point, that *too much trouble* is the sole justification of our intervention. One cannot say the aim is not attained as to satisfaction: *it is attained*. Not a definitive ethical position — but at a certain level this is how analysts approach the problem, knowing a little more than others about the normal and the abnormal: the arrangements between what works and what doesn't form a continuous series; what we face in analysis is a system in which everything arranges itself and attains its own sort of satisfaction. If we meddle, it is insofar as we think there are other paths — shorter ones, for example. In any case, if we refer to the drive, *it is insofar as the state of satisfaction is to be rectified at the level of the drive*. The clinical phenomenology here is among the seminar's most consequential: the symptom *works* — suffering is a mode of satisfaction, the drive is satisfied in the worst — and the analyst's warrant is not that satisfaction fails but that it costs too much.

This satisfaction is paradoxical: looked at closely, something new enters — *the category of the impossible*, absolutely radical in the foundations of the Freudian conceptions. *The path of the subject — to pronounce the term with respect to which alone satisfaction can be situated — passes between two walls of the impossible.* The function of the impossible must be approached with prudence, like every function presented in negative form; do not take it by negation. That method would carry us to the possible — and the impossible is not necessarily the contrary of the possible; or rather, *since the opposite of the possible is assuredly the real, we shall be led to define the real as the impossible*. Lacan sees no obstacle — the less so since in Freud the real appears in this form: *obstacle to the pleasure principle*. The real is the collision (*le heurt*), the fact that things do not arrange themselves at once as the hand wills that reaches toward outer objects. But that is an illusory and reduced conception of Freud's thought on the point: the real is distinguished — as said last time — by its *separation from the field of the pleasure principle, by its desexualization, by the fact that its economy consequently admits something new, which is precisely the impossible*. Here the seminar's most quoted formula — *the real is the impossible* — is minted, and its derivation should be kept attached to it: not the frustrating obstacle (that is the naive reading), but a modal category — what the desexualized economy of "reality" cannot admit — the real as what the pleasure-reality apparatus structurally excludes.

And the impossible is present also *in the other field*, as essential: the pleasure principle is characterized precisely by this, that the impossible is so present there that *it is never recognized as such*. The idea that the pleasure principle satisfies by hallucination is there to illustrate it. *The drive, seizing its object, learns in some way that this is precisely not how it is satisfied.* For if at the departure of the drive's dialectic one distinguishes *Not* from *Bedürfnis*, need from drive-exigency, it is precisely because *no object of any need can satisfy the drive*. Even were you to stuff the mouth — this mouth that opens in the register of the drive — *it is not with food that it satisfies itself: it is, as one says, with the pleasure of the mouth*. Which is why, in analytic experience, the oral drive is met at the last term, in a situation where it does nothing but *order the menu*. What goes to the mouth returns to the mouth, and exhausts itself in that pleasure. The anorexia of Lesson VIII (eating the nothing) finds its general law: the drive's circuit is auto-erotic in structure — mouth satisfied by mouth — with the object as pretext, not nourishment.

**The object: indifferent, and circled.** So Freud: *as for the object in the drive, let it be known that it has, strictly speaking, no importance. It is totally indifferent.* Never read Freud without ears pricked — such things should make them move. How conceive the object so that in every drive it is indifferent? For the oral drive it is evidently not food, nor memory of food, nor echo of food, nor the mother's care, but something called *the breast* — which seems to go without saying because of belonging to the same series. If Freud remarks that the object in the drive has no importance, *it is probably that the breast is entirely to be revised as to its function as object*. To this breast in its function as object — *object a, cause of desire*, as Lacan brings the notion — must be given a function such that its place in the drive's satisfaction can be said. The best formula: *the drive goes around it* (*la pulsion en fait le tour*) — "tour" to be taken with the ambiguity French gives it: at once *turn*, the boundary-stone one turns around, and *trick*, the sleight-of-hand. The two prior dismantlings converge on this one: aim without attainment, satisfaction without the object's help — therefore the object is not the drive's target but the *hollow* its circuit rounds; the breast revised from nourishing thing to circled void, and the pun (tour/trick) marking that the circuit is also a conjuring: satisfaction obtained by going around what is never obtained.

**The source: the border.** Last, the source — where, if one wished at all costs to bring vital regulation back into the drive, one would think to find the angle. But *why are the so-called erogenous zones recognized only at those points differentiated for us by their border-structure* (*structure de bord*)? Why speak of the mouth and not the esophagus or the stomach, which participate just as much in the oral function? At the erogenous level we speak of the mouth — and not only the mouth: the lips, the teeth, what Homer calls *the enclosure of the teeth*. So too for the anal drive: it is not enough to say a certain living function is integrated into a function of exchange with the world — excrement; there are other excremental functions and other participating elements than the *margin of the anus*, which is nonetheless specifically what defines itself for us as the source and departure of a certain drive. The anatomical selection is the theoretical point: of the whole digestive tube, only the *rims* — cuts, edges, margins — are erogenous; the drive's sources are border-structures, which is to say the body enters the drive only where it presents the topology of the cut (the same edge-structure the interior eight and the nasse-orifice formalize).

Whence the lesson's title-thesis: *if there is anything the drive resembles, it is a montage*. Not a montage conceived in a finality-perspective — that is modern instinct-theory, where the presentification of a montage-image is striking: the specific form that makes the hen flatten to the ground if a paper cut in hawk-shape passes overhead — something triggering a more-or-less appropriate reaction, whose cunning is precisely to show it is not necessarily appropriate. Not that sort of montage. *The montage of the drive is a montage that presents itself first as having neither head nor tail — in the sense in which one speaks of montage in a surrealist collage.* Bringing together the paradoxes defined at the levels of *Drang*, object, aim, the image that comes would show *the working of a dynamo plugged into the gas-tap; a peacock's feather comes out and tickles the belly of a pretty woman, who is there for good, for the beauty of the thing*. And it grows interesting in that the drive defines, according to Freud, all the forms in which such a mechanism can be *inverted* — which does not mean turning the dynamo around: *one unrolls its wires; it is they that become the peacock's feather; the gas-tap passes into the lady's mouth, and a rump emerges in the middle*. The surrealist machine is a rigorous emblem, not a joke: heterogeneous parts (energy-source, ornament, erotic surface) coupled without natural articulation, satisfying by their functioning alone — and "inversion of the drive" (active/passive, seeing/being-seen) operating not on a natural mechanism but by re-splicing the collage.

That is what Freud shows as developed example. Read the text before next time: at every instant the leap, without transition, from the most heterogeneous images to one another — all passing only through *grammatical* references, whose artifice will be easy to grasp. For how can one purely and simply say, as Freud will, that exhibition is the contrary of voyeurism, or masochism the contrary of sadism? He advances it for purely grammatical reasons — inversion of subject and object — *as if the grammatical object and subject were real functions*; easy to demonstrate that they are not, and our structure of language suffices to make the deduction impossible. But what, around this play, he conveys concerning the essence of the drive is what will be defined next time as *the trace of the act* (*le tracé de l'acte*). The critique is double-edged and both edges will matter in Lesson XIV: Freud's active/passive reversals are grammar, not psychology — invalid as deduction, revealing as symptom — for the drive's essence *is* articulated like a grammar (a sentence to be conjugated through its voices), and its truth lies in the circuit that conjugation traces.

**Réponses.** Green raises the capital point — the four terms as discontinuous elements — and asks about the set-aside *Drang*: if the drive is finally vowed to the combinatory by discontinuity, there is the contradiction of an energy conceived as at once constant force and subject to variation; and he confesses the *economic* point of view remains ill-perceived in Lacan's teaching. Lacan promises the route and gives its first landmark, from a chapter of energetics: in a limited system, each point can be noted scalar-wise for potential energy relative to its neighbors; each point then has a derivative, inscribable as a vector, and the vectors compose; and there is a law, curious at first sight but held fundamental — *what, of such a vector-field, crosses a certain surface — which is nothing other than what I would call the gap (béance), in that it is defined by a border-structure — is, for a given surface, a constant. The variations of the system being what they may, what stands at the level of the integration of potential — what is called the flux — is constant.* What the *Drang* would be, then: something connotable *only in relation to the Quelle, insofar as the source inscribes in the drive's economy this border-structure*. The deep physiological variations, inscribed in the totality of the organism, are subject to all the rhythms, even to the discharges occasioned by the drive; what characterizes the *Drang* is *the maintained constancy — measured, to take an image worth what it is worth, by an opening, individualized and variable up to a point. That is: people have bigger or smaller mouths* (*plus ou moins grande gueule*) — "which it would sometimes be fitting to take into account in the selection of analysts." The answer is a small triumph of the seminar's method: Green asks for economics and receives Gauss — the flux-through-a-surface theorem — by which the constancy of the *Drang* is derived from the border-structure of the source: constant force because constant flux through a rim, the drive's "energy" a theorem of its topology; with the joke about analysts' big mouths folding the invocatory drive, and the profession, into the formula. And the suspended point: Freud underlines that when the system engages the *Umwelt* there is discharge, but toward the *Triebreiz* there is *barrier* — to which no one attends. What can it mean? *There is no barrier — except that the investment is in the field itself*: because the field itself carries the investment, barrier-functioning cannot apply to it. Flight is impossible from an excitation lodged in the very surface that would flee.

Mathis asks about the border-structure: with mouth and anal margin, is erotization localized at the two extremities — and what of the esophageal, gastric level, sniffing, vomiting, the tracheal? Lacan limited himself to the two borders concerned in the tract; he could as well have said that *the rheumy edge of our eyelids, our ear, our navel are also borders*, all in the function of erotism. The analytic tradition refers always to the strictly focalized image of zones reduced to their border-function — which does not mean other zones do not intervene in our symptomatology: *they intervene in that zone of fall I call desexualization, and function of reality*. Example: it is in the function where the sexual object slides down the slope of reality and presents itself as *a packet of meat* that the form of desexualization arises so manifest that in the hysteric it is called the *reaction of disgust*. Not that pleasure is localized in the erogenous zones: desire concerns — heaven knows we know it only too well — quite other things than the organism, while implicating the organism at various levels. But the central function of the drive: *it is precisely insofar as annexed, connected zones are excluded that others take their erogenous function, become specified sources for the drive*. And the closing symmetry: there are two great slopes of desire as it can arise in the fall of sexualization — *on one side, disgust, engendered by the reduction of the sexual partner to a function of reality of whatever sort; on the other, what I called, apropos the scopic function, invidia, envy. Envy is something other than the scopic drive, and disgust something other than the oral drive.* The answer completes the doctrine of the border by its negative: the erogenous rims are constituted by exclusion (the tube desexualized so the lips can burn), and the excluded returns as the two affects of de-sexualization — disgust where the object collapses into meat (the oral drive's other), envy where another's closed satisfaction appears (the scopic drive's other) — a pairing that quietly maps the hysteric's disgust and Augustine's pale child onto the same structural operation.

### **XIV. The Partial Drive and Its Circuit**

*13 May 1964\.* On the board, Heraclitus, fragment B 48: *τῷ τόξῳ ὄνομα βίος, ἔργον δὲ θάνατος* — the bow's name is life (*biós*), its work is death. The fragment presides over the whole lesson, which assembles what Lesson XIII took apart: the drive as a closed circuit around the lost object, every drive partial, the aim distinguished from the goal, the *Schaulust* and sado-masochism read as three-time structures, and the drive as the sole permitted transgression of the pleasure principle. Miller's keywords: *die ganze Sexualstrebung; every drive is partial; the drive, sex and death; the alleged stages; the Schaulust; sado-masochism.*

The opening is polemical housekeeping: reading in the *Psychoanalytic Quarterly* Edward Glover's "Freudian or Neo-Freudian," entirely directed against Alexander's constructions, Lacan smells "a sordid odor of staleness" — to see combated in the name of obsolete criteria the construction of a man of great talent (which Lacan himself attacked most formally fourteen years ago, at the 1950 Psychiatry Congress). At least, through all the vicissitudes his own discourse meets, it can be said to obstruct the transmission of analytic experience "in an absolutely cretinizing way." Then the thread: the drive was broached after positing transference as the enactment of the reality of the unconscious *as sexuality* — and he finds himself arrested on what that affirmation itself comports. If we are sure sexuality is present in action in the transference, it is insofar as at certain moments it manifests openly *in the form of love*. Is love, then, the summit-point, the achieved moment, the indisputable factor presenting sexuality in the *hic et nunc* of transference? *To this the clearest objection is Freud's text* — not isolated but central — on the drives and their vicissitudes: an article divided into two slopes, the dismantling of the drive and the examination of *das Lieben*, the act of love. The lesson's frame is thus the transference-question itself: transference-love looks like sexuality in person; the 1915 paper proves it is not — love and drive are disjoint, and the disjunction organizes everything to the end of Part Three.

**Every drive is partial.** Freud expressly formulates that love can in no way be considered the representative of *die ganze Sexualstrebung* — the tendency, the forms, the convergence of the sexual effort as achieving itself in a *Ganze*, a graspable whole that would summarize its essence and function. *Kommt aber auf damit nicht zu* \[Stimmung\] — "that won't do at all," he cries, answering the ambient suggestion, which we analysts have rendered by all sorts of formulas that are so many deceptions. The whole article shows that with respect to the biological finality of sexuality — reproduction — the drives as they present themselves in the process of psychic reality are *partial drives*. In their structure, in the tension they establish, the drives are bound to an economic factor depending on the conditions in which the pleasure principle is exercised at the level of the *Real-Ich* — conceivable as the central nervous system functioning not as a relational system but as one destined to ensure a certain *homeostasis of internal tensions*. And the thesis: *it is by reason of the reality of the homeostatic system that sexuality enters play only in the form of partial drives. The drive is precisely that montage by which sexuality participates in psychic life, in a way that must conform to the gap-structure that is the structure of the unconscious.* The two prior dismantlings meet here: sexuality-as-whole (the *ganze Strebung*, the genital totality of the maturation-myth) never enters the psyche; what enters are partial samplings, shaped to the unconscious's own structure of gap and border — the drive as sexuality's mode of appearance under the regime of the signifier.

The placement of sexuality between the two extremes of analytic experience: the primally repressed is *a signifier*, and what builds above it as symptom is a scaffolding of signifiers — repressed and symptom homogeneous, reducible to signifier-functions, inscribable finally in synchronic terms. At the other extremity, *interpretation*: bearing on the factor of a special temporal structure defined as metonymy; in its term interpretation points to desire, to which in a certain sense it is identical — *desire is, in sum, interpretation itself*. In the interval: *sexuality*. Had sexuality not manifested itself in the form of the partial drives as dominating the whole economy of this interval, our experience would reduce to a *mantic* — a divination — for which the neutral term psychic energy would then do, but where what constitutes the presence, the *Dasein*, of sexuality would be lacking. The readability of sex in interpreting unconscious mechanisms is always *retroactive*; it would be only of interpretation's nature if we could not be assured that at each instant of the history the partial drives intervened efficaciously *in time and place* — and not erratically. That infantile sexuality is not a wandering ice-block torn from the great floe of adult sexuality, intervening as seduction on an immature subject — this proved itself at once in analysis, with a pregnancy surprising after the fact. From the *Three Essays*, Freud posits it as essentially *polymorphous, aberrant*; the charm of an alleged infantile innocence was broken.
