But its very early — almost too early — imposition made us pass too fast over what it represents in essence: *with respect to the agency of sexuality, all subjects are equal, from the child to the adult — they deal only with what of sexuality passes into the networks of the constitution of the subject, into the networks of the signifier — sexuality is realized only by the operation of the drives insofar as they are partial drives, partial with respect to the biological finality of sexuality.* The paragraph performs a double correction: against the seduction-theory's picture (adult sexuality invading childhood), and against the maturation-picture (childhood ripening toward the genital whole) — for both child and adult, sexuality exists only as strained through the signifier's mesh; no one, at any age, has the whole.

Whence the coinage: the integration of sexuality into the dialectic of desire passes through the bringing-into-play of what in the body deserves the term *apparatus* (*appareil*) — "if you will hear by that what the body, with respect to sexuality, can *appareiller* — fit itself out with — as distinct from what bodies can *apparier* — pair off." The pun is the doctrine: the drive-body is rigged, outfitted, not paired; sexuality reaches the subject as equipment, never as coupling. And if everything is muddled in discussing the sexual drives, it is because one does not see that *the drive doubtless represents, but only represents, and partially, the curve of the accomplishment of sexuality in the living being. How be surprised that its last term is death? Since the presence of sex in the living being is bound to death.* Then Heraclitus, from Diels's monument — a lesson of wisdom that, before the whole circuit of scientific elaboration, goes straight to the mark: *to the bow (biós) is given the name of life (bíos) — and its work is death*. What the drive integrates from the outset in its whole existence is *a dialectic of the bow, I would even say of archery*. The fragment is perfectly chosen: one written word, two accents — life and the death-dealing bow — the signifier itself carrying the sex-death knot; and the archery-image tools the next section, where aim and goal come apart.

**The circuit: aim and goal.** Freud introduces the drive by a most traditional route, using at every moment the resources of language, not hesitating to ground himself on something belonging only to certain linguistic systems — the three voices, active, passive, reflexive (middle). *But that is only an envelope*: the signifying reversion is one thing, what it clothes another. *What is fundamental, at the level of each drive, is the going-and-returning (aller et retour) in which it is structured.* Remarkable that Freud can designate the two poles only by verbs — *beschauen und beschaut werden*, to see and to be seen; *quälen und gequält werden*, to torment and be tormented: from the first, Freud presents as acquired that no part of the drive's course can be separated from its going-and-returning, its fundamental reversion, its *circular character*. Equally remarkable that to illustrate this *Verkehrung* he chooses the *Schaulust* and what he can designate only by gluing two terms: *sado-masochism*. And speaking of these — especially masochism — he insists there are *not two times in these drives, but three*: one must distinguish the return-in-circuit from what appears — or does not appear — in a *third* time: the apparition of *ein neues Subjekt*, to be understood thus: *not that there was already one — namely, the subject of the drive — but that it is new to see a subject appear. This subject, which is properly the other, appears insofar as the drive has been able to close its circular course. Only with its apparition at the level of the other can the function of the drive be realized.* This is the lesson's central structural claim, and it must be read exactly: the drive has no subject at its origin (it is "acephalous," as the Réponses will say); a subject arrives only in the third time, at the circuit's closure, and arrives *as the other* — the grammar's "new subject" is not the drive's owner but its terminal apparition.

On the board, the circuit: the rising-and-redescending arrow that, *Drang* at its origin, crosses the surface constituted by the *border* — considered in the theory as the source, the *Quelle*, the erogenous zone. *The tension is always a loop* (*boucle*), inseparable from its return upon the erogenous zone. Here the mystery of the *zielgehemmt* clears: the drive attains its satisfaction without attaining its aim — *if* the aim is defined by the biological function, the realization of reproductive pairing. But that is not the aim of the partial drive. What is? Suspend the answer and split the word: two senses, noted in English where they are expressive. *Aim*: charge someone with a mission — that does not say what he must bring back but *by what path he must pass; the aim is the trajectory*. The other form is the *goal*: in archery, the goal is not the bird you bring down — *it is having scored the hit* (*avoir marqué le coup*) *and thereby attained your but*. *If the drive can be satisfied without having attained what, from the viewpoint of a biological totalization of the function, would be satisfaction in its reproductive end, it is because it is a partial drive, and its aim is nothing other than this return in circuit.* The English pair does what French *but* cannot: the drive's *aim* (path) is the circuit itself, and its *goal* (the scored hit) is the closure of the loop — satisfaction is topological, not teleological; the target is beside the point.

The theory is in Freud: the ideal model of auto-erotism, he says somewhere, would be *a single mouth kissing itself* — a luminous, dazzling metaphor, asking only to be completed by a question: in the drive, is this mouth not what could be called *an arrowed mouth* (*bouche fléchée*) — *a sewn-up mouth, in which, in analysis, we see the pure agency of the oral drive point at its maximum, in certain silences, closing on its own satisfaction*? In any case, what forces distinguishing this satisfaction from pure auto-erotism of the erogenous zone is *that object we too often confound with what the drive closes upon — that object which is in fact only the presence of a hollow, a void, occupiable, Freud tells us, by any object whatever, and whose agency we know only in the form of the lost object, petit a. The objet petit a is not the origin of the oral drive; it is not introduced as the primitive food: it is introduced from the fact that no food will ever satisfy the oral drive, except by going around the eternally lacking object.* Lesson XIII's *tour* is here sealed as definition: the *a* is not a first object regretted but a void constituted by the circuit — cause, not target; and the clinical incarnation (the analysand's silence as the oral drive's sewn mouth, satisfaction closing on itself in the session) shows the theory operating on the couch.

**Against the stages.** Does the circuit spiral — the oral drive continuing into the anal as the next *stage*, a dialectical progress engendered by opposition? Already too generous a question for those habituated, in the name of some mystery of development, to hold the thing acquired, inscribed in the organism. The conception seems supported by the fact that the emergence of sexuality in its so-called achieved form is indeed an organic process — but there is no reason to extend that to the relation between the partial drives. *There is no relation of engendering between one partial drive and the next. The passage from the oral to the anal drive is produced not by a process of maturation but by the intervention of something that is not of the field of the drive — by the intervention, the turning-round, of the demand of the Other.* Bring in the other drives of the (rather short) series and one would be hard put to situate the *Schaulust*, or what will be distinguished in its time as the invocatory drive, in any historical succession, or to establish the least relation of deduction or genesis. *There is no natural metamorphosis of the oral drive into the anal drive* — whatever appearances the symbolic play of the so-called anal object (feces in its negative incidence relative to the phallus) may occasionally give, experience demonstrates no continuity from anal phase to phallic phase, no natural metamorphosis. Dolto's stages (Lesson V's Réponses) here receive their final liquidation: the series oral–anal–scopic–invocatory is not a developmental ladder but a set of discrete montages, and what sequences the first two is the Other's demand — weaning, training — history from outside, not ripening from within.

The drive stands under the rubric of the *konstante Kraft* sustaining it as *stationary tension*. Note even Freud's metaphors for its issues: *Schub* — thrust — which he at once translates by the image it carries in his mind, *a lava-spout*, a material emission of the energetic deflagration produced in successive times, completing, one after another, the return-shaped path. Does not the Freudian metaphor incarnate the fundamental structure — *something that goes out from a border, redoubles its closed structure, following a trajectory that makes return, and whose consistency nothing assures but the object, as something that must be gone around*? This articulation makes the drive's manifestation *the mode of an acephalous subject* (*sujet acéphale*): everything articulates in terms of tension, and has relation to the subject only by *topological community*. The unconscious was articulated as situated in the gaps that the distribution of signifying investments installs in the subject, figured in the algorithm by the lozenge \[◊\] set at the heart of every relation between the unconscious, reality, and the subject: *it is insofar as something in the apparatus of the body is structured in the same way — by reason of the topological unity of the gaps in play — that the drive takes its role in the functioning of the unconscious.* This is the answer to Lesson XIII's opening objection (the drive as biological bedrock refuting the signifier-doctrine): drive and unconscious communicate not by content but by *shared topology* — border, gap, rim on both sides — the body enters the unconscious where its edges rhyme with the signifier's cuts.

**Schaulust, and the truth of sado-masochism.** Follow Freud on the *Schaulust*: seeing, being-seen — the same thing? How even sustainable, except by inscribing it in signifiers? Or is there another mystery? There is — and to enter it, consider that the *Schaulust manifests itself in perversion*. Underline: *the drive is not the perversion*. The enigmatic character of Freud's presentation comes precisely from his wanting to give a *radical structure — in which the subject is not yet placed. What defines perversion, on the contrary, is precisely the way the subject places himself in it.* And a word on reading Freud: the precious thing about his texts in this ground-breaking matter is that, like a good archaeologist, *he leaves the dig in place* — so that even unfinished, we can know what the unearthed objects mean; when Fenichel passes through, he does as one used to do — gathers everything into his pockets and display-cases, in an order completely arbitrary, so no one can find anything again.

What happens in voyeurism? At the moment of the voyeur's act, where is the subject, where the object? *The subject is not there as seeing, at the level of the scopic drive; he is there as pervert, and situates himself only at the culmination of the loop. As for the object — the loop turns around it: it is missile, and with it, in perversion, the target is hit.* *The object here is the gaze — the gaze that is the subject, that reaches him, that scores the bullseye.* Recall the Sartre analysis: if it makes the agency of the gaze arise, it is not at the level of the other whose look surprises the subject at the keyhole — *it is that the other surprises him, the subject, as wholly hidden gaze*. Here is the ambiguity of the scopic drive: *the gaze is that lost object, suddenly refound, in the conflagration of shame, by the introduction of the other.* Until then, what does the subject seek to see? *What he seeks to see, know it well, is the object as absence. What the voyeur seeks and finds is only a shadow, a shadow behind the curtain: he will fantasize there any magic of presence, the most graceful of girls, even if on the other side there is only a hairy athlete. What he seeks is not, as one says, the phallus — but precisely its absence* — whence the pre-eminence of certain forms as objects of his search. *What one looks at is what cannot be seen.* The Sartre-correction of Lesson VII is completed from inside the drive: the shame of the keyhole is the subject discovering himself *as* the lost gaze-object refound; and the voyeur doctrine — desire aimed at absence, the curtain worth more than what it hides — rejoins Parrhasios' veil exactly.

If the structure appears with the other's introduction, it truly completes itself only in its reversed, return form — *the true active drive. In exhibitionism, what the subject aims at is what is realized in the other; the true aim of desire is the other, as forced, beyond his implication in the scene: not merely the victim, but the victim as referred to some other who looks at her.* And with this key, the knot that has so obstructed the understanding of *masochism*: Freud articulates most firmly that at the departure of the sado-masochistic drive *pain counts for nothing*. It is a matter of *Herrschaft*, of *Bewältigung* — mastery, overpowering — violence done to what? To something so nameless that Freud comes to — and at the same time recoils from — finding its first model, conformably to everything here enounced, in *a violence the subject does to himself, for ends of mastery*. He recoils, with good reason: *the ascetic who flagellates himself does it for a third party* — and that is not what Freud means to grasp; he wants only to designate the return, the insertion on the subject's own body, of the drive's departure and end. At what moment, Freud asks, does *the possibility of pain* enter the sado-masochistic drive — pain suffered by what has become, at that moment, the subject of the drive? *At the moment the loop has closed, when there has been reversion from one pole to the other, when the other has entered play, when the subject has taken himself as term, terminus of the drive. At that moment pain enters play insofar as the subject experiences it from the other.* He will become, in this theoretical deduction, a *sadistic* subject insofar as the completed loop of the drive has brought the other's action into play. Masochism is thus not sadism inverted (the grammatical illusion of Lesson XIII) but sadism's *ground*: pain is not the drive's motor but a late arrival, felt only when the subject occupies the object-place at the loop's closure — the priority the Réponses will state flatly.

*What is at stake in the drive is finally revealed here: the path of the drive is the only form of transgression permitted to the subject with respect to the pleasure principle.* The subject will perceive that his desire is only a vain detour — a fishing-trip — for hooking the *jouissance* of the other, insofar as, the other intervening, he will perceive that *there is a jouissance beyond the pleasure principle*. The forcing of the pleasure principle by the incidence of the partial drive: by this we conceive that the partial drives, ambiguous, are installed on the limit of an *Erhaltungstrieb* — the maintenance of a homeostasis — and its capture by *the veiled figure of sexuality*. It is insofar as the drive testifies to the forcing of the pleasure principle that we have testimony that *beyond the Real-Ich another reality intervenes* — and we shall see by what return it is this reality, in the last term, that gave the *Real-Ich* its structure and diversification. The term *jouissance*, sparingly used in this seminar, here receives its systematic slot: what lies beyond the pleasure principle, reached only by the drive's circuit through the other — desire itself demoted to "vain detour" angling for it. Lesson V's "beyond" (the *Trieb* behind the dream) is thus named.

**Réponses.** Miller asks the drive's relation to the real, and the differences between the object of the drive, of the fantasy, and of desire. Lacan's answer is a small treatise, worth its architecture. *The object of the drive* is to be situated at the level of an *acephalous subjectivation* — a subjectivation without subject, "a bone, a structure, a trace" (*un os, une structure, un tracé*), representing one face of the topology; the other face is the subject holed (*troué*) by his relations to the signifier — and those holes come from somewhere. In Freud's first constructions — the stabilizing networks of signifying crossroads — what is aimed at is the maintenance of homeostasis: not merely threshold-crossing but *repartition of the paths* (Freud even assigns metaphorical diameters to them). And Freud says formally somewhere that *it is the pressure of what in sexuality must be repressed to maintain the pleasure principle — the libido — that permitted the progress of the mental apparatus itself*, for instance the installation of that investment-possibility called *Aufmerksamkeit*, attention. The determination of the *Real-Ich* — satisfying the pleasure principle while invested, defenseless, by the risings of sexuality — is responsible for its structure. At this level no subjectivation need even be counted: *the subject is an apparatus. This apparatus is something lacunary, and it is in the lacuna that the subject installs the function of a certain object, as lost object. That is the status of the objet a in the drive.* The promised "return" is here delivered in outline: the pressure of the repressed libido *built* the reality-apparatus (attention included) — the beyond made the this-side — and the drive-object is the lost object lodged in the apparatus's own lacuna, pre-subjective.

*In the fantasy*, the subject is frequently unperceived, *but he is always there* — in dream, reverie, any of the more or less developed forms: the subject situates himself as *determined by* the fantasy. *The fantasy is the support of desire; it is not the object that supports desire.* The subject sustains himself as desiring in relation to an ever more complex signifying ensemble — visible in the scenario-form the fantasy takes, where the subject, more or less recognizable, is somewhere, *split, divided, habitually double*, in relation to an object that mostly does not show its true face either. Next time, the *structure of perversion*: properly speaking *an inverse effect of the fantasy — it is the subject who determines himself as object, in his encounter with the division of subjectivity*. And the point there was no time for: the subject assuming the role of the object is exactly what sustains the reality of the so-called sado-masochistic drive — *which exists at only one point: in the masochistic situation itself*. It is insofar as the subject *makes himself* the object of another will that the sado-masochistic drive not only closes but *is constituted*. Only in a second time, as Freud indicates, does sadistic *desire* become possible in relation to a fantasy — sadistic desire exists in a crowd of configurations, in the neuroses too, but that is not yet sadism properly speaking. See *Kant avec Sade*: *the sadist himself occupies the place of the object, but without knowing it, for the benefit of another, for whose jouissance he exercises his action as sadistic pervert.* Several possibilities, then, of the *objet a*'s function — *which is never found in the position of the aim of desire*: it is either pre-subjective, or the foundation of an identification of the subject, or the foundation of an identification denied by the subject. *In this sense, sadism is only the disavowal (dénégation) of masochism* — a formula that clarifies much concerning sadism's true nature. And the object of desire, in the common sense, is *either a fantasy that is in reality the support of desire, or a lure*.

On the lure — carrying all the preliminary questions on the subject's relation to the real — Freud's analysis of love will permit the advance: the necessity Freud is under of referring to the relation of the *Ich* to the real *to introduce the dialectic of love* — whereas the neutral real is the desexualized real, which did not intervene at the level of the drive — will be most enriching concerning the function of love, namely *its fundamentally narcissistic structure*. That there is a real is absolutely not in doubt; that the subject has constructive relation to it only in strict dependence on the pleasure principle — *the pleasure principle unforced by the drive* — is the point of emergence of the *love-object*. The whole question is how the love-object can come to fill a role analogous to the object of desire — *on what equivocations rests the possibility for the love-object to become object of desire*. "Have I brought you some light by this exposition?" — *"Some light and some shadows."* The closing exchange packages Part Three's remaining program with complete precision: love arises on the desexualized, unforced side (narcissism, the *Ich* and its real) and the drive on the forced side (jouissance beyond the principle); their non-coincidence — and the equivocation by which love borrows desire's object — is exactly what Lesson XV must untangle.

### **XV. From Love to the Libido**

*20 May 1964\.* The lesson that closes Part Three delivers, at speed and with declared regret at the speed, the doctrine everything since Lesson X has been staging: love and drive assigned to two disjoint fields; the drive's grammar corrected into the reflexive-causative *se faire...*; and the myth Lacan sets against Aristophanes — the *lamella* — by which the libido is defined as an organ. Miller's keywords: *the subject and the Other; the narcissistic field; the drive-field: making oneself... seen, heard, sucked, shat; the myth of the lamella.* The announced point comes first: *the libido is not something fleeting, fluid; it does not distribute or accumulate, like a magnetism, in the focalization-centers the subject offers it. The libido is to be conceived as an organ — in both senses of the term: organ-part of the organism, and organ-instrument.*

The apologetic prelude carries doctrine: some obscurity was complained of last time — but that is our field's characteristic. The unconscious is commonly figured as a cellar, if not a cavern (Plato's is evoked) — *not the good comparison: the unconscious is rather something close to a bladder, and this bladder, provided one puts a little light inside, can serve as a lantern* — no surprise if the light sometimes takes a while to come on. The bathetic image is a precise anti-Platonism: not a cave whose shadows conceal true forms, but a membrane — a surface again — that illuminates only when lit from within.

In the subject who alternately shows and hides himself in the unconscious's pulsation, *we grasp only partial drives*: the *ganze Sexualstrebung*, representation of the totality of the sexual drive, *is not there* — Freud says it, and everything of Lacan's experience concurs. To those here who lack that experience, their presence answers for a certain confidence in what — in the role Lacan occupies toward them, that of the Other — will be called *good faith*. Precarious, supposed — for where does the subject's relation to the Other finally terminate? That the subject as such is in uncertainty because *divided by the effect of language* is what Lacan teaches, "I, as Lacan, following the traces of the Freudian dig." *By the effect of speech the subject realizes himself ever more in the Other — but he pursues there no more than a half of himself. He will find his desire only ever more divided, pulverized, in the circumscribable metonymy of speech.* The effect of language is mixed all along with this fund of the analytic experience: *the subject is subject only from being subjected* (*assujettissement*) *to the field of the Other; the subject proceeds from his synchronic subjection in that field*. Which is why he must get out of it — and in the getting-out, at the end, he will know *that the real Other has, just as much as he, to get out of it, to extricate himself*. There the necessity of good faith imposes itself, founded on the certainty that the same implication of difficulty with respect to the ways of desire is also in the Other. And the vertiginous coda: *truth, in this sense, is what runs after truth — and that is where I run, where I take you, like Actaeon's hounds, after me. When I have found the goddess's lair I shall doubtless change into a stag, and you will be able to devour me — but we still have a little time ahead of us.* The Actaeon figure, constant in Lacan since the "Freudian Thing," here absorbs Lesson VII's elegy for Merleau-Ponty into a self-portrait: the teacher as quarry of his own pack, truth as the chase and the dismemberment at its end — transference, again, folded into the teaching that describes it.

**The sorting: love from the belly, drive from the heart.** Freud, then — figured last time as apostolic — is now given Léon Bloy's image from *Le Salut par les juifs*: three old men at some tarpaulin, given over, in one of the forms of Israel's vocation, to the fundamental occupation called *la brocante* — dealing in second-hand goods. *They sort.* One thing goes on one side, another on the other. Freud puts on one side the partial drives, on the other, love. *He says: it's not the same.* The drives necessitate us in the sexual order — *that comes from the heart*. To our great surprise, he teaches that love, on the other side, *comes from the belly — it's the yum-yum* (*le miam-miam*). Under the clowning, the article's scandal is stated exactly: the 1915 text derives love from the *Ich* and its self-preservative interests (the "belly," the incorporative), and sexuality-in-act from the partial drives — the everyday hierarchy (love as sexuality's crown) inverted at the root.

Which lights something fundamental to analytic experience: *the genital drive, if it exists, is not at all articulated like the other drives* — despite the love-hate ambivalence. In his premisses and his own text Freud properly contradicts himself, saying ambivalence can pass for a characteristic of the drive's *Verkehrung* — and then, examining it, that ambivalence and reversion are not at all the same. *If therefore the genital drive does not exist, it can only go get itself f... fashioned* (*se faire f... façonner* — the stumble printed) *elsewhere — on the other side from the drive, on the left of my schema: on the right, in the field of the Other.* And that rejoins exactly what analytic experience teaches: *the genital drive is subject to the circulation of the Oedipus complex, to the elementary and other structures of kinship* — what is called, insufficiently, the field of culture: insufficiently, because that field is supposed to found itself on a no man's land where genitality as such would subsist, *whereas it is in fact dissolved, not gathered — for nowhere in the subject is the ganze Sexualstrebung graspable*. Yet for being nowhere, it is *diffuse* — which is what Freud tries to make felt. The thesis is the seminar's most radical clinical claim, quietly delivered: there is no genital drive as a natural montage; "genitality" exists only as fashioned in the Other — Oedipus, kinship, the signifier — the maturational ideal of the genital character dissolved into a cultural artifact.

Everything Freud says of love accents that to conceive it one must refer to *another structure* than the drive's — divided into three levels: the real, the economic, the biological; with three corresponding oppositions: at the level of the real, *what interests and what is indifferent*; at the economic level, *what gives pleasure and what displeasure*; and only at the biological level does the *activity-passivity* opposition present itself in its proper form, the only one grammatically valid — *loving / being loved*. Freud invites us to consider that love in its essence is to be judged only as *sexual passion of the gesamt Ich* — a hapax in his work, to be given the sense of what is drawn when he accounts for the pleasure principle: that network of arcs, lines linking junction-points, whose closed circle marks what must be conserved of tensional homeostasis — least tension, necessary derivation, diffusion of excitation into a thousand channels whenever in one of them it could be too intense. The filtering of stimulation toward discharge: there is the apparatus, the *cap* (*calotte*) — to be circumscribed on a sphere — where what he calls the stage of the *Real-Ich* is first defined, and to which he attributes the qualification *autoerotisch*.

From this the analysts concluded — since it had to be situated somewhere in "development," and Freud's word is gospel — that the infant must hold everything around it indifferent. "One wonders how things can hold together in a field of observers for whom articles of faith so crushingly outweigh observation" — for if there is one thing the nursling gives no idea of, it is disinterest in what enters its perceptual field. That there are objects from the earliest neo-natal time is beyond doubt; *autoerotisch* cannot possibly mean disinterest toward them. Read Freud: the second time, the economic time, consists precisely in this — the second *Ich* (second by right, second in a logical time) is the *Lust-Ich* he calls *purifiziert*, the purified pleasure-ego, instituted *in the field exterior to the cap* of the first *Real-Ich*. The *autoerotisch* consists in this — Freud himself underlines it — that *there would be no arising of objects if there were not objects good for me*: it is the criterion of the arising and repartition of objects. Here the *Lust-Ich* is constituted — and also the field of *Unlust*, of the object as *remainder*, as foreign. *The object good to know — and for good reason — is the one defined in the field of Unlust*, while the objects of the *Lust-Ich*'s field are *lovable*: *hassen* — hating — with its profound link to knowledge, is the other field. The reading rescues Freud's text from the developmental literalists twice over: "auto-erotism" is a logical structure (the pleasure-ego's purification), not an observational claim about indifferent babies; and the Freudian triple (love/indifference, love/hate, loving/being-loved) is stratified so that hate is bound to knowledge and the foreign remainder — the *Unlust*\-object as ancestor of the *objet a*.

At this level there is *no trace of drive-functions*, save those that are not true drives and that Freud calls *Ichtriebe*. *The level of the Ich is non-drive* (*non-pulsionnel*) — *and it is there, read the text attentively, that Freud founds love.* Everything defined at the *Ich*\-level takes sexual value, passes from *Erhaltungstrieb* to *Sexualtrieb*, only by *the appropriation, the seizure, of each of these fields by one of the partial drives*. Freud says properly that — *Vorhebung des Wesentlichen*, to bring out the essential — it is in a purely passive, non-drive way that the subject registers the *äusseren Reize*, what comes from the external world; *his activity comes only durch seine eigenen Triebe, from his own drives*. And before drawing the consequences, note the classical character of this conception of love — *to wish someone their good* (*se vouloir son bien*): exactly the equivalent of what tradition calls the physical theory of love, *the velle bonum alicui of Saint Thomas* — which, by reason of the function of narcissism, has for us exactly the same value. Lacan has long underlined the captious character of this alleged altruism, *satisfied with preserving the good of whom? — of the one who, precisely, is necessary to us.* The Thomist reference completes the demolition: even the tradition's noblest formula of benevolent love is re-read as narcissism's economy — the beloved's good preserved as one preserves one's own equipment.

**Activity-passivity does not reach the sexual.** So Freud seats love's bases — and only with activity-passivity does what properly concerns the sexual relation enter play. But does that relation *cover* the sexual relation? See the Wolf Man and other passages of the *Five Psychoanalyses*: Freud explains that the polar reference activity-passivity is there *to name, to cover, to metaphorize what remains unfathomable in sexual difference*. Nowhere does he ever maintain that the masculine-feminine relation is psychologically graspable otherwise than by the *representative* of the activity-passivity opposition: *as such, the masculine-feminine opposition is never attained*. Whence the importance of the verb repeated here, particularly sharp: this opposition *pours itself, molds itself, injects itself* (*se coule, se moule, s'injecte*) — "it is an arteriography" — and even so the masculine-feminine relations do not exhaust it. The radiological image is exact: activity-passivity is the contrast-dye that renders the vessels of sexual difference visible without being the vessels — representation, never the thing; the thesis that will become "there is no sexual relation" is here in its 1964 form: the difference itself never reaches the psyche.

Of course activity-passivity accounts for much in the domain of love. But what we deal with is precisely this *injection* of sado-masochism — not at all to be taken at face value as to properly sexual realization. In the sexual relation all the intervals of desire enter play — *what value has my desire for you?*, eternal question of the lovers' dialogue. But the alleged value of *feminine masochism*, as it is called, should be placed in the parenthesis of a serious interrogation: *it belongs to that dialogue which can be defined, at many points, as a masculine fantasy* — and much suggests that sustaining it is *complicity on our part*. Rather than trusting Anglo-Saxon survey-research ("which would not yield much on this subject"), or alleging women's consent ("which means nothing"), we analysts will limit ourselves, more legitimately, to the women of our own group: *it is quite striking that the representatives of this sex in the analytic circle are specially disposed to entertain the basal credence in feminine masochism. No doubt there is a veil here that should not be lifted too quickly, concerning the interests of the sex.* An excursion, but profoundly linked — we shall return to this joint. The passage is among the seminar's most pointed interventions in analytic politics: "feminine masochism" — Deutsch's and Bonaparte's doctrine — is diagnosed as a masculine fantasy sustained with the complicity of the profession, its female theorists included; and the veiled "interests of the sex" in maintaining it are flagged for a return the seminar will not have time to make.

However that may be, at this level nothing takes us out of *the field of love — that is, the frame of narcissism*, which Freud indicates in so many words is made of *the insertion of the autoerotisch into the organized interests of the ego*. Inside this frame there can well be representation of external objects, choice and discernment, possibility of knowledge — in short the whole field of classical psychology is included. *But nothing — and this is why all affective psychology failed until Freud — nothing there yet represents the Other, the radical Other, the Other as such.* This representation of the Other is lacking precisely *between the two opposed worlds that sexuality designates for us in the masculine and the feminine*. Pushing to the maximum: the virile ideal and the feminine ideal are figured in the psyche by something other than activity-passivity — they arise properly from a term Lacan did not introduce, with which a woman psychoanalyst pinned the feminine sexual attitude: *the masquerade*. And the masquerade is not the parade necessary at the animal level for mating (where adornment shows generally on the male's side): *in the human domain the masquerade has another sense — precisely, to play at the level no longer imaginary but symbolic*. Joan Riviere's concept, met in Lesson IX as scopic lure, is here promoted to its full station: where nothing represents the Other or the sexual difference, the ideals of masculinity and femininity are *symbolic* costumes — womanliness (and virility) as masquerade in the register of the signifier, not display in the register of the image. From here, it remains to show that sexuality as such makes its re-entry and exercises its proper activity *through the intermediary — paradoxical as it seems — of the partial drives*.

**Se faire: the drive's true grammar.** Everything Freud spells of the partial drives shows the circular movement traced on the board — the thrust going out through the erogenous rim to return to it as its target, after rounding the *objet a*. And the thesis, whose test is a punctual examination of the whole text: *it is by this that the subject comes to attain what is, properly speaking, the dimension of the great Other.* The radical distinction: between *loving oneself through the other* — which, in the narcissistic field of the object, leaves no transcendence to the included object — and *the circularity of the drive, in which the heterogeneity of the going and the return shows a gap in its interval*. What have seeing and being-seen in common? Freud opposes *beschauen* — looking at a foreign object, an object properly so called — to *beschaut werden*, being looked at by a foreign *person*: *an object and a person are not the same thing*. At the end of the circle, say, they come loose; the dotted line escapes us somewhat. To bind them, Freud must grip them at the base — where origin and point rejoin — and try to find their union precisely at the point of return: *the root of the scopic drive is to be taken entirely in the subject, in the fact that the subject sees himself*. Only — being Freud, he does not fool himself: it is not seeing oneself in the mirror but *selbst ein Sexualglied beschauen* — "he looks at himself, I will say, in his sexual member." Only — careful\! — that won't do either, for this statement is identified with its inverse, whose humor no one has remarked: *Sexualglied von eigener Person beschaut werden* — "somewhat as the number two rejoices in being odd, the sex, or the willy, rejoices in being looked at. Who has ever really grasped the subjectivable character of such a sentiment?"

In fact the articulation of the loop is obtained by changing *one* of Freud's terms in the last statement: keep *eigenes Objekt* (the object properly so called, which is in fact what the subject reduces himself to), keep *von fremder Person*, keep *beschaut* — but in place of *werden* put *machen*: *what is at stake in the drive is the making-oneself-seen* (*se faire voir*). *The activity of the drive concentrates in this se faire* — and carrying it over to the field of the other drives may give some light. The single-word emendation is the lesson's technical summit: not passive *being seen* (werden) but causative-reflexive *making oneself seen* (machen) — the drive's third time is an *activity* that installs the subject as object for the Other; grammar corrected into structure, and Freud's own joke (the exhibited member "rejoicing") dissolved.

The series, at speed — filling holes Freud left open in his enumeration. After *se faire voir*, one Freud does not even mention: *se faire entendre*, making-oneself-heard — with a structural difference stated in passing: *the ears are, in the field of the unconscious, the only orifice that cannot close*; whereas the making-seen arrow truly returns toward the subject, *the making-heard goes toward the other*. The oral drive: one speaks of devoration-fantasies, being gobbled up — the *autrified* term of the oral drive, bordering all the resonances of masochism; but put things to the wall: since we refer to nursling and breast, and nursing is sucking, *the oral drive is the making-oneself-sucked* (*se faire sucer*) — *it is the vampire*. Which lights up that singular object Lacan strains to unstick from the food-metaphor: *the breast is also something appliquéd* (*plaqué*) — *which sucks what? the organism of the mother*. Sufficiently indicated, at this level, is the subject's claim upon something separated from him but belonging to him, with which he must complete himself. And the anal drive — "a little relaxation here" — seems not to work at all; *and yet se faire chier* — to be bored stiff / to make oneself shat — *has a sense\!* When one says that, one is dealing with the eternal pest (*l'emmerdeur éternel*). One is wrong to identify the famous scybalum simply with its function in the obsessional's metabolism; wrong to amputate it from what it represents, on occasion, of *the gift*, and its relation to defilement, purification, catharsis; wrong not to see that from here comes *the function of oblativity* — "in a word, the object here is not very far from the domain called that of the soul." What does this rapid survey reveal? *Does it not seem that, in the turning-round its pouch represents, the drive, invaginating through the erogenous zone, is charged with going to seek out something that, each time, answers in the Other?* At the level of the *Schaulust*, the gaze — indicated only to treat later the effects on the Other of this movement of appeal. The completed table (se faire voir, entendre, sucer, chier — with the vampire-breast that sucks the mother, and the placenta soon to come) rebuilds all four drives on one syntax: each a way of making oneself an object soliciting the Other, the "call" whose transferential effects are reserved for Part Four.

**The lamella.** Section 4 marks the relation of the drive-cycle's polarity to something always at the center: *an organ, in the sense of instrument, of the drive* — this ungraspable organ, this object we can only round, "this false organ" — now to be interrogated. *The organ of the drive is situated in relation to the true organ.* To make it felt — and to maintain that this is the only pole in the domain of sexuality within our reach of apprehension — Lacan permits himself a *myth*, under the historical sponsorship of what the *Symposium* puts in Aristophanes' mouth concerning the nature of love. The permission is itself framed: to his former hearers he only ever gave "the plough and the ploughshare" — the unconscious made of language — never saying "children, there is a treasure in this field"; three very good works resulted, three and a half years ago. But now: *the treasure can be found only by the way I announce* — and this way *participates in the comic*, absolutely essential to understanding the least Platonic dialogue, a fortiori the *Symposium*: it is, if you like, a *canular*, a student hoax. Aristophanes' fable is a defiance to the centuries — it has crossed them without anyone trying to do better. "I am going to try." The staging deserves its weight: where Aristophanes explains love by the nostalgia of the severed half for its complement, Lacan will explain the libido by another severance altogether — and insists on the comic register as Plato's own, the truth-bearing joke.

So: *I am going to speak to you of the lamella.* To accent the hoax-effect, recall *l'hommelette* — the little-man-omelette, "easier to animate than primordial man, in whose head one must always put a homunculus to make him go." *Each time the membranes of the egg break, from which the foetus about to become a newborn will issue, imagine for an instant that something flies off — which one can do with an egg as well as with a man — namely the hommelette, or the lamella.* The lamella is *something extra-flat, which moves like the amoeba — only a bit more complicated. But it passes everywhere.* And since it has a relation to what the sexed being loses in sexuality, it is — as the amoeba is in relation to sexed beings — *immortal*: it survives every division, subsists through every scissiparous intervention. *And it runs.* Not reassuring: "just suppose it came to envelop your face while you sleep peacefully..." Hard to see how we would not enter into struggle with a being of such properties — not a comfortable struggle. *This lamella, this organ whose characteristic is not to exist but which is nonetheless an organ — I could give you more on its zoological place — is the libido.*

*The libido, qua pure life instinct — that is, immortal life, irrepressible life, life that itself needs no organ, simplified and indestructible life. It is precisely what is subtracted from the living being by its being subject to the cycle of sexed reproduction. And of this, all the enumerable forms of the objet a are the representatives, the equivalents.* The *objets a* are only its representatives, its figures: the breast — as equivocal, as characteristic element of mammalian organization, *the placenta for example* — well represents *that part of himself the individual loses at birth, which can serve to symbolize the most profound lost object*. The same reference could be evoked for all the others. The myth is anti-Aristophanic at the joint: what sexed reproduction cuts from the living being is not its other half (the complementary lover) but *immortal life itself* — the loss is of life, not of a partner — and the *objet a* series (breast-as-placenta first) figures that irreparable subtraction. Whence the answer to Miller's earlier question: the drive-object stands in for a loss constitutive of sexed life as such.

Then the closing construction, joining the myth to the signifier. In the world of the *Real-Ich*, of the ego, of knowledge, everything can exist as now — including you and consciousness — *without there being, whatever one thinks, the least subject*. If the subject is what Lacan teaches — determined by language and speech — *the subject, in initio, begins in the locus of the Other, insofar as there the first signifier arises*. And what is a signifier? *That which represents a subject — for whom? not for another subject, but for another signifier.* To illustrate: discover in the desert a stone covered with hieroglyphs — you do not doubt for an instant that a subject was behind to inscribe them; but to believe each signifier addresses itself *to you* is an error — the proof being that you may understand nothing of them; you define them as signifiers by being sure that *each relates to each of the others*. That is what is at stake in the subject's relation to the field of the Other: *the subject is born insofar as, in the field of the Other, the signifier arises. But by this very fact, that — which before was nothing, if not subject to come — freezes into a signifier* (*se fige en signifiant*). The desert-stone thought-experiment is the axiom's best gloss in the whole seminar: subjecthood is inferred from the inter-referral of marks, not from address — and the subject's birth is simultaneously his petrifaction into the mark, the division Lesson XVI will formalize as alienation.

The relation to the Other is precisely what makes arise for us what the lamella represents — *not the sexed polarity, the relation of masculine to feminine, but the relation of the living subject to what he loses from having to pass, for his reproduction, through the sexual cycle*. Thus is explained *the essential affinity of every drive with the zone of death*, reconciling the two faces of the drive — *which at once presentifies sexuality in the unconscious and represents, in its essence, death*. And thus, too, the unconscious as what opens and closes: *its essence is to mark that time by which, from being born with the signifier, the subject is born divided. The subject is that arising which, just before, as subject, was nothing, but which, scarcely appeared, freezes into a signifier.* On the conjunction of the subject in the field of the drive with the subject as evoked in the field of the Other — on this effort to rejoin — depends that there be *a support for the ganze Sexualstrebung. There is no other. It is only there that the relation of the sexes is represented at the level of the unconscious.* For the rest, the sexual relation is delivered to the hazards of the field of the Other — to the explanations given it — *delivered to the old woman from whom — it is no vain fable — Daphnis must learn how one goes about making love.* Part Three thus closes on its harshest and most consequential result, dressed as pastoral: no drive, no instinct, carries the sexual relation; its only "support" is the precarious junction of drive-circuit and signifier — and beyond that, sex is learned from the Other, like Daphnis from Lycaenion. Heraclitus's bow (Lesson XIV) is paid out in full: sexuality enters the unconscious as death's representative.

**Réponses.** Wahl asks about the loss suffered by the sexed living being, then the activity-passivity articulation. On the first, Lacan grants a lack in his discourse and supplies the join: *the lamella has a border* (*bord*); *it comes to insert itself on the erogenous zone — one of the body's orifices — insofar as these orifices, all our experience shows, are bound to the opening-closing of the gap of the unconscious. The erogenous zones are bound to the unconscious because it is there that the presence of the living is knotted.* The organ of the libido, the lamella, is what binds to the unconscious the oral and anal drives — to which Lacan adds the scopic, and the one that should almost be called *the invocatory drive*, which has, as said incidentally ("nothing I say is pure joking"), *the privilege of not being able to close*. The supplement is structurally essential: the lamella is not free-floating myth — it docks at the rims, so that Lesson XIII's border-topology, Lesson XIV's circuit, and the myth of lost life are one apparatus; and the invocatory drive's unclosable orifice (the ear) marks it, as promised, closest to the unconscious's own unclosable opening. On activity-passivity: at the level of the drive it is *purely grammatical* — support, artifice, employed by Freud to make the drive's going-and-returning graspable — never reducible to reciprocity. At each of the three times, a, b, c, in which Freud articulates each drive, the formula *se faire voir, entendre* and the whole list must be substituted — *which fundamentally implies activity*: rejoining Freud's own distinction of the two fields, drive-field and narcissistic love-field, with reciprocity of loving/being-loved at the level of love, and in the other field *a pure activity durch seine eigenen Triebe for the subject*. "You follow? In fact it leaps to the eye that even in their allegedly passive phase, the exercise of a drive — masochistic for example — demands that the masochist give himself, if I may put it so, *un mal de chien*" — a dog's own trouble. The final joke seals the doctrine of *se faire*: there is no passive drive; even suffering is an accomplishment — the masochist labors at his making-himself-object — activity all the way down, and love alone reciprocal.

## **The Field of the Other, and Back to the Transference**

### **XVI. The Subject and the Other: Alienation**

*27 May 1964\.* Part Four opens with the first of the two logical operations by which the subject is constituted in the field of the Other — alienation, formalized on the set-theoretic operation of union and the forced choice of the *vel*. Miller's keywords: *the sexual dynamic; aphanisis; the Piagetic error; your money or your life\!; the "why?".* The lesson also introduces its twin, separation, which Lesson XVII will complete.

The opening answers an old objection: if psychoanalysis is to constitute itself as science of the unconscious, one must start from the unconscious structured like a language — from which Lacan deduced a topology whose end is to account for *the constitution of the subject*. It was objected, "in a time I hope surpassed," that giving structure the dominant role neglects the *dynamic* so present in our experience — even eluding the Freudian principle that this dynamic is in essence, through and through, *sexual*. He hopes the year's course, at its culmen last time, shows that the dynamic loses nothing there.

**Recapitulation: the two lacks.** The new element added to the dynamic: the opposition, at the entry of the unconscious, of the two fields of the subject and the Other. *The Other is the locus where the chain of the signifier is situated, commanding everything that will be able to presentify itself of the subject; it is the field of that living being in which the subject has to appear.* On the side of that living being, the drive manifests itself essentially — every drive being, by essence of drive, partial; none represents the totality of the *Sexualstrebung* as presentifying in the psyche the function of *Fortpflanzung*, reproduction. On the biological plane, who would not admit that function? What is affirmed after Freud, who witnesses it in every way, is that *it is not represented as such in the psyche. In the psyche there is nothing by which the subject could situate himself as male being or female being.* The subject situates only *equivalents* — activity and passivity, far from representing it exhaustively; Freud even adds the irony that this representation is neither so thoroughgoing nor so exclusive — *durchgreifend und ausschliesslich* — male/female being represented only by activity (manifested through the *Triebe*) and passivity (passivity only toward the exterior, *gegen die äusseren Reize*). Only this division makes necessary what analytic experience first brought to light: *the ways of what one must do as man or as woman are entirely abandoned to the drama, to the scenario, placed in the field of the Other — which is properly the Oedipus*. What one must do as man or woman, the human being always has to learn *entirely from the Other* — Daphnis's old woman again: a last field, that of sexual accomplishment, whose paths the innocent finally does not know.

That the partial drive alone orients him there — sole representative in the psyche of the consequences of sexuality — is the sign that *sexuality represents itself in the psyche by a relation of the subject deduced from something other than sexuality itself. Sexuality installs itself in the field of the subject by the way of lack.* And *two lacks here overlap* (*se recouvrent*). One arises from the central defect around which turns the dialectic of the subject's advent to his own being in the relation to the Other — the fact that the subject depends on the signifier, and the signifier is first in the field of the Other. This lack takes up *the other lack, the real, anterior lack, to be situated at the advent of the living being — at sexed reproduction: what the living being loses of its portion of life in reproducing itself by the sexed way*. This lack is *real* because it relates to something real: *the living being, from being subject to sex, has fallen under the blow of individual death*. The architecture of the whole Part is here in two sentences: a signifying lack (the subject's aphanisis under the signifier) superimposed on a real lack (the mortality that sex imports) — Lesson XV's lamella now the second term of a formal pair.

Whence the Aristophanes-correction restated as doctrine: the pursuit of the complement knots the image pathetically *and delusively* — it is the other, the sexual half, that the living being seeks in love, says the myth; analytic experience substitutes *the search by the subject not of the sexual complement, but of the part of himself forever lost*, constituted by the fact that he is only a sexed living being and is no longer immortal. And therefore — for the same reason that the sexed living being is induced to its sexual realization *by the lure* — *the drive, the partial drive, is fundamentally death drive* (*pulsion de mort*), *and represents in itself the portion of death in the sexed living being*. The famous identification (partial drive \= death drive) thus arrives not as paradox-mongering but as a corollary: since what the drive circles is the lost immortal life, every drive commemorates the death that sex installed — Freud's dualism of Eros and Thanatos re-soldered from the side of the lamella.

The libido, accordingly, is designated not as a field of forces but as *an organ* — and this organ is *irreal* (*irréel*). *Irreal is not imaginary: the irreal is defined by articulating itself to the real in a way that escapes us, and that is precisely what necessitates that its representation be mythical, as we make it. But being irreal does not prevent an organ from incarnating itself.* Materialization at once: one of the most ancient forms incarnating this irreal organ in the body is *the tattoo, the scarification*. *The gash has quite properly the function of being for the Other* — situating the subject in it, marking his place in the field of the group's relations — *and at the same time it has an evidently erotic function, perceived by all who have approached its reality.* The unary trait of Lesson XI (the first tattoo) is thus rejoined from the side of the drive: the cut in the flesh is at once signifier-for-the-Other and libidinal organ incarnate — the two lacks stitched in one mark. And a last recapitulating precision: in the drive's movement, the arrow that leaves for the target fulfills its function only by really emanating from it and returning upon the subject; *the pervert is he who, in short-circuit, more directly than any other, succeeds his stroke, integrating most profoundly his function as subject with his existence as desire*. The drive's reversion is altogether other than the ambivalence-variation that passes the object from hate's field to love's according to whether it profits the subject's well-being: *one does not become masochistic because the object is no good for one's aim; Freud's young homosexual does not become homosexual because her father disappoints her — she could have taken a lover. Each time we are in the dialectic of the drive, something else commands.* The drive's dialectic distinguishes itself fundamentally from what is of the order of love as from what is of the subject's good — the sorting of Lesson XV enforced against every utilitarian or frustration-based reading of object-choice.

**The signifier's circle without reciprocity.** Everything arises from the structure of the signifier, founded on the function first called the *cut* and now articulated as the topological function of the *border*. The subject's relation to the Other is engendered entirely in a process of gap — without which everything could be there: relations between beings in the real, "up to and including you who are there," could be engendered in terms of inversely reciprocal relations; that is what psychology and a whole sociology strive for, and they can succeed where only the animal domain is concerned, imaginary capture sufficing to motivate all sorts of living behavior. Psychoanalysis recalls that human psychology belongs to another dimension — which the philosophical way would have sufficed to maintain, had it not proved insufficient for lack of a sufficient definition of the unconscious: *the facts of human psychology cannot be conceived in the absence of the function of the subject defined as the effect of the signifier*. The processes here are circular between subject and Other — from the subject called to the Other, to the subject from having seen himself appear in the Other's field, from the Other returning — *circular, but, by nature, without reciprocity: for being circular, it is dissymmetrical*. The lesson's logical program is set: to construct the precise asymmetry — against Piaget's "reciprocity," against every intersubjective mirror — of a circle that never exchanges its poles.

The ambiguity of the *sign* is that it represents something *for someone* — and this someone can be many things, the whole universe even, since we are told information circulates there as the negative of entropy; any node where signs concentrate can be taken for a someone. Against this: *a signifier is what represents a subject for another signifier*. The signifier, produced in the field of the Other, makes the subject of its signification arise — *but it functions as signifier only by reducing the subject-in-instance to being no more than a signifier, petrifying him by the same movement in which it calls him to function, to speak, as subject*. There, properly, is the temporal pulsation in which is instituted the characteristic of the departure of the unconscious as such — *closure*. The two definitions (sign-for-someone vs. signifier-for-another-signifier), and the desert-hieroglyphs of Lesson XV, here receive their dynamic consequence: the very operation that summons the subject to speak petrifies him — call and eclipse in one stroke — and this pulsation *is* the opening-closing of the unconscious that Lesson II described phenomenally.

An analyst sensed this at another level and tried to signify it in a term never since exploited: *aphanisis*, disappearance. Jones, its inventor, took it for something rather absurd — *the fear of seeing desire disappear*. But aphanisis is to be situated more radically, *at the level where the subject manifests himself in that movement of disappearance which I have qualified as lethal* — in another way, *the fading of the subject*. The correction of Jones is surgical: not a fear the subject has (of losing desire) but the movement the subject *is* — disappearance as the subject's mode of manifestation under the signifier.

Illustration that this mainspring directs concrete observation and lifts its blindnesses: *the Piagetic error* — "for those who might think this a neologism, I stress that it concerns Monsieur Piaget" — the error lodged in the notion of the child's *egocentric discourse*, defined as the stage lacking what "this Alpine psychology" calls reciprocity. Reciprocity is far from the horizon of what should concern us; the notion of egocentric discourse is a contresens. The child in this famous discourse — tape-recordable — does not speak *for himself*, as is said. No doubt he does not address the other, on the theoretical repartition of the *I* and the *you* — *but there must be others there*: it is while they are there, the little ones, all together at their operation-games (as in certain "active-education" methods), that they speak — addressing no one in particular: *they speak, if you permit me the word, à la cantonade* — to the wings, to whom it may concern. *This egocentric discourse is a "let him hear who has ears\!"* (*à bon entendeur, salut\!*). The observation is re-read exactly: what Piaget's reciprocity-scale scores as pre-social self-talk is speech *addressed to the field of the Other as such* — no particular you, but the locus — the child already installed in the structure the lesson is formalizing.

Whence the constitution of the subject in the Other's field, as the little arrow on the board designates: seized at his birth in that field, *the characteristic of the subject of the unconscious is to be, beneath the signifier that develops its networks, its chains and its history, at an indeterminate place*. More than one dream-element — almost all — can be the point where we diversely situate him in interpretation. When one believes one can make the dream say anything one wants, one has understood nothing — "it must be said that psychoanalysts do not explain themselves very well": *interpretation is not pliable to any sense; it designates only a single sequence of signifiers. But the subject can in effect occupy diverse places, according as one puts him under one or another of these signifiers.* The two claims must be held together, for together they answer the hermeneutic suspicion (Lesson XII's Ricoeur, Lesson X's Szasz): the signifying chain of an interpretation is univocal — no arbitrariness — while the subject's *place* in it is mobile; rigor about the chain, indeterminacy of the subject: exactly the pair alienation will formalize.

**The vel of alienation.** The relation in question — border-process, circular process — is supported by the little lozenge \[◊\] used as algorithm in the graph, precisely because it must be integrated into some finished products of this dialectic: impossible not to integrate it into the fantasy itself — $◊a — or into that radical knot where demand and drive conjoin, $◊D, "which one could call the cry." Keep to the lozenge: it is *a border, a functioning border*, given a vectorial direction (counter-clockwise, settled by the fact that in our writing one reads left to right). "Careful\! these are supports for your thought, not without artifice — but there is no topology that does not require some artifice to support it: precisely the result of the fact that the subject depends on the signifier — in other words, of a certain impotence of your thought." The small *v* of the lozenge's lower half is the *vel* of the first operation. Perhaps rather silly things — "but logic always is a bit. If one does not go to the root of the silly, one is infallibly precipitated into idiocy (*la connerie*)" — witness the alleged antinomies of reason, the catalogue of all catalogues that do not contain themselves, which gives logicians vertigo no one knows why; the solution is very simple: *the signifier with which one designates the same signifier is evidently not the same signifier as the one by which one designates the other — it leaps to the eye*. The word *obsolete*, insofar as it can signify that the word *obsolete* is itself obsolete, is not the same word on one side and the other. Russell's paradox is dispatched in a sentence — by the use/mention split that is, again, the enunciation/statement split of Lesson XI — and the dispatch is not a digression: it licenses the third *vel* about to be defined, a logical novelty grounded in the signifier's non-self-identity.

The *vel* of the first essential operation founding the subject: *alienation*. "This alienation, my God, one cannot say it does not circulate these days — whatever one does, one is always a bit more alienated, in economics, politics, psycho-pathology, aesthetics, and so on. It might not be a bad thing to see in what the root of this famous alienation consists." Does it mean — what Lacan seems to maintain — that the subject is condemned to see himself arise *in initio* only in the field of the Other? "That could be it. Well — not at all, not at all, not at all." *Alienation consists in this vel which — if the word "condemned" raises no objection — condemns the subject to appear only in that division articulated just now: if he appears on one side as meaning (sens), produced by the signifier, on the other he appears as aphanisis.* The triple "not at all" deserves attention: alienation is not the banal thesis of social determination (the subject as creature of the Other's field) — that reading is refused — but a *choice-structure*, a division internal to the subject's very apparition.

A *vel* worth illustrating, to differentiate it from the two known uses of *or*: the exhaustive (I go here *or* there — choosing one excludes the other) and the equivalential (one side or the other, who cares). There is a third — and symbolic logic, useful for its clarities, teaches the distinction needed: the operation called *union* (*réunion*). Adding two collections is not uniting them: five objects in one circle, five in the other — added, ten; but if two belong to both, *the union will contain only eight*. Childish to recall — but it gives the notion that *this vel is supported only by the logical form of union*. *The vel of alienation is defined by a choice whose properties depend on this: that there is, in the union, one element comporting that whatever choice is made, its consequence is a neither-one-nor-the-other* (*ni l'un, ni l'autre*). *The choice is only of knowing whether one means to keep one of the parts, the other disappearing in any case.*

Illustrated on what interests us — *the being of the subject, the one who is there beneath the meaning*: *If we choose being, the subject disappears, escapes us, falls into non-meaning; if we choose meaning, the meaning survives only shorn* (*écorné*) *of that portion of non-meaning which is, properly speaking, what constitutes, in the realization of the subject, the unconscious.* In other terms: it is of the nature of this meaning, as it emerges in the field of the Other, to be, in a great part of its field, *eclipsed by the disappearance of being, induced by the very function of the signifier*. The Venn diagram on the board (Being ∪ Meaning, with the lunule of non-meaning) is the seminar's most transmissible formalization, and its exact reading matters: the choice is forced — being alone is mute non-sense, so meaning must be chosen — and the chosen meaning arrives amputated, the shorn crescent being *the unconscious itself*. The unconscious is the price of speaking: the portion of non-meaning subtracted from every meaning the subject can have.

A direct implication, "only too unperceived — an evidence one does not see": *alienation has the consequence that interpretation does not have its last mainspring in delivering the significations of the way the psychic travels. That bearing is only prelude. Interpretation aims not so much at meaning as at reducing the signifiers to their non-meaning* (*non-sens*) *so that we can refind the determinants of the subject's whole conduct.* See Leclaire at Bonneval, applying these theses: he isolated the *Licorne* sequence not — as the discussion believed — in its significative dependence but precisely in its *irreducible and senseless character as a chain of signifiers*. The technical consequence is the lesson's sharpest yield, and it inverts the hermeneutic picture of analysis for good: interpretation ends not in richer sense but in the exposure of the senseless signifying skeleton ("Poord'jeli," Leclaire's unicorn-formula) that determines the subject — the crescent of non-meaning reached through the meaning, not covered by it.

And this alienating *or* is no arbitrary invention, no *vue de l'esprit*: *it is in language. This "or" exists* — linguistics should distinguish it too. Example at once: *Your money or your life\!* (*La bourse ou la vie\!*) *If I choose the money, I lose both. If I choose life, I have life without the money — namely, a life shorn.* "I see I have made myself sufficiently understood." And the pedigree: it is in *Hegel* that the appellation "alienating vel" finds legitimate justification — economizing our strokes, it is a matter of engendering *the first alienation, that by which man enters the way of slavery: Freedom or life\!* If he chooses freedom — *couic\!* — he loses both immediately; if he chooses life, he has life amputated of freedom. The two apologues calibrate the concept exactly: the mugger's alternative and the master-slave struggle share the same defective disjunction — one term (life) is the *necessary* term whose loss carries off everything, the other is forfeit in every case — and Lacan's subject stands to meaning as Hegel's slave to life: surviving, at the price of the shorn part.

Something particular must be in this — call it *the lethal factor*: present in certain repartitions shown by the play of signifiers at the heart of life itself — "they are called chromosomes, and it happens that among them there is one with a lethal function." Its control-case: bring death itself into one of the fields. *Freedom or death\!* Here, because death enters play, an effect of slightly different structure occurs: *in both cases I shall have both*. Freedom, after all, is like the famous freedom of labor for which the French Revolution fought — it can just as well be the freedom to die of hunger, which is what it led to throughout the nineteenth century, whence certain principles have since needed revision. Choose freedom: it is the freedom to die. And the curious thing: *in the conditions where one says freedom or death, the only proof of freedom you can give, in the conditions indicated, is precisely to choose death — for there you demonstrate that you have the freedom of choice.* A Hegelian moment too — *the Terror*. The variant is not an ornament: substituting death for the forfeitable term flips the structure (both lost either way, or both "had" in a grim sense), and the Terror's logic — freedom provable only in dying — exhibits the lethal factor naked: the vel's asymmetry is a death-function lodged in the signifying repartition itself, as a lethal gene in the chromosome's combinatory.

**Separation, introduced.** The second operation, given the late hour, is only introduced. It completes the circularity of the subject's relation to the Other, *but an essential torsion is demonstrated in it*. Where the first time is founded on the substructure of *union*, the second is founded on *intersection* — the product: the elements belonging to both sets — situated precisely in that same lunule where the form of the gap, the border, is refound. This second operation is as essential as the first, *because it is there that we shall see the field of the transference point* (*poindre*). Its name, the second new term: *separation* (*la séparation*). And at once the philology, which is a program: *separare, to separate* — to the equivocation of *se parare*, to dress oneself, to defend oneself, to furnish oneself with what is needed to be on guard; and further — the Latinists authorize it — to *se parere*, *to engender oneself*, to procure oneself. And how, from this level, does the subject have to *procure* himself? "There is the origin of the word that in Latin designates *to engender*. It is juridical — as, curiously, are all the Indo-European words designating bringing into the world: *parturition* itself originates in a word whose root means nothing else than *to procure a child for the husband* — a juridical, and let us say social, operation." The etymological chain (separare → se parare → se parere) compresses the coming doctrine: the subject's second operation is at once a parting, a self-equipping, and a *self-engendering* — and even birth, in the language's memory, is a juridical act in the field of the Other.

The mechanism, sketched: separation arises *from the overlapping of two lacks*. The subject encounters a lack *in the Other*, in the very intimation the Other's discourse makes to him: in the intervals of that discourse there arises, radically locatable in the child's experience, *he says that to me, but what does he want?* In this interval cutting the signifiers — part of the very structure of the signifier — is the lodging of what in other registers was called metonymy: *there crawls, there slides, there flees, like a ferret, what we call desire. The desire of the Other is apprehended by the subject in what does not fit* (*ce qui ne colle pas*), *in the lacks of the discourse of the Other* — *and all the child's "why?"s testify less to an avidity for the reason of things than they constitute a putting-to-the-test of the adult: a "why are you telling me that?" ever re-suscitated from its fund, which is the enigma of the adult's desire.* The child's questioning, psychology's emblem of curiosity, is re-read as the probe of the Other's want — the interval between signifiers as the burrow of desire.

Now, to answer this hold, the subject — "like Gribouille" (the fool who jumps in the river to escape the rain) — *brings the answer of the antecedent lack, of his own disappearance, which he here situates at the point of the lack perceived in the Other. The first object he proposes to that parental desire whose object is unknown is his own loss — Can he lose me?* (*Veut-il me perdre?*) *The fantasy of his death, of his disappearance, is the first object the subject has to put in play in this dialectic — and he does put it in play, as we know by a thousand facts, were it only anorexia nervosa.* We know too that the child commonly agitates the fantasy of his own death in his love-relations with his parents. *One lack covers the other* (*un manque recouvre l'autre*): the dialectic of the objects of desire, making the joint of the subject's desire with the Other's desire — "I told you long ago it was the same" — passes through this: *that it is not answered directly. A lack engendered by the preceding time serves to answer the lack raised by the following time.* The formula gathers the whole operation in advance of its formalization: to the enigma "what does the Other want?" the subject answers with his own aphanisis — offering his disappearance (can he lose me?) as the object of the Other's opaque desire — lack laid upon lack in the lunule; and anorexia (Lesson VIII's eating-the-nothing) stands as its clinic. The session closes marking the two elements advanced in this "new and fundamental logical operation": *non-reciprocity, and the torsion in the return*.

**Réponses.** Miller: does Lacan not mean to show that the alienation of a subject *born in, constituted by, and ordered to a field exterior to him* is radically distinct from the alienation of a self-consciousness — in short, must one not understand: *Lacan against Hegel?* — "That is very well said — just the contrary of what Green said to me a moment ago, coming up and squeezing my hand, at least morally: *death of structuralism, you are the son of Hegel*. I do not agree. I think that in saying *Lacan against Hegel* you are much closer to the truth — though of course it is not at all a philosophical debate." Green, from the floor: *"The sons kill the fathers\!"* The exchange is the right coda and states the demarcation the lesson's Hegel-borrowings require: the vel is taken *from* Hegel (the master-slave forced choice) and turned *against* him — for the Hegelian alienation of self-consciousness is a moment destined for dialectical recovery, while the Lacanian alienation is constitutive and unrecoverable: no Aufhebung returns the shorn crescent; the subject does not re-appropriate his being from the Other. Green's parting shot — sons killing fathers — lands better than he perhaps intended: it names the transferential structure (Lesson XII's apostles) in which the whole scene, Lacan included, is caught.

### **XVII. The Subject and the Other (II): Aphanisis**

*3 June 1964\.* Miller's keywords: *the question of the Vorstellungsrepräsentanz; freedom; representation and the Hegelian lure; the desire of Descartes; scepticism, certainty, and the sujet supposé savoir; the little letters; the value of the Pavlovian experiment.* The lesson completes the alienation-separation machinery by identifying the *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz* as the *binary signifier* whose fall constitutes primal repression; settles the Hegel-question in passing; and gives the seminar's fullest reading of Descartes, at whose center the term everything has been waiting for is minted for the analytic field: *the sujet supposé savoir is the analyst.*
