The prelude gilds a maxim of method: "when I told you, at the start of our meetings, *I do not seek, I find*" — Picasso's phrase — it meant that in Freud's field one has only to bend down to pick up what is there to be found: *nachträglich*, neglected in its real scope though lying in plain sight; the *einziger Zug*, the unary trait, to someone's surprise. Today's find: the importance, already designated on last session's schema, of what Freud calls, at the level of repression, the *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz* — with a philological glance at the linking *s* German inserts against normal declension: two terms, *Vorstellung* and *Repräsentanz*.

**Alienation refrased; the translation quarrel.** The alienating vel can be re-articulated: *no X without Y — but no Y with X*. The slave's dialectic: no freedom without life — but there will be no life *with* freedom for him. From one to the other a necessary condition — and this necessary condition becomes precisely *the sufficient reason that causes the loss of the original demand*. And perhaps this occurs also in some of those who follow Lacan: no way to follow him without passing through his signifiers — but passing through his signifiers comports that feeling of alienation which incites them to seek, per Freud's formula, *the little difference*. Unfortunately the little difference makes them lose the bearing of the direction shown. Thus the affair he would gladly have spared himself: someone — "more or less animated by concern for the privileges of university authority" — presumed to correct his translation of *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz*. Lacan had insisted, reading *Die Verdrängung* (the article following "The Unconscious" in the metapsychological series), that Freud underlines *it is not the affect that is repressed* — the affect goes wandering elsewhere, where it can ("there will always be enough professors of psychology to justify, with the patient, its taking on sense precisely where it is no longer in its place") — and that what is repressed is not the represented of desire, the signification, but *the representative* — translated literally — *of the representation* (*le représentant de la représentation*). The correctors say: the *representative* representative (*représentant représentatif*). Seemingly nothing — but a just-published little book on psychosomatics builds on it an argument about some alleged misrecognition in "what must be called my theory of desire," footnoting an unfindable passage of two students to the effect that they make desire the representative representative of *need*; and the book's *sole pertinent remark* is its retort: *we would rather say that desire is the non-representative representative*. "Now that is precisely what I mean, and what I say — for what I mean, I say — in translating *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz* by representative of the representation." The quarrel is not vanity: everything turns on whether the repressed element *represents* (depicts, resembles, carries the sense of) anything. Lacan's genitive reading — the delegate *of* representation, standing in its place without resembling it — makes the repressed a pure signifier; the rejected reading smuggles representation back in, and with it desire as a picture of need.

**The binary signifier.** Localization on the schema of the original mechanisms of alienation: *the subject appears first in the Other, insofar as the first signifier, the unary signifier, arises in the field of the Other and represents the subject for another signifier — which other signifier has as its effect the aphanisis of the subject.* Whence the division of the subject: appearing somewhere as meaning, he manifests himself elsewhere as fading, as disappearance. *There is, then, so to speak, a matter of life and death between the unary signifier and the subject qua binary signifier, cause of his disappearance. The Vorstellungsrepräsentanz is the binary signifier.* And *this signifier constitutes the central point of the Urverdrängung* — of what, having passed into the unconscious, will be, as Freud indicates, the point of *Anziehung*, the point of attraction by which all the other repressions will be possible, all the other similar passages to the place of the *unterdrückt*, of what has passed underneath as signifier. The axiom of Lesson XV thus receives its dynamic completion: S1 represents the subject for S2 — and S2, the binary signifier, is exactly Freud's *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz*: the signifier whose falling-under (primal repression) is one and the same event as the subject's aphanisis, and which thereafter, from below, magnetizes every later repression. Metapsychology's most obscure term is soldered to the alienation-diagram.

The way back from the vel is *separation*: by it the subject finds *the weak point of the primitive couple of the signifying articulation, insofar as it is alienating in essence. It is in the interval between the two signifiers that lies the desire offered to the subject's locating, in the experience of the discourse of the Other — of the first Other he deals with: say, to illustrate, the mother. It is insofar as her desire is beyond or short of what she says, intimates, makes arise as meaning — insofar as her desire is unknown — it is at this point of lack that the desire of the subject is constituted.* And the return has its torsion: by a process "not without deception," what the subject refinds is not what animates his movement of refinding — he returns to the initial point, *that of his lack as such, of the lack of his aphanisis*. The consequences for the cure itself — the torsion essential to integrating *the phase of exit from the transference* — are reserved; for now, the essential of desire's function: *it is insofar as the subject comes to play his part in separation that the binary signifier, the Vorstellungsrepräsentanz, is unterdrückt, fallen underneath.* The circuit is now complete and can be said in one breath: alienation installs the subject under S1 at the price of S2's fall (and his own fading); separation attacks the interval between S1 and S2, where the Other's desire slips — and playing one's part there is what *represses* the binary signifier. Desire and primal repression are two faces of a single operation on the interval.

Whence, "however metaphysical it may seem," a light on the term technique uses as if it went without saying — *to liberate something*: what plays out here is that term deserving the qualification of *phantom* — *freedom*. *What the subject has to free himself from is the aphanisic effect of the binary signifier* — and looking closely, nothing else is at stake in the function of freedom. Not for nothing did the two most evident supports of the vel structure the position of the *slave* and the *master*. The slave: freedom-or-life resolves into no-freedom-without-life — life forever shorn of freedom. But look further: *the master's alienation is structured in exactly the same way. For if Hegel indicates that the master's status is instituted in the struggle to the death of pure prestige, it is indeed because it is by making his choice pass through death that the master, too, constitutes his fundamental alienation.* One might say death is no more spared the master than the slave — he will have it at the end, there is the limit of his freedom. Too little: that death is not the death constitutive of his alienating choice, the death of the struggle of pure prestige. *The revelation of the master's essence manifests itself at the moment of the Terror, when it is to him that one says freedom or death — and he evidently has only death to choose, to have freedom.* And the supreme image of the master: Sygne de Coûfontaine, of the Claudelian tragedy long commented in an earlier seminar — she who would abandon nothing of the master's register, and whom the values she sacrifices to bring, beyond her sacrifice, only the necessity of renouncing, to her depths, *her very being*: illustrating, at the end, *the radical alienation of freedom in the master himself*. The page settles accounts with Kojève's Hegel from inside: the master is not the vel's winner but its other case — alienated *through* death as the slave through life — and Claudel's heroine (forced to marry her family's betrayer, dying with the facial tic that says no) stands as the proof that even absolute fidelity to the master's register ends in the forced choice against one's own being. Freedom, phantom in politics, is real in analysis only as the work against the aphanisic effect of S2.

**Repräsentanz: the diplomat.** Need it be accented how *Repräsentanz* is to be heard? — as things happen at the real level where communication is effected in every human domain: *these representatives are what we commonly call, for example, the representative of France*. What do diplomats do when they dialogue? Each functions toward the other as *pure representative*, and above all *their proper signification must not intervene*: each is to register only what the other transmits in his pure function as signifier, taking no account of what the other is as presence, as man, more or less congenial. *Interpsychology is an impurity in this game.* The signifier is to be registered as such — *at the opposite pole from signification*. Signification enters play in the *Vorstellung*: with representation we are in psychology, the world's objects taken in charge within a subject's parenthesis where a whole series a, a′, a″ unrolls — the subjectivity from which the theory of knowledge is suspended. Every representation requires a subject, but *this subject is never a pure subject*: if one believes each subject sustains himself in the world with his original *Weltanschauung*, then the road of truth passes — as a backward psycho-sociology still indicates — through the survey, the totalization, the statistics of world-views. "And things could be so, if there were in the world subjects, each charged with representing certain conceptions of the world." There, moreover, is *the essential flaw of philosophical idealism* — untenable, and never radically maintained: *there is no subject without, somewhere, aphanisis of the subject, and it is in this alienation, this fundamental division, that the dialectic of the subject is instituted.* The diplomat-apologue is the best gloss the *Repräsentanz* will get: an ambassador counts not by his qualities but by his place in a formal exchange — as the repressed signifier counts without resembling — and idealism's "flaw" is precisely to build on representing subjects while the subject, divided, is never simply there to represent.

And the Hegel-question answered on the way: does it not suffice to say that, by the fact of the vel — sensitive point, balance point — *there is no arising of the subject at the level of meaning except from his aphanisis in the Other place, that of the unconscious*; and that *this comports no mediation*? Lacan wagers, if provoked, to show that the effective experience inaugurated in the aim of absolute knowledge never brings us anything that could illustrate the Hegelian vision of successive syntheses — nothing even promising the moment Hegel obscurely ties to that stage, pleasantly illustrated under the title *the Sunday of life* (Queneau's novel), *when no opening would remain gaping at the heart of the subject*. And the source of the *Hegelian lure* must be indicated: *it is included in the Cartesian "I think"* — the inaugural point that introduces into history, into our experience, into our necessity, the vel of alienation, forever preventing its misrecognition: *in the Cartesian step the vel was taken for the first time as the constituent of the dialectic of the subject, thenceforth ineliminable in its radical foundation.* Lesson XVI's "Lacan against Hegel" here gets its argument: no mediation, no Aufhebung, no Sunday of life — the gap does not close; and Hegel's entire dialectic is genealogically derived from (and convicted by) the Cartesian vel it tried to sublate.

**Descartes' desire; the sujet supposé savoir.** What distinguishes the Cartesian step from the ancient search for *epistēmē* — and from the scepticism that was one of its terms — is to be articulated from the double function of alienation and separation. What does Descartes seek? *Certainty.* "I have," he says, "*an extreme desire to learn to distinguish the true from the false* — underline *desire* — *to see clearly — in what? — in my actions, and to walk with assurance in this life.*" Something quite other than the aim of knowledge: not the step of a dialectician or a professor, still less of a cavalier. His biography is marked above all by his wandering in the world, his encounters, his secret design — *larvatus prodeo*, I advance masked; and Lacan notes it (though holding biography secondary to a work's sense) because Descartes himself underlines that his path is essential to communicating his method: not, like Bacon shortly before, the general means of conducting one's reason, but *his* method, begun in that direction with the desire to distinguish true from false in order to see clearly *in my actions* — adding that if his path seems no good to others, that is their affair; let them glean what they can. Thus the Cartesian project is re-read under the seminar's own categories: its motor is a *desire* (of certainty), its register the act and the singular path, not the accumulation of *savoir* — Descartes as the first case of the very structure (desire, certainty, the field of knowledge set aside) that analysis inherits.

Is the weight of knowledge absent? Of course not — he begins there: knowledge to spare, there always is, there still is. Formed by the best professors, out of La Flèche, pupil of the Jesuits — no lack of knowledge, or of sapience. And dare one say it is precisely from his exit from the Jesuits that he carries the acute sentiment of that overabundance? Is there not, at the heart of what is transmitted through a certain humanist wisdom, something like a hidden *perinde ac cadaver* — not where it is usually put, in the alleged death demanded by Ignatius's rule ("these Jesuits, as I see them from outside, seem to me always rather present, even rather frisky") — no: *the death in question is the one hidden behind the very notion of humanism, at the quick of every humanist consideration. Even in that term one tries to animate under the title of human sciences, there is something we shall call a corpse in the cupboard* (*un cadavre dans le placard*). The aside is a thesis in passing, aimed at 1964's institutional battles over the "sciences humaines": humanism transmits, under its plenitude of knowledge, a cadaver — the dead subject of accumulated *savoir* — and Descartes' новизна was to walk out of that plenitude.

His new way was not refutation of uncertain knowledges — he lets them run in peace, and with them all the rules of social life. Around him, at this inaugural moment of the subject's emergence, swarm the *libertins* — "like the other term of the vel of alienation": in reality Pyrrhonians, sceptics, named by Pascal though without sufficient freedom of accent. And a rectification of what scepticism is: *not* the successive, enumerable doubting of all opinions and all roads of knowledge, but *the holding of the subjective position: one can know nothing*. Montaigne — to be distinguished — is truly the one centered not on a scepticism but *on the living moment of the aphanisis of the subject*: fecund in that, an eternal guide, beyond his historical turning-point. But scepticism proper *is something we no longer know. Scepticism is an ethics. It is a mode of sustaining man in life that implies a position so difficult, so heroic, that we can no longer even imagine it* — perhaps precisely because of the passage found by Descartes, *which leads the search for the road of certainty to the very point of the vel of alienation, to which there is only one exit — the way of desire.* The taxonomy is exact and consequential: ancient scepticism was an *ethical stance* on the vel (holding the position of not-knowing as a way of life); Montaigne lived the fading itself; Descartes converted the impasse into a *desire* — and thereby founded the subject whose only exit from alienation is desire, the subject analysis receives.

This desire of certainty ends, for Descartes, only in doubt — but choosing this path led him to operate *a most singular separation*. Certainty, for Descartes, is not an acquired moment: *it must be, each time, by each one, repeated. It is an ascesis* — a point of orientation peculiarly difficult to sustain in the sharpness that makes its value; properly speaking, *the instauration of something separated*. His error, one could say, is to believe it a knowledge — to say he *knows something* of this certainty, not making the "I think" a mere vanishing-point. But he did something else, concerning the unnamed field where all those suspended knowledges wander: *he put the field of these knowledges at the level of that vaster subject, the subject supposed to know* (*le sujet supposé savoir*) — *God*. Descartes could only reintroduce His presence — "but in what a singular way\!" Here the question of the eternal truths: to be assured no deceiving God faces him, he must pass through the medium of a God — less a perfect than an *infinite* being. Does Descartes then remain hooked, as everyone till him, on the requirement that all search for science be guaranteed by actual science existing somewhere, in an existing being called God — *that is, by God's being supposed to know*?

"It may seem I am taking you far from the field of our experience — and yet, I recall it here both to excuse myself and to hold your attention at the level of our experience: *the subject supposed to know, in analysis, is the analyst.*" Next time: how it happens that *we* have no need of the idea of a perfect and infinite being — "who would dream of attributing those dimensions to his analyst?" — for the function of the *sujet supposé savoir* to be introduced. The seminar's most influential coinage thus enters not as a theory of the analyst but as a *Cartesian inheritance*: the structural place of the supposed knower — occupied by God to guarantee the field of knowledge — is what the analysand's certainty-seeking installs the analyst in; the joke about perfection marks the difference to be explained (a supposition without attributes) and Lesson XVIII's task is set.

**The little letters.** How does Descartes rid himself of his supposed knower? *By his voluntarism, the primacy given to God's will* — "assuredly one of the most extraordinary fencing-strokes ever delivered in the history of the mind: the eternal truths are eternal because God wills them so." Appreciate the elegance: a whole portion of the truths — precisely the eternal ones — is left in God's charge. Descartes means, and says, that if two and two make four it is because God wills it, quite simply: *it is His affair. And it is true that it is His affair — two and two making four is not something self-evident without His presence.* Illustration: between clear and confused, simple and complex ideas, Descartes' method sets *the order to be followed*. It is quite possible, after all, that one plus one plus one plus one *not* make four — and the vel of alienation is exactly the example: in the cardinal order it would give something like 1 \+ (1 \+ (1 \+ (1 \+ ...))) — *each time a new term is introduced, one or more of the others risks slipping through our fingers*. To arrive at four, what matters is not the cardinal but the *ordinal*: a first mental operation, then a second, a third, a fourth — miss the order and you miss them; whether at the end it makes three, four or two is relatively secondary: *that is God's affair.* The arithmetic parable encodes the subject-theory: under the regime of the vel, counting loses terms (the fading subject cannot hold his own units — the "one one" of Lesson XI); what the subject *can* secure is order, succession, the pure before-and-after of operations — the cardinal total being consigned to the Other.

What Descartes introduces — illustrated at once, since with the *Discourse* come his geometry and dioptrics — is this: *he substitutes the little letters a, b, c of his algebra for the big ones. The big letters are, if you like, the letters of the Hebrew alphabet with which God created the world — which have a reverse side, and to each of which corresponds a number. The difference of Descartes' little letters is that they have no number, that they are interchangeable, and that only the order of commutations will define their process.* And that number already implies the Other's presence: the number-series can be figured only by introducing *zero*, more or less covertly — *and zero is the presence of the subject who, at that level, totalizes*: not extractable from the dialectic of subject and Other; the apparent neutrality of this field *hides the presence of desire as such*. Thus Descartes inaugurates the point of departure of *a science in which God has nothing to do* (*n'a rien à voir*) — "for the characteristic of our science, and its difference from the ancient sciences, is that no one even dares, without ridicule, to ask whether God knows anything of it — whether God leafs through the treatises of modern mathematics to keep up to date." The reprise of Lesson III is now complete and sharpened: the Cartesian God, supposed to know, absorbs the eternal truths precisely so that the letters may be emptied of Him — creation-letters with numbers and reverses giving way to unnumbered, commutable marks — the birth of formalization by the *unloading* of the supposed knower. And the closing designation of the year's last aim: *to pose the question of the position of analysis in science — can analysis be situated in our science, considered as the one in which God has nothing to do?* The question binds Part Four's remainder: analysis inherits the god-free science *and* re-generates, in the transference, a subject supposed to know — the tension Lessons XVIII–XX must resolve.

**Réponses.** Green proposes articulating the *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz* to the mirror — the subject referred to the supposed knower *in the mirror*. "Yeah... well — I shall not follow you in that direction. Because I think it is a short-circuit." The correct branch-point is the *virtual point of the function of freedom*, where the choice, the vel, manifests between signifier and subject — illustrated by the avatars of that freedom "which, in the end, is of course never refound by anyone serious"; and then Descartes, who scarcely worries about it *except in act*: it is in act, by the way in which he finds his certainty, that *his* freedom passes — "which does not mean he bequeaths it to us as a bank account." The refusal matters: routing the *sujet supposé savoir* through the mirror would re-imaginarize it (the specular other as knower), exactly the confusion the concept exists to bar.

Then, "since I am on this terrain," the promised excursus: *psychosomatics*. The psychosomatic is *not a signifier — but is nonetheless conceivable only insofar as the signifying induction at the level of the subject has occurred in a way that does not bring into play the aphanisis of the subject*. The little book's one essential remark — that desire is not representative of need — points here: at this place the *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz* will greatly limit our play of interpretation, *because the subject, qua aphanisis, is not interested* (*n'y est pas intéressé*) *in it*. It is insofar as a need comes to be interested in the function of desire that psychosomatics can be conceived as something other than the mere chatter of saying there is a psychic lining to everything somatic — known for ages. *If we speak of psychosomatics, it is insofar as desire must intervene there: the link of desire is conserved, even if we can no longer take account of the aphanisis-function of the subject.* Compressed as it is, this is the seminar's theory of the psychosomatic lesion, and it has had a long clinical afterlife: an induction by the signifier that bypasses the subject's fading — mark without division, "holophrased," the body written on directly where the interval of aphanisis did not open.

And Pavlov, to finish. The conditioned-reflex experiment is possible only insofar as *the exercise of a biological function is dismountable* — that to which we can attach the unifying, totalizing function of need — dismountable because more than one organ interferes: having made the dog secrete at the sight of meat, what interests you is to *cut* the thing at the point of salivary secretion and show it articulable with something functioning as signifier — *since made by the experimenter. In other words, the Other is there.* But as to the alleged psyche of the unfortunate animal, it demonstrates absolutely nothing; even the alleged neurosis-effects are not neurosis-effects, for the simple reason that *they are not analyzable by speech*. The major interest of conditioned reflexes is *to teach us what the animal can perceive*: we use the signifier — not a signifier for it, but functioning as such by being inscribed in a difference — to see what differential is possible at the level of its *perceptum*, which does not at all mean it will be the *percipiens* in the subjective sense; there being here no other subject than *the subject of the experimenter*. "In truth, we interrogate the animal about our own perception." Limiting the scope of the Pavlovian experiments thus restores their very great importance — their effective scientific benefits are these, and for nothing else are they actually employed. Finally the opening question they reveal: the animal translates between our signifiers a sort of *equivalence* — the dog conditioned to a hundred visual incitations per second reacts to a hundred auditory ones: number intervening as *pure frequency* in the Pavlovian signal — not yet full signifier-status ("except for us who count the frequencies"), but the animal's untaught crossing from hundred to hundred across registers may let us go further on properly perceptive structure — and it re-poses the realism of number, in the wake of an arithmetic "literally barred by the intrusion of algebrism." "I do not say I resolve this question by formulating it... I have profited from the question posed to tell you the things I wanted to tell you and had not. Let us leave it there." The Pavlov-critique is the mirror-image of the psychosomatic remark and completes the lesson's economy: in the laboratory the Other (the experimenter) is present and the subject absent — signal without subject; in the psychosomatic, signifier without fading — mark without subject: two limit-cases bracketing, from either side, the ordinary regime in which signifier, aphanisis, and desire hold together.

### **XVIII. Of the Subject Supposed to Know, of the First Dyad, and of the Good**

*10 June 1964\.* Miller's keywords: *the confidence placed in the analyst; as soon as there is a subject supposed to know, there is transference; belief; alienation grasped in the fort-da; alienation in pleasure.* The lesson generalizes the coinage of Lesson XVII into the seminar's definition of transference; corrects the fort-da a second time (alienation, not mastery); models the psychosomatic and psychosis on the *holophrased* first couple of signifiers; and rebuilds the Lust-Ich as alienation running the other way — the ground on which the *objets a* are finally tabled as the objects "good for nothing."

**Formation, ceremony, and the pivot.** The opening states, without ornament, what the seminar has been from Lesson I: "the aim of my teaching has been, and remains, *the formation of analysts*." A topic on the day's agenda of analytic research — and yet, in the analytic literature, its principles slip away; in the experience of all who have passed through this formation, the insufficiency of criteria is replaced by something of the order of *ceremony*, which, for what is at stake, can translate only one way: *simulation*. For the psychoanalyst there is *no beyond, no substantial beyond*, to which what he feels founded on in exercising his function could refer. What he obtains, though, is of inexpressible price: *the confidence of a subject as such*, and the results it comports by the ways of a certain technique. He does not present himself as a god; he is not God for his patient. What then does this confidence signify — around what does it turn? The one who trusts, and is rewarded, may elude the question; *the psychoanalyst cannot*: his formation requires that he know, in the process where he leads his patient, around what the movement turns — it must be *transmitted to him, and in an experience*. That pivot-point is *the desire of the psychoanalyst*. Lesson I's institutional question (the ceremony of authorization, the circular definition) is thus refound at the end of the course and answered: what stands where the missing criterion should be is not a credential but a desire — the seminar's hidden pole made the explicit hinge of training.

Recapitulation of Descartes: his step, in origin and end, goes not essentially toward science but toward *his own certainty*; it is at the principle of something that is not science as meditated since Plato, but *La science* — "the accent on the *La*": the science in which we are caught, which forms the context of everyone's action in the time we live, and from which the psychoanalyst himself cannot escape, since it is part of his conditions too. It is in relation to *that* science that psychoanalysis must be situated — possible only by articulating to the phenomenon of the unconscious the revision made of the foundation of the Cartesian subject. First, then, the phenomenology of the transference.

**The definition.** Transference is a phenomenon in which *the subject and the psychoanalyst are included together*. To divide it into transference and counter-transference — whatever the boldness or breeziness of what one permits oneself on the theme — *is never anything but a way of eluding what is at stake*. The demarcation is worth pausing on: the counter-transference literature of the 1950s (Heimann, Little, Racker) treated the analyst's feelings as a second phenomenon facing the first; Lacan dissolves the pair — one phenomenon, two included positions — which is why the analyst's desire, not his feelings, is the operative term. And transference is essential, tied to desire as the nodal phenomenon of the human being, *discovered before Freud* — perfectly articulated, with extreme rigor, in a text where love is debated: Plato's *Symposium* (a year of the seminar was spent demonstrating it). The essential, initial moment to which the question of the analyst's action must be referred is the one where it is said that *Socrates never claimed to know anything, save what concerns Eros — that is, desire*. By this alone — and because in the *Symposium* Plato goes further than anywhere in indicating the comedy-signification of his dialogues, pushing here to mime — Plato indicated, most precisely, *the place of the transference*. The circle from Lesson I closes: Socrates' desire, invoked there beside Freud's, is now shown to be the first formalization of transference itself — the man who knows nothing but desire is the first occupant of the place the analyst holds.

Then the sentence on the board, abbreviated S.s.S.: *as soon as there is, somewhere, the subject supposed to know, there is transference.* What does the organization of psychoanalysts signify, with its certificates of capacity, if not that it indicates *to whom one may address oneself to represent this subject supposed to know*? Yet it is certain, and known to all, that no psychoanalyst can claim to represent, however thinly, an absolute knowledge. In one sense, then, there could be, if there is one, *only one* such subject — and this *one alone* was, in his lifetime, Freud. That Freud, concerning the unconscious, was *legitimately* the subject one could suppose to know sets apart everything about the analytic relation as engaged, by his patients, with him. *He was not only the subject supposed to know. He knew* — and gave us that knowledge in terms one may call indestructible, in that since their emission they support an interrogation never yet exhausted; no progress, however small, has been made that did not deviate whenever one of the terms Freud ordered was neglected. "That shows us sufficiently what the function of the subject supposed to know is." The function — and its consequence, Freud's *prestige* — stand at the horizon of every analyst's position, and *constitute the drama of the social, communal organization of psychoanalysts*. The passage does three things at once: defines transference by a supposition (not an affect, not a repetition of persons); marks the founder as the unique case where supposition and fact coincided — which is why analysis-with-Freud is structurally apart, and why his desire (Lessons I, IV, XII) governs the field; and diagnoses the institution (the "drama," the certificates, Lacan's own excommunication) as the sociology of that supposition.

Who can feel fully invested with this subject supposed to know? Not the question. The question is, for each subject, *from where he takes his bearings to address himself* to it. Whenever this function can be, for the subject, incarnated in anyone — analyst or not — it results from the definition that *transference is thenceforth founded*. If things go so far that the function is already determined for the patient in some figure accessible to him, a special difficulty results for whoever takes him on in analysis; and even "the stupidest analyst" — a function designated only as one designates in logic that mythical number, the greatest expressible in so many words — notices it, and directs the analysand toward what remains for him the subject supposed to know. The details matter clinically: the supposition pre-exists the analyst and may be lodged elsewhere (a former teacher, a rival school, a dead master); the analyst holds the place *insofar as he is the object of the transference*, and experience proves the subject, entering analysis, is far from according him that place.

**Deception, error, and the residue of infallibility.** Leave aside the Cartesian hypothesis that the psychoanalyst deceives — not absolutely excluded from the phenomenology of certain entries into analysis. What limits the patient's confidence most, above all at the start, is the threat that the psychoanalyst be deceived *by him*. How often do we learn only very late a biographical detail "as big as that" — say, that at some moment the subject caught the pox. *Why didn't you tell me sooner?* — "Precisely," the analysand will say: *had I told you sooner, you might have attributed to it at least a part, even the ground, of my troubles — and if I am here, it is not for you to give my troubles an organic cause.* An example of unlimited bearing — social prejudice, scientific debate, the confusion still surrounding the very principle of analysis — given here only to illustrate that the patient withholds elements *so that the analyst not go too fast*. And he who can be deceived — should he not a fortiori be suspected of being able, quite simply, *to be mistaken* (*se tromper*)? *There is the limit. It is around this being-mistaken that the tipping-point lies, the balance of that subtle, infinitesimal point I mean to mark.* Granted that analysis can be, in some subjects, questioned at its very departure and suspected of being a lure — how does it happen that around this being-mistaken *something stops*? Even to the analyst put in question, *credit is given for a certain infallibility somewhere* — which will sometimes attribute intentions to a chance gesture: *You did that to test me\!* Lesson XI's triad (deceiving, being deceived, being mistaken) is here brought to its point: the supposition of knowledge survives every doubt about the analyst's person — the paranoid-seeming "you did it on purpose" is not an error about the analyst but the *structure* of the supposition showing through: somewhere, it is supposed, nothing is chance.

**Not wanting to desire.** The Socratic discussion introduced the theme that recognition of the conditions of the good in itself would have, for man, something irresistible. "The paradox of the teaching — not of Socrates (what do we know of him except by the Platonic comedy?), I will not even say of Plato, who develops in the terrain of the comic dialogue and leaves all questions open — but of a certain exploitation of Platonism, which one may say perpetuates itself in general derision. For who does not know, in truth, that the most perfect recognition of the conditions of the good will never prevent anyone from rushing into its contrary?" What, then, is at stake in the confidence placed in the analyst — what credit can we give him for *willing* this good, and what is more, *for another*? Explanation by three degrees. *Who does not know from experience that one can not want to enjoy* (*ne pas vouloir jouir*)? — who does not know the recoil that the approach of jouissance as such, with its atrocious promises, imposes on everyone? *Who does not know that one can not want to think?* — "the universal college of professors is there to witness it." *But what can it mean, not to want to desire?* All analytic experience — which here only gives form to what is for everyone at the very root of his experience — witnesses that *not wanting to desire and desiring are the same thing*. Desiring comports a phase of defense that makes it identical with not wanting to desire; not wanting to desire is *wanting not to desire* — the discipline to which precisely those devoted themselves who sought an exit from the impasses of the Socratic interrogation and were not only philosophers but religious in their way: *the Stoics, the Epicureans*. And the topological seal: *the subject knows that not wanting to desire has in itself something as irrefutable as that Moebius band which has no other side — traversing it, one returns mathematically to the surface supposed to double it.* The Hellenistic schools are read as the historical proof of the Moebius-structure of desire: their ascesis of not-desiring is desire's own continuation on the same one-sided surface — no reverse side on which to stand free of it.

*It is at this rendezvous-point that the analyst is awaited. Insofar as the analyst is supposed to know, he is supposed also to set out to meet unconscious desire.* Whence the figure of the double axe: *desire is the axis, the pivot, the handle, the hammer, by which is applied the force-element, the inertia, lying behind what is first formulated, in the patient's discourse, as demand — namely, the transference. The axis, the common point of this double axe, is the desire of the analyst, which I designate here as an essential function.* "And let no one tell me that I do not name this desire, for it is precisely the point that can be articulated only by the relation of desire to desire." The relation is internal: man's desire is the desire of the Other. Is not the element of alienation reproduced here? If it is only at the level of the Other's desire that man can recognize his desire — as the Other's desire — is that not something that must seem to obstruct his vanishing, a point where his desire can never recognize itself? Not raised, and not to be raised — for analytic experience shows that *it is by seeing a whole chain play out at the level of the Other's desire that the subject's desire is constituted*. And in the relation of desire to desire something of alienation is conserved, *but not with the same elements*: not with the S1 and S2 of the first couple — but with, on one side, *what is constituted from primal repression, from the fall, the Unterdrückung, of the binary signifier*, and on the other, *what appears first as lack in what is signified by the couple of signifiers, in the interval that links them: the desire of the Other*. The architecture of the transference is thus fully drawn: demand (the patient's discourse) is the axe-head; the shared haft is desire-to-desire — the analyst's desire meeting the subject's at the interval; and the terms of this second-level alienation are the fallen S2 (the unconscious) and the Other's desire (the interval) — the two products of Lessons XVI–XVII.

**Formulas to hold; the holophrase.** Some formulas as grappling-points, "lacking which thought can only slip." Alienation is tied essentially to *the function of the couple of signifiers*: it is essentially different whether there are two or three. To grasp the subject's function in the signifying articulation one must operate *with two*, because only with two can he be cornered (*coinçable*) in alienation; as soon as there are three, the sliding becomes circular — passed from the second to the third, it returns to the first, though not from the second. The aphanisis-effect under one of the two signifiers is tied to the definition — in the language of modern mathematics — of *a set of signifiers*: a set such that if there exist (the theory's reversed E, ∃) only two, the phenomenon of alienation is produced: the signifier represents the subject for the other signifier, whence at the level of the other signifier *the subject vanishes*. Whence, again, the error in the rival translation of the *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz* — *which is the S2 of the couple*. The formalization is minimal and pointed: alienation is a two-element phenomenon (the corner with no third way out — the vel); add a third signifier and circulation begins — discourse, sliding, history; the subject's founding pinch happens strictly in the dyad of the title.

What one of the students' texts sensed but expressed askew — omitting precisely the fundamental character of the subject's function — is the constant talk of the relation of signifier and signified: "the ABC of the question." Certainly Lacan once had to put on the blackboard what was formulated at the root of the Saussurean development — to show his starting point; but he showed at once that it was efficacious and manageable *only by including the function of the subject at the original stage*. Reducing the signifier to *nomination* — a label stuck on a thing — lets the whole essence of language escape. And the little book (already convicted of infatuation) proves also "crass ignorance" in implying that *this* is what the Pavlovian experiment involves. What can be situated at the level of the conditioned reflex is assuredly not the association of a sign with a thing: whether Pavlov recognizes it or not, it is the association of a signifier — characteristic of every experimental condition instituted *with the cut* one can make in the organic organization of a need — a manifestation at the level of a cycle of interrupted needs, refound here as *the cut of desire*. "And — as one says, *that is why your daughter is mute* — that is why the animal will never learn to speak. At least by that route": being one time behind, the experiment can provoke in it all sorts of disorders, but, not being up to now a speaking being, *it is not called to put in question the desire of the experimenter* — "who, moreover, if he were interrogated himself, would be quite embarrassed to answer." The Molière tag from Lesson I returns with its full weight: the dog's muteness and the daughter's have the same explanation-that-explains-nothing — but here the true reason is given: speech begins where the Other's desire can be questioned, and the laboratory forecloses exactly that question (the experimenter's own desire being, as ever, the unanalyzed remainder).

Articulated thus, the experiment permits situating *the psychosomatic effect*: "I will go so far as to formulate that *when there is no interval between S1 and S2, when the first couple of signifiers solidifies, holophrases itself, we have the model of a whole series of cases* — though in each the subject does not occupy the same place." On the board, the schema: S1 over S2, the chain of meanings (s, s′, s″...) and the series of identifications i(a, a′, a″...). It is insofar as, for example, *the retarded child* (*l'enfant débile*) takes the place, bottom right, of that S — with respect to the something to which *the mother reduces him to being no more than the support of her desire in an obscure term* — that *the psychotic dimension is introduced into the education of the retarded*: precisely what "our colleague Maud Mannoni," in a book just out and recommended, tries to designate for those who may be commissioned to lift that mortgage. And assuredly something of the same order is at stake in *psychosis*: this solidity, this massive seizure of the primitive signifying chain, *is what forbids the dialectical opening that manifests itself in the phenomenon of belief*. The holophrase-doctrine, compressed into a paragraph, is one of the seminar's most productive legacies: psychosomatic lesion, mental deficiency, psychosis — three cases (with the subject differently placed in each) of one structure: the S1–S2 interval welded shut, hence no aphanisis, no dialectic, no question of the Other's desire; Mannoni's clinic (the child as frozen signifier of the mother's desire) cited as its working demonstration.

**Unglauben and Casanova.** At the ground of paranoia itself — which seems to us all animated by belief — reigns the phenomenon of *Unglauben*: *not* the not-believing-in-it, but *the absence of one of the terms of belief — the term in which the division of the subject is designated*. If no belief is full and entire, it is because no belief does not suppose, at its ground, that *the last dimension it has to reveal is strictly correlative with the moment when its meaning is about to vanish*. Witness Mannoni (Octave, present in the room) on a misadventure of Casanova, "the most amusing and demonstrative considerations": at the term of a mystification that succeeds to the point of moving the celestial powers — unleashing around him a storm that genuinely terrifies him — the personage, who till then has pursued the most cynical adventure with a little goose who gives him the motive for the circle of imbeciles he draws along, *for having seen his mystification take on its meaning, realize itself, himself enters a veritable collapse* — comic to find in a Casanova who defies earth and heaven at the level of his desire — *falling into impotence, as if he had truly met the figure of God to stop him*. The doctrine of belief is exact and double: paranoia's certainty is not too much belief but a *missing term* of it (no division, no vanishing-point — the *Unglauben* twin of the holophrase just described); and Casanova is the inverse proof from the swindler's side — belief functions only while its meaning holds short of realization; let the trick *come true* and the subject collapses at the point where his division should have been. (Octave Mannoni's later formula — "I know well, but all the same" — is visibly under construction here.)

**The fort-da, second correction.** The little book again presents the fort-da as threadbare — "one all but apologizes for taking up once more this fort-da on which everyone has wiped his feet" — as an example of primordial symbolization now in the public domain. "Well\! one commits nonetheless a gross error": it is *not* from the pure and simple opposition of *fort* and *da* that it draws the inaugural force its repetitive essence explains. To say the subject merely institutes himself in a function of *mastery* is "a foolishness" (*une sottise*). In the two phonemes are incarnated *the very mechanisms of alienation — which express themselves, paradoxical as it may seem, at the level of the fort*. *No fort without da — and, if one may say, without Dasein. But precisely — contrary to what the whole phenomenology of Daseinsanalyse tries to grasp as the radical foundation of existence — there is no Dasein with the fort. That is: one has no choice.* If the little subject can exercise himself at this game, *it is precisely that he does not exercise himself at all — for no subject can grasp this radical articulation. He exercises himself with the help of a little reel — that is, with the objet a.* The function of the exercise with this object refers to *an alienation, and not to some supposed mastery* — which one can hardly see increased by indefinite repetition, whereas the indefinite repetition at stake *brings to light the radical vacillation of the subject*. Lesson V's correction is thus completed by Lesson XVI's machinery, with a dart at Binswanger on the way: the fort-da is the vel played in two phonemes — *fort* the term under which the subject fades (no choosing presence; being-there forfeited in any case) — and the reel-*a* is what the child manipulates *because* the articulation itself cannot be grasped; the game does not master loss, it *exhibits* the forced choice, and its repetition displays vacillation, not conquest.

**Alienation in pleasure.** The closing construction, on the two board-schemas. In *Triebe und Triebschicksale* Freud puts love at once at the level of the real, of narcissism, of the pleasure principle in its correlation with the reality principle — deducing that ambivalence is absolutely different from the *Verkehrung*, the circular movement. At the level of love the schema is staged, Freud says, in two times. First an *Ich* defined objectively by the solidary functioning of the central nervous system with the condition of homeostasis — tensions kept at a certain lowest level ("which cannot be the lowest, because that would be death — and the thing was envisaged by Freud in a second time"). Outside it, if there is an outside, only indifference — and at this level, since tension is at stake, *indifference simply means inexistence*. Yet Freud says the rule of auto-erotism is *not* the inexistence of objects but their functioning *solely in relation to pleasure*: in the zone of indifference there is differentiated what brings *Lust* and what brings *Unlust* — everyone has always seen the ambiguity of the term *Lustprinzip*, "since some indeed write it Unlustprinzip." The question is then to figure this stage — to articulate homeostasis and pleasure, *for that something brings pleasure is already too much for equilibrium*. The proposed schema: the ICH as apparatus tending to homeostasis; *Lust* — "not properly a field: always an object, an object of pleasure which, as such, is mirrored in the ego. This mirror-image, this bi-univocal correlate of the object, is the purified Lust-Ich of which Freud speaks — what, in the Ich, satisfies itself with the object as Lust.\* What is *Unlust*, by contrast, is what remains *unassimilable, irreducible to the pleasure principle: from it, Freud tells us, the non-ego will be constituted. It is situated — note it well — inside the circle of the primitive ego; it bites into it* (*mord sur lui*), *without the homeostatic functioning ever managing to resorb it. There is the origin of what we shall refind later in the function called that of the bad object.* Under the diagram, the caption: *la preuve par l'objet a* — the proof by the objet a. The reading of Freud's 1915 topology is precision-work: pleasure enters the ego only as mirrored object (the *Lust-Ich* as reflection, hence love's narcissism, Lesson XV); unpleasure is an *internal* foreign body — the non-ego constituted *inside* the ego's circle, biting it — so the Kleinian bad object is derived, not imported, and the crescent-structure of alienation is found already operating in Freud's pleasure-schema.

You see above all that what structures the level of pleasure *already gives the first sketch of a possible articulation of alienation* — but "in the other direction." The *Lust* says, as it were, from the exterior zone: "ah, the *Ich* — that is something one should really attend to"; and as soon as it attends to it, *the perfect tranquillity of the Ich disappears: the Lust-Ich distinguishes itself, and by the same stroke the Unlust, foundation of the non-ego, falls* (*choit*) — without the apparatus disappearing; simply, at a primitive level, that same *shearing* (*écornure*) valorized in the subject-Other dialectic is produced, *here in the other sense*. Its formula: *no good without evil, no good without suffering* — which keeps to this good and evil a character of alternation, of possible dosage, into which the signifying articulation would be reduced, *and falsely*. For everyone knows *hedonism fails, skids, in explaining the mechanics of desire*: passing to the other register, the alienating articulation, it is expressed quite differently — "I almost blush to agitate these rags with which imbeciles have played for so long, like *beyond good and evil*, without knowing exactly what they speak of" — namely: *no evil without a good resulting from it; and when the good is there, no good holds together with the evil*. That is why an ethics situated in the pure and simple register of pleasure fails, and why, "most legitimately, Kant objects to it that *the sovereign good can in no way be conceived as the infinitization of some little good* — for there is no possible law to be given of what can be the good in objects. The sovereign good, if that confusing term must be kept, can be refound only at the level of the law" — and *Kant avec Sade* demonstrated what that means. At the level of the left-hand schema, passivity, narcissism, ambivalence govern the dialectic of pleasure; *its term is, properly speaking, what is called identification.* The pages fold the seminar's ethics into its logic: hedonism's failure is formal (pleasure's good-and-evil alternate and dose; the alienating vel does not — its disjunction is the forced kind), Kant is enlisted exactly where he refutes eudaimonism (no law of the good-in-objects), and love's terminus — identification — is derived from the mirror-symmetry of *Lust* and *Lust-Ich*: what cannot be kept outside, one has as image within.

And the final formula, tabling the objects at last: it is *the recognition of the drive* that permits constructing, with greatest certainty, the functioning called division of the subject, or alienation. And how was the drive recognized? In this: that far from the dialectic of the subject's unconscious being limitable to the reference to the *Lust*\-field, to the images of beneficent, favorable objects, *we have found a certain type of objects that, in the end, can serve for nothing* (*ne peuvent servir à rien*). *These are the objets a: the breasts, the feces, the gaze, the voice. It is in this new term that lies the point that introduces the dialectic of the subject as subject of the unconscious.* The criterion is the lesson's last gift and retro-illuminates the whole seminar: the *Lust*\-objects are good-for (mirrored, lovable, identifiable-with); the *a*\-objects are *useless* — unassimilable to any good — and their discovery (Freud's, Abraham's, the clinic's) is what *proved* the division of the subject, since nothing in a pleasure-economy could motivate them.

**Réponses.** Safouan: "I always have difficulty grasping the difference between the object in the drive and the object in desire — and now, with the *ça* and the object in the drive, I lose the thread." — "Listen, it is a question of terminology" — and kind of you to ask, since it can serve everyone. There is a heap of very agreeable things we believe we desire, insofar as we are healthy; we can say nothing of them but that *we believe we desire them* — transmissible, but not analytic theory. The objects in the *Lust*\-field have so fundamentally narcissistic a relation with the subject that "in the end the mystery of the alleged regression from love to identification has its reason in the symmetry of those two fields I designated for you as Lust and Lust-Ich. *What one cannot keep outside, one always has the image of within. It is as silly as that, identification with the love-object* — and I do not see why it made so many difficulties, even for Freud. That, my dear fellow, is the love-object." And you see it when you speak of objects lacking the singular value attached to the drive-object: you say, as Freud remarked — *I love mutton stew*. Exactly the same as saying *I love Madame So-and-so* — "with this difference, that *that* you say to her, which changes everything. You say it to her for reasons I will explain next time." You love mutton stew; *you are not sure of desiring it*. Take the experience of *the beautiful butcher's wife: she loves caviar — only she doesn't want any. That is why she desires it.* Understand: *the object of desire is the cause of desire, and this object-cause of desire is the object of the drive — that is, the object around which the drive turns.* Since the dialogue is with someone who has worked through the texts, the tight formula: *it is not that desire clings to the object of the drive — desire goes around it* (*en fait le tour*), *insofar as it is acted* (*agi*) *in the drive. But not all desire is necessarily acted in the drive: there are also empty desires, mad desires, arising from this — that something was forbidden you*; from its being forbidden, you cannot for a time do otherwise than think of it — "that is still desire." *But each time you deal with an object of good, we designate it — a question of terminology, but justified — as an object of love.* Next time: the relation among love, transference, desire. The exchange is the seminar's clearest table of its objects and belongs beside the *Traumdeutung*'s butcher's wife it cites: love-objects (mutton stew, Madame) belong to the *Lust*/narcissism circuit — declared, mirrored, identifiable; the object of desire is the *cause*, not the aim, identical with the drive-object as the circled hollow; desire divides into acted (routed through a drive) and empty/mad (sustained by interdiction alone — the caviar refused so that desire may stand); and the difference "you say it *to her*" reserves for Lesson XIX precisely the hinge — the address, the Other — on which love and transference will be split apart.

### **XIX. From Interpretation to Transference**

*17 June 1964\.* The penultimate lesson. Miller's keywords: *field of the ego and field of the Other; metaphor; interpretation is not open to all meanings; indetermination and determination of the subject; love, transference, desire; the slave; the ego-ideal and the petit a.* The lesson corrects a formalization of metaphor gone wrong, delivers the seminar's doctrine of interpretation (aimed at the irreducible non-sense signifier, verified on the Wolf Man and the zero at the denominator), completes the theory of transference-love as deception, and — at the last — distinguishes the two identifications: the ego-ideal's unary trait against the singularly different identification introduced by separation, whose support is the *objet a*.

**The tact of usage; the two fields; next year.** The vocabulary today is all-too-familiar: identification, idealization, projection, introjection — terms not easy to handle precisely because they *make sense*. Projection and introjection pass, in some eyes, for reciprocals; yet Lacan long ago pointed out that one belongs to a field dominated by the symbolic, the other by the imaginary — so that in at least one dimension *they do not meet*. Intuitive use, from the feeling of understanding each in isolation, is the source of all the slippages — the common fate of discourse. In common discourse the speaker of his mother tongue expresses himself so surely, with such perfect tact, that it is to *the most common user, the uninstructed man*, that one has recourse to learn a term's proper usage: as soon as man merely wants to speak, he orients himself in *the fundamental topology of language*, very different from the simplistic realism to which the man at ease in "science" too often clings — witness expressions taken truly at random: *à part soi* (to oneself), *bon gré mal gré* (willy-nilly), *une affaire* as distinct from *une chose à faire* — implying the *enveloping topology* in which the subject recognizes himself when he speaks spontaneously. And if Lacan can address psychoanalysts and locate the implicit topology their terms refer to, it is because on the whole — however incapable they often are, for lack of teaching, of articulating it — they use them adequately, with the common speaker's spontaneity; only when they force an observation, "understanding where they do not understand," does the usage go forced. The methodological preamble deserves note: the warrant for the seminar's topology is not stipulation but *the tact of ordinary usage* — analytic vocabulary, like the mother tongue, already knows more than its users can say, and formalization only renders that implicit knowledge; a linguist's premise applied to the metalanguage of analysis itself.

The board carries the two-field schema of Lesson XVIII: the primordial *Ich*, objectifiable in the nervous apparatus, the homeostatic field, with *Lust* distinguished from *Unlust*. Freud marks that the *Ich* manifests as organized — a narcissistic sign — and precisely thereby is articulated to the field of the real: in the real it distinguishes, privileges, only what reflects itself in its field by an effect of *Lust*, as return to homeostasis. But what does not favor homeostasis and maintains itself at all cost as *Unlust* *bites still deeper into its field*: the *Unlust* inscribes itself in the ego *as non-ego, negation, shearing of the ego* — not to be confounded with what surrounds it, the vastness of the real: the non-ego is distinguished *as foreign body, fremde Objekt*, sitting in the lunule of the two Euler circles. On the register of pleasure, then, an objectifiable foundation — like the scientist foreign to the object whose functioning he registers. "Only, we are not just that — and even to be that, we must also be *the subject who thinks*": implicated quite differently, insofar as we depend on the field of the Other, "there for some time before we came into the world, whose circulating structures determine us as subject." Everything in analysis must be sorted between the two fields — some things happen at the level of the *Ich*, others in the field of the Other, and confounding them means understanding nothing; of the Other's field the essential articulations are the two functions defined as alienation and separation.

And the announcement: the discourse of the following year will be entitled — provisionally — *the subjective positions of being* (*les positions subjectives de l'être*): "if I trusted what offers itself, I would say the subjective positions of *existence*, with all the favor that term enjoys in the ambient air; unfortunately that would permit rigorous application only at the level of the neurotic — which would not be so bad, moreover." The whole preparation on the foundations of analysis must deploy itself, "since nothing centers properly except from the position of the subject," in showing what the articulation of analysis, starting from desire, permits illustrating. (History's footnote: the announced seminar, *Crucial Problems for Psychoanalysis*, would indeed pursue these positions — and the swipe at "existence" is one more demarcation from Daseinsanalyse, after Lesson XVIII's.)

**Metaphor and the bar: interpretation is not open to all meanings.** An article already referred to — "in an effort not without merit" — tried to give form to the language-structure of the unconscious and ended by *translating* Lacan's formula of metaphor. The formula was essential and usable, since it manifests the dimension where the unconscious appears insofar as signifying *condensation* is fundamental to it. Open-air condensation is visible in the least poetic metaphor — whence the example of *Booz endormi* in "The Agency of the Letter": of all poems, the one that "sings in the most memories" in French; no unfavorable example for analysts, introduced together with *the paternal metaphor*. Its quick: the creation of sense in designating Booz — divine father and instrument of God at once — by the metaphor *His sheaf was neither miserly nor hateful*: the dimension of sense it opens is nothing less than what appears in the terminal image, *the golden sickle carelessly thrown in the field of stars* — the dimension hidden in the poem, "more hidden than you think," for it is not enough to conjure the pruning-hook with which Jupiter flooded the world with the blood of Chronos \[the Hesiodic castration, with the dynasties compressed\]: in the biblical perspective the castration at stake is of quite another order, present with all the echoes of the history, down to Booz's invocation — *How shall there arise from me, an old man, a posterity?* And — "you would know it much better had I given this year the seminar I intended on the Names-of-the-Father" — *the Lord with the unpronounceable name is precisely he who watches over the childbearing of barren women and men past age. The fundamentally trans-biological character of paternity, introduced by the tradition of the destiny of the chosen people, has something originally repressed there, which always resurges in the ambiguity of the limp, the stumble, the symptom, the non-encounter, dustuchia, with the meaning that remains hidden.* The paragraph is the interrupted seminar's ghost given one page of body: biblical fatherhood — trans-biological, an affair of the signifier and the promise, not of generation — is the repressed whose return limps through history as symptom; Lesson II's *clocherie* and Lesson VI's *dustuchia* are joined to the Name-of-the-Father, and Hugo's sickle marks where the Greek castration-myth and the biblical order divide.

Then the correction. The author trusted the *fraction*\-formalism resulting from marking the signifier/signified link by a bar. Not absolutely illegitimate: at certain moments the bar marks a value properly expressed by fractional use in the mathematical sense. "But of course it is not the only one: from signifier to signified there is another relation, that of the *effect of sense*" — and precisely where metaphor's sense-effect is to be marked, one absolutely cannot, without precaution, manipulate the bar in a fractional transformation, as if it were a proportion: from A/B × C/D one may build a four-storey formula — and this was judged clever for metaphor, arguing that to the weight, in the unconscious, of the final signifier incarnating the metaphor with its newly created sense, there should answer some *pinning* of two signifiers to each other in the unconscious. The formula cannot satisfy — "first because one ought to know that there can be no such relations of the signifier to itself, *the property of the signifier being that it cannot signify itself* without engendering some fault of logic": witness the antinomies that intervened as soon as exhaustive logical formalization of mathematics was attempted — the catalogue of catalogues not containing themselves is evidently not the same catalogue when it figures in the definition and when it is inscribed in the catalogue. "It is so much simpler to notice that what happens is that *a substitutive signifier has come to the place of another signifier to constitute the metaphor-effect. It sends the signifier it has driven out elsewhere.*" And if one wants to keep a fractional handling: *put the vanished signifier, the repressed signifier, below the principal bar — at the denominator, unterdrückt.* The technical stakes are high and the correction exact: Laplanche-Leclaire's four-decker (the Bonneval formalization) welded S to S across the bar, making the unconscious a signifier-signifier "pinning" — from which they drew the conclusion Lacan now rejects by name: *it is false that interpretation is open to all meanings* on the pretext that it is only a mad link of signifier to signifier. "That is to concede to those who rise against the uncertain character of analytic interpretation that indeed all interpretations are possible — which is properly absurd." That the *effect* of interpretation is to isolate in the subject a kernel, a *Kern* (Freud's word) of *non-sense*, does not make interpretation itself non-sense.

The doctrine, then, in full: *interpretation is a signification, and not just any one. It comes here in the place of the s \[the signified\] and reverses the relation by which the signifier has, in language, the signified as its effect. Its effect is to make arise an irreducible signifier.* One must interpret at the level of the *s* — not open to all senses, not just anything: a signification, doubtless only approximate — but what is there, rich and complex when it is the subject's unconscious, is *destined to make arise irreducible, non-sensical signifying elements, made of non-sense*. Leclaire's work illustrated particularly well *the crossing of significative interpretation toward signifying non-sense*, producing, apropos of his obsessional, the formula *Poordjeli*, linking the two syllables of *licorne* and permitting the introduction into its sequence of a whole chain where his desire is animated. The two-step is the yield of Part Four for technique: interpretation delivers a determinate signification (against the "all meanings" surrender) *in order to* expose, beyond it, the senseless signifier to which the subject is subjected (against hermeneutic sense-restoration) — Lesson XVI's claim ("interpretation aims at reducing the signifiers to their non-meaning") now equipped with its formula and its exhibit.

*Interpretation is not open to all meanings... but that signification is not, for the advent of the subject, the essential. What is essential is that he see, beyond that signification, to what signifier — non-sense, irreducible, traumatic — he is, as subject, subjected.* Materialized in experience: take the greatest and most sensational of Freud's psychoanalyses, where one sees better than anywhere the problem of the conversion of fantasy and reality converge *on something irreducible, non-sensical, functioning as originally repressed signifier* — the Wolf Man. The Ariadne's thread for reading it: *the abrupt apparition of the wolves in the window of the dream plays the function of the s, as representative of the loss of the subject*. Not only is the subject fascinated by the gaze of the wolves ("seven, though in his drawing there are only five"): *their fascinated gaze is the subject himself*. And the whole case-history demonstrates that at each stage of the subject's life something came to *re-work the value of the determining index* constituted by this original signifier — the adventures of the father, the sister, the mother, Grusha the maid: so many times enriching the subject's unconscious desire with something to be put, *as signification constituted in the relation to the desire of the Other, at the numerator*. The gaze-doctrine of Part Two and the fraction of Part Four meet in the wolves: the dream's staring pack is the subject's own lost gaze (Lesson XIV's voyeur refound "in the conflagration"), functioning as the primally repressed signifier at the denominator, while life's encounters write and rewrite the numerator.

Now the logical necessity: the subject as *x* constitutes himself only from the *Urverdrängung*, the necessary fall of this first signifier — constituted *around* it, unable to substitute for it as such, "since that would require the representation of one signifier for another, whereas here there is only one, the first." In this *x* two faces: the constituting moment where significance falls (placed in its function at the level of the unconscious), and the *return-effect* of the relation conceivable from the fraction — to be introduced with prudence, but well indicated by the effects of language. *Everyone knows that if zero appears at the denominator, the value of the fraction no longer has sense but takes, by convention, what mathematicians call an infinite value. In a certain way, this is one of the times of the constitution of the subject. Insofar as the primordial signifier is pure non-sense, it becomes the bearer of the infinitization of the value of the subject — not open to all senses, but abolishing them all, which is different.* Whence the necessity of the word *freedom* in handling alienation: *what founds, in the sense and radical non-sense of the subject, the function of freedom, is properly this signifier that kills all senses.* And therefore it is false that the signifier in the unconscious is open to all senses: *it constitutes the subject in his freedom with respect to all senses, but that does not mean he is not determined there* — for at the numerator, in the place of the zero, the things inscribed are significations, dialectized in the relation to the desire of the Other, *and they give the subject's relation to the unconscious a determinate value*. The fraction S/0 is the seminar's last great formalization, and its two-sidedness answers Miller's keyword-pair (indetermination *and* determination): denominator zero — the fallen, senseless S1 — infinitizes the subject, freeing him from every sense (the truth in existentialist "freedom," reduced to a signifying effect); the numerator — the significations written by the Other's desire — determines him. Free as to sense, determined as to value: the exact correction of both determinism and liberty.

A pre-indication for next year: analysis forces us toward a formalization such that *the mediation of this infinite of the subject with the finitude of desire can be made only by the intervention of what Kant introduced, "with such freshness," under the name of negative magnitude* — the freshness mattering, "for between forcing philosophers to reflect on the fact that minus-one is not zero, and ears going deaf again at such discourse thinking who cares, there is a distance"; men survive only by being at each instant forgetful of all their subjective conquests — which remain conquered, "but it is rather they who are conquered by the effects of these conquests," with confusion the first redoubtable consequence. *Negative magnitude, then: there we shall find how to designate one of the supports of what is called the castration complex — namely, the negative incidence in which the phallus-object enters it.* Kant's 1763 essay, first invoked in Lesson II for the gap in causality, returns at the far end of the course to notate castration: −φ is not zero, not absence, but a negative magnitude — a real subtraction with positive effects — the mediation between the infinitized subject and finite desire.

**Love, transference, desire; the slave.** Progress on what agitates us: *transference is unthinkable except as taking its departure in the subject supposed to know*. And now one sees better *what* he is supposed to know: *that from which no one can escape, as soon as he formulates it — quite simply, signification.* This implies — "and that is why I first made the dimension of his desire arise" — *that he cannot refuse himself to it*. This privileged point is the only one in which we can recognize the character of *an absolute point with no knowledge at all: absolute precisely in being no knowledge, but the point of attachment that links his very desire to the resolution of what is to be revealed*. The supposition is thus given its content and its paradox in one stroke: what the analyst is supposed to know is signification as such (the inescapability of meaning — exactly what Lesson XVII's God underwrote); and the point is "absolute" because empty of knowledge, filled instead by a desire pledged to the revelation. *The subject enters the game from this fundamental support: the subject is supposed to know, simply by being subject of desire.*

What then happens is the commonest apparition of the *transference-effect: this effect is love*. Clear that, like all love, it is locatable, as Freud indicates, only in the field of narcissism: *to love is, essentially, to want to be loved* (*aimer, c'est essentiellement vouloir être aimé*). *What arises in the transference-effect opposes itself to revelation.* Love intervenes in its function, here revealed as essential — *its function of deception* (*tromperie*). Love is indeed a transference-effect, *but its face of resistance*: we are bound to await this effect in order to interpret, and at the same time we know it closes the subject to the effect of our interpretation — "the alienation-effect, in which is articulated, in the subject's relation to the Other, the effect that we are, is here absolutely manifest." The knot of Lesson X (closure as condition of interpretation) is retied with its final term: the closing agent is love — narcissistic, reciprocal (Lesson XV), wanting-to-be-loved — the *Lust*\-circuit mobilized against the revelation the supposition of knowledge set in motion.

Here the point always eluded, "which Freud articulates, and which is not the excuse but the *reason* of the transference": *nothing can be attained in absentia, in effigie*. Meaning: *transference is not, by nature, the shadow of something formerly lived. Quite the contrary: the subject, insofar as subjected to the desire of the analyst, desires to deceive him of that subjection by making himself loved by him — proposing of himself that essential falseness which is love. The transference-effect is this effect of deception insofar as it repeats itself presently, here and now.* It is repetition of what passed *only in being of the same form* — "not ectopy, not the shadow of the old deceptions of love: it is *the isolation, in the actual, of its pure functioning of deception*." Lesson V's suspended ambiguity (transference given as effigy, yet the real refusing effigies) is finally resolved by inversion: Freud's *in effigie* phrase does not lament that analysis works on shadows — it states why transference is *actual*: because nothing is destroyed in effigy, the deception must be produced live, here and now, in its pure present form. Whence: *behind so-called transference-love, what there is, is the affirmation of the link of the analyst's desire to the patient's desire* — which Freud translated in a rapid conjuring-trick, a lark-mirror, "*after all, it is only the patient's desire* — so as to reassure the colleagues. It is the patient's desire, yes — *but in its encounter with the desire of the analyst*." The primal scene of Lesson XII (Freud reassuring Breuer) is here generalized into the standard doctrine's founding sleight-of-hand — and corrected.

This desire of the analyst — "I will not say I have not yet named it, for how does one name a desire? *A desire, one circumscribes it* (*on le cerne*)." History gives track and trace. Is it not singular, the echo between the ethics of analysis and *Stoic* ethics? — what is Stoic ethics at bottom, "if not — shall I ever have time to demonstrate it — *the recognition of the absolute regency of the desire of the Other*: that *Thy will be done\!* taken up in the Christian register." But a more radical articulation solicits us: the relation of the desire of *the master* and *the slave* — "Hegel says it is resolved; it is in no way resolved." Since the next course is the last, some pointed indications of direction.
