If the master is situated only by an original relation to the assumption of death, it is very hard to give him a graspable relation to desire — "I speak of the master in Hegel, not the ancient master, of whom we have some portrait, namely Alcibiades," whose relation to desire is precisely quite visible: he comes asking Socrates for something he does not know what it is, but calls *agalma* — "that mystery which, in the mist surrounding Alcibiades' gaze, represents *something beyond all goods*." And how see anything but a first sketch of *the technique of locating the transference* in Socrates' answer — not what he said when Alcibiades was young (*attend to your soul*) but what befits the florid and hardened man: *attend to your desire — attend to your own onions* (*occupe-toi de tes oignons*): namely, Agathon — "a height of irony on Plato's part to have incarnated them in a man at once futile and absurd, almost clownish" (the verses Plato gives him on love being the very indication of a futility bordering the buffoonish — the least apt object, surely, to retain a master's desire; and that he is called *Agathon*, the name to which Plato gave sovereign value \[the Good\], superadds an involuntary but incontestable note of irony). *Thus the desire of the master appears, from its entry into history, the term by nature most astray* (*le plus égaré*). By contrast, *when Socrates desires to obtain his own answer, it is to him who has no right to make his desire count — to the slave — that he addresses himself. That answer, from him, he is always assured of obtaining.* And the splice that ends the section: "*the voice of reason is low*, Freud says somewhere, *but it always says the same thing*. No one makes the connection that Freud says exactly the same of unconscious desire: its voice too is low, *but its insistence is indestructible*. Perhaps there is a relation between the two. *It is in the direction of some kinship that we shall have to direct our gaze toward the slave, when it is a matter of locating what the desire of the analyst is.*" The page is the seminar's deepest hint about its hidden pole, and it should be read slowly: the analyst's desire is tracked not through the master (whose desire, deathbound, is history's most lost — Agathon its derisory first object) but through the *slave* — the one without right to desire, from whom the answer always comes (the Meno's slave-boy, geometry drawn from the unentitled) — kin to the unconscious itself, low-voiced and indestructible. Socrates redirecting Alcibiades' *agalma*\-demand toward Agathon is the first transference-interpretation; and the analyst, like the slave, answers from the place of no-right — his desire circumscribed, never named.

**The two identifications: ego-ideal and objet a.** Two closing remarks, founded in Freud's location of identification — where there are enigmas *for Freud himself*: he seems astonished that the regression from love to identification occurs so easily, beside texts articulating that love and identification are equivalent in a certain register, and that narcissism and overestimation of the object, *Verliebtheit*, are exactly the same in love. Freud stopped here — "find in the texts the various *clues*, as the English say" — for lack, Lacan believes, of sufficiently distinguishing something. In the identification-chapter of *Massenpsychologie*, the accent was put on the *second* form of identification, to locate and detach the *einziger Zug*, the unary trait — *the foundation, the kernel of the ego-ideal*. What is it? *A privileged object in the field of Lust? No.* The unary trait is not in the first field of narcissistic identification (which Freud, "very curiously," incarnates in a sort of primitive model taken by the father, *anterior to the libidinal investment on the mother* — "a mythical time, assuredly"). *The unary trait, insofar as the subject clings to it, is in the field of desire, which can in any case constitute itself only in the reign of the signifier, at the level of the relation of the subject to the Other. It is the field of the Other that determines the function of the unary trait*, in that from it is inaugurated a major time of identification in the topography Freud then developed: *idealization, the ego-ideal* — the traces of this first signifier shown on the primitive bone where the hunter notches the count of his kills \[Lesson XI's tally\].

And the mainspring of the ego-ideal's incidence: *the intercrossing by which the unary signifier comes to function in the field of Lust — in the field of primary narcissistic identification*. Described elsewhere as the mirror-directed aim of the ego-ideal: the subject clings to the reference-point of *the one who looks at him in the mirror* — the parent who, before the mirror, holds him up — and sees appear *not his ego-ideal but his ideal ego, the point where he desires to please himself in himself* (*se complaire en lui-même*). "There is the function, the mainspring, the efficacious instrument constituted by the ego-ideal. Not long ago a little girl told me sweetly that *it was high time somebody attended to her so that she could appear lovable to herself*. She gave the innocent avowal of the mainspring that enters play in the first time of the transference: *the subject has a relation to his analyst whose center is at the level of that privileged signifier called the ego-ideal, insofar as from there he will feel himself both satisfying and loved*." The mechanism of the optical model (Lesson XI's nasse-coda) is thus finally spelled: I(A) — the point in the Other from which one is seen as lovable — commands i(a), the image in which one pleases oneself; and the first stratum of transference is exactly this: the analyst installed at the ego-ideal, the patient lovable-to-himself from there. Which is why every analysis of transference that *ends* there — Balint's identification with the analyst (Lesson XI) — ends in the imaginary.

*But there is another function, instituting an identification of a singularly different nature, introduced by the process of separation.* Its support: *that privileged object, discovery of analysis, whose very reality is purely topological — the object around which the drive turns, the object that makes a bump, like the wooden egg in the weave that, in analysis, you are darning — the objet a.* The darning-egg (*œuf de bois*) is the last of the seminar's homely figures and among its best: the *a* is not in the fabric but under it — the invisible support around which the mending of the subject's weave is done. This object supports what, in the drive, is defined by the fact that the signifier's entry into man's life permits him to *make arise the sense of sex*: for man, because he knows signifiers, *sex and its significations are always capable of presentifying the presence of death*. The life-drive/death-drive distinction is true as manifesting two aspects of the drive — *on condition of conceiving that all the sexual drives articulate themselves at the level of significations in the unconscious, insofar as what they make arise is death — death as signifier and nothing but as signifier* — "for can one say there is a being-for-death?" (*un être-pour-la-mort*). Under what conditions the signifier death can spring up fully armed in the treatment is comprehensible only from this articulation. The Heidegger-dart is the sharpest of the seminar's demarcations from fundamental ontology: no *Sein-zum-Tode* — death enters the subject not as ownmost possibility but as a signifier among significations, which is exactly why (Lessons XIV–XVI) the drives carry it.

*By the function of the objet a, the subject separates himself, ceases to be bound to the vacillation of being, to the sense that makes the essential of alienation.* Long indicated by traces: the phenomenology of *verbal hallucination* is inconceivable unless one understands the very term used for it — *voices*: *it is insofar as the object voice is present in it that the percipiens is present in it. Verbal hallucination is not a false perceptum; it is a deviated percipiens.* The subject is *immanent* to his verbal hallucination. Which must make us pose the question of what we try to obtain in analysis concerning *the accommodation of the percipiens*: until analysis, the path of knowledge was always traced in that of *a purification of the subject, of the percipiens*. "Well — we say that we found the assurance of the subject in his encounter with *the filth* (*la saloperie*) *that may support him — with the petit a, of which it is not illegitimate to say that its presence is necessary.*" Think of Socrates: *his inflexible purity and his atopia are correlative — intervening at every instant, there is the demonic voice.* Will you say the voice that guides Socrates is not Socrates himself? An enigma, which tempted the psychographers of the early nineteenth century — "great merit on their part to have dared, since now no one would touch it." A new trace for what we mean by *the subject of perception*. "Do not make me say what I do not say — the analyst is not obliged to hear voices. But all the same": read Theodor Reik, direct and familiar pupil of Freud, *Listening with the Third Ear* — "I do not approve the formula, as if two were not enough to be deaf with" — yet he maintains that this third ear serves him to hear some voice that speaks to him to warn him of *deceptions*: he is of the good epoch, the heroic epoch, *when one knew how to hear what speaks behind the patient's deception*. "We have certainly done better since, because *we* know how to recognize in these twists, these cleavages, the objet a — assuredly still barely emerged." The close reverses the entire epistemological tradition in four sentences: where philosophy purified the percipiens (Descartes scrubbing the subject to a point), analysis *founds* the subject on his encounter with the residue — the voice, the filth, the *a* — Socrates' daimon standing as the exemplary case: absolute purity *correlative* with the object-voice, atopia and daimonion one structure. The second identification — separation's, with the *a* — is thereby the anti-purification: not becoming the ideal, but assuming the leftover.

**Réponses.** Kaufmann asks the relation among Booz, Reik, and what Lacan said of the father at the opening of Chapter VII of the *Traumdeutung*. — "It is quite clear. *He is asleep*, that's all. He is asleep so that we may be asleep with him — that is, so that we understand only what is to be understood." Lacan wanted to bring in the Jewish tradition to take things up where Freud left them: "it is not for nothing, all the same, that *the pen fell from Freud's hands on the division of the subject* \[the unfinished *Splitting of the Ego*\], and that just before, with *Moses and Monotheism*, he had made the most radical putting-in-question of the Jewish tradition." Whatever the historically contestable character of its supports, to introduce at the heart of Jewish history the radical, absolutely evident distinction of *the prophetic tradition* from another message was — as Freud was aware, and wrote in every way — *to make collusion with the truth a function essential to our operation as analysts. And precisely, we can trust ourselves to it, consecrate ourselves to it, only insofar as we dethrone ourselves of all collusion with the truth.* The answer strings the burning-child father (asleep so that we sleep), Booz (asleep under the trans-biological promise), and Moses into one line — the dead-or-sleeping father as the frame of transmission — and mints, in passing, the analyst's paradoxical ethic: serve the truth-function by renouncing every collusion with truth, the exact negative of the *sujet supposé savoir* he occupies.

And, "since we are somewhat among familiars," the amusing observation from that morning: in a normal scientific career it is encumbering to be the study-master or lab-chief of an *agrégé* whose ideas you must accommodate for your advancement — most encumbering for scientific thought. "Well\! there is a field, that of analysis, where if anywhere the subject is there only to seek his habilitation for *free* research in the sense of a veridical exigency, and can consider himself authorized only from the moment he operates freely there. Well\! by a sort of singular vertigo-effect, it is *there* that they will try to reconstitute, to the maximum, the hierarchy of university habilitation, and make their aggregation depend on another already aggregated. It goes even further: when they have found their path, their mode of thought, their very way of moving in the analytic field, *from the teaching of a certain person*, it is *from others, whom they consider imbeciles*, that they will try to obtain the authorization, the express qualification, that they are indeed capable of practicing analysis. One more illustration of the difference and the conjunctions, the ambiguities, between the analytic field and the university field. If one says that *the analysts themselves form part of the problem of the unconscious* — does that not seem to you a fine illustration, and a fine occasion to analyze?" The closing anecdote is autobiography barely veiled — his own students seeking accreditation from the society that expelled him — and it seals Lesson X's thesis (the analysts as the *caput mortuum*, the presence of the analyst inside the concept of the unconscious) with the argument that would become, in 1967, the *proposition* on the pass: authorization by hierarchy is the vertigo of a field whose only authorization is the free operation itself.

## **It Remains to Conclude**

### **XX. In You More Than You**

*24 June 1964\.* The last lesson. Its epigraph — set by Miller where the keyword-blocks stood — is the sentence the lesson itself will forge, and the seminar's most famous: *I love you, but, because inexplicably I love in you something more than you — the objet petit a — I mutilate you.* The lesson gathers everything: the question of imposture (analysis between religion and science); the "liquidation" of transference dismantled; the fable of the Chinese restaurant; hypnosis as the confusion of I and *a*, analysis as their maintained distance — "an inverted hypnosis"; the traversal of the fantasy; and, against the dark God of the sacrifice, the closing definition of the desire of the analyst.

**The order of truth; imposture.** It remains to conclude the discourse held in these precincts "by reason of circumstances that presentified, in the course of my teaching, something which, after all, one of the fundamental notions advanced here accounts for — that of *dustuchia*, the ill-encounter." The suspended step — the Names-of-the-Father — had to yield to the question at stake since the beginning of this teaching: *what is the order of truth that our praxis engenders?* What can make us certain of our praxis is what has been given here as base-concepts under the four rubrics — the unconscious, repetition, transference, and the drive, "whose sketch, you have seen, I was led to include within my exploration of transference." And the question transposes into the esoteric formula: *how can we be assured that we are not in imposture?* The opening move closes the ring of Lesson I with complete formality: the excommunication itself is filed under the seminar's own concept (the missed encounter), and the authorization-question of the first session returns as the last — no longer "by what am I authorized?" but "how assure ourselves we are not impostors?" — the question that, Lacan says, hangs in suspense not only in opinion but *in the intimate life of every psychoanalyst*, a contained, excluded, ambiguous presence against which the analyst ramparts himself with ceremonies, forms, and rites (Lesson XVIII's "simulation" again).

Imposture is the opening onto analysis's relation with *religion*, and thereby with science. The eighteenth century — the man of the Enlightenment, "who was also the man of pleasure" — put religion in question as fundamental imposture; who today would take what touches religion under that simplistic parenthesis? To the ends of the earth, even where the struggle is waged against it, religion today enjoys universal respect. And the question is also that of *belief*, presentified by us in less simplistic terms: we have the practice of the fundamental alienation in which all belief sustains itself — that double subjective term by which *it is at the moment the signification of belief seems most profoundly to vanish that the being of the subject comes to light from what was, properly speaking, the reality of that belief*. Conquering "superstition" does not temper its effects in being — whence our difficulty in recognizing what, in the sixteenth century, the status of genuine *unbelief* could have been: we are, in our epoch, "incomparably and paradoxically disarmed"; our only rampart — the religious have admirably sensed it — is that *indifference* in matters of religion, as Lamennais says, *whose status is precisely the position of science*. Lesson XVIII's Casanova-doctrine is here scaled up to the history of secularization: belief is not refuted but *realized* at the point of its vanishing signification (Febvre's famous problem of sixteenth-century unbelief cited by implication), and modern unbelief is not a stronger negation but a different structure — the scientific separation.

For it is insofar as science *elides, eludes, sections* a determinate field in the dialectic of the subject's alienation — insofar as science situates itself at the precise point defined as *separation* — that it can sustain the mode of existence of the man of science: his style, his mores, his mode of discourse, the precautions by which he shelters from the questions bearing on the very status of the science he serves. And the greater problem is the status of *the body of scientific acquisition*: "we shall conceive its bearing only by recognizing that it is, in the subjective relation, *the equivalent of what I have called the objet petit a*." The two sentences carry a whole epistemology, continuous with Lessons XII and XVII: science is institutionalized separation (the subject's fading sectioned off — the scientist protected from his own question), and the accumulating corpus of science functions in the culture as *object a* — cause of desire, remainder, the un-integrable thing "in us more than us" at civilization's scale. Whence the persisting ambiguity about what in analysis is reducible to science: it implies *a beyond of science* in the modern, Cartesian sense — by which analysis could fall under a classification ranking it with what its forms and history so often evoke: *a Church, and hence a religion*.

The only way to broach it: religion, among man's modes of posing the question of his existence — and beyond, as mode of subsistence of the self-interrogating subject — distinguishes itself by a dimension proper to it *and struck with forgetting*: in every religion meriting the name there is an essential dimension of reserving *something operative, called a sacrament*. Ask the faithful, or the priests: what differentiates confirmation from baptism? — for if it is a sacrament, if *it operates*, it operates on something; where it washes sins, where it renews some pact ("I put the question-mark: is it a pact? is it something else? what passes through this dimension?") — in all the answers we shall always find that mark by which is evoked *the beyond of religion, operative and magical*. And within religion, for perfectly defined reasons — separation, the impotence of our reason, our finitude — *that* is what is marked with forgetting. It is insofar as analysis, with respect to the foundation of its own status, finds itself struck with *a similar forgetting* that it comes to be marked, in ceremony, with "what I shall call the same empty face." The structural comparison is exact and double-edged: religion's operative core (the sacrament) is what religion itself forgets; analysis's operative core — about to be named as the desire of the analyst and the *a* — undergoes the same forgetting, and the same ceremonial blankness results (the rites of Lesson XVIII). The analogy analysis/Church holds precisely at the point of the shared amnesia.

*But analysis is not a religion. It proceeds from the same status as La science. It engages itself in the central lack where the subject experiences himself as desire... in the gap opened at the center of the dialectic of the subject and the Other. It has nothing to forget, for it implies no recognition of any substance on which it claims to operate — not even that of sexuality.* And the startling elaboration: on sexuality, in fact, *analysis operates very little*: it has taught us nothing new about the sexual operation — "not even a scrap of erotological technique has come out of it; there is more on that score in the least of those much-reprinted books that come to us from the depths of an Arab, Hindu, Chinese, even on occasion our own tradition. Psychoanalysis touches sexuality only insofar as, in the form of the drive, it manifests itself in the defile of the signifier, where the dialectic of the subject is constituted in the double time of alienation and separation. Analysis has not kept, on the field of sexuality, the promises one might mistakenly have expected of it — it has not kept them because it does not have to keep them. It is not its terrain.\*" The passage is the seminar's last great act of demarcation and the definitive gloss on Lesson XII's title: analysis is not a science *of* sexuality (no ars erotica — the Kama Sutra outdoes it) but of sexuality's passage through the signifier's defiles; no substance anywhere, hence nothing to forget, hence not a religion — the whole difference resting on the pre-ontological discipline held since Lesson III.

On its own terrain, however, analysis distinguishes itself by "that extraordinary power of errancy and confusion which makes of its literature something that, I assure you, will need very little distance to be filed, entire, under the rubric of the *fous littéraires*" — the literary madmen. Even a sympathetic book — Bergler's *Basic Neurosis*, with its "je ne sais quoi of cheekiness," assembling numerous observations certainly locatable in practice — goes astray: what Bergler brings on the function of *the breast* is lost in a vain topical debate on the superiority of man over woman and woman over man — "matters which, for raising the most passional elements, are also, concerning what is at stake, of the least interest." For want of a sufficient location of the partial object's proper function, the book, interesting in itself, is vowed to an errancy that brings its result to the edge of nullity. The example is chosen to pivot: everything now turns on *the function of the objet a* in the movement of analysis.

**"Liquidation of the transference" dismantled; the sentence.** The *objet a* signals itself, in the very process sustained by transference, by a special status. Everyone has in his mouth, "without absolutely knowing what he means," the term *liquidation of the transference*. What can it mean? To what accountancy does "liquidation" refer — or is it some operation in an alembic: *it has to flow, and drain off somewhere*? If transference is the putting-into-action of the unconscious, does one mean transference could liquidate *the unconscious* — do we no longer have an unconscious after an analysis? Or is it *the subject supposed to know* that should be liquidated as such? "It would be singular all the same that this subject supposed to know — supposed to know something of you, and who in fact knows nothing of it — could be considered liquidated at the very moment when, at the end of the analysis, he begins, about you at least, to know a bit. It is thus at the moment he would take on the most consistency that the subject supposed to know would have to be supposed vaporized." If "liquidation" has a sense, it can only be *the permanent liquidation of that deception by which the transference tends to be exercised in the direction of the closure of the unconscious* — the mechanism explained by the narcissistic relation in which the subject makes himself lovable object, inducing the Other into a mirage-relation where he convinces him of being lovable. The argument is a small logical gem aimed at the standard doctrine of termination: the supposition of knowledge cannot dissolve when it is at last (partially) *true*; what ends is not the supposition but the love-deception it powered — and Freud designates that deception's "natural outcome" in *identification*.

The identification in question — Freud articulates it "with much finesse" in the two chapters of *Massenpsychologie* already flagged, "Identification" and "Being-in-Love and Hypnosis" — *is not specular, immediate identification: it is its support*. It supports the perspective *chosen by the subject in the field of the Other* from which specular identification can be seen under a satisfying aspect: *the point of the ego-ideal is that from which the subject will see himself, as one says, as seen by the other* — permitting him to sustain himself in a dual situation satisfying from the point of view of love. As specular mirage, *love has the essence of deception*: situated in the field instituted at the level of the pleasure-reference, of that sole signifier necessary to introduce a perspective centered on the ideal point, capital I, placed somewhere in the Other, *from which the Other sees me, in the form in which it pleases me to be seen*. Lesson XIX's mirror-machinery is here given its final compact statement — I(A) commanding i(a), love as the mirage strung between them — as the ground against which the paradox stands out: in this very convergence to which analysis is called by transference's face of deception, *something is encountered that is paradox — the discovery of the analyst* \[the analyst's discovery: the *a*\], comprehensible only at the other level, that of alienation.

Then the sentence, "presentified in a more syncopated way": *the analysand says, in sum, to his partner, to the analyst: I love you, but, because inexplicably I love in you something more than you — the objet petit a — I mutilate you.* There is the sense of the *mammal complex*, whose relation to the oral drive Bergler sees well — "except that the orality in question has absolutely nothing to do with food, and its whole accent is in this effect of *mutilation*." And the pendant: *I give myself to you, the patient says again — but this gift of my person, as the other says — mystery\! — changes inexplicably into the gift of a shit* (*cadeau d'une merde*) — "a term equally essential to our experience." When this turn is obtained at the term of interpretative elucidation, one understands retroactively the vertigo of the *blank page* in a certain gifted personage hooked at the edge of the psychotic — the page where his ineffable intellectual effusions halt, which he literally cannot touch, "because he can apprehend it only as toilet paper." The sentence and its pendant are the seminar's whole doctrine of love-in-transference in two lines: love aims past the beloved at the *a* lodged in him ("in you more than you"), and therefore loves as *mutilation* — extraction of the organ-piece; while the counter-gift of self collapses into the anal object (Lessons VIII, XV) — the sublime and the excremental exchanging places at a stroke, with the writer's blank page as clinical exhibit.

**The Chinese restaurant.** How to image the incidence of this ever-refound object in the movement of the transference? The fable begun "the other day in a more intimate circle," now given its end. What happens when the subject begins to speak to the analyst — to the subject supposed to know, but of whom it is certain he as yet knows nothing? To him is offered something that will first, necessarily, form itself as *demand* — "who does not know that this is what oriented all thought on analysis in the direction of a recognition of the function of frustration?" But *what* does the subject demand? — that is the whole question, "for the subject knows very well that whatever his appetites, whatever his needs, none will find satisfaction there, except at most *by organizing its menu*." In the Épinal image of childhood, the poor beggar at the cookshop door feasts on the aroma of the roast: here *the aroma is the menu — signifiers, since one does nothing but speak*. And the complication that makes the fable: *the menu is written in Chinese*. First time: order the translation from the *patronne* — imperial pâté, spring rolls, and the rest. If it is your first visit, the translation may tell you no more, and you finally ask the *patronne*: *Advise me* — "which means: *what do I desire in all this? — it is for you to know.*" But is it really supposed that so paradoxical a situation should end there? At this point, where you hand yourself over to the divination of the *patronne* whose importance you have watched swell and swell — "would it not be more adequate, if your heart tells you, and if the thing presents itself advantageously, to go tickle her breasts a bit? For one does not go to a Chinese restaurant only to eat, but to eat *in the dimensions of the exotic*: if my fable means anything, it is insofar as alimentary desire has *another sense* than alimentation — it is here the support and symbol of the *sexual* dimension, the only one to be rejected from the psyche. The drive, in its relation to the partial object, is subjacent there." The apologue compresses Lessons XI–XV into a single scene: demand (the order), the treasury of the Other (the untranslatable menu), the swelling of the *sujet supposé savoir* ("advise me" \= "you know my desire"), and the pivot where demand's true object shows itself as the drive's partial object — the *patronne*'s breast, not the food: desire supported on demand but aimed elsewhere, at the body of the Other.

"Paradoxical, even flippant, as this little apologue may seem, it is exactly what is at stake in the reality of analysis. It is not enough that the analyst support the function of Tiresias: *he must also, as Apollinaire says, have breasts*" (*qu'il ait des mamelles* — *Les Mamelles de Tirésias*). Meaning: *the operation and maneuver of the transference are to be regulated in a way that maintains the distance between the point from which the subject sees himself lovable — and that other point where the subject sees himself caused as lack by the a, and where the a comes to plug the gap constituted by the inaugural division of the subject. The petit a never crosses that gap.* Refer to the gaze, the most characteristic term for the *a*'s proper function: *the a presents itself, in the field of the mirage of the narcissistic function of desire, precisely as the unswallowable object that remains stuck in the throat of the signifier. It is at this point of lack that the subject has to recognize himself.* The technical doctrine is now complete: the analyst must occupy both places at once — Tiresias (the knower of both sexes, the I-point of supposition) *and* the bearer of the breast (the *a*, object of the drive) — and the whole art is the maintained *distance* between them; while the *a* itself receives its last and best epithet: the bone in the signifier's throat, at which point — not at the ideal — the subject must recognize himself.

Whence the topologization of transference by the *interior eight* of the *Identification* seminar: the double curve folding back on itself, each half coming to lie along every point of the preceding half. As a plane defined by the cut, paper and a few glued tabs give a precise idea: the lobe this surface constitutes at its point of return covers another lobe, the two continuing by a form of border — no contradiction even in ordinary space, provided one abstracts from three dimensions, since only a topological reality limited to the function of a surface is at stake; one part of the plane, where the other returns upon it by its edge, determines *a sort of intersection*. This intersection has a sense outside our space, structurally definable without reference to three dimensions, by a certain relation of the surface to itself as, returning on itself, *it traverses itself* at a point to be determined: *this line of traversal is, for us, what can symbolize the function of identification*. On the board, the labeled schema: *D, the line of demand; I, the line of intersection, "identification"; T, the point of transference; d, desire.* By the very work that leads the subject, in saying himself in analysis, to orient his speech along the resistance of the transference — deception of love as well as of aggression — something is produced whose closure-value is marked in the very form of *the spiral developing toward a center*: the edge comes back onto the plane constituted by the locus of the Other, from the place where the subject, realizing himself in his speech, institutes himself at the level of the subject supposed to know. The topology is the argument: the analysand's spiraling speech, wound by transference toward identification (the self-traversal line I), would close there — *unless* something pulls the other way.

Hence the verdict: *every conception of analysis that articulates — "and God knows it articulates itself with innocence" — the end of analysis as identification with the analyst, thereby avows its limits. Every analysis doctrined as having to end by identification with the analyst reveals, by the same stroke, that its true motor is elided. There is a beyond of this identification, and this beyond is defined by the relation and distance of the objet petit a to the idealizing capital I of identification.* Balint's termination-doctrine (Lessons XI, XVI) receives here its formal refutation: identification is a *line* on the surface, a crossing-point — not the curve's closure; taking it for the end erases the motor (the analyst's desire, the *a*).

**Hypnosis inverted; the traversal of the fantasy.** The detail of practice implied cannot be entered; refer to Freud's chapter "Being-in-Love and Hypnosis," where Freud excellently distinguishes from hypnosis the state of love, even in its extreme forms (*Verliebtheit*), giving the most accented doctrinal location to what can be read everywhere else, "if one knows how to read." *There is an essential difference between the object defined as narcissistic — the i(a) — and the function of the a.* Things stand such that the mere sight of Freud's schema of hypnosis gives at the same time *the formula of collective fascination, an ascending reality at the hour he wrote*. Freud's schema — reproduced on the board — designates the *object* ("in which you must recognize what I call the a"), the ego, and the ego-ideal, the curves marking *the conjunction of the a with the ego-ideal*: Freud gives hypnosis its status *by superposing at the same place the objet a as such and the signifying reference-point of the ego-ideal*. And the elements to understand it are already given: the *a* can be identical to *the gaze* — Freud points the knot of hypnosis precisely in formulating that the object is an element "assuredly hard to grasp, but incontestable": *the gaze of the hypnotizer*. Recall the whole function of the gaze and its fundamental relations to the stain: that there is already in the world something that looks before there is a view to see it; that the ocellus of mimicry is the indispensable presupposition of the fact that a subject can see and be fascinated; *that the fascination of the stain is anterior to the view that discovers it* — whence the gaze-function in hypnosis "can be filled, in sum, by a crystal stopper, or anything at all, provided it shines." *To define hypnosis by the confusion, at one point, of the ideal signifier in which the subject locates himself with the a — that is the most assured structural definition that has been advanced.* The convergence is the seminar's crowning identification: Part Two's gaze-doctrine, Part Four's I/a distinction, and Freud's 1921 crowd-psychology (with its "ascending reality" — the fascist mass a year before the March on Rome, and everything after) locked into one formula: hypnosis — and the hypnotized crowd — is I and *a* collapsed into one place; the shining object installed at the point of the ideal.

"Now, who does not know that it was by distinguishing itself from hypnosis that analysis instituted itself? *For the fundamental mainspring of the analytic operation is the maintenance of the distance between the I and the a.*" The reference-formulas: *if the transference is what, of the drive, sets demand apart* (*écarte la demande de la pulsion*), *the desire of the analyst is what brings it back there* (*l'y ramène*). *And by this way, it isolates the a, puts it at the greatest possible distance from the I that the analyst is called by the subject to incarnate. It is from this idealization that the analyst has to fall* (*déchoir*) *in order to be the support of the separating a* (*le support de l'a séparateur*), *insofar as his desire permits him, in an inverted hypnosis* (*une hypnose à l'envers*), *to incarnate, himself, the hypnotized.*" The definition of analysis is thus a chiasmus of hypnosis, and every term earns its place: the hypnotist fascinates by occupying I-and-*a* fused; the analyst is summoned to the I, *falls* from it, and occupies the *a* — the shining object's place, but as separator, not fascinator — becoming "the hypnotized" (the one who lends his gaze rather than casts it). And: *this crossing of the plane of identification is possible. Every one of those who have lived through the analytic experience with me to the end, in the training analysis, knows that what I say is true.* The personal attestation — unique in the seminar — marks that the claim is not speculative: the pass beyond identification has been made.

*It is beyond the function of the a that the curve closes, where it is never spoken of, concerning the exit from analysis: namely, after the locating of the subject in relation to the a, the experience of the fundamental fantasy becomes the drive.* What then becomes of the one who has passed through the experience of this opaque relation to the origin, to the drive? *How can a subject who has traversed the radical fantasy live the drive? That is the beyond of analysis, and it has never been broached.* It is at present approachable only at the level of the analyst, insofar as it would be demanded of him to have traversed in its totality the cycle of the analytic experience. *There is only one psychoanalysis: the training analysis* (*la psychanalyse didactique*) — meaning one that has looped this loop to its term. And the loop must be traversed *several times* — there is no other way to account for *durcharbeiten*, working-through, than to conceive how the loop must be run more than once ("I shall not treat it here; it introduces new difficulties, and I cannot say everything, since only the foundations are at stake"). The schema left "as guide of the experience as well as of the reading" indicates: *transference exercises itself in the direction of bringing demand back to identification; it is insofar as the desire of the analyst, which remains an x, tends in the exactly contrary direction — that the crossing of the plane of identification is possible, by the intermediary of the separation of the subject in the experience. The experience of the subject is thus brought back to the plane where, from the reiteration of the unconscious, the drive can presentify itself.* Here, in six sentences, is the seminar's legacy-doctrine, the one its whole posterity has worked over: the *traversal of the fantasy* (the formula's first appearance); the fantasy's conversion into the drive as the never-said beyond of analysis; the analyst's desire as an *x* — circumscribed, never named (Lesson XIX) — pulling against identification; the reduction of all analysis to the training analysis, loop run more than once; and *durcharbeiten* given, at last, a structural reason.

**The dark God, Spinoza, Kant; the final definition.** The interest has already been indicated of situating at the level of the *objet a* what man has, for three centuries, defined in science. Perhaps the traits appearing today so glaringly under the aspect of the *mass media*, perhaps our very relation to a science that ever more invades our field, are illuminated by reference to those two objects whose place in a fundamental tetrad was indicated: *the voice — quasi-planetarized, even stratospherized, by our apparatuses — and the gaze, whose invasive character is no less suggestive: for by so many spectacles, so many fantasies, it is not so much our vision that is solicited as the gaze that is aroused.* The two-sentence media-theory (radio-voice, screen-gaze — the *a*\-objects industrialized) is left "elided" to accent something "quite essential": *there is something profoundly masked in the critique of the history we have lived: presentifying the most monstrous and supposedly surpassed forms of the holocaust — the drama of Nazism.* And the thesis, weighed word by word: *I hold that no sense of history, founded on Hegeliano-Marxist premises, is capable of accounting for this resurgence — by which it is proved that the offering to dark gods* (*dieux obscurs*) *of an object of sacrifice is something to which few subjects can avoid succumbing, in a monstrous capture.* Ignorance, indifference, the averted gaze may explain under what veil the mystery remains hidden; but for whoever can direct toward the phenomenon a courageous gaze — "and, once more, there are few, assuredly, who do not succumb to the fascination of the sacrifice in itself" — *the sacrifice signifies that, in the object of our desires, we try to find the witness of the presence of the desire of that Other I here call the dark God.* The page is the seminar's gravest, and its placement is the argument: the theory of the *a* and the Other's desire, built across twenty lessons, is here made to bear on the Shoah — against every progressivist dialectic (the "Hegeliano-Marxist premises" refused a third time) — as the structure of sacrifice: the object offered so that the Other's desire may be made to exist; fascination (the hypnosis-formula just given) naming the capture to which "few can avoid succumbing," the seminar's ethics condensed into the demand for a *courageous gaze*.

The eternal sense of sacrifice, "which no one can resist, unless animated by that faith so difficult to sustain, which perhaps only one man has been able to formulate plausibly — *Spinoza, with the Amor intellectualis Dei*." What was wrongly called his pantheism *is nothing else than the reduction of the field of God to the universality of the signifier* — from which proceeds a serene, exceptional detachment with respect to human desire: insofar as Spinoza says *desire is the essence of man* and institutes desire in the radical dependence of the universality of the divine attributes — thinkable only through the function of the signifier — he attains that unique position by which the philosopher ("and it is not indifferent that it should be *a Jew detached from his tradition* who incarnated it") can merge with a transcendent love. *This position is not tenable for us. Experience shows that Kant is more true* — and *Kant avec Sade* proved that his theory of conscience, of practical reason, sustains itself only by giving a specification of the moral law which, examined closely, *is nothing other than desire in the pure state* — the very desire that culminates in the sacrifice, properly speaking, *of everything that is the object of love in its human tenderness — I say: not only the rejection of the pathological object, but its sacrifice and its murder*. "That is why I wrote *Kant avec Sade*. There is the example of the eye-opening effect (*effet de désillement*) that analysis permits on so many efforts, even the noblest, of traditional ethics." The two philosophers close the ring opened in Lessons I and III: Spinoza — the seminar's first excommunicate, the *herem* — refused *not* as false but as untenable: his intellectual love of God is the one exit from sacrifice, purchased by reducing God to the signifier's universality, a serenity we cannot hold; Kant confirmed as "more true" in the terrible sense — the pure moral law unmasked as pure desire, sacrificial to the point of murdering the pathological object — so that traditional ethics' noblest form and the dark God's altar stand revealed as neighbors.

A limit-position, permitting us to grasp *that man cannot sketch his situation in a field of refound knowledge except by having first traversed the limit where, as desire, he is chained. Love, which some have thought we degraded* (*ravalé*), *can be posited only in that beyond where, first, it renounces its object.* And this is what permits understanding that *every shelter in which a livable, tempered relation of one sex to the other can be instituted requires the intervention — it is the teaching of psychoanalysis — of that medium which is the paternal metaphor.* The suspended seminar signs the conclusion: the Name-of-the-Father, forbidden as a title, returns as the last mediating term — the condition of any tempered sexual bond — while love is rehabilitated exactly where Lesson XV's critics thought it demolished: as what can exist only beyond its object, past the renunciation.

Then the final paragraph, entire: *The desire of the analyst is not a pure desire. It is a desire to obtain absolute difference* (*la différence absolue*), *the one that intervenes when, confronted with the primordial signifier, the subject comes for the first time into the position of subjecting himself to it* (*de s'y assujettir*). *There alone can arise the signification of a limitless love, because it is outside the limits of the law, where alone it can live.* Every clause pays a debt. "Not a pure desire": against Kant just convicted (pure desire \= sacrifice) — the analyst's desire is impure, circumscribed, an *x*. "Absolute difference": the contrary of identification — not that the subject become like the analyst, or like his ideal, but that he stand, for the first time, before the primordial signifier (the S1 of Lessons XVI–XVII, the senseless, subjecting mark) *as* subjected — assuming the determination instead of being lived by it: separation completed. And the last sentence gives love its final status, unannounced and unprepared except by everything: a limitless love is signifiable only outside the law's limits — beyond the objects, beyond the ideal, beyond the sacrifice — the point where the seminar, having dismantled love as deception for eleven lessons, restores it as the horizon of the analytic act itself. The session — and the seminar — ends there, 24 June 1964; no Réponses are recorded.

## **Notice and Postface**

**Miller's Notice.** The half-page that closes the transcription states its principle: "we wished here to count for nothing, and to procure, of the spoken work of Jacques Lacan, *the transcription that will be authoritative and will stand, in the future, for the original — which does not exist*." The stenography cannot count as original — mishearings swarm in it, nothing supplies gesture and intonation — yet it is the sine qua non version, gauged and corrected word by word, "the discard not amounting to three pages." The most delicate task was *inventing a punctuation*, "since every scansion — comma, period, dash, paragraph — decides the sense"; a readable text was had at that price, and the same principles will govern all the years of the seminar. The Notice deserves commentary because it states, with disarming candor, the paradox every reader of this book inherits: the "original" of a spoken teaching does not exist; the authoritative text is an invention (above all of punctuation, which "decides the sense") declared to *stand for* an absent original — Miller's editorship assuming, in miniature, the very structure of the *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz* the seminar expounds: a stand-in holding the place of what was never there as text.

**Lacan's Postface** (dated 1 January 1973, contemporary with *Encore*). Nine years after the seminar, in the compacted, punning style of the last period, Lacan wagers on the book's fate: *"Thus will this volume be read — I bet."* Not like the *Écrits*, which sell, "they say — but that is so as not to read them"; and not because they are difficult: *"a written text, in my sense, is made not to be read"* (*fait pour ne pas se lire*) — "it says something else." What one has just read is therefore *not* a written text: it is — a word discovered "thanks to the modesty of J.A.M., Jacques-Alain, Miller by name" — a *transcription*: "what is read passes through the writing and remains intact there." And what *is read* is exactly what Lacan speaks of, "since what I say is vowed to the unconscious — that is, to what is read before all else." Must he insist? "Naturally: since here I am not writing. Were I to, I would post-*efface* my seminar, not postface it." The play (postfacer/posteffacer) carries the doctrine: the postface's whole argument is a late-Lacanian theory of the difference between the *écrit* (not-to-be-read, Joycean — "it is Joyce who introduces the written as not-to-read... everywhere equally little to be read") and the *dire*, the act of saying, which the transcription lets pass through intact. Credit is rendered to Miller for having convinced him, "his course \[on the seminar\] enduring," that *what is read of what I say is read no less from the fact that I say it — the accent to be put on the saying* (*le dire*), "for the *I* can still run."

The middle of the text re-litigates reading itself. Against the school's alphabetization ("one learns to read by being alpha-bêtified" — *s'alphabêtissant*, alphabet crossed with *bête*), the child who learns that giraffe and monkey are both written with G learns that the letter "has nothing to do with being read, since it does not answer to it"; dysorthography is judgeable only by taking *the function of the written as another mode of the speaking being in language*. It would already be something if *se lire* — reading oneself/being read — were understood properly *"there where one has the duty to interpret"*: that speech is precisely where what it says is *not read* is what the analyst startles at, after the era when he puffed himself up with "giving ear until he could no longer stand." And the Lemberg joke of Lesson XI returns for its last service: the "why do you lie to me by telling the truth?" story shows that the recourse by which *Lemberg is read in place of Cracow* is the railway timetable — precisely *not* a reading-book; "but the function of the written then makes not the timetable but the very rail of the track. *And the objet (a) as I write it — it is the rail by which what inhabits, indeed shelters, the demand to be interpreted comes to the plus-de-jouir*" — the surplus-enjoyment, the term of the intervening years grafted here onto the seminar's object. The sentence is the postface's doctrinal payload: the *a* re-described, in 1973 vocabulary, as the *rail* — not the signpost to be read but the track on which interpretation's demand runs toward surplus-jouissance — the seminar's object-cause retrofitted with the political economy of *jouissance* developed in Seminars XVI–XX.

Then the Japanese excursus: a Japanese woman was beside herself at the "impudence" imputed to him of having made the word his measure; he understood only later what the sensibility receives there from a writing that, from *on-yomi* to *kun-yomi* \[the double Chinese and Japanese readings of each character\], "reverberates the signifier to the point that it is torn by so many refractions" — the least newspaper, the crossroads sign sustaining it; nothing helps so much to remake the rays that, streaming through so many sluices, brought to daylight what came from the source through Amaterasu. "To the point that I told myself the speaking being can there subtract himself from the artifices of the unconscious — which do not reach him, from his closing himself to them. A limit-case, to confirm me." (The claim — Japan as the limit-case of a subject without the Western unconscious, by virtue of its writing — is the postface's most contested legacy, elaborated the same year in "Avis au lecteur japonais.") Follows the address to the reader: *"You do not understand my writing. So much the better: that will give you a reason to explain it. And if it remains unresolved, you will be quits with the embarrassment"* — a word, he reminds us, restored to its fate in the seminar on anxiety, the year before what is printed here: "which is to tell you that one does not rid oneself of it as easily as of me." Rereading "what was my speech," what strikes him is *"the sureness that preserved me from talking nonsense with respect to what came to me since"* — the risk each time seems entire, "and that is what makes my fatigue"; that J.A.M. spared him this lets him think it will be nothing for the reader — and also makes him believe that if he escapes, it is that "of the written I have more than I be-lieve" (*j'écrois* — écrire folded into croire).

The final page returns to Genesis and the real. For us who *écroyons* less than in Japan, what the text of Genesis imposes is that *ex nihilo nothing is created there but of the signifier* — "which goes without saying, since indeed it is worth no more." The inconvenience: that *existence* depends on it — that of which only the saying is witness; "that God proves himself by it should long ago have put him back in his place" — the one the Bible poses as not myth but history, "in which the gospel according to Marx does not distinguish itself from our others." The dreadful thing is that the relation from which the whole affair is fomented concerns nothing but *jouissance*, and that the interdict religion projects onto it — sharing with the panic from which philosophy proceeds at this spot — makes a crowd of *substances* surge up as substitutes for the only proper one: *"that of the impossible-that-one-speak-of-it, from its being the real."* Could this "stance-from-underneath" (*stance-par-en-dessous* — substance dismembered) not deliver itself more accessibly in that form for which the written of the *poem* already makes the saying least stupid? Is that not worth constructing, "if it is indeed what I presume of promised land for this new discourse that analysis is?" — not that the *relation* could ever be expected from it, "of which I say that it is its *absence* that makes the speaking being's access to the real," but *"the artifice of the channels by which jouissance comes to cause what is read as the world — that, one will agree, is what makes it worthwhile that what is read of it avoid the onto- — Toto, take note — the onto-, indeed the ontotautology. No less than here."* The close is a signature across the nine years: the seminar's pre-ontological discipline (Lesson III) reappears as the mockery of "ontotautology"; "the real is the impossible" (Lesson XIII) returns as the impossible-to-speak-of; "there is no sexual relation" — the *Encore*\-thesis — is stated in passing as the absence that gives access to the real; and Genesis's creation by the signifier (Lesson XVII's Hebrew letters) is reduced to its just worth. The last three words — *pas moins qu'ici*, "no less than here" — bet the book itself against ontology: this transcription too, if it is read as it asks to be, avoids the trap of being; it will have been, like the teaching it stands for, a saying and not a substance.
